# PHASE 11: Architecture Patterns and Low-Level Design

> **What Phase 11 is for.** Phases 1–10 made you a competent engineer. Phase 11 makes you the person on the team who can *reason about structure* — who can say why the order service should not import the HTTP layer, why `singleton` is a smell, why the read path and write path should be the same table on day one and probably different tables on day one thousand. This is the phase where interviewers stop asking "does it work" and start asking "why is it shaped like that."

> **How to use this file.** Read 11.1 and 11.2 completely; they are load-bearing. Skim 11.3 but be able to defend every row of the decision framework in 11.3.13, because that table is the single most-likely-to-be-asked artifact in this phase. Drill 11.4 until you can draw every class diagram from memory in under three minutes. Then work through the walkthroughs, then implement the ten problems cold, then critique the twelve broken designs, then run the Q&A and the rapid-fire.

🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** for the whole of 11.1.2 (Singleton), 11.1.8 (Observer), 11.1.10 (Strategy), 11.1.11 (State), 11.2.3 (SOLID), 11.2.2 (composition over inheritance), 11.3.1 (monolith), 11.3.5 (monolith→microservices), 11.4.1 (parking lot), 11.4.2 (LRU cache).

---

## 11.1 Design Patterns

### 11.1.1 What a pattern is, and the three things every pattern has (intent, participants, consequences)

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A design pattern is a named, reusable solution to a recurring design problem. It is not a library, not a language feature, and — critically — not code you copy. It is *vocabulary plus structure plus a documented cost*. When a reviewer says "that's a Strategy here," they are not commenting on your code style; they are naming a known trade-off so the next person does not have to rediscover it.

The formal GoF definition (Gamma, Erich, Helm, Johnson, Vlissides, 1994) has four parts; the three that matter in an interview are the rest:

- **Intent** — what problem it solves, stated in one sentence that does not mention the solution's mechanics. "Allow a client to add new behaviour without changing a class that already uses it." Note the absence of the word "Strategy." A pattern's intent should survive a total rewrite of its implementation.
- **Participants** — the collaborating classes with the roles they play and, crucially, *which roles are optional*. A pattern's participants include things that are frequently forgotten ("the Subject must also provide attach/detach") and things that are frequently mistaken for requirements ("the ConcreteObserver does not need to know about the Subject").
- **Consequences** — what you gain, what you pay, and the questions the pattern defers. Consequences are the whole point. A pattern whose consequences section says "none, it's just better" is marketing, not engineering.

**Mechanics: the anatomy of a pattern description.** When you explain a pattern in an interview, use the same four beats every time:

1. **The forcing function.** What makes the naive version hurt. (Six subclasses and every new country is a new class? Fifteen constructor arguments and call sites are unreadable?)
2. **The structure.** The participants and who holds references to whom.
3. **The mechanics.** What actually happens at runtime, message by message.
4. **The bill.** Consequences: what it costs, what new problems it creates, when to undo it.

**The four-part vocabulary you should be able to recite.**

| Part | Question it answers | Singleton's answer |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Intent | Why does this pattern exist? | Ensure a class has exactly one instance and provide a global access point. |
| Motivation | What concrete problem in the naive code forces its use? | Two `AppConfig` objects with divergent values; tests that cannot stub the config. |
| Structure | Who knows about whom? | Client → Singleton; Singleton holds private static instance; no one holds a reference to anyone. |
| Consequences | What did I just buy and what did I just pay? | Shared state by construction; harder to test; hidden dependency; lifecycle owned by the class, not the container. |

**The three levels of pattern, and why the distinction matters.** Patterns exist at three altitudes, and mixing them up is a common interview tell:

- **Idioms** — low-level, language-specific, invisible in the diagram. `const` over `let`, a guard clause, early return, a named boolean. Idioms are what a reviewer comments on in a line-level review.
- **Design patterns** — object-level structure. Singleton, Strategy, Observer, Command. These show up in class diagrams and are the subject of 11.1.
- **Architectural patterns** — component-and-connection-level structure. Monolith, event-driven, CQRS, hexagonal. These show up in the *deployment and dependency* diagram rather than the class diagram, and they are the subject of 11.3. A common mistake is citing "Strategy pattern" as an architectural decision; Strategy tells you how to structure a class, not how to deploy a system.

**Why patterns exist at all (the forces they balance).**

- *Change forces.* Some variability in a system changes often (pricing rules, notification channels), some never (the fact that an order has lines). Patterns are mostly about *absorbing variability in the right place* so the stable parts stay stable. Gang of Four catalogued 23 patterns; at least 10 of them exist solely to make one axis of change not ripple into the rest of the program.
- *Knowledge forces.* Someone already burned three weeks making a distributed cache coherent. Encoding their lesson as a pattern means the next team inherits the lesson instead of repeating the burn.
- *Constraint forces.* Some systems genuinely must be built in a certain shape (a protocol state machine, an undo stack). Patterns make the shape explicit rather than emergent.

**A pattern is not automatically correct.** The GoF book's own warning is worth quoting: *knowing the trade-offs is the point; a pattern applied without regard to consequences is a liability.* Section 11.1.18 is entirely about this. Interviewers will penalise you more for applying Observer to a synchronous, three-subscriber, in-process fan-out than they will penalise you for a plain method call.

**Real code: the smallest pattern that is not a pattern.** Start here, because it calibrates your judgment about when structure is warranted.

```ts
// NAIVE: the problem before the pattern. Three call sites, all coupling to the shape of pricing.
function checkout(cart: Cart, userId: string) {
  const vip = lookupVip(userId);            // domain knowledge leaking into the caller
  const subtotal = cart.lines.reduce((s, l) => s + l.price * l.qty, 0);
  const discount = vip ? subtotal * 0.1 : 0; // another "if" waiting to happen
  return persist({ userId, subtotal, discount, total: subtotal - discount });
}

// PATTERN: the variability (which pricing rule) is named and isolated.
// Cost paid: one interface, one file per rule, one extra object per call. Benefit bought:
// adding a rule touches exactly one new file and no existing file.
interface PricingRule {
  readonly name: string;
  apply(cart: Cart, ctx: PricingContext): number;
}
class VipDiscountRule implements PricingRule {
  readonly name = 'vip-discount';
  constructor(private readonly rate: number) {}
  apply(cart: Cart, ctx: PricingContext) {
    return ctx.vip ? cart.subtotal * this.rate : 0;
  }
}
function checkout(cart: Cart, userId: string, rule: PricingRule, ctx: PricingContext) {
  const subtotal = cart.subtotal;
  const discount = rule.apply(cart, ctx);
  return persist({ userId, subtotal, discount, total: subtotal - discount });
}
```

Note the direction of the dependency change: after the refactor, `checkout` does not know that VIPs exist. That is the actual payoff — *inversion of the decision, not extra classes.*

**When to use a pattern vs not.**

- Use it when you have **observed** the same structural change arriving three times, or when the variability is *stated in the requirements as a likely axis of growth* (multi-tenant, multi-currency, multi-channel, multi-region).
- Do not use it because a blog post used it, because it appears in a portfolio, or because you want to look senior.
- Signals you are probably over-applying: your interfaces all have exactly one implementation and no test double and no second feature; your "strategy" is chosen by a `switch` in the factory; your "pattern" doubles the file count without changing a single `if` into a substitutable seam.

**Trade-offs, universally.** Every pattern has (a) an indirection cost at runtime or in reading, (b) a mental cost for the next reader, (c) a *localisation* cost — the logic that used to be in one readable block is now spread across files. The only reason to pay (a)+(b)+(c) is that the thing being abstracted changes faster than the reader can re-learn it.

**Production considerations.**

- Patterns interact badly with **profiling and stack traces**: deep decorator/proxy chains make a production stack trace 40 frames of `ProxyHandler.handle → Decorator.run → …`.
- Patterns interact badly with **serialization**: a `Strategy` stored as a field needs a type discriminator to deserialize; pick names that are stable, not minified or generated.
- Patterns interact badly with **dependency injection**: a good DI container *is* a Strategy/Factory/Command implementation. If you hand-roll a pattern that your container already provides, you are writing a worse version of a maintained library.
- Patterns interact badly with **transactions**: Command, State, and Saga all tempt you to move work across a commit boundary.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Naming a class `XxxManager` or `XxxHelper` and calling it a pattern. `StrategyManager` that switches on a type is not Strategy; it is a switch in a disguise.
2. Pattern without a seam. If there is no interface at the variability boundary, you have not made the axis of change substitutable, only relocated it.
3. Interface with one method but implemented by a class that also does five unrelated things — the interface is a lie about capability.
4. Forgetting the *removal* test: can you delete the pattern and put the code back, and will the diff be smaller than the pattern? If yes, the pattern was not earning its keep.
5. Treating GoF as a checklist. 23 patterns is a menu, not a curriculum.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which of the 23 patterns have you used in production, and what did it cost?"* — have one real answer with a real number.
- *"Give me a pattern that hurt you."* — the best possible answer; do not say "none."
- *"What is the difference between a pattern and a framework?"* — a framework controls the flow of control and your code fills slots; a pattern is a piece of your code that you assemble.
- *"When would you deliberately *not* introduce an interface?"* — when there is exactly one implementation, no test double is needed, and the type is sealed to your own module.

---

### 11.1.2 Singleton and why it is almost always the wrong answer

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Singleton restricts a class to exactly one instance and provides a global point of access to it.

```ts
// The textbook form, and every problem it creates in six lines.
class AppConfig {
  private static instance: AppConfig;
  private constructor(private readonly values: Record<string, string> = {}) {}
  static getInstance(): AppConfig {
    if (!AppConfig.instance) AppConfig.instance = new AppConfig();
    return AppConfig.instance;
  }
  get(key: string) { return this.values[key]; }
}
```

**Mechanics.** Static field, private constructor, static accessor. The invariant is "at most one instance per process/module/realm" and the class is responsible for enforcing it.

**Why it is almost always the wrong answer.** Five distinct problems, and it is worth being able to name all five because interviewers like to distinguish people who *meme* "singleton is bad" from people who can *argue* it:

1. **It is a hidden dependency.** `AppConfig.getInstance()` appears in the middle of a function whose signature promises nothing about configuration. The dependency is invisible to the reader, to the type system, and to every tool that traces calls. Compare `new BillingService(config)` — the dependency is in the signature, greppable, and free to vary.
2. **It is untestable.** You cannot pass a different instance to the code under test. Tests mutate global state, so they cannot run in parallel and they leak state between cases. This is why singleton code produces the notorious `beforeEach { Config.reset() }` in every spec file that touches it.
3. **It owns its own lifecycle.** Singletons initialise lazily, forever, on first use. In a server, that means initialisation can happen on the hot path of the first request under a lock; in a test, it means state survives between tests. Both are the container's job, not the class's.
4. **It destroys testability of module boundaries.** It creates a *global mutable variable*, which means any two parts of the program can interfere. Under concurrency you now need to reason about which of your 40 threads might be reading config mid-reassignment.
5. **It is not actually one instance, it is one instance per realm.** In Node with ESM/CJS interop you get two; in a browser with iframes you get several; in a cluster you get one *per worker*. So Singleton usually fails to deliver even the thing it promises while still delivering the costs.

**Real code: the three replacements, in order of preference.**

```ts
// (1) BEST: no singleton at all. Pass the thing in. Dependencies are visible; tests inject fakes freely.
class OrderService {
  constructor(
    private readonly repo: OrderRepository,   // injected, not fetched from a global
    private readonly clock: Clock,            // deterministic time in tests
    private readonly logger: Logger,          // no import of a global logger
  ) {}
  place(cmd: PlaceOrder): Order {
    return this.repo.save(Order.create(cmd, this.clock.now()));
  }
}

// (2) Module-scope constant, if the state truly never varies per-unit-of-work.
export const CURRENCY_MINOR_UNITS = new Map([['USD', 2], ['EUR', 2], ['JPY', 0]]);
// Immutable, not a lazy singleton: no initialisation ordering hazard, no test contamination,
// and "override for a test" is a normal argument instead of a hack.

// (3) Container-managed lifetime, when you genuinely want one-per-app object (connection pool, cache).
// The container owns the lifecycle and the test can swap the binding in its own root container.
container.register(ConnectionPool, { lifetime: 'singleton' });
```

**A genuinely defensible Singleton.** Not "always wrong" — there is a narrow band where it holds:

- The object is **stateless or immutable** (a pure function table, a lookup table of currency codes, a regex cache). Then "singleton" degenerates to "module constant" and there is no coupling problem.
- The object is a **process-level resource with expensive acquisition** — a thread pool, a native library handle, an open TLS context. Here the real concerns are thread-safe lazy init and guaranteed release. Prefer an explicit `acquire()`/`release()` or a container-managed singleton over a class-managed one.
- The language/runtime **gives you process-wide state anyway** — a global event loop, a standard output stream, an interpreter registry — and re-implementing it adds nothing.

The killer test: **"If I want this to be different in a test, can I?"** If the answer is "I'd have to reset static fields and hope no other test reads them," it is a Singleton and it is wrong.

```ts
// The fix pattern: make the seam explicit at the boundary rather than global.
class Config {
  constructor(private readonly source: ConfigSource) {}
  get(key: string) { return this.source.read(key); }
}
// Test doubles are now ordinary objects. No reset(). No ordering dependency.
const testConfig = new Config({ read: (k) => fixtures[k] });
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** process-scoped, expensive, immutable-ish resources — connection pools, hardware handles, compiled regex caches, ID generators with a documented seeding rule, feature-flag snapshots in a serverless *warm* container (with the explicit understanding that a cold start resets them).
- **Don't use:** configuration, loggers, caches, connection registries, random-number sources, anything a test needs to control, anything with mutable state that two requests touch.

**Trade-offs.** Singleton gives you: cheap access, one guaranteed instance, a natural home for process-wide caches. It costs you: invisible coupling, impossible isolation, poor concurrency semantics, lifecycle you cannot control, and — the one that bites hardest — a codebase where *every* class could secretly reach *every* other class, so the dependency graph is a star with the Singleton at the centre and no edges you can see.

**Production considerations.**

- Lazy init under concurrent access in Node is single-threaded so mostly safe; in a Java service the classic double-checked-locking bug is a real production incident. Prefer eager init or a holder class.
- Singletons *survive redeploys in long-lived processes* — a cached value in a singleton is the reason a config change "didn't take effect" until someone restarted the pod.
- In serverless, a singleton is per warm container and *is* the recommended way to reuse a connection across invocations. But that is "reuse the client," not "hold mutable domain state."

**Common mistakes.**

1. "Singleton" used to mean "module-level `let`" with no discipline about reassignment.
2. A singleton registry keyed by string names — that is just a Map with extra steps, but a common one.
3. Breaking the "one instance" invariant in tests by assigning the private static field directly (TypeScript `private` is compile-time only; `as any` reaches it at runtime).
4. Resetting global state between tests instead of eliminating it.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you test a singleton?"* — reset-and-reorder is a wrong answer; injection is the right one.
- *"Your singleton connection pool is shared by 200 request handlers and one of them leaks a connection. What happens?"* — pool exhaustion, all threads block, the whole service stalls; you want per-request acquisition, a release in a `finally`, and a pool that fails fast rather than waiting forever.
- *"Where have you seen Singleton used correctly in a real codebase?"* — a process-level UUID generator, a metrics registry, an immutable config snapshot.

---

### 11.1.3 Factory Method, Abstract Factory, and the concrete vs abstract distinction

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.**

- **Factory Method** defines an interface for *creating one kind of object* (a single product) and defers the concrete class to subclasses. The method that builds the thing lives in the same class hierarchy as the thing being built, or in a dedicated creator that subclasses override.
- **Abstract Factory** provides an interface for creating a *family of related objects* without specifying their concrete classes. The caller gets a "kit" whose parts are guaranteed to be mutually compatible.

**The concrete vs abstract distinction you must be able to state.** A **concrete factory** names its products; an **abstract factory** does not. The practical consequence: an abstract factory's callers are written against *families* and remain unaware of product classes; a concrete factory's callers must know the product types.

```ts
// ---- Factory Method: one product type, subclass decides the concrete class.
abstract class DocumentExporter {
  protected abstract createDocument(): Document;   // <-- the factory method
  export(data: ExportData): Blob {
    const doc = this.createDocument();
    doc.write(data);
    doc.close();
    return doc.toBlob();        // the invariant: every exporter does write→close→toBlob
  }
}
class PdfExporter extends DocumentExporter { protected createDocument() { return new PdfDocument(); } }
class CsvExporter extends DocumentExporter { protected createDocument() { return new CsvDocument(); } }

// ---- Abstract Factory: a family, guaranteed compatible.
interface UiKit {
  button(): Button;
  input(): Input;
  font(): Font;
}
interface Button { render(): string; }
class DarkButton implements Button { render() { return '<button class="dark">'; } }
class DarkUiKit implements UiKit {
  button() { return new DarkButton(); }
  input()  { return new DarkInput(); }
  font()   { return new InterFont(); }
}
// The caller cannot build a DarkButton with a LightFont, because it never holds a class name.
function renderApp(kit: UiKit) {
  return `<style>${kit.font().css()}</style>${kit.button().render()}${kit.input().render()}`;
}
renderApp(new DarkUiKit());
```

**When abstract factory is the right answer (and it is a narrow answer).** It genuinely pays when you have **two or more dimensions that must be kept consistent** — theme × density, database vendor × deployment region, cloud × auth provider. The moment the dimensions are independent, the factory is ceremony and you want plain constructor injection.

```ts
// The honest reason abstract factory is under-used: the combinatorics.
// 3 themes × 4 densities = 12 kit classes. 5 themes × 3 densities = 15. This is where a
// factory stops being "one class per product" and becomes a matrix you must maintain.
```

**Mechanics, precisely.** In Factory Method the *subclass* chooses the product; the *interface* fixes the algorithm and leaves one step open. In Abstract Factory the caller chooses the *family*; the factory knows the pairing.

- Factory Method has **one** abstract creation method per creator class.
- Abstract Factory has **several** abstract creation methods per factory, and returns products of *several* types.
- Abstract Factory is usually composed *with* a strategy or registry to select the family.

**Real code: the version you will actually write in a service.** Most of the time "factory" means a registry that maps a discriminator in persisted data or a request payload to a constructor — because the thing you are constructing has to survive a restart.

```ts
type NotificationChannel = 'email' | 'sms' | 'push';
interface ChannelSender { send(to: string, body: string): Promise<DeliveryReceipt>; }

// Naive: a switch that grows a branch per channel and is closed to extension.
//   function send(ch: NotificationChannel, ...) { switch (ch) { /* ever-growing */ } }

// Registry factory: adding a channel is one entry, no edit to the dispatcher.
const SENDERS: Record<NotificationChannel, (deps: Deps) => ChannelSender> = {
  email: (d) => new EmailSender(d.smtp, d.templates),
  sms:   (d) => new SmsSender(d.twilio, d.origins),
  push:  (d) => new PushSender(d.fcm, d.tokens),
};
export function senderFor(ch: NotificationChannel, deps: Deps): ChannelSender {
  const make = SENDERS[ch];
  if (!make) throw new UnknownChannelError(ch);   // explicit failure beats a silent no-op
  return make(deps);
}
// Persisted rows store the discriminator ('email'), which must be stable forever.
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Factory Method:** when a base class owns an algorithm with one variable step and you want subclasses to vary only that step. Also when you need to *test* the algorithm with a stub product.
- **Abstract Factory:** when product families must be mutually consistent and the number of families is small.
- **Not:** when you have one product and no anticipated variation — `new PostgresUserRepo(db)` is better than `RepoFactory.create('postgres', db)`.
- **Not:** when the "factory" just forwards to a `switch`. That is a switch. Admitting it is a sign of honesty.

**Trade-offs.** Both add a layer and a place for the bug to hide (`UnknownChannelError` at 3am is not fun). Both make stack traces longer and make "find all places that construct X" a semantic search instead of a grep. What they buy: the caller stops depending on concrete classes, which is what makes the system testable and swappable. What they cost: **you cannot grep your way to all construction sites any more**, so you need the factory to be the one obvious place — otherwise you will end up with four half-implemented factories and a "why does the test env use a different DB driver" bug.

**Production considerations.**

- Factories must be **total**: unknown key ⇒ throw, not `undefined`.
- Factory-constructed objects with external resources need a matching release path; if the factory caches, the cache is a singleton by another name and needs an eviction policy.
- Persisted discriminators are a **schema**: renaming a product class must not rename the string in the database. Keep a separate stable mapping.
- Factories that read the environment to pick a product are a hidden dependency on process state; pass the environment *into* the factory as an argument.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Confusing Factory Method with Abstract Factory in the interview. Say explicitly: "one product, subclass picks" vs "family, caller picks."
2. A "factory" that is a class with one static method — that is a namespaced function; prefer the function.
3. Using Abstract Factory for a single axis of variation.
4. Factory returning `null`/`undefined` for unknown input, turning a config typo into a silent production no-op.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When is a plain `new` better than a factory?"* — always available answer, always the right instinct; say it confidently.
- *"How would you test that every channel is implemented?"* — exhaustiveness over the `Record` key type plus a test that iterates the channel union and asserts `senderFor` returns a sender for each; a `satisfies Record<Channel, …>` annotation makes the compiler enforce it.
- *"Your factory caches senders per channel. What happens when SMTP credentials rotate?"* — you hold stale credentials in a process-wide cache; either do not cache or make the cache observe credential version.

---

### 11.1.4 Builder and the telescoping-constructor problem it solves

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Builder constructs a complex object by accumulating configuration in steps, then calling `build()`. It solves the *telescoping constructor*: a class whose constructor signature grows one parameter per optional feature, producing call sites like `new HttpClient(a, b, undefined, c, undefined, d, false)`.

**The problem, stated precisely.** Four independent optional features → `2⁴` = 16 constructors, or a constructor with 7 parameters of which 4 are nullable sentinels. Both are worse than a builder: the first is combinatorial explosion, the second is *positional blindness* where swapping two arguments of the same type compiles fine and does the wrong thing.

```ts
// Telescoping: readable at the definition site, unreadable at the call site.
class HttpClient {
  constructor(baseUrl: string, method: Method, path: string, query?: Q, body?: B,
              headers?: H, timeoutMs?: number, retries?: number) { /* ... */ }
}
new HttpClient('https://api', 'GET', '/orders', undefined, undefined, { auth: t }, 5000, 3);
// Which is which? Nobody knows. Reviewers nod. Nobody catches the swap.
```

**Mechanics.** A builder holds a mutable partial state, exposes a fluent (chaining) interface that returns `this`, validates in the builder (which is where you can produce *useful* errors because you see all the fields at once), and produces an immutable object in `build()`.

```ts
type Query = Record<string, string | number | boolean>;
type Body = unknown;
type Headers = Record<string, string>;

class HttpClientBuilder {
  private baseUrl?: string;
  private method: Method = 'GET';
  private path?: string;
  private query: Query = {};
  private body: Body = undefined;
  private headers: Headers = {};
  private timeoutMs = 5_000;
  private retries = 0;

  baseUrl(v: string)     { this.baseUrl = v; return this; }
  method(m: Method)      { this.method = m; return this; }
  path(p: string)        { this.path = p; return this; }
  query(q: Query)        { this.query = q; return this; }
  body(b: Body)          { this.body = b; return this; }
  header(k: string, v: string) { this.headers = { ...this.headers, [k]: v }; return this; }
  timeout(ms: number)    { this.timeoutMs = ms; return this; }
  retries(n: number)     { this.retries = n; return this; }

  // Validation lives here because here is the only place that sees the whole picture.
  build(): HttpClient {
    const missing = ([['baseUrl', this.baseUrl], ['path', this.path]] as const)
      .filter(([, v]) => v === undefined).map(([k]) => k);
    if (missing.length) throw new MissingBuilderFields(missing);   // ONE error, all fields
    if (this.method === 'GET' && this.body !== undefined)
      throw new IllegalCombination('GET', 'body');                // cross-field rule
    if (this.retries < 0) throw new NegativeRetries(this.retries);
    return new HttpClient(this.baseUrl!, this.method, this.path!, this.query,
                          this.body, this.headers, this.timeoutMs, this.retries);
  }
}

const client = new HttpClientBuilder()
  .baseUrl('https://api').path('/orders').method('POST')
  .header('auth', token).timeout(2_000).build();
```

**Builder with mandatory fields done properly.** Two flavours, both correct, and knowing which is better is worth a sentence in the interview:

```ts
// Flavour A: required fields are constructor args to the BUILDER — impossible to build without them.
class OrderBuilder {
  constructor(private readonly customerId: string, private readonly currency: Currency) {}
  line(sku: string, qty: number) { (this.lines ??= []).push({ sku, qty }); return this; }
  discount(c: Money) { this.disc = c; return this; }
  build(): Order { /* ... */ }
}

// Flavour B: TypeScript discriminated "makeValid" trick — the compiler enforces the required shape.
function request<T>(body: T & { method: 'POST' | 'PUT' } | T & { method: 'GET' }) { /* ... */ }
const ok  = request({ method: 'POST' });            // compiles
const bad = request({ method: 'GET', body: 1 });    // compile error: GET may not carry a body
// This is Builder enforced by the type system instead of by runtime validation.
// Prefer it when the invariant is expressible in types; prefer runtime validation otherwise
// (values from the network are never type-checked).
```

**Immutable-with-copy builder.** When the object is a persistent immutable value (very common in functional TypeScript), the "builder" is just a chain of copies, and it is *strictly better* because there is no mutable intermediate state at all.

```ts
class HttpRequest {
  constructor(readonly spec: {
    readonly baseUrl: string; readonly method: Method; readonly path: string;
    readonly query: Query; readonly body: Body; readonly headers: Headers;
    readonly timeoutMs: number; readonly retries: number;
  }) {}
  withMethod(method: Method)  { return new HttpRequest({ ...this.spec, method }); }
  withHeader(k: string, v: string) {
    return new HttpRequest({ ...this.spec, headers: { ...this.spec.headers, [k]: v } });
  }
}
// Usage is identical to a fluent builder, but the object is never partially built:
// "half-built request" is unrepresentable.
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** ≥4 optional configuration axes; options that must be mutually exclusive or require cross-field validation; objects whose construction involves intermediate expensive steps (buffers, streams, compiled SQL); fixtures in tests (a `userBuilder()` that gives you valid defaults and one override per call is one of the highest-value builders in any codebase).
- **Don't use:** when the object has 2–3 simple fields — a builder is more code than the constructor and nobody asked for it.
- **Don't use:** to hide a missing required field instead of modelling it — a builder that lets you build an invalid object is worse than a constructor.
- **Don't use** as a general substitute for "just pass the dependencies" — DI does that job better.

**Trade-offs.** Cost: one more class per complex type, an extra allocation, a mutable window in the classic form, and a longer type signature chain that some IDEs handle poorly. Benefit: call sites become self-documenting, validation has a whole-object view, and the "optional with a default" explosion stops. Note that the **copy-builder** (`withX`) removes the mutable-window problem and is often strictly better than the classic builder in TypeScript — say that in the interview, it is a distinguishing point.

**Production considerations.**

- **Build once, cache the built object.** A builder that creates a new connection pool per call is a bug factory. Builders are for *configuration*, not per-request work.
- Builder validation errors must be **aggregate**, not fail-fast on the first field. A user-facing builder that reports one error per round trip is a bad experience.
- If a builder is used to build an **untrusted** object (from JSON), you must validate the values, not just the presence of fields; the type system is not involved at that boundary.
- Builders that set **security-relevant** fields (auth headers, TLS verification) should not be publicly reachable from request input. Make the sensitive setters internal or require a token object.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Builder with all-optional state and no validation → produces `{}` objects that fail three layers down.
2. Calling `build()` twice and mutating the builder between the calls, assuming the first object updates. Classic builder does not; copy-builder does. Decide and document.
3. Leaking the builder: `const b = new Builder(); use(b)` and then mutating. Make the builder internal to the factory.
4. Using a builder to work around not knowing the domain. If you cannot state the required fields, the problem is the model, not the constructor signature.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When would you prefer an options object over a builder?"* — when the fields are few, homogeneous, and the type system can enforce the illegal combinations (`method`/`body` above).
- *"Your builder validates nothing. Where does the error surface?"* — three layers later, as a 500, with a stack trace pointing at the wrong file.
- *"How do you stop people constructing this object directly?"* — make the constructor `private`/module-private and expose only `create()`/`Builder.build()`.

---

### 11.1.5 Adapter, Facade, and Bridge

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Three patterns that all sit between a client and something else. The distinction is *what kind of difference* they absorb.

| | Absorbs | Shape | Typical example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Adapter** | Interface difference | Wraps one object, exposes another interface | Legacy SDK wrapper, ORM around a raw DB driver |
| **Facade** | Complexity (many subsystems) | Wraps *many* subsystems, exposes a simple interface | `OrderFacade.placeOrder()` over pricing + inventory + billing |
| **Bridge** | **Two independent axes of variation** | Abstract × concrete, abstracted to two hierarchies | JDBC `Driver` × `Connection`; SQLite writer × storage engine |

**Adapter.** Converts the interface of a class into another interface the client expects. Two flavours: *object adapter* (composition, holds a wrapped object) and *class adapter* (inheritance, multiple inheritance — impossible in TypeScript, so use composition; note it in the interview because the interviewer may ask which you would implement).

```ts
// Client wants a Money value. Vendor SDK wants cents as a number in a specific currency table.
interface Money { readonly minorUnits: number; readonly currency: string; }
interface PaymentGateway {
  charge(m: Money, idempotencyKey: string): Promise<{ id: string; status: 'ok' | 'declined' }>;
}

// Object adapter: wraps the vendor, translates the interface AND the units/edge cases.
// The valuable part is not the method rename; it is absorbing the vendor's 12 idiosyncrasies
// in exactly one file, so changing vendors is a one-file change.
class StripeAdapter implements PaymentGateway {
  constructor(private readonly stripe: StripeClient, private readonly limits: Limits) {}
  async charge(m: Money, key: string) {
    if (m.currency !== this.limits.settlementCurrency)
      throw new CurrencyMismatch(m.currency, this.limits.settlementCurrency);
    if (m.minorUnits > this.limits.maxMinorUnits)
      throw new AmountTooLarge(m.minorUnits, this.limits.maxMinorUnits);
    const r = await this.stripe.paymentIntents.create({
      amount: m.minorUnits,                 // vendor uses the same unit: document that assumption
      currency: m.currency.toLowerCase(),    // vendor demands lowercase — vendor quirk, absorbed here
      idempotency_key: key,                 // our key becomes their idempotency key
    }, { idempotencyKey: key });
    return { id: r.id, status: r.status === 'succeeded' ? 'ok' : 'declined' };
  }
}
```

The design rule that makes an adapter good: **the adapter knows everything about the vendor's quirks and nothing about business rules.** The moment your adapter starts deciding whether a customer is a fraud risk, it has become a domain service wearing an adapter's name.

**Facade.** Provides a single simplified entry point to a subsystem. The critical distinction: a Facade's simplification is *eagerness and convenience*, not translation.

```ts
class OrderFacade {
  constructor(
    private pricing: PricingService,
    private inventory: InventoryService,
    private billing: BillingService,
    private orders: OrderRepository,
  ) {}
  // One call the API layer makes instead of four, in the right order, with the right compensation.
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder): Promise<PlacedOrder> {
    const quote = await this.pricing.quote(cmd);
    const reservation = await this.inventory.reserve(quote.items);   // 1. reserve
    try {
      const charge = await this.billing.charge(quote.total, cmd.idempotencyKey);
      return await this.orders.append(PlacedOrder.from(cmd, quote, reservation, charge));
    } catch (e) {
      await this.inventory.release(reservation);                      // 2. compensate
      throw e;
    }
  }
}
```

A Facade should be thin. The moment your Facade contains 200 lines of orchestration logic it has become an application service (fine, but name it that), and the moment it is 2000 lines it is a God object (see 11.2.6).

**Bridge.** Separates an abstraction from its implementation so both vary independently. It is *two* hierarchies with a composition relationship at the leaf.

```ts
// Two axes: (1) what a driver is / what an API does  (2) what wire protocol it speaks.
// Without Bridge, adding a new wire protocol forces a new subclass of BOTH sides — 2n changes.
interface Statement          { execute(sql: string): ResultSet; close(): void; }
interface Driver             { connect(dsn: string): Statement; readonly dialect: Dialect; }

// Implementations (axis 2): Postgres, MySql, Sqlite, DuckDb — do not know about Jdbc at all.
class PostgresDriver implements Driver {
  readonly dialect: Dialect = 'postgres';
  constructor(private pool: PgPool) {}
  connect(dsn: string) { /* ... */ }
}

// Abstract (axis 1): Jdbc-style API. Does not know which wire protocol it will get.
class JdbcClient {
  constructor(private readonly driver: Driver) {}    // <-- THE BRIDGE: abstraction ↔ impl
  query(sql: string) {
    const st = this.driver.connect(process.env.DSN!);
    try { return st.execute(this.driver.dialect === 'postgres' ? toPgSql(sql) : sql); }
    finally { st.close(); }
  }
}
// Now: new protocol = 1 new Driver. New API = 1 new class that takes a Driver. Never both.
```

**Interviewer recognition test:** "If I add requirement X, how many files must change, and in how many existing hierarchies?" Bridge's whole purpose is making that number `1` in each independent axis. If your Adapter or Facade answer to that question with "many", you have put the wrong pattern in.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Adapter:** always, whenever a third-party or legacy interface meets your interface. Cost is near zero and the payoff (one place to fix vendor breakage) is enormous. This is the highest value-to-cost ratio pattern in the entire catalog.
- **Facade:** when a caller needs a *workflow*, not a single operation. Do not use it to hide a single subsystem's API — a facade with one subsystem is a pointless wrapper.
- **Bridge:** when you can name two axes that both change. If you can only name one, you want a plain interface. Bridge is the most frequently over-applied of the three.

**Trade-offs.**

- Adapter adds a call frame and hides the vendor's real capabilities — a developer debugging a Stripe error has to know an adapter exists, which means the adapter needs good error wrapping (`StripeAdapterError` with `vendorCode` preserved). Never swallow the vendor error into `new Error('Payment failed')`.
- Facade hides capability, which is great for consumers and terrible for the platform team. It also becomes an attractive nuisance: everyone adds a method to it, and it turns into a God object with 90 methods. Mitigate by having several narrow facades (`CheckoutFacade`, `AdminFacade`) rather than one `PlatformFacade`.
- Bridge doubles the number of classes before it produces any value, and makes the stack traces longer.

**Production considerations.**

- Adapters are where **vendor API version drift** lives. Pin the vendor SDK version, and write a contract test suite that runs against the vendor's sandbox in CI so a breaking change fails the build rather than production.
- Adapters must be **idempotent-safe at the boundary**, since retries are a property of the integration, not the caller.
- Facades that perform multi-step workflows need the Saga pattern (11.1.16) — the compensation logic in the example above is a three-step saga and it is *inside a Facade*, which is the correct place for it only if the Facade is really an application service.
- Bridge implementations are long-lived objects; if your Driver holds a pool, the lifetime decision is now the Bridge's problem, not the caller's.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Adapter that leaks vendor types into the domain (`charge` returns the vendor's raw response object). The whole value evaporates the first time the vendor renames a field.
2. Facade with a `switch` inside it deciding which subsystem to call based on a flag — that is a Strategy wearing a Facade costume; extract the Strategy.
3. Bridge created for a single implementation ("we might add a database later"), producing an interface with one class and a comment saying "so we can swap later."
4. Confusing Adapter and Facade in the interview. If you can name the number of subsystems being wrapped, use that word: Adapter = one, Facade = many.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which of the three is the most useful and why?"* — Adapter, because its cost is one class and its benefit is isolating all third-party volatility in one file.
- *"Your Facade has 40 methods. Is that still a Facade?"* — it is a God object with a nice name; split by use case and by client role.
- *"Show me the Bridge test: what does it prove?"* — that adding an implementation touches no abstraction file and vice versa. If you cannot show the two-hierarchy picture, you have written a Template Method with an extra step.

---

### 11.1.6 Decorator vs Wrapper, and where JavaScript's own language features do this for free

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A Decorator attaches additional behaviour to an object by *wrapping it* while keeping the same interface. A "wrapper" in the looser sense is anything that holds a target and forwards to it; the difference is that a Decorator is expected to preserve the interface exactly, so it is substitutable for the thing it wraps.

**Why it exists: the two axes that inheritance cannot vary.**

```ts
// Four independent responsibilities on one object: 2^4 = 16 subclasses, and only 12 exist today.
class Notifier {
  send(msg: string) { /* ... */ }
}
class LoggingNotifier     extends Notifier { /* ... */ }
class RetryingNotifier    extends Notifier { /* ... */ }
class LoggingRetrying     extends Notifier { /* duplicate of RetryingLogging */ }
class RateLimitedLogging  extends Notifier { /* duplicate of LoggingRateLimited */ }
// Combinatorial explosion: 16 classes, and half of them are copy-paste with different ordering.
```

Decorators collapse that to `n` classes that compose at runtime, and the ordering becomes explicit data.

```ts
interface Notifier { send(msg: string): Promise<void>; }

class LoggingNotifier implements Notifier {
  constructor(private readonly inner: Notifier, private readonly log: Log) {}
  send(msg: string) { this.log.debug('sending', msg); return this.inner.send(msg); }
}
class RetryingNotifier implements Notifier {
  constructor(private readonly inner: Notifier, private readonly max: number, private readonly delay: Ms) {}
  async send(msg: string) {
    for (let attempt = 1; ; attempt++) {
      try { return await this.inner.send(msg); }
      catch (e) {
        if (attempt >= this.max) throw e;
        await sleep(this.delay * 2 ** (attempt - 1));   // exponential backoff
      }
    }
  }
}

// Composition: any combination, order explicit, zero subclasses.
const notifier = new LoggingNotifier(new RetryingNotifier(new SmtpNotifier(smtp), 3, 200));
```

Note the ordering dependency: `Logging(Retrying(Smtp))` logs *each attempt*; `Retrying(Logging(Smtp))` logs *one call and retries the logging*. Decorators are not order-independent, and an interviewer will check whether you know that. Say it out loud.

**A decorator that gets the interface subtly wrong is worse than no decorator.** Three real traps:

1. **Not all methods are forwarded.** If `Notifier` grows `sendBatch` next month and the decorator forwards only `send`, the decorator silently stops being substitutable. Prefer an explicit `implements` and let the compiler catch it — or a Proxy-based generic decorator for interface-agnostic wrapping.
2. **Decorating the *identity*.** Code that does `if (a === b)` or uses the object as a `Map` key breaks if the decorator returns a different object. Pass identity through explicitly when it matters.
3. **Changing the return type.** `RetryingNotifier` returning `Promise<void>` where the inner returns a receipt means the interface is not preserved and callers lose information. Decide whether decorators can narrow/transform values; if yes, document it.

```ts
// A generic, interface-agnostic decorator using Proxy. Powerful, and it has real costs.
function logged<T extends object>(target: T, tag: string, log: Log): T {
  return new Proxy(target, {
    get(obj, prop, recv) {
      const v = Reflect.get(obj, prop, recv);
      if (typeof v !== 'function') return v;
      return (...args: unknown[]) => {                 // NOTE: breaks method identity and `this` typing
        const t0 = performance.now();
        try { return v.apply(obj, args); }             // apply to obj, not recv: correct for private fields
        finally { log.debug(`${tag}.${String(prop)} ${(performance.now() - t0).toFixed(1)}ms`); }
      };
    },
  }) as T;
}
// Costs to state in the interview: every property access is a trap (getter on the hot path),
// method identity changes, private-class-field access needs the apply(obj) fix above,
// `instanceof` still works (Proxy preserves the prototype) but `Object.keys` enumeration
// and spread behave differently. Great for tracing, dangerous as a default.
```

**Where the language does this for free.** Three cases where a decorator is *unnecessary* and a language feature is strictly better — recognising these is what separates "knows the patterns" from "applies patterns":

1. **`Proxy` and `Reflect`** — the built-in decorator. Use it for tracing, validation at the boundary, memoisation, and lazy loading; do not use it on the hot data path because every access becomes a trap.
2. **Default parameters and `??`** — optional configuration. `function f(a, { retries = 3, timeout = 5000 } = {})` is a Builder for two fields. Do not build a class.
3. **Middlewares as a chain** — an array of `(next) => (input) => output` functions is the decorator pattern with less ceremony, and it composes left-to-right by array order. Express/Koa/Redux middleware chains are Decorator; when you say "we use decorators for cross-cutting concerns", a reviewer may ask why you did not just add one middleware.

```ts
// Middleware = decorator chain. Three lines, zero classes.
type MW<In, Out> = (next: (i: In) => Promise<Out>) => (i: In) => Promise<Out>;
const chain = (mws: MW<Req, Res>[], terminal: (r: Req) => Promise<Res>) =>
  mws.reduceRight<((r: Req) => Promise<Res>)>(
    (next, mw) => (r: Req) => mw(next)(r), terminal);

// Decorator: wrapping a repo call with timing.
function timed<T>(inner: Repo<T>, log: Log): Repo<T> {
  return {
    async find(id: string)   { const t = performance.now(); try { return await inner.find(id); }
                                                    finally { log.trace('repo.find', performance.now() - t); } },
    async save(x: T)        { const t = performance.now(); try { return await inner.save(x); }
                                                    finally { log.trace('repo.save', performance.now() - t); } },
    // Every method forwarded by hand. This is the maintenance tax — say it out loud.
  };
}
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** cross-cutting concerns that apply to a *subset* of instances or calls, at runtime-determined combinations, where inheritance would multiply. Canonical: logging, timing, retries, caching, authorisation, transaction boundaries, idempotency keys, circuit breaking.
- **Use** when you need the decoration to be **data-driven** — a list of concerns chosen per-tenant or per-endpoint. That is the real reason decorators exist that Template Method does not cover.
- **Don't use:** for concerns that apply to *all* instances (put them in the class or in a framework-level middleware), or when there is only one combination (no composition is happening, so you have added a layer for nothing).
- **Don't use:** as an excuse to avoid naming a concept. If "Retry" is a decorator around `send`, then `RetryPolicy` is a decorator around `send` too — you have two names for one thing.

**Trade-offs.** Cost: an extra object and a call frame per layer; stack traces get long and uninformative (`at Decorator.send … at Decorator.send …`); debugging requires unwinding; the wrapper must be kept in sync with the interface by hand (the single biggest source of bugs). Benefit: no combinatorial class explosion, runtime-selectable composition, per-instance configuration, and clean ordering semantics.

**Production considerations.**

- **Ordering is behaviour.** Establish a convention (e.g. metrics outermost, then logging, then retry, then rate limit, then the real call) and write it down, because "logging outside retry vs inside retry" is the difference between one log line and three.
- **Never let a decorator swallow an exception.** Preserve the cause, and translate it into a domain error *with* the cause.
- **Decorators and transactions:** a decorator that opens a transaction must be *outside* any decorator that retries, or the retry will run inside a dead transaction. This is a classic production deadlock.
- **Latency budget:** each decorator adds a stack hop; on a hot endpoint five decorators are measurable. Measure.
- **Idempotency:** a retry decorator makes at-least-once delivery a fact. Therefore the *innermost* operation must be idempotent, or it must accept an idempotency key. Every retry decorator needs this paired with it.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Forgetting to forward a method → `undefined is not a function` at 2am on a method added six months ago.
2. Retrying a non-idempotent write → double charges, duplicate emails.
3. Capturing `this` wrong in an arrow-wrapped method.
4. Decorating something that is only ever constructed in one place with one combination — pure overhead.
5. Nesting decorators four deep in a composition root with no names, so nobody can tell what order things run in.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Log once or three times?"* — depends on order; show both compositions and say which is which.
- *"How do you ensure your decorator keeps up with interface changes?"* — `implements` on a concrete decorator for compile-time coverage, an interface-agnostic `Proxy` decorator, or a test that reflects over the interface and asserts every method is forwarded.
- *"Decorator or middleware for an HTTP request?"* — if it is HTTP-specific and applies to all routes, middleware; if it applies to a subset of objects or is not HTTP-shaped, decorator.

---

### 11.1.7 Proxy

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A Proxy is a stand-in for another object that intercepts operations on it. In design-pattern terms it is the structural version of Decorator: instead of hand-forwarding each method, you intercept property access generically. JavaScript ships this as a language feature, so "the Proxy pattern" and "the JS `Proxy` API" are the same conversation.

**Mechanics: the 13 traps, and which ones matter.** `new Proxy(target, handler)` supports `get`, `set`, `has`, `deleteProperty`, `ownKeys`, `defineProperty`, `getPrototypeOf`, `setPrototypeOf`, `isExtensible`, `preventExtensions`, `apply`, `construct`, `getOwnPropertyDescriptor`. Five matter in practice:

```ts
const trace = <T extends object>(obj: T, label: string, log: Log): T =>
  new Proxy(obj, {
    get(t, p, r)      { const v = Reflect.get(t, p, t); log.trace(`${label}.${String(p)}`);
                         return typeof v === 'function' ? v.bind(t) : v; },
    set(t, p, v)      { log.trace(`${label}.set(${String(p)})`); return Reflect.set(t, p, v, t); },
    has(t, p)         { return Reflect.has(t, p); },
    deleteProperty(t, p) { log.trace(`${label}.delete(${String(p)})`); return Reflect.deleteProperty(t, p); },
    ownKeys(t)        { return Reflect.ownKeys(t); },
  });

// Invariants that bite: a non-configurable, non-writable own property MUST report the same value
// from get; ownKeys MUST include all non-configurable own keys of the target; a `get` that returns a
// different function each call breaks `===` comparisons on methods and can break `Object.keys`.
```

`Reflect.*` is not decoration: using `Reflect` instead of hand-writing the default behaviour is what makes the proxy forward correctly for accessors, receivers, and `this`-typed private fields. `Reflect.get(t, p, t)` with the *target* as receiver is required if the target has private class fields (`#x`), because private fields are brand-checked against the receiver.

**Real uses, in rough order of production value.**

1. **Boundary validation and normalisation.** A Proxy in front of parsed external input converts "trust the payload" into "validate the payload", without you writing a DTO per field.

```ts
// Deep freeze + validate + default-fill at the edge, one place, no per-field ceremony.
function hardened<T extends object>(raw: unknown, spec: FieldSpec): T {
  const out = structuredClone(raw);
  for (const [k, rule] of Object.entries(spec)) {
    const v = (out as Record<string, unknown>)[k];
    const coerced = rule.coerce(v);
    if (!coerced.ok) throw new InvalidField(k, coerced.reason);
    (out as Record<string, unknown>)[k] = coerced.value;
  }
  return deepFreeze(out) as T;
}
```

2. **Lazy initialisation / virtual fields.** A Proxy that materialises a field from a loader on first access removes the `if (!this.x) this.x = load()` pattern from constructors.

3. **Memoisation.** Intercept `get`, look up a WeakMap cache, compute on miss. Careful: the cache key must be the *receiver*, not the proxy, or you get infinite recursion. And you must not memoise anything with a side effect.

4. **Read-only views.** A Proxy that throws on `set`/`delete`/`defineProperty` makes immutability real rather than `readonly`-by-convention (`readonly` in TypeScript compiles away; `Object.freeze` is shallow).

5. **Deprecation shims and API migration.** Wrap a legacy module and log which old methods are still called — you get migration telemetry for free, and the shim disappears when the log goes quiet.

6. **ORM/SDK property-access translation** — a snake_case API exposed as camelCase.

7. **Tracing instrumentation** — the `logged` decorator from 11.1.6.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use** when the interception is *generic* (applies to all properties) or *derived from data* (a spec-driven validator), or when the value is genuinely lazy and expensive.
- **Use** in dev/test code: a debug Proxy that logs unexpected property reads is a fantastic way to find which properties of a mock a test actually depends on.
- **Don't use** on hot paths: every property access becomes a trap with megamorphic dispatch; a Proxy-heavy inner loop can be several times slower than the plain version, and it defeats inline caches.
- **Don't use** when a plain function or a class does the job. `Proxy` is the most powerful and least readable tool in this file; reach for it when a data-driven rule makes hand-written code unmaintainable.
- **Don't use** for security. A Proxy is not a sandbox — `getPrototypeOf`, `getPrototypeOf` chains, `Symbol` access, and `Function.prototype.toString` all leak structure. Anything adversarial gets a real realm (`vm`, WASM, a subprocess).

**Trade-offs.** You gain: one place for a whole class of concerns, zero per-field code, composability. You pay: (a) readability — a reader sees `p.foo` and cannot know a trap runs; (b) debuggability — the stack shows the proxy, not the target; (c) invariants — you can throw `TypeError` from a proxy that violates an invariant, which is the worst class of bug; (d) performance on hot paths; (e) `typeof`, `instanceof`, and identity subtleties that surprise people (`p instanceof Foo` works; `p.constructor` returns your trapped value if you trap it wrong).

**Production considerations.**

- **Never** return a different value than the target for non-writable, non-configurable properties.
- `ownKeys` must be consistent with `getOwnPropertyDescriptor` results, or `Object.keys` throws "proxy trap result is inconsistent".
- If you trap `get` for functions, bind to the target (or `Reflect.get(t,p,t)`) or methods using private fields throw `TypeError: Cannot read private member`.
- Proxies around classes break `super` in some edge cases; prefer wrapping *instances*/plain objects than prototypes.
- A Proxy around a network client leaks the target if you forget `getPrototypeOf`; combine with an allowlist if the input is untrusted.
- Memory: a WeakMap-keyed cache keyed by the *proxy* (rather than the target) leaks nothing, because the WeakMap entry dies with the proxy — but it also never hits. Key by target and store the proxy.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Forgetting `Reflect` and losing accessor behaviour.
2. Catching everything in `get` and returning `undefined` for typos — silent `undefined is not a function` three layers away.
3. Using a Proxy as a validation boundary and calling it "security".
4. Proxying the request object in a hot express handler "for convenience".
5. Not remembering that `for...in` uses `ownKeys` + `getOwnPropertyDescriptor`, and `JSON.stringify` uses `ownKeys` + `get` — a proxy that only traps `get` will serialise to `{}`.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"What happens if your proxy `get` trap returns a function for every property?"* — `then` becomes a function, so the proxy becomes *thenable*, and `await proxy` hangs forever or resolves to the wrong thing. This is the classic Proxy footgun.
- *"How do you debug a Proxy?"* — `console.dir`/`util.inspect` custom hooks, plus logging inside the traps; the target is reachable via a closure if you keep it.
- *"Why not just use getters?"* — getters are per-class and cannot be data-driven across third-party objects; Proxy is the only interception point that works on objects you do not control.

---

### 11.1.8 Observer and the event emitter as its canonical implementation

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Observer defines a one-to-many dependency: when a Subject changes state, all registered Observers are notified automatically. The canonical implementation in every language is the event emitter / event bus.

**Mechanics.**

```ts
type Listener<T> = (payload: T) => void | Promise<void>;

class Emitter<Events extends Record<string, unknown>> {
  private readonly listeners = new Map<keyof Events, Set<Listener<never>>>();

  on<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K, fn: Listener<Events[K]>): () => void {
    let set = this.listeners.get(evt);
    if (!set) this.listeners.set(evt, (set = new Set()));
    set.add(fn as Listener<never>);
    return () => this.off(evt, fn);            // unsubscribe handle is mandatory
  }
  off<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K, fn: Listener<Events[K]>) {
    this.listeners.get(evt)?.delete(fn as Listener<never>);
  }
  emit<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K, payload: Events[K]): void {
    // Copy before iterating: a listener may subscribe/unsubscribe during dispatch.
    for (const fn of [...(this.listeners.get(evt) ?? [])])
      (fn as Listener<Events[K]>)(payload);
  }
  listenerCount<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K) { return this.listeners.get(evt)?.size ?? 0; }
}
type Events = { 'order.placed': { id: string; total: number }; 'order.cancelled': { id: string } };
const bus = new Emitter<Events>();
const off = bus.on('order.placed', p => analytics.track(p));
```

**Six design decisions in that 20-line class. All six are interview questions.**

1. **`on` returns an unsubscribe function.** If it does not, you cannot remove a listener, and you leak the listener *and its captured scope* for the life of the process.
2. **A `Set`, not an array.** Duplicate subscriptions are a real bug class (a module re-imported in dev adds the listener twice; you get double-sent notifications in dev only, which is the worst possible place to find it).
3. **Copy before iterating.** A listener that unsubscribes during dispatch otherwise mutates the collection mid-loop: in JS that skips listeners; in other languages it throws `ConcurrentModificationException`. This is a genuine production incident (self-unsubscribing one-shot listeners).
4. **Per-event keying.** A single array means `off` is O(n) and `emit` must filter; a `Map<event, Set>` makes both cheap and makes "no listeners for this event" detectable.
5. **Typed event map.** `Record<string, unknown>` gives you a compile error when a payload shape changes and a typo error on the event name. This is the difference between an event bus as an asset and as a liability.
6. **Error isolation.** The version above lets a throwing listener abort the remaining listeners. That is a decision: decide it explicitly. The robust version catches per-listener, reports the error, and continues — because "one bad analytics listener stopped order confirmation" is a real bug.

```ts
// Production hardening, in order of how often it saves you.
emit<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K, payload: Events[K]) {
  const errors: unknown[] = [];
  for (const fn of [...(this.listeners.get(evt) ?? [])]) {
    try { void (fn as Listener<Events[K]>)(payload); }
    catch (e) { errors.push(e); reportListenerFailure(evt, e); }   // continue, don't abort
  }
  if (errors.length) logAggregate(evt, errors);
}
// And the async variant, where ordering and concurrency become explicit:
//   (a) fire-and-forget: emit() returns void; slow listeners run concurrently; no ordering guarantee.
//   (b) sequential await: emit() returns Promise; deterministic order; one slow listener blocks all.
//   (c) parallel await + Promise.allSettled: deterministic *start* order, concurrent execution.
// Choose (a) for in-process notification and (c) for anything whose completion you must observe.
export async function emitAsync<K extends keyof Events>(evt: K, payload: Events[K]): Promise<void> {
  const fns = [...(this.listeners.get(evt) ?? [])];
  const rs = await Promise.allSettled(fns.map(fn => (fn as Listener<Events[K]>)(payload)));
  const failed = rs.filter((r): r is PromiseRejectedResult => r.status === 'rejected');
  if (failed.length) logAggregate(evt, failed.map(f => f.reason));
}
```

**Observer's two cousins and the distinction that matters.** Sync callback list (above), **event bus / publish-subscribe** (broader, looser coupling, often cross-process — see 11.1.17), and **reactive streams** (backpressure + operators + cancellation). Know which you are using:

- **Observer:** the Subject *knows* its Observers and typically calls them directly. Coupling is compile-time visible.
- **Reactive (RxJS):** cold/hot semantics, backpressure via `bufferSize`, cancellation via `AbortSignal`, and `switchMap`/`concatMap` for concurrency control. If you have any rate-limited source, you want this, not a callback list.
- **Pub/Sub (11.1.17):** publishers and subscribers never reference each other, usually across a broker.

```ts
// Backpressure and cancellation: the two things a naive emitter cannot do.
// With a callback list, a slow consumer cannot tell a fast producer to slow down,
// and unsubscribing does not propagate downstream. Streams do both.
import { Subject, switchMap, takeUntil } from 'rxjs';
const clicks$ = new Subject<{ x: number; y: number }>();
clicks$.pipe(
  switchMap(p => fetchPreview(p)),     // cancel the in-flight request when a new click arrives
  takeUntil(closed$),                 // teardown: stop everything when the component closes
).subscribe(render);
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** when the set of reactions to an event is *open* (will grow), when reactions are optional and independent (analytics, cache invalidation, email), when the number of subscribers varies per deployment, or when you genuinely want to break a compile-time dependency on a downstream consumer.
- **Use** for **in-process, synchronous, low-fan-out** notification of a domain fact (`OrderPlaced` to 3 internal listeners).
- **Don't use** when the caller *needs the result* of a reaction (that is a function call with a return value, not an event).
- **Don't use** to avoid a method call you did not feel like writing. An event bus used to hide a `sendReceiptEmail()` call is worse than the direct call.
- **Don't use** in-process for anything with a slow subscriber: you have coupled an unrelated request's latency to your emitter. That is the classic reason to move to a real queue.

**Trade-offs.**

*Gain:* decoupling (Subject does not know Observers), one action → many reactions, dynamic subscription, extensibility without editing the Subject.
*Cost:* the control flow becomes invisible. You cannot click "go to definition" from the Subject to the reaction. Stack traces cross a boundary. Errors are decoupled from causes. Order matters and is easy to get wrong. Long-lived listeners leak. Debugging means "grep for the event name", which in a large codebase means "grep the whole repo".

**Production considerations.**

- **Memory leak #1: anonymous listeners.** `[1,2,3].forEach(n => bus.on('tick', () => handle(n)))` — each closure captures `n`, and if you never unsubscribe, everything stays reachable. Store the unsubscribe handles.
- **Listener max.** Node warns above 10 listeners on one emitter; treat it as a design smell: either the Subject has too many reactions (split it) or something is subscribing per request (leak).
- **Error handling policy.** Decide per-emitter: fail-fast (good for invariants, bad for optional reactions) or isolate-and-continue (good for analytics, bad for money). Emit the failure to a monitoring path, do not just `console.error`.
- **Ordering guarantees.** Document: registration order? concurrent? at-most-once or at-least-once? If you go cross-process, you must design for at-least-once and idempotent handlers (11.3.6).
- **Backpressure.** In-process emitters have none. If a subscriber is a network call, you need a queue or a stream.
- **Versioning.** The event name + payload shape is a public contract. Changing `order.placed` payload is a breaking change for every subscriber, deployed or not. Put a version in the name (`order.placed.v2`) or make additive changes only.
- **Observability.** Instrument `listenerCount`, dispatch duration per listener, and error counts. An emitter is the best place to put a "which subscriber is slow" histogram.

**Common mistakes.**

1. No unsubscribe path → leak. Always return the disposer; always call it in a `dispose()`/`finally`.
2. Listener registered twice → double processing in dev only.
3. Mutating the listener collection during dispatch.
4. Emitting an object you then mutate (the listener sees the mutated version) — freeze or copy the payload.
5. Using a global bus with untyped string event names → typos become silent runtime no-ops. Type the map.
6. `async` listener errors become unhandled rejections → one `unhandledRejection` per failure.
7. Emitting from inside a listener recursively → infinite loop or reentrancy bug. Guard with a flag or a depth limit.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Where do you unsubscribe in a web framework?"* — on request completion (`res.on('finish')`), on client disconnect, and in a `finally` that also covers exceptions; in a component, on unmount.
- *"Observer or pub/sub for 'invoice paid'?"* — if both sides live in the same deployable, Observer; if they can fail independently and you need retry, pub/sub.
- *"How would you test this?"* — inject the emitter, subscribe a spy, assert call and payload; and assert the disposer removes it.
- *"What happens with 10,000 listeners?"* — dispatch becomes O(n) per emit; you need sharding or a different mechanism.

---

### 11.1.9 Command and the undo stack

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Command encapsulates a request as an object. That object can carry parameters, be stored, be queued, be logged, and — the reason the pattern exists in most UI code — be *executed later or reversed*.

**The three capabilities that distinguish Command from "a function in an object":** deferred execution (queue), serialisation (persist the command, replay it), and inversion of control (`undo`).

**Mechanics, and the invariant that makes undo work.**

```ts
interface Command<R = void> {
  readonly label: string;
  execute(): Promise<R>;
  undo?(): Promise<void>;
}

// The undo stack. Non-negotiable invariant: a command may only be undone if it is the
// LAST command in the stack. Pop from the end or refuse. Never "undo the middle one".
class UndoStack {
  private readonly stack: ExecutedCommand[] = [];
  private limit: number;

  constructor(limit = 100) { this.limit = limit; }

  async run<R>(cmd: Command<R>): Promise<R> {
    const result = await cmd.execute();
    this.stack.push({ label: cmd.label, undo: cmd.undo ? () => cmd.undo!() : undefined });
    if (this.stack.length > this.limit) this.stack.shift();   // bounded memory; oldest becomes un-undoable
    return result;
  }

  get canUndo() { return this.stack.length > 0; }
  get depth()   { return this.stack.length; }

  async undoLast(): Promise<string | null> {
    const top = this.stack.at(-1);
    if (!top) return null;
    if (!top.undo) { this.stack.pop(); return top.label; }   // no inverse: pop, do not pretend
    await top.undo();                                          // may throw -> see below
    this.stack.pop();
    return top.label;
  }

  async clear() { this.stack.clear(); }
}
```

**The tricky part, and it is what interviewers actually probe: undo is not `redo` and it is not rollback.** Three failure modes you must be able to name:

1. **Non-invertible commands.** `ResizeCanvas(w, h)` cannot be inverted without storing the old size. So every invertible command must capture its prior state *at construction or execute time*, not try to reconstruct it at undo time.
2. **Partial failure.** `MoveShape` succeeded, `RecolorShape` failed. Now the stack top is `RecolorShape`, which never ran, but the state reflects a moved shape. You must decide: push only on success (above), or push before and mark as failed. The correct default is **push only on success** and record the failure separately.
3. **Non-LIFO dependencies.** Undoing `AddB` after `AddA` was undone breaks the invariant. This is why real editors store *commands in a document timeline* with an inverse stored at execution time, not a naive stack.
4. **Server authority.** Undo on the client that the server disagrees with is a data-integrity incident. Undo must be a *command sent to the authority* that returns an inverse, not a client-side state edit. This is the difference between undo in Figma and undo in a notepad.

```ts
// Correct invertible command: captures prior state at construction.
class SetTextCommand implements Command<void> {
  private before?: string;
  constructor(private readonly doc: Document, private readonly sel: Selection, private readonly next: string) {}
  get label() { return 'Set text'; }
  async execute() { this.before = this.doc.textAt(this.sel); this.doc.replace(this.sel, this.next); }
  async undo()   { if (this.before === undefined) throw new UndoUnavailable('no prior state captured');
                   this.doc.replace(this.sel, this.before); }
}

// A command that CANNOT be inverted must say so, not silently no-op.
class SendEmailCommand implements Command<Receipt> {
  get label() { return 'Send email'; }
  async execute() { return send(this.mail); }
  // no undo(): undo() is optional and the stack pops without inverting. This is honest design.
}
```

**The second Command use case: serialisable command log / event log replay.** This is the bridge to event sourcing (11.3.8). Because commands are data, you can persist them and rebuild state:

```ts
interface LoggedCommand { at: string; actor: string; kind: string; args: unknown[]; }
// Rebuild: fold the log through the same handlers.
// Deterministic reducer required: given the same log, you must get the same state, every time.
// That rules out Date.now(), Math.random(), and unordered Map iteration inside handlers — inject them.
type Reducer<S> = (s: S, c: LoggedCommand) => S;
function replay<S>(initial: S, log: LoggedCommand[], reduce: Reducer<S>): S {
  return log.reduce(reduce, initial);
}
```

This is also **the queue/consumption mechanism**: a Command put on a queue is a message; a worker deserialises it and calls `execute()`. The pattern's value here is that the same object can be executed synchronously in a unit test and asynchronously on a worker.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** undo/redo; operation queues and job dispatch; operation logging and replay; throttled/serialised access to a mutable resource (each command captures its own parameters so no shared mutable state leaks); anything where you need to *defer* or *retry with identical parameters*.
- **Use** for `Command` + `Invoker` + `Receiver` when you need a **queue that serialises access** — one worker, commands in order, no locking.
- **Don't use:** for every method call. Wrapping `add(a,b)` in a `AddCommand` class with an `execute()` that calls `calculator.add(a,b)` adds a class and zero capability.
- **Don't use** when the receiver must run immediately and return a value to the caller — that is a function.

**Trade-offs.** Gain: deferred/queued execution, serialisability, undo, logging, uniform error handling and retry at the invoker level, and a uniform place to add authorisation/validation ("every command passes through the same guard").
*Cost:* one class per operation (a lot of files), serialisation coupling (a Command's fields become a wire format), the state-snapshot memory cost for invertible commands, and — the big one — **undo in a distributed system is a distributed-transaction problem**, not a local stack.

**Production considerations.**

- **Bounded stack.** Never unbounded; a text editor holding a million commands is an OOM. Bound it and make the eviction policy visible ("earliest 100 commands are not undoable").
- **Redo stack** is the mirror: on `undo`, push the command and any captured inverse onto the redo stack; on `execute`, clear the redo stack.
- **Coalescing.** Repeated identical commands (typing) should collapse — replace the top of the stack if it has the same `coalesceKey` and is recent. This is what keeps memory bounded in real editors.
- **Serialisation of commands is a public API.** Version the argument shape; old logs must still replay. Write a deserialiser with defaults for added fields.
- **Security:** a persisted command log is a script. Never `eval` it; dispatch through an allowlist of known kinds. Command logs from untrusted sources are a remote-code-execution bug.
- **Transactions:** the invoker, not the command, knows about the transaction boundary. Do not put DB commits inside `execute()`.
- **Idempotency:** any command that will be retried must be safe to re-run, or must carry an idempotency key.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Undoing a command whose state was changed by a later command → corrupted state. Enforce LIFO and be explicit that the stack is the source of truth.
2. Assuming undo is available for every command.
3. Unbounded memory.
4. Mixing undo with server state.
5. Commands capturing `Date.now()` inside `execute()` → replay produces different state than original execution. Capture at construction.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How does Figma's undo work?"* — inverse actions recorded at execution time, stored in a document timeline, applied in reverse; and a design tool has to reconcile client undo with server state.
- *"Where does Command end and job/message queue begin?"* — a Command is the payload; the queue is the Invoker. A Command without a queue is just a deferred method call.
- *"Your Command class is 90% boilerplate. Is that OK?"* — yes for the ones needing undo/serialisation; no for the rest. Use generics: `function defineCommand<R>(label: string, fn: (…a) => R, inverse?: (…) => void)` to get a family of commands from one factory.

---

### 11.1.10 Strategy and the switch statement it replaces

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Strategy defines a family of interchangeable algorithms, encapsulates each in its own class, and makes them independently swappable via a common interface.

**The problem.** A decision embedded in a control-flow structure cannot be unit-tested in isolation, cannot be added without editing the original file, and cannot be swapped at runtime.

```ts
// BEFORE: the switch. Note the three things it is bad at.
function price(items: Item[], ctx: Customer): Money {
  let total = Money.zero(ctx.currency);
  for (const it of items) {
    switch (ctx.segment) {                       // (1) adding a segment = editing this file
      case 'vip':      total = total.add(it.price.mul(0.9)); break;
      case 'student':  total = total.add(it.price.mul(0.85)); break;
      case 'employee': total = total.add(it.price.mul(1.0)); break;
      case 'b2b':      total = total.add(it.price.mul(0.8)); break;   // (2) four nearly-identical branches
      default:         total = total.add(it.price);
    }
    if (ctx.segment === 'vip' && ctx.orderCount > 10)  // (3) second, unrelated decision, same place
      total = total.sub(freeShipping(ctx.currency));
  }
  return total;
}
```

**AFTER.** One interface, one class per algorithm, one seam to inject in tests.

```ts
interface PricingStrategy {
  readonly segment: Segment;
  unitPrice(item: Item, ctx: Customer): Money;
  orderAdjustment(subtotal: Money, ctx: Customer): Money;   // split out (3)
}

class VipPricing implements PricingStrategy {
  readonly segment = 'vip' as const;
  unitPrice(i: Item) { return i.price.mul(0.9); }
  orderAdjustment(sub: Money, ctx: Customer) { return ctx.orderCount > 10 ? sub.sub(freeShipping(ctx.currency)) : sub; }
}
```
