```ts
class StudentPricing implements PricingStrategy { /* ... */ }
class DefaultPricing implements PricingStrategy {
  readonly segment = 'default' as const;
  unitPrice(i: Item) { return i.price; }
  orderAdjustment() { return Money.zero('USD'); }        // careful: don't fake the currency
}

class PriceCalculator {
  // The choice happens ONCE, outside the algorithm. That is the whole point.
  constructor(private readonly strategies: Readonly<Record<Segment, PricingStrategy>>) {}
  quote(items: Item[], ctx: Customer): Money {
    const s = this.strategies[ctx.segment];
    const base = items.reduce((t, i) => t.add(s.unitPrice(i, ctx)), Money.zero(ctx.currency));
    return base.add(s.orderAdjustment(base, ctx));
  }
}

// Selection is data, not control flow — so it can be config, DB, or a test double.
const strategies = { vip: new VipPricing(), student: new StudentPricing(),
                     employee: new EmployeePricing(), b2b: new B2bPricing(), default: new DefaultPricing() };
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** when a decision is *made at runtime from data* (config, DB column, request field, user segment), when the alternatives are genuinely different *algorithms* rather than variations of one, and when each alternative has enough behaviour to deserve its own test file.
- **Use:** when you want the *decision to be testable in isolation* — the biggest practical win.
- **Don't use** for a `switch` over an enum with 3–4 trivial branches, each one line. That is the "switch that is fine"; replacing it with 5 files and a registry is a net loss in readability.
- **Don't use** when all branches share 95% of their body (the Strategy classes become 95% identical, or worse, they all delegate to a shared helper so you gained a layer and no clarity).
- **Don't use** when the alternatives differ only in *data* — that is a table/config, not a strategy. `{ vip: 0.9, student: 0.85 }` beats five classes.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* open/closed, runtime swappable, per-strategy tests, no central place where the decision list can rot.
*Cost:* the algorithm is no longer visible in one place — the reader has to go find `strategies`. More files. If `strategies` is a module-level object, it has become a global lookup table (fine) but a *missing key* is a runtime `undefined`. Also: strategies that need per-call context either take it in the constructor (which makes them per-context objects) or in every method signature (which grows the interface).

**Real code: the dependency-injected version that scales better.** For N strategies and M call sites, the context-in-every-method approach is noisy. Pass the context once:

```ts
interface PricingRule { apply(ctx: PricingContext, item: Item): Money; }
const RULES: Record<Segment, PricingRule> = { /* … */ };
export function priceItem(ctx: PricingContext, item: Item): Money {
  return (RULES[ctx.segment] ?? RULES.default).apply(ctx, item);
}
// In TypeScript the Record with an explicit `default` key plus a `satisfies` check gives you
// exhaustiveness at compile time; the `?? RULES.default` is belt-and-braces for untyped input
// coming off the wire, where the segment is `string`, not `Segment`.
```

**Production considerations.**

- **Selection must fail loudly.** Unknown segment ⇒ explicit default or throw, never `undefined`.
- **Strategies hold state?** Prefer stateless strategies held in a DI container as singletons; if a strategy must be stateful, construct it per request.
- **Persistence:** the segment string in the database is a contract. Renaming `'vip'` to `'vip-annual'` without a migration will silently price orders with the default rule. Migrate the data.
- **Version strategies.** Pricing logic changes; if a decision was made 6 months ago you may need to reproduce it. Store a `pricingRuleVersion` on the resulting order. This is the same instinct as event sourcing (11.3.8).
- **Metrics:** emit the selected strategy name as a dimension. "vip is 4% of orders and 40% of discounts" is a business insight you cannot get otherwise.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Strategy classes that are one-liners delegating to a shared function — ceremony.
2. `switch` moved into a `StrategyFactory` — the same coupling with extra files.
3. Strategies that also do I/O (a "strategy" that fetches pricing from a service) — then it is a collaborator, name it that.
4. Selecting the strategy *inside* the algorithm — the classic failure; decision must be outside.
5. Mutating strategy state per call and sharing a single instance across concurrent requests.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When is a switch better than a Strategy?"* — when the branches are trivial, few, and never change; say this without hesitation.
- *"How do you pick the strategy?"* — from data, not from a switch. Show it.
- *"Difference between Strategy and State?"* — Strategy: interchangeable, chosen by the client, independent of internal state. State: the object *itself* changes strategy as its state changes; the client never chooses. This is the most common confusion in pattern interviews.

---

### 11.1.11 State and the state machine connection (cross-reference 4.3.7)

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · See §4.3.7 for the algorithmic form (minimax, DP over game states).

**Definition.** State lets an object alter its behaviour when its internal state changes, by delegating to a strategy object per state. The object appears unchanged to the client but its *available operations* change.

**The problem: the boolean explosion.** A `Document` with `isDraft`, `isPublished`, `isArchived`, `hasSignoff` gives you `2³` = 8 combinations, most of them illegal, and *every* method needs to check a subset of the booleans.

```ts
// BEFORE: behaviour depends on state, expressed as a combinatorial guard soup.
class Doc {
  private draft = false; private published = false; private archived = false;
  edit()  { if (this.archived) throw new Archived(); if (!this.draft) throw new NotDraft(); /* … */ }
  publish() { if (!this.draft || this.archived) throw new IllegalTransition(); /* … */ }
  archive() { if (!this.published) throw new NotPublished(); /* … */ }
  reopen()  { if (!this.published || this.archived) throw new Illegal(); /* … */ }
  // 4 methods x guard combinations = every method knows about every state. Adding a state means
  // auditing every method. There are no legal combinations visible anywhere in the code.
}
```

**AFTER.** One behaviour per state; illegal operations simply do not exist.

```ts
interface DocState {
  readonly name: string;
  edit(d: DocData, input: string): DraftState;
  publish(d: DocData): PublishedState;
  archive?(d: DocData): ArchivedState;      // optional operation: only some states support it
  reopen?(d: DocData): DraftState;
}

class DraftState implements DocState {
  readonly name = 'draft';
  edit(_: DocData, input: string) { return new DraftState(input); }
  publish(d: DocData) { return new PublishedState(d); }
  archive(d: DocData) { return new ArchivedState(d); }   // drafts may be archived
  // reopen is absent: calling it is a compile error, which is the entire point
}
class PublishedState implements DocState {
  readonly name = 'published';
  edit(_: DocData) { throw new IllegalTransition('published', 'edit'); }  // or: create a new draft version
  publish(_: DocData) { return this; }                     // idempotent no-op
  archive(d: DocData) { return new ArchivedState(d); }
  reopen(d: DocData) { return new DraftState(d); }
}
class ArchivedState implements DocState {
  readonly name = 'archived';
  edit()     { throw new IllegalTransition('archived', 'edit'); }
  publish()  { throw new IllegalTransition('archived', 'publish'); }
  archive()  { return this; }
}

class Doc {
  constructor(private data: DocData, private state: DocState) {}
  edit(input: string)     { this.state = this.state.edit(this.data, input); this.data = { ...this.data, input }; }
  publish()               { this.state = this.state.publish(this.data); }
  archive()               { if (!this.state.archive) throw new Unsupported('archive'); this.state = this.state.archive(this.data); }
  get status()            { return this.state.name; }
}
```

**The State-vs-Strategy distinction, precisely (memorise this).** Strategy: the *client* picks, the strategy does not know the context, strategies are interchangeable and the context does not change which one is used. State: the *object* picks as a consequence of its own state transition, and the state object both provides behaviour and returns the next state. If your "state" objects don't return the next state, you wrote a Strategy.

**Three concrete benefits you must be able to argue.**

1. **The legal graph becomes data.** You can draw it, put it in a table, generate docs from it, and — critically — write a *reachability test*: enumerate all states and all operations and assert the transition table matches your spec. With a boolean representation this test is impossible to write.
2. **The state name is available for free** for metrics, error messages, and API responses. `doc.status` is `this.state.name`.
3. **Adding a state does not require auditing every method** — you add a class and register it in the transition map.

**Real code: the transition-table form, which is often better than classes.** For a machine with many states and regular transitions (order lifecycle, KYC, deployment pipeline), a table is more compact, easier to verify, and easier to load from config.

```ts
type OrderStatus = 'pending' | 'paid' | 'shipped' | 'delivered' | 'cancelled' | 'refunded';
type OrderEvent   = 'pay' | 'ship' | 'deliver' | 'cancel' | 'refund';

// status -> event -> next status. Absent key = illegal transition. One table, whole machine.
const ORDER_MACHINE: Record<OrderStatus, Partial<Record<OrderEvent, OrderStatus>>> = {
  pending:   { pay: 'paid', cancel: 'cancelled' },
  paid:      { ship: 'shipped', refund: 'refunded', cancel: 'cancelled' },
  shipped:   { deliver: 'delivered' },
  delivered: { refund: 'refunded' },
  cancelled: {},
  refunded:  {},
};

function transition(from: OrderStatus, evt: OrderEvent): OrderStatus {
  const next = ORDER_MACHINE[from][evt];
  if (!next) throw new IllegalTransition(from, evt);   // explicit, message names both
  return next;
}

// Exhaustive by construction: adding a status without filling the row is a compile error.
const _check: Record<OrderStatus, Record<OrderStatus, true>> = { /* … */ };
function assertExhaustive(s: OrderStatus) {
  const row = ORDER_MACHINE[s];
  (Object.keys(row) as OrderEvent[]).forEach(e => { if (!transition(s, e)) throw new Error('unreachable'); });
}
// The meta-test that earns its keep in CI: for every (status, event) pair, either a transition
// exists or it throws. Walk the reachable set from 'pending' and assert you reached every status
// you declared. This catches typos and unreachable states.
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** when an object has (a) ≥3 states, (b) a transition graph with real constraints, (c) behaviour that differs by state, and (d) illegal combinations that must be impossible rather than merely forbidden.
- **Use** for: order/invoice/subscription lifecycles, workflow engines, protocol state machines, game entity AI, UI wizard steps, job pipelines.
- **Don't use:** two states (use a boolean and a guard), or states that differ only in data (use a field).
- **Don't use:** when transitions must be *auditable for compliance* and the transitions happen inside framework callbacks — then persist an explicit transition log/event instead of relying on the object.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* behaviour that cannot be called in the wrong state, self-documenting legal graph, no guard soup, per-state unit tests, free status name.
*Cost:* one class per state (or a table plus a dispatcher), an extra indirection, and — the real cost — **persistence**. A state-pattern object must be reconstructible after a restart, so you need to persist `state.name` and be careful the state classes carry no un-serialisable behaviour. A transition table with explicit `from`/`to` in the DB is often easier to reason about under audit than a class graph.

**Production considerations.**

- **Guard against invalid stored states.** A corrupt/legacy row with `status = 'archived_pending_publish'` must not crash the worker; validate on read and route to a dead-letter path.
- **Concurrent transitions need compare-and-set**, not just a table: `UPDATE orders SET status='shipped' WHERE id=$1 AND status='paid'` — check the row count, and if zero, someone else already transitioned it. Otherwise you get double-shipping.
- **Instrument every transition** as a metric with `from`/`to` labels; the transition matrix in your dashboard is how you find bugs.
- **Timeouts are transitions.** "If not paid within 30 min → cancelled" is a scheduled transition; make it a first-class event (`expire`) with the same table and the same CAS.
- **Never make a transition do unbounded I/O** inside the state object; enqueue and return.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Guard soup left in place *plus* states added → two sources of truth.
2. Illegal transition silently ignored (`if (state.archive) …` with no else) → the bug becomes "nothing happened".
3. Not using CAS → lost updates and duplicate side effects.
4. States holding state that should live on the context object, so copying the state object loses data.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Show me how you'd test that the machine is complete."* — enumerate `(status, event)` pairs; assert either a transition or a throw; BFS the reachable set and compare against the declared status list.
- *"Where does the state live across a request?"* — persisted in the row; the class is reconstructed per request from `status`.
- *"How is this different from a `status` column with a `switch`?"* — the switch keeps the guards scattered and the legal graph implicit; the table co-locates the graph so it can be tested.

---

### 11.1.12 Template Method and the inheritance trap

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Template Method defines the skeleton of an algorithm in a base class and lets subclasses override specific steps. It is inheritance-based, and that is both its use and its fatal weakness.

**The honest case for it.** An algorithm with a *stable* skeleton and *few, well-understood* variable steps, where the hierarchy is genuinely a "is-a" taxonomy and the base class is owned by the same team as the subclasses.

```ts
abstract class ImportJob {
  // Final template: the algorithm. Subclasses cannot reorder or skip these steps.
  async run(input: Source): Promise<Result> {
    const rows    = await this.fetch(input);
    const parsed  = this.parse(rows);
    const valid   = this.validate(parsed);
    if (!valid.ok) return this.onValidationFailure(valid.errors);
    const saved   = await this.persist(valid.value);
    this.audit(saved);
    return saved;
  }
  protected abstract fetch(i: Source): Promise<RawRow[]>;
  protected abstract parse(rows: RawRow[]): Unknown[];      // usually pure; make it abstract only if it varies
  protected validate(rows: Unknown[]) { /* shared, has a default */ return ok(rows); }
  protected async persist(rows: ValidRow[]) { throw new NotImplemented(); }
  protected audit(r: Result) { /* shared */ }
  protected onValidationFailure(e: Error[]) { return Result.rejected(e); }
}
class CsvImportJob extends ImportJob { /* fetch/parse */ }
class S3ImportJob  extends ImportJob { /* fetch/parse, different transport */ }
```

**The inheritance trap, stated concretely.** Template Method's cost is that the *skeleton is fixed in the base class*, so:

1. **The hook explosion.** When you need a job that skips validation but adds a transform, you either override `validate` to return a lie (`return ok(rows)` without validating), or you get an `if` in the base class (`if (this.shouldValidate)`), and now the template has a hole shaped exactly like your new requirement. After three such requirements the template is a switch with steps around it.
2. **Every subclass sees every step.** A subclass that only wants to override parsing is forced to implement `persist`, because the base declared it abstract. You have created coupling to steps it does not care about.
3. **Testing requires subclassing.** You cannot test the template without faking the steps, so your test file defines a `TestImportJob extends ImportJob` — and if the base class has any *concrete* step that touches the network or the DB, your test is broken or slow. Template Method is hostile to composition-based test doubles precisely because the seams are inheritance-based, not constructor-injection-based.
4. **The hierarchy is permanent.** Every new variation is a new subclass; the tree only grows. You cannot compose "a CSV fetcher with an S3 parser" because they are peers in the hierarchy, not layers.
5. **Shadowing hazards.** A subclass declaring a private method with the same name as a base method silently breaks polymorphism; `super.method()` calls get confusing.

```ts
// The composition-based replacement: template as a list of functions.
// Same skeleton, but the steps are constructor-injected, so tests pass fakes and the order is data.
type Step<I, O> = (input: I, ctx: Ctx) => Promise<O>;
interface Pipeline<I, O> {
  fetch: Step<I, RawRows>;
  parse: Step<RawRows, Unknown[]>;
  validate: Step<Unknown[], ValidRows>;
  persist: Step<ValidRows, Result>;
}
async function runPipeline<I>(p: Pipeline<I, ValidRows>, input: I): Promise<Result> {
  const rows   = await p.fetch(input, ctx);
  const parsed = await p.parse(rows, ctx);
  const valid  = await p.validate(parsed, ctx);
  if (!valid.ok) return Result.rejected(valid.errors);
  return p.persist(valid.value, ctx);
}
```

Note that the composition version is *also* a Template Method — the algorithm skeleton is still in one place. The difference is that the seams are injectable. Some interviewers call this "template as composition"; others will say Template Method is definitionally inheritance-based and so this is just a pipeline. Both positions are defensible; the useful thing is to say *"the skeleton is the value; inheritance is not required for it."*

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use (rarely):** algorithm with ≥5 steps and a fixed order, where only 1–2 steps vary, and the number of subclasses will stay under ~3. Classic: JUnit lifecycle hooks, `java.util.logging`, JDBC `Template`, ORM lifecycle callbacks, HTTP request pipeline in a framework you control.
- **Use** when you *are building the framework* and want to force an extension structure on your users — the framework author legitimately owns the skeleton.
- **Don't use** in application code you own when there is more than one or two variable steps.
- **Don't use** when you need runtime composition (choose behaviour from data).
- **Don't use** when the subclass needs to *skip* or *reorder* steps — the pattern structurally forbids it, so you will fight it.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* algorithm in one place, guaranteed step order, shared default steps, extension points that are enforced by the compiler.
*Cost:* inheritance coupling, test-hostile seams, hook explosion, no runtime composition, and the guarantee-of-order property becomes a straitjacket the moment a real requirement disagrees.

**Production considerations.**

- If you use it in a framework, provide a **protected final `run()`** so callers cannot bypass the skeleton.
- Make hooks `protected` and document them; every hook is an API you cannot change.
- Instrument the base class, not the subclasses — one metrics counter in the template beats twenty scattered ones.
- If a step is expensive and idempotent, the template can cache it; note that as a feature because it is the reason some frameworks' templates exist.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Overriding a step to *skip* it with an empty body → silent no-ops.
2. Adding a boolean flag to a hook (`protected validate(strict: boolean)`) to serve two subclasses → the template is now a Strategy in disguise.
3. Abstract steps that subclasses cannot meaningfully implement (forcing `throw new NotImplemented()`) → the hierarchy is a lie.
4. `super` calls in hooks so a subclass cannot run without the base behaviour → nobody can override anything safely.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"When is inheritance the right tool?"* — for genuine is-a taxonomies that are stable and shallow: `NotImplementedError extends Error`, `AppError extends Error`, framework extension points. Not for code reuse.
- *"Rewrite Template Method with composition."* — the pipeline above; be ready to say what you lose (nothing important) and what you gain (testable seams, runtime composition).
- *"How do you unit-test a Template Method?"* — by subclassing a fake and asserting the *order* of calls, using a recording list; that is the only real assertion worth making, so say that.

---

### 11.1.13 Repository, Unit of Work, and Data Mapper

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

These three are the persistence-side trio. Get them right and your domain code stops knowing that a database exists; get them wrong and you have three names for the same leaky abstraction.

**Data Mapper.** Decouples the in-memory object model from the persistence representation. Its own anti-pattern is the **Active Record** model, where the object knows how to save itself.

```ts
// Data Mapper: two representations, one explicit translation function.
// The strong benefit: your domain object is a plain object with no driver, no identity map,
// no lazy-loading proxy — so it is trivially serialisable, comparable in tests, and shareable.
interface OrderRow { id: string; customer_id: string; total_minor: number; currency: string;
                     status: string; placed_at: string; lines: OrderLineRow[] | null }
interface Order { id: string; customerId: string; total: Money; status: OrderStatus; placedAt: Date;
                  lines: readonly OrderLine[] }

class OrderMapper implements Mapper<OrderRow, Order> {
  static toDomain(r: OrderRow): Order {
    return { id: r.id, customerId: r.customer_id,
             total: new Money(r.total_minor, r.currency as Currency),
             status: r.status as OrderStatus,           // see "trust boundary" below
             placedAt: new Date(r.placed_at),
             lines: (r.lines ?? []).map(l => ({ sku: l.sku, qty: l.qty, unitPrice: l.unit_price_minor })) };
  }
  static toRow(o: Order): OrderRow {
    return { id: o.id, customer_id: o.customerId, total_minor: o.total.minorUnits, currency: o.total.currency,
             status: o.status, placed_at: o.placedAt.toISOString(),
             lines: o.lines.map(l => ({ sku: l.sku, qty: l.qty, unit_price_minor: l.unitPrice.minorUnits })) };
  }
}
// The mapper is the ONE place that knows `total_minor` exists. Everything above it says `Money`.
```

**Repository.** A collection-like abstraction over persistence: `add`, `get`, `remove`, and queries expressed in domain terms. Its purpose is **domain-persistence separation**, not genericity.

```ts
interface OrderRepository {
  findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null>;
  save(o: Order): Promise<void>;
  // A GENERIC repository is usually a mistake: it exposes the storage model in its signature
  // (T's fields are storage fields) and every caller can do anything. Prefer per-aggregate repos.
  findByCustomer(customerId: string, page: Page): Promise<Page<Order>>;
  withPendingFulfilment(): Promise<Order[]>;
}

// The two decision tests for "is this repository doing its job?"
// 1. Could you swap Postgres for an in-memory implementation without touching any caller?  (yes = good)
// 2. Does any caller's *business logic* depend on the shape of what the repo returns? (yes = leak)
```

**Unit of Work.** Tracks the set of objects changed during a business transaction and coordinates persisting them and committing the transaction. It solves three problems at once: *identity*, *change tracking*, and *transaction demarcation*.

```ts
interface UnitOfWork {
  readonly orders: OrderRepository;              // repos bound to THIS unit, not global
  registerNew(o: Order): void;
  registerDirty(o: Order): void;
  async commit(): Promise<void>;                 // flush + transaction commit
  async rollback(): Promise<void>;               // discard the change list AND roll back
}

// A workable implementation over a real client.
class SqlUnitOfWork implements UnitOfWork {
  private readonly dirty = new Set<DomainObject>();
  private readonly added = new Set<DomainObject>();
  private readonly handlers: Record<string, (rows: object[], tx: Tx) => Promise<void>>;

  constructor(private readonly tx: Tx, handlers: Record<string, (rows: object[], tx: Tx) => Promise<void>>) {
    this.handlers = handlers;
  }
  get orders() { return new SqlOrderRepository(this.tx, this); }

  async commit() {
    // (1) Insert new rows first: FK dependencies require parents before children.
    for (const o of this.added)  await this.handlers[kind(o)]!([toRow(o)], this.tx);
    // (2) Then updates, ordered so that aggregates whose rows are referenced go last.
    for (const o of this.dirty)  await this.handlers[kind(o)]!([toRow(o)], this.tx);
    // (3) Flush order matters; if handlers have their own dependency graph, topologically sort.
    await this.tx.commit();
    this.added.clear(); this.dirty.clear();
  }
  async rollback() { await this.tx.rollback(); this.added.clear(); this.dirty.clear(); }
}
// The subtle part worth stating out loud: flush ORDER is a real correctness concern.
// An order's lines reference the order row; a payment row references the order; a shipment
// references both. Wrong flush order = FK violation or, worse, silent inconsistency.
```

**The three tools that replace manual change tracking.** Know these, because in production you rarely hand-roll `dirty`:

- **Explicit marking** (above): `registerDirty(obj)` — most control, most to forget.
- **Identity Map** + dirty tracking via `Object.freeze` + a Proxy that records sets (from 11.1.7) — automatic, magic, hard to debug.
- **Session-attached objects** (Hibernate `Session`, EF `DbContext`, Active Record) — you give up domain purity and gain automatic change tracking and lazy loading.

**The Lazy Loading trap, which is where purity dies.** Lazy loading requires the domain object to hold a reference to the session, which means: (a) it is no longer serialisable, (b) a detached object throws `LazyInitializationException`, (c) accessing `order.lines` inside a serialiser triggers queries you did not write, (d) N+1 queries. The modern answer is **explicit loading**: fetch what you need inside the repository, return fully-formed aggregates, and let N+1 be impossible by construction.

```ts
// Explicit loading, one round trip. The whole N+1 problem disappears at the boundary.
async findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null> {
  const { rows } = await this.sql`SELECT o.*, json_agg(l.*) FILTER (WHERE l.id IS NOT NULL) AS lines
                                FROM orders o LEFT JOIN order_lines l ON l.order_id = o.id
                                WHERE o.id = $id GROUP BY o.id`;
  return rows[0] ? OrderMapper.toDomain(rows[0]) : null;
}
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Data Mapper:** whenever the storage shape differs from the domain shape, or you want the domain model free of persistence concerns. This is the default choice in a serious codebase.
- **Repository:** when you have multiple aggregates, want to fake them in tests, or want queries expressed in domain language. Do **not** build a generic `BaseRepository<T, TId>` that mirrors your ORM — that is a wrapper with no value.
- **Unit of Work:** when a business operation touches several aggregates and must be atomic. If every operation touches exactly one row and the DB's own transaction handles it, a Unit of Work is overhead.
- **Active Record:** legitimate for internal tools, admin panels, read-mostly models, and small CRUD services. Say this out loud — "Active Record is not a sin, it is a scope choice."

**Trade-offs.**

*Repository:* gain is substitutability and domain-purity; cost is an extra layer, and the risk of a "leaky repository" that exposes ORM queries (`repo.createQueryBuilder()`) — at which point you have implemented Active Record badly and lost the domain layer.

*Unit of Work:* gain is atomicity + change tracking + identity in one concept. Costs: (a) scope confusion — one UoW per HTTP request means one transaction per request, and a slow request holds locks; (b) explicit dirty registration is easy to forget; (c) flush ordering; (d) long-running work inside the transaction.

*Data Mapper:* gain is independence and testability. Costs: mapping code (lots of it — mitigate with codegen or a small fluent mapper), the mapper must be maintained alongside schema changes, and hand-written mappers drift. Mitigation: `satisfies`-checked row types, generated mappers, and a test that round-trips every enum.

**Production considerations.**

- **The `status` cast above is a trust bug.** `r.status as OrderStatus` asserts a value the database did not guarantee. Parse and validate; on unknown values throw a `DataIntegrityError` and alert — do not pass an invalid status into the domain.
- **N+1:** the single most common repository bug. Log query counts per request in dev and assert a budget in tests (`expect(queryCount).toBeLessThan(10)`).
- **Transaction scope:** per request, not per operation, not per batch. Annotate `runInTransaction` and forbid nested transactions.
- **Concurrency:** `save` on a read-modify-write without a version column loses updates. Add `version int` and do `UPDATE … WHERE id=$1 AND version=$2`, retrying on zero rows (optimistic concurrency). This is the single most important production detail in this section.
- **Pessimistic vs optimistic:** `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` when conflicts are frequent and short; version column when rare. Long lock waits are worse than retry loops.
- **Bulk operations** must bypass per-entity hooks; be explicit about that.
- **Soft deletes** turn `findById` into a filtered query and every unique constraint into a partial index. Know the cost before choosing.
- **Mapper drift** is caught by a round-trip test: `toRow(toDomain(row))` equals `row` for a fixture set.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Leaking ORM types (`Promise<User[]>` where `User` is the ORM model) through the repository interface.
2. Entity objects used as API response bodies → you accidentally expose lazy loads and internal fields.
3. Forgetting to register dirty objects → silent data loss, the worst bug class in this list.
4. UoW spanning the whole HTTP request including a call to a slow third-party API → locks held for seconds, and you cannot roll back the third-party call.
5. Caching ORM entities across sessions → detached exceptions and stale reads.
6. `Map` iteration order relied upon for flush order. Always sort explicitly.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"What is the difference between Repository and DAO?"* — DAO is a persistence-oriented abstraction exposing CRUD/query over the storage model; Repository is a domain-oriented collection abstraction with domain-named queries and invariants. DAO is about the database; Repository is about the aggregate.
- *"Do you need a Unit of Work?"* — only if a business transaction spans more than one row you write. One-row-per-operation services should let the DB handle it.
- *"How do you avoid N+1?"* — explicit loading in the repository; never return partially-loaded aggregates.
- *"Active Record vs Data Mapper for this codebase?"* — argue both, then pick based on domain complexity: rich domain → mapper; thin CRUD → Active Record.

---

### 11.1.14 Dependency Injection and IoC containers

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Dependency Injection (DI) is supplying a class's collaborators from outside rather than constructing them inside. Inversion of Control (IoC) is the general principle; a container is one mechanism for achieving it. Critical distinction: **DI does not require a container.**

```ts
// Constructor injection: DI without any framework, and this is the version to advocate.
class OrderService {
  constructor(private readonly orders: OrderRepository,
              private readonly payments: PaymentGateway,   // an interface → a seam you can fake
              private readonly clock: Clock) {}            // an interface → deterministic tests
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder) {
    const now = this.clock.now();                 // test controls "now"
    const order = Order.create(cmd, now);
    const receipt = await this.payments.charge(order.total, cmd.idempotencyKey);
    await this.orders.save(order.withReceipt(receipt));
    return order;
  }
}
// Composition root — the ONE place where concrete classes are chosen.
function buildOrderService(env: Env): OrderService {
  const db = new PgPool(env.DATABASE_URL);
  return new OrderService(
    new SqlOrderRepository(db),
    new StripePaymentGateway(new Stripe(env.STRIPE_KEY)),   // swap per environment
    new SystemClock(),
  );
}
// Tests do not touch the composition root at all:
const svc = new OrderService(new FakeOrderRepository(), new FakePaymentGateway(), new FrozenClock(t0));
```

**Why DI.** Every one of these is a *testability* argument, and you should say "testability" first because it is the honest reason.

1. **Fakes instead of mocks.** A constructor parameter typed as an interface can be replaced with an in-memory implementation. A `new` inside a method cannot.
2. **Determinism.** Inject `Clock`, `IdGenerator`, `Random`, and your tests stop being flaky. A codebase that injects `Clock` has no `jest.useFakeTimers`; a codebase that calls `Date.now()` deep in a domain service has flaky tests forever.
3. **Scope control.** Whether something is a singleton, per-request, or per-test becomes a decision in the composition root rather than an accident of `static`.
4. **Decoupling for change.** Swapping a payment provider touches the composition root and the adapter, not the service.

**The five kinds of injection, and which to use.**

| Kind | Example | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Constructor | `new Service(repo, clock)` | **Use this.** Explicit, immutable, testable. |
| Setter | `svc.setLogger(l)` | Use only for genuinely optional, late-bound cross-cutting (metrics, tracer). Mutable state, easy to forget. |
| Interface segregation | pass a narrow `PaymentCharge` instead of a fat `PaymentGateway` | **Use.** Reduces the mocking surface. |
| Parameter injection | `save(entity, clock)` | Only inside a framework (Spring does this). Spreads dependencies into signatures. |
| Service locator | `Container.get(Repo)` inside the method | **Anti-pattern.** Hides the dependency completely; untestable without container configuration; defeats static analysis and cycle detection. |

**An IoC container.** It knows how to build object graphs and their lifetimes. The parts worth knowing in an interview:

- **Registration:** `bind(Foo).to(PostgresFoo)`, `bind(Token).to(Impl).singleton()`, `bind(Foo).toFactory(...)`.
- **Resolution:** the container walks the graph, resolving each constructor parameter by type token, recursively. This is why it must handle circular dependencies — and why it usually cannot.
- **Lifetimes:** `transient` (new each time), `singleton` (one per container), `scoped` (one per request — **the one you usually want**), `container` (one per DI container in a modular system).
- **Wiring by type token:** interfaces need a token (the interface type itself, or a symbol/string) since you cannot `new` an interface.
- **Auto-wiring** (constructor parameter reflection): convenient, but implicit. **Explicit wiring** is more verbose and far easier to read and debug. Most senior engineers prefer explicit.
- **Compilation / build-time wiring:** generate the container from a build step so the graph is known before runtime and missing registrations fail at startup, not at 3am.

```ts
// A hand-rolled micro-container, to make the mechanics concrete (and to argue "you may not need one").
type Token<T> = { readonly __t?: unique symbol; readonly type: abstract new (...a: any[]) => T };
function token<T>(name: string): Token<T> { return { type: null as any, name } as any; }

class Container {
  private readonly factories = new Map<unknown, (c: Container) => unknown>();
  private readonly cache = new Map<unknown, { value: unknown; scope: 'singleton' }>();
  private readonly scoped = new Map<unknown, unknown>();          // per-request
  private readonly building = new Set<unknown>();                   // cycle detection

  register<T>(t: Token<T> | abstract new (...a: any[]) => T,
              make: (c: Container) => T, scope: 'singleton' | 'scoped' | 'transient' = 'transient') {
    const key = typeof t === 'function' ? t : t.type ?? t;
    this.factories.set(key, make);
    if (scope === 'singleton') this.cache.set(key, { value: undefined, scope });
    return this;
  }
  resolve<T>(t: Token<T> | abstract new (...a: any[]) => T): T {
    const key: any = typeof t === 'function' ? t : t;
    if (this.building.has(key)) throw new CircularDependency(String(key));
    const factory = this.factories.get(key);
    if (!factory) throw new NoRegistration(key);
    if (this.cache.has(key)) return this.cache.get(key)!.value as T;
    if (this.scoped.has(key))  return this.scoped.get(key) as T;
    this.building.add(key);
    try {
      const value = factory(this);
      if (this.cache.get(key)?.scope === 'singleton') this.cache.set(key, { value, scope: 'singleton' });
      this.scoped.set(key, value);
      return value;
    } finally { this.building.delete(key); }   // finally, so a throwing factory doesn't poison the graph
  }
  // The per-request scope is a fresh child map — this is the mechanism behind "scoped" lifetimes.
}
```

**When to use a container vs manual wiring.**

- **Manual wiring (a composition root function):** application code, services, anything you own. Verbose but greppable, compile-time checked, trivial to debug, no magic.
- **Container:** frameworks, plugin architectures, large graphs with many implementations, multi-tenant binding sets where the *same* app code must run with different bindings per tenant.
- **Neither, at the HTTP edge:** routes should receive already-constructed handlers.

**Trade-offs of a container.** *Gain:* you stop repeating `new` chains, you get lifetimes and scoping for free, you can swap bindings per environment. *Cost:* **your dependency graph becomes runtime data.** Debuggers cannot follow it, stack traces get a resolution frame, mistakes become startup-time "no registration for X" instead of compile-time errors, IDE "go to definition" breaks, and the graph can hide coupling that looks clean in the code. The worst outcome is a codebase where classes have no constructors at all and take everything from a service locator — that is DI without any of its benefits: still hidden dependencies, but now untyped.

**Production considerations.**

- **Scoped lifetime is the correct default** for request-scoped things: current user, transaction, request logger, correlation id.
- **Never inject the container itself** into application classes (that is the service locator smell). Inject leaf collaborators.
- **Cycle detection at resolution time** with a clear message: `A -> B -> C -> A`. Most DI containers fail with an unhelpful stack overflow.
- **Resolution cost** on a hot path is real: pre-resolve per request in middleware, do not resolve in a loop.
- **Startup validation**: resolve the whole graph at boot in a smoke test so a missing registration fails on deploy, not on the first request that hits it.
- **Multi-tenancy:** bind per tenant in a child container; do not put `tenantId` in a singleton and hope.
- **Reflection-based auto-wiring breaks under minification/bundling** unless the metadata is explicit; note this if you are discussing a TypeScript + bundler stack.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Injecting concrete classes everywhere → cannot fake them → tests are integration tests wearing a unit-test hat.
2. Service locator inside methods.
3. Singleton lifetime for something holding request state → cross-request data leaks (the classic: a singleton holding the current user).
4. `transient` for a database pool → 10,000 pools.
5. Forgetting the `finally` in cycle detection → one throwing dependency poisons every later resolution.
6. Container doing business logic resolution (`resolve(PricingService)` where pricing depends on the request) → hidden, untestable decisions.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Do you use a DI container? Why/why not?"* — the strong answer: manual wiring in application code; a container only where the graph genuinely varies (plugins, per-tenant bindings). Show the composition root.
- *"How do you scope a transaction to a request?"* — a scoped container per request, with the transaction object registered as scoped and disposed at request end.
- *"What's a circular dependency and is it ever legitimate?"* — usually a design smell (two classes that want to talk should be split or coordinated by a third); occasionally legitimate in mutually-recursive domain structures, solved by a deferred lookup or by restructuring.
- *"How does DI interact with Singleton?"* — DI *is* the answer to Singleton: you get a shared instance with an explicit lifetime and a testable seam.

---

### 11.1.15 Circuit breaker, bulkhead, and the resilience pattern family

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A **resilience pattern** protects a system from failure in a dependency by failing fast, shedding load, or degrading gracefully, instead of propagating the failure upward.

**Circuit breaker.** States: `Closed` (normal), `Open` (fail fast), `Half-Open` (probe). It trips after a threshold of failures in a window, stays open for a cooldown, then lets a limited number of probes through.

```ts
type BreakerState = 'closed' | 'open' | 'half-open';
interface BreakerCfg {
  failureThreshold: number;      // consecutive failures to open (or ratio in window)
  openMs: number;                // cooldown before probing
  halfOpenMax: number;           // concurrent probes allowed
  successThreshold: number;      // successes in half-open needed to close
  minRequests: number;           // ignore low-volume periods (don't trip on 1/1)
}

class CircuitBreaker {
  private state: BreakerState = 'closed';
  private failures = 0; private successes = 0; private openedAt = 0; private inFlight = 0;

  constructor(private readonly cfg: BreakerCfg, private readonly now: () => number = Date.now) {}

  async call<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>, fallback?: (e: Error) => T): Promise<T> {
    this.transitionIfDue();
    if (this.state === 'open') {
      // (1) FAIL FAST: reject immediately. The whole point — do not consume the dependency's resources.
      throw new CircuitOpen(`open until ${this.openedAt + this.cfg.openMs}`);
    }
    if (this.state === 'half-open' && this.inFlight >= this.cfg.halfOpenMax) throw new CircuitOpen('half-open saturated');
    this.inFlight++;
    try {
      const v = await fn();
      this.onSuccess();
      return v;
    } catch (e) {
      this.onFailure();
      if (fallback) return fallback(e as Error);      // degraded response, not an error
      throw e;
    } finally { this.inFlight--; }
  }
  private transitionIfDue() {
    if (this.state === 'open' && this.now() - this.openedAt >= this.cfg.openMs) {
      this.state = 'half-open'; this.successes = 0; this.inFlight = 0;
    }
  }
  private onSuccess() {
    if (this.state === 'half-open' && ++this.successes >= this.cfg.successThreshold) { this.state = 'closed'; this.failures = 0; this.successes = 0; }
    else if (this.state === 'closed') this.failures = 0;
  }
  private onFailure() {
    if (this.state === 'half-open') { this.state = 'open'; this.openedAt = this.now(); this.successes = 0; return; }
    if (this.state === 'closed' && ++this.failures >= this.cfg.failureThreshold) { this.state = 'open'; this.openedAt = this.now(); }
  }
  get snapshot() { return { state: this.state, failures: this.failures, openedAt: this.openedAt }; }
}
// CRITICAL DETAIL most implementations get wrong: a timeout inside `fn` is what makes the breaker
// useful. Without a deadline, the dependency hangs, your threads exhaust, and the breaker never
// sees a failure. Timeout + breaker are a pair, not two independent features.
```

**The threshold decision (this is what interviewers ask).** Consecutive failures is simple and wrong for low-traffic endpoints (one unlucky request at 3am opens the breaker for the whole night). Prefer a **failure ratio over a rolling window with a minimum request count**: `if (requestsInWindow >= 20 && failures/requestsInWindow > 0.5) open`. Also: **only count failures that indicate the dependency is unhealthy** — a 400 from your own validation bug should never trip the breaker. Classify errors.

**Bulkhead.** Isolate failure domains so one exhausted resource cannot consume everything. Two forms, both worth naming:

```ts
// (1) Thread/concurrency bulkhead: a per-dependency semaphore so the failing dependency can only
//     consume its own permits. Without this, a slow dependency's queued requests consume the
//     shared executor and starve every other dependency — including ones that are healthy.
class Bulkhead {
  private available: number;
  private readonly waiters: (() => void)[] = [];
  constructor(private readonly capacity: number, private readonly queueLimit = 0) { this.available = capacity; }
  async acquire(): Promise<Release> {
    if (this.available > 0) { this.available--; return this.releaseOnce(); }
    if (this.queueLimit === 0) throw new BulkheadFull('rejected immediately');  // FAIL FAST, don't queue
    if (this.waiters.length >= this.queueLimit) throw new BulkheadFull('queue full');
    return new Promise(res => this.waiters.push(() => { this.available--; res(this.releaseOnce()); }));
  }
  private releaseOnce(): Release {
    let done = false;
    return () => { if (done) return; done = true;
      this.available++;
      const next = this.waiters.shift(); if (next) next(); };
  }
}
// Per-dependency bulkheads: paymentsBulkhead = new Bulkhead(20), searchBulkhead = new Bulkhead(50),
// recsBulkhead = new Bulkhead(100). Payments down → 20 stuck requests, everything else keeps working.

// (2) Resource bulkhead: separate connection pools / thread pools / queues per tenant or per
//     dependency. A "noisy neighbour" (one tenant hammering a shared pool) is the other motivation.
```

**The rest of the family.** Know these names and one line each:

- **Timeout** — the single highest-value pattern. Every remote call gets a deadline; the default is "no timeout" which is the worst default.
- **Retry** with exponential backoff + jitter. Jitter is not optional: without it, N clients retry at the same instant after a failure and re-create the overload you were trying to relieve (the *thundering herd*). Use full jitter: `sleep = random(0, base * 2^attempt)`.
- **Rate limiter** (11.4.3) — limit *rate*, breaker limits *failure*.
- **Fallback / graceful degradation** — a cheaper answer when the primary is unavailable (stale cache, default recommendation, "recommendations temporarily unavailable" instead of a 500). Distinguish fallback from circuit-open error: a fallback is a *successful degraded response*.
- **Load shedding** — reject deliberately when overloaded, prioritising important requests. Requires knowing your priority; admission control beats queue collapse.
- **Hedging** — send a duplicate request to a second replica after a delay fraction of the p99; reduces tail latency at the cost of extra load. Use sparingly and only for idempotent reads.
- **Backpressure** — make the queue bounded and reject at the producer. An unbounded queue converts a latency problem into a memory problem and then an outage.
- **Bounded concurrency** — caps in-flight work per dependency; simpler and often more effective than a queue.
- **Idempotency + deduplication** — required for retry to be safe; the reason retries are dangerous on writes.
- **Dead-letter queue** — messages that cannot be processed after retries go to a place a human can see, with the original payload and error.
- **Health check vs readiness probe vs liveness probe** — liveness should fail only on unrecoverable states (never on a slow dependency, or you restart-loop the whole service during a dependency outage).

**When to use vs not.**

- **Timeout:** always. No exceptions for "internal" services.
- **Retry:** only for **idempotent** operations, only with a budget (max attempts × max total time < caller's own deadline), and never for validation/4xx errors.
- **Breaker:** for every outbound dependency whose failure you would otherwise propagate.
- **Bulkhead:** whenever you share a thread pool, connection pool, or queue across dependency boundaries.
- **Fallback:** only where you can honestly define a degraded answer. Shipping a fake "0 results" as a fallback for a broken search index is worse than an error.
- **Not:** resilience patterns on local in-process calls — the cost is real and there is no network to fail.

**Trade-offs.** Every pattern converts *one* failure mode into *another*: latency (queueing), memory (buffering), correctness (stale fallback data), load (retries). The discipline is to have a **budget per layer**: connect timeout, read timeout, max attempts, total deadline, and make them nested so the inner budget is always smaller than the outer's. A retry budget larger than the caller's deadline is a bug that produces work nobody will read.

**Production considerations.**

- **Retry budget** as a percentage (e.g. retries ≤ 10% of total requests) with dynamic backoff; avoid retry storms by refusing retries above budget.
- **Circuit breaker per dependency, not global.** One global breaker takes down a healthy dependency because an unrelated one failed.
- **Half-open probes must be few and must be real work.** Half-open with unlimited probes = instant re-trip = flapping.
- **Breakers and deploys interact:** a rolling deploy produces a burst of cold connections; use a ramp and a jittered breaker open period.
- **Instrument everything:** breaker state transitions, rejected count, fallback count, retry count, timeout count. A dashboard where these are all zero for a month is either a very stable system or a broken one.
- **Fallbacks must be observable** as fallbacks. Otherwise you serve wrong answers for a week and nobody knows.

**Common mistakes.**

1. No timeout anywhere → one slow dependency saturates the whole request path.
2. Retrying non-idempotent writes → double charges, duplicate shipments.
3. Retrying a 400 → amplifies a client bug into a self-inflicted DoS.
4. Synchronous backoff inside a request handler → thread exhaustion.
5. One global breaker.
6. Breaker counting all exceptions including validation errors.
7. Fallback that returns empty results, silently.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which one gives the most benefit for the least risk?"* — **timeout**, always. Say this first.
- *"How do you choose the failure threshold?"* — ratio over a window with a minimum sample size, not consecutive count; measure your real error distribution.
- *"Breaker open and the client keeps hammering. Is that better or worse?"* — better: fast rejections use far less capacity than hanging requests, and they surface as an explicit error you can alert on.
- *"What is a thundering herd and how do you prevent it in retries?"* — jitter; and a retry budget.

---

### 11.1.16 Saga and the distributed-transaction pattern family

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A Saga is a sequence of local transactions, each in its own transaction, with **compensating actions** to undo the logical effects of completed steps when a later step fails. It replaces the 2PC (two-phase commit) database transaction across services.

**Why 2PC is not available in microservices.**

| | 2PC | Saga |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Atomicity | All-or-nothing, all-or-nothing guaranteed | Eventual: partial state possible, then compensated |
| Coupling | Coordinator blocks all participants | Participants independent |
| Availability | If coordinator or a participant is down, *everyone* blocks | Participants stay available |
| Latency | Rounds of prepare/commit | One round trip per step |
| Failure mode | In-doubt transactions need a recovery log | Compensation may itself fail |
| Requires | Same transaction manager, XA support | Nothing beyond normal local transactions |
| Scalability | Poor; holds locks while coordinating | Good |

**The two flavours, and this is the key question in the interview.**

```ts
// ---- ORCHESTRATED: a central coordinator drives the steps and issues compensations.
// Pros: explicit flow, easy to see the whole saga, easy to add a step/timeout.
// Cons: the coordinator knows every step (coupling), it is a deployment/bottleneck, and it can
//       become a God service. But it is BY FAR the easier thing to build and to debug.
class PlaceOrderSaga {
  constructor(
    private readonly reserveStock: (cmd: ReserveStock) => Promise<Reservation>,
    private readonly authorizePay:  (cmd: AuthorizePay) => Promise<Authorization>,
    private readonly releaseStock:  (r: Reservation) => Promise<void>,      // compensation
    private readonly voidPay:       (a: Authorization) => Promise<void>,     // compensation
    private readonly log: SagaLog,
  ) {}

  async execute(cmd: PlaceOrder): Promise<OrderId> {
    const steps: Compensable[] = [];
    try {
      const res = await this.compensate(steps, () => this.reserveStock({ sku: cmd.sku, qty: cmd.qty }));
      const auth = await this.compensate(steps, () => this.authorizePay({ amount: cmd.amount, key: cmd.idempotencyKey }));
      await this.log.commit(cmd.id);
      return auth.orderId;
    } catch (e) {
      // Compensations run in REVERSE order, each independently retried, and failures are recorded
      // for manual resolution rather than thrown away.
      for (const s of [...steps].reverse()) {
        try { await s.compensate(); }
        catch (ce) { await this.log.compensationFailed(s.name, ce); }   // record and continue
      }
      await this.log.failed(cmd.id, e);
      throw new SagaAborted(cmd.id, e);
    }
  }
  private async compensate<T>(stack: Compensable[], fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const v = await fn();
    stack.push({ name: fn.name, compensate: this.compensationFor(fn) });
    return v;
  }
}
```

```ts
// ---- CHOREOGRAPHED (event-driven): each service reacts to events and publishes its own.
// No coordinator. Steps are wired by events. Easiest to scale; HARDEST to debug and to reason about.
// Trade-off that must be stated: you trade explicit control flow for implicit coupling and
// hard-to-see failure paths.
async function onOrderPlaced(e: OrderPlaced, deps: Deps): Promise<void> {
  await deps.inventory.reserve(e.items);          // emits StockReserved | StockRejected
  await deps.payments.authorize(e.total, e.key);  // emits PaymentAuthorized | PaymentFailed
}
// If StockRejected arrives, the payment service must already have a handler to void the auth,
// and the order service must have a handler to mark the order cancelled. Nothing enforces that
// those handlers exist; a missing one is an order stuck "pending" forever.
```

**The canonical "choreographed saga leaks" example, worth memorising because it is the best illustration of the trade-off.** Adding `verifyFraudScore` to a 4-step choreography means editing the payment, the fraud, the notification, *and* the order service — 4 services changed to add one step, because each of them hard-codes "what happens next". An orchestrated saga with 4 steps means editing one file plus one adapter per service (2 files total), because the sequence lives in one place.

**Compensation is not rollback. Know these four differences.**

1. **Compensation cannot undo an observable side effect.** `email.send()` cannot be unsent; you can send an apology. `Inventory.decrement()` can often be re-incremented but the *history* still shows a decrement, and a warehouse may have physically picked the item. This is the core reason sagas need domain modelling of "semantic rollback".
2. **Compensation may fail, repeatedly.** Retry it independently, with its own retry budget, and persist its state so a crash mid-compensation resumes rather than leaks.
3. **Compensation must be idempotent** — because it will be retried.
4. **Compensation can be impossible to order.** Compensating step 3 without compensating step 2 can leave you worse off (e.g. refunding before cancelling a shipment you then cancel anyway — you end up with a return you did not want). Strict reverse order is the default, and the "already-refunded" checks in compensating actions matter.

**Steps: choreography, orchestration, and hybrid.** The real-world answer most mature teams converge on: **orchestrate the flow, choreograph the reactions.** A saga orchestrator issues commands and waits for events; the services do their local work and emit events; anything not central (notifications, analytics, cache warming) stays choreographed because its failure does not affect correctness.

**Persistent saga state (this is what makes it production-grade).** An in-memory saga dies with the process.

```ts
interface SagaStateRow {
  saga_id: string; order_id: string; current_step: string; status: 'running' | 'done' | 'compensating' | 'failed';
  payload: unknown; attempts: number; updated_at: string; version: number;
}
// The engine: a durable state machine per saga row, driven by a poller or by a queue.
// Recovery on boot: rows in 'running' past a timeout are re-evaluated — either resume the step
// (idempotent) or start compensation. Rows in 'compensating' are retried until success or until
// the manual-intervention threshold, then flagged for an operator.
// CRITICAL: each step must be idempotent because crash-recovery re-executes the current step.
```

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** when a business transaction spans services and you accept eventual consistency: order + payment + inventory + shipping + notification.
- **Use:** when 2PC is unavailable (different databases, different vendors) or too slow/fragile.
- **Don't use:** when everything is in one database — then a local ACID transaction is strictly better (simpler, stronger, faster).
- **Don't use** for consistency you cannot compensate: charging someone's card and committing them to a price they no longer accept. Compensation there is a *customer service problem*, not a transaction problem.
- **Don't use** for read-heavy flows where a stale read is acceptable — cache and be done.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* services stay autonomous and available; no distributed lock; failures are handled explicitly and can be inspected.
*Cost:* **intermediate inconsistent states are visible to users.** This is the deal. You need: idempotency everywhere, compensating actions designed with the domain, user-facing states for "pending", manual-repair tooling, and a reconciliation process. Also: development velocity drops — sagas are hard to reason about, and testing them means simulating each failure point, which means you need a fault-injection harness.

**Production considerations.**

- **Idempotency keys on every step**, persisted, with a unique constraint to make duplicates impossible. This is not optional; retries are inevitable.
- **Timeouts on every step**, and the saga's total budget must be less than the caller's.
- **Outbox pattern for event publication** — see 11.3.6. Without it you have "the DB committed but the event wasn't published", which is the single most common saga bug.
- **Compensation ordering and idempotency**, plus a compensation log that an operator can replay.
- **Pessimistic vs optimistic saga**: pessimistic compensates everything; optimistic compensates only the sensitive steps. Optimistic is faster and cheaper; pessimistic is safer. Choose per step.
- **Dashboard**: every saga's current step, age, and attempts. A saga stuck for 4 hours is an incident; without a dashboard you find out from a customer.
- **Reconciliation job**: periodically compare intended vs actual state and repair. Sagas are not self-healing by construction.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Compensation assumes it can undo a real-world side effect.
2. No idempotency keys → duplicate charges on retry.
3. Compensation not retried/persisted → leaked resources.
4. Choreography where the flow logic is duplicated in every service, so adding a step means touching all of them.
5. Ignoring the intermediate state in the UI: the order sits in "processing" and the customer refreshes 40 times.
6. No timeout on a step → the saga hangs forever holding a reservation.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Why not just use a distributed transaction?"* — different databases/vendors, XA unavailable, 2PC blocks availability and holds locks, and coordinator failure causes in-doubt transactions.
- *"Your saga is stuck in `compensating`. What do you do?"* — look at the compensation log, retry with backoff, and if it keeps failing, route to manual repair; the design must have an operator path, not just code.
- *"Orchestrated or choreographed?"* — orchestrate the correctness-critical sequence; choreograph the non-critical reactions. Justify with the "add a step" file-count argument.

---

### 11.1.17 Pub/Sub, and how it differs from Observer

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Publish/Subscribe decouples publishers from subscribers *completely*: the publisher does not know subscribers exist, and subscribers do not know publishers exist. Delivery happens through a broker or mediator. Often asynchronous and cross-process.

**The precise difference from Observer.** The diagram is nearly identical; the *coupling* is not.

| | Observer | Pub/Sub |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Subject knows Observers | **Yes** — it holds the list and calls them | **No** — it publishes to a broker |
| Coupling | Compile-time, in-process | Runtime, often cross-service |
| Subscriber knows Subject | Usually yes (it imports the Subject type) | No — it knows only the topic |
| Delivery | Synchronous, same process, in-line | Async (usually), possibly at-least-once |
| Failure of one subscriber | Can affect the others (unless isolated) | Independent, retried by the broker |
| Scaling subscribers | Vertical, one process | Horizontal, add consumers |
| Evolve a subscriber | Requires deploying the Subject's process | Deploy the new subscriber only |
| Failure mode | Bug in your code | Broker down, duplicate delivery, ordering |

**The three questions to answer when choosing.** *Who knows whom? Who is affected by whose failure? Who needs to scale independently?* If subscribers must scale or deploy independently, or if failure isolation matters, you need a broker.

```ts
// ---- Observer: subject holds subscribers. In-process, synchronous.
const bus = new Emitter<Events>();
bus.on('order.placed', p => analytics(p));
bus.emit('order.placed', { id, total });

// ---- Pub/Sub: broker in the middle. Any process, any language, any deploy cadence.
//   Producer: publish to a topic; no subscriber registry exists in this codebase at all.
await broker.publish('orders.placed.v1', { id, total, occurredAt }, {
  key: id,                      // partition key → ordering per entity, not globally
  headers: { 'traceparent': currentTraceparent(), 'tenant': tenantId },
  idempotencyKey: `order.placed:${id}`,
});
//   Consumer: subscribe; may be a service that did not exist when the event was published.
broker.subscribe('orders.placed.v1', async (msg) => {
  const e = parseOrderPlaced(msg);          // (1) parse+validate: unknown fields are fine
  if (await inbox.seen(msg.id)) return;     // (2) idempotency: at-least-once means duplicates
  await projections.upsert(e);              // (3) the actual work, in ONE local transaction with the inbox
  await inbox.mark(msg.id);
}, { concurrency: 8, visibilityTimeoutMs: 30_000 });   // (4) concurrent + ack-after → at-least-once
```

**The five properties you must be able to discuss, because they define the whole design.**

1. **Delivery semantics.** `at-most-once` (ack before handling — you lose data on crash), `at-least-once` (ack after handling — you get duplicates; this is the only sane default), `exactly-once` (only within a transaction that includes both the inbox write and the effect — i.e. **effectively-once**, achieved with the inbox table, not by the broker). State clearly: *exactly-once delivery does not exist; effectively-once processing does, via an idempotent inbox in the same local transaction as the effect.*
2. **Ordering.** Brokers guarantee order *per partition*, not globally. If `OrderPlaced` and `OrderShipped` must be ordered, both must share a partition key (the order id). Cross-entity ordering requires a different design.
3. **Durability and replay.** The event log is durable; consumers track their offset. This gives you replay (rebuild read models — 11.3.7), which is the single most valuable property of event-driven systems and should be your main argument for them.
4. **Schema evolution.** Producers and consumers deploy independently. Rules: additive changes only; tolerant reader (ignore unknown fields, require the ones you need); never rename or remove a field; new fields must be optional or have defaults; version in the topic name (`orders.placed.v2`) with a migration period, or a registry with compatibility checks.
5. **Failure semantics.** Poison messages (retries exhausted → DLQ), backpressure (consumer lag), poison-pill prevention, and the fact that a slow consumer in a partition blocks that partition.

**Events vs commands on a broker.** Name them differently or you will regret it: commands are **imperative and addressed to exactly one logical handler** (`OrderAggregate.reserve.v1`, key = orderId, a handler must exist or it is a bug); events are **declarative facts in the past tense** with **zero or many** handlers (`OrderReserved.v1`, facts that have already happened and cannot be denied). A broker topic named `ProcessPayment` is a command; `PaymentProcessed` is an event. Getting this wrong produces systems where "what happens next" is unknowable because it depends on who is subscribed.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use** for: cross-service notification, scaling consumers independently, building read models, decoupling release cadence, integrating with external parties, decoupling release cadence, telemetry/audit pipelines, workflow fan-out that must not fail the caller.
- **Use** for "domain event inside one service, 2–4 in-process listeners" — here **Observer is better** (no broker to run, transactions possible, easier debugging).
- **Don't use** for: anything where the caller needs the result; anything where you cannot tolerate eventual consistency; anything where you cannot operate a broker.
- **Don't use** as an excuse to avoid thinking about the flow. **The flow moves; it does not disappear.** Choreographed pub/sub means the sequence of steps lives in *no single place*. That is the whole cost.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* independent deployment and scaling, failure isolation, replay, new consumers with no producer change, natural audit trail.
*Cost:* **operational complexity** (a new stateful system to run, back up, secure, and observe), **eventual consistency visible to users**, **harder debugging** (you cannot follow a request; you follow a trace), **schema governance**, **exactly-once illusions**, and **the loss of the ability to read the flow**. Every one of these must be paid before the first benefit arrives.

**Production considerations.**

- **Outbox pattern** for publishing (11.3.6): write the event to an outbox table in the same transaction as the state change, then a relay publishes and marks it sent. This eliminates the dual-write problem. If you do nothing else in this section, do this.
- **Consumer idempotency is mandatory**: inbox table + unique constraint on message id, written in the same transaction as the effect.
- **Version every topic**, enforce compatibility in CI with a schema registry or a "tolerant reader" test that deserialises an old fixture with the new code and vice versa.
- **DLQ with alert** and a documented replay procedure (re-drive from DLQ after a fix; ensure handlers are idempotent so replay is safe).
- **Partition key choice is a permanent schema decision** (it determines ordering guarantees). Changing it later means a migration.
- **Consumer lag is the primary SLI** for event-driven systems; alert on it.
- **Correlation/causation ids on every message** (`traceparent`, `causationId`) — without them, distributed tracing across async boundaries is guesswork.
- **Backpressure**: bound consumer concurrency, monitor lag, and shed when lag exceeds a threshold rather than letting every consumer grind.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Publishing events inside the same transaction as the DB write, without an outbox → "committed but never published".
2. Assuming exactly-once → duplicate charges.
3. Renaming a field in a payload → a consumer that was deployed 3 days ago breaks.
4. Publishing events for things that must be ordered, on different keys.
5. Using events for the *primary* flow of a domain that needs immediate answers → confusing, slow, and eventually consistent.
6. No `traceparent` → undebuggable async flows.
7. A topic with 40 subscribers and no owner per subscriber, so nobody knows who may safely change the schema.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Observer vs pub/sub — when do you actually need the broker?"* — when subscribers scale/deploy independently or you need failure isolation, replay, or a read model.
- *"How do you guarantee exactly-once?"* — you don't; you get effectively-once via an inbox in the same transaction. The broker's exactly-once claims do not cover your side effects.
- *"What happens if a subscriber is down for an hour?"* — lag grows, then it catches up; capacity must handle catch-up; alert on lag, not on subscriber count.
- *"Why not just call the other service?"* — because that couples availability and deployment. Say which one you would choose here and why.

---

### 11.1.18 Pattern anti-patterns: when applying a pattern makes it worse

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A pattern anti-pattern is the application of a named pattern in a context where its costs exceed its benefits — producing code that is more abstract, less readable, and no more correct than what it replaced.

**The master heuristic: patterns trade *local* simplicity for *global* flexibility.** You pay locally (one more file, one more indirection) and hope to win globally (change one place when variability arrives). Three questions decide whether the trade paid:

1. **Has the variability actually arrived?** Not "might". Has it arrived twice already?
2. **Is the number of participants small enough that a reader can hold it?** More than ~5 participants in a diagram and you have lost more than you gained.
3. **Is the pattern's cost concentrated where it hurts most?** Indirection in a hot loop or in a CRUD endpoint hurts; indirection in a rarely-changed extension point does not.

**Anti-pattern catalogue, one per pattern, with the smell and the fix.**

| Pattern | How it goes wrong | The smell you will see | The fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Singleton** | Global state + hidden dependency | `X.getInstance()` inside a method; `static reset()` in tests | Inject it; ask for a `Container` singleton lifetime instead |
| **Factory Method / Abstract Factory** | Factory that forwards to a `switch` | `switch (type) { … default: return new … }` inside the factory | Use a registry `Record<Kind, ctor>`; or admit it is a switch and delete the factory |
| **Builder** | Builder that can build anything, including nothing | All-optional fields; no `build()` validation | Required fields in the builder's constructor; or an options object |
| **Adapter** | Adapter containing business rules | `if (customer.isPremium)` inside the adapter | Business rules in a domain service; adapter is translation only |
| **Facade** | Facade as God object | 80 methods, mixed read/write, everyone adds to it | Split by client use case; introduce an application service |
| **Bridge** | Bridge for a single implementation | Interface with exactly one implementor, comment "for future" | Delete it; use the concrete class |
| **Decorator** | Decorator forgetting to forward a method | `undefined is not a function` after someone adds a method | `implements` the interface; a forwarding test |
| **Proxy** | Proxy on a hot path / used as security | Every property access logged in prod; `Proxy` around request objects | Use plain code; use a Proxy only for data-driven rules at the boundary |
| **Observer** | God emitter with 200 event names, no types | Stringly-typed `emit('order.placed')` with no payload type | Typed event map; split the emitter; move cross-process to pub/sub |
| **Command** | One class per trivial method, no undo/queue | `AddTwoCommand` with an `execute()` | Use a function; use Command when it buys deferral/undo/serialisation |
| **Strategy** | Strategy selected by a switch inside the factory | Same problem as the factory anti-pattern | Registry from config; or data table if the branches are data |
| **State** | States that return nothing / no next state | `state.handle()` with `void` returns | It is a Strategy — rename it or make it return the next state |
| **Template Method** | Hook explosion; subclasses that lie | `protected validate() { return true; }` to skip a step | Compose the pipeline; inject steps |
| **Repository** | Generic `BaseRepository<T>` mirroring the ORM | `repo.qb()` in a service; `Repo<any>` | Per-aggregate repos with domain-named methods |
| **Unit of Work** | UoW spanning the whole HTTP request including remote calls | Locks held for seconds | Narrow the transaction; never include a third-party call |
| **DI container** | Service locator; no constructors; unresolvable graph | `Container.get(X)` in a method; circular deps | Composition root + constructor injection |
| **Circuit breaker** | Breaker counting 4xx; global breaker | One breaker per app | Per dependency; count only dependency-health errors |
| **Saga** | Choreography with copied flow logic in every service | Adding a step touches 5 services | Orchestrate the correctness-critical flow |
| **Pub/Sub** | Events for the primary domain flow | `await publish('ProcessPayment')` | Synchronous call for the flow; events for reactions |

**The two most important anti-patterns in practice.**

1. **The Pattern Tax.** When a team learns patterns, they start converting *every* `if` into a class. The measurable outcome: 400 files instead of 60, a new hire takes 3 weeks to understand the checkout flow, and the "flexibility" is never used. The fix is a written rule: *a new file requires a named axis of variation.* Reviewers enforce it. This single rule prevents more damage than any pattern advice.

2. **Diagrammatic Design.** Choosing a pattern because the UML diagram looks impressive, not because a stated requirement varies. The tell: nobody can name the requirement that would justify the indirection. The fix in an interview is to be asked "which requirement drove this?" — if you cannot answer, revert.

**How to review someone else's patterns.** A five-question checklist that works in a real code review:

1. **Point to the requirement that makes this abstract.** If there is none, it should be concrete.
2. **Count the participants.** Over ~5 in one collaboration, the reader's working memory is now the bottleneck.
3. **Find the decision.** Where does the choice of implementation live? If it is a `switch`, the abstraction is decorative.
4. **Delete test.** Remove the pattern, restore the inline code, and diff. If the diff is smaller than the pattern, the pattern loses.
5. **Trace one real request end to end.** If you cannot narrate the flow through the pattern layers without opening 8 files, the indirection is exceeding the abstraction.

**How to talk about this in an interview without sounding anti-pattern.** The winning posture is *eclectic and reasoned*: "I use X when Y, and I deliberately keep Z concrete because …". Anyone can list patterns; the value is in the conditions. The worst answer is "I avoid patterns because they are over-engineering" (you have not thought about the value) or "I use all the GoF patterns" (you have not thought at all).

**Common mistakes.**
- Treating "pattern anti-pattern" as licence to write unstructured code.
- Removing a pattern from a codebase that genuinely varies, to look minimal.
- Reviewing patterns by name rather than by requirement.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which pattern did you remove?"* — the best answer to this question in the entire interview.
- *"How do you decide when a switch is fine?"* — when the branches are few, short, and stable, and when the decision point is the only place that should know about the options.
- *"Our team has 400 files in the payment service. Is that bad?"* — not inherently; it depends on whether the variability is real and whether one person can still narrate the main flow.

---

## 11.2 OOP, SOLID, and Clean Code

### 11.2.1 Encapsulation, abstraction, polymorphism, inheritance

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Encapsulation.** Two distinct things people conflate:

- *Data encapsulation*: keeping representation private so it can change freely. The mechanism is access control plus the discipline not to expose mutable internals.
- *Behavioural encapsulation*: keeping **preconditions and invariants** inside the class that owns the state, so an invalid object cannot exist.

```ts
// BAD: representation leaks AND invariants live in callers.
class Account {
  balanceCents: number;
  currency: string;
  withdraw(amount: number) { if (amount > this.balanceCents) throw new Error('no'); this.balanceCents -= amount; }
}
// Callers can write acct.balanceCents = -999999, or acct.currency = 'XXXX' mid-transaction,
// or call withdraw(-100) and *increase* the balance. Every invariant is somebody's problem.

const acct = new Account(); acct.balanceCents = 100; acct.currency = 'USD';
acct.withdraw(50);                          // 50
acct.balanceCents = -500;                   // now negative. No error. Total mess.

// GOOD: behavioural encapsulation. Money is a value with invariants; the account enforces them.
class Money {
  private constructor(readonly minorUnits: number, readonly currency: Currency) {}
  static of(minor: number, c: Currency): Money {
    if (!Number.isSafeInteger(minor)) throw new NonIntegerAmount(minor);   // cents are integers, always
    if (!SUPPORTED.has(c)) throw new UnsupportedCurrency(c);
    return new Money(minor, c);
  }
  static zero(c: Currency) { return new Money(0, c); }
  add(o: Money): Money {
    if (o.currency !== this.currency) throw new CurrencyMismatch(this.currency, o.currency);
    const sum = this.minorUnits + o.minorUnits;
    if (!Number.isSafeInteger(sum)) throw new Overflow(sum);             // catches the 2^53 cliff
    return new Money(sum, this.currency);
  }
  mul(f: number): Money {
    if (!Number.isFinite(f) || f < 0) throw new BadFactor(f);
    return new Money(Math.round(this.minorUnits * f), this.currency);
  }
  compare(o: Money): number { return this.minorUnits - o.minorUnits; }
}
// Every construction goes through a validating factory. Every operation returns a NEW Money.
// Invalid money is unrepresentable, which is strictly stronger than "documented not to happen".
```
