**Encapsulation is about *where invariants live*, not about getters.** A class with 12 getters and a `setX` per field has no encapsulation; it has a public data structure with extra steps. The real question for any getter: *can a caller corrupt state through this?* If yes, remove it.

```ts
// Progressive disclosure: expose behaviour, not representation.
class Order {
  get total(): Money { return this.lines.reduce((t, l) => t.add(l.lineTotal), Money.zero(this.currency)); }  // fine: derived
  set total(_: Money) { throw new Error('Order total is derived from lines'); }                                // never
}
```

**Abstraction.** Hiding *how* while exposing *what*. Distinct from encapsulation: encapsulation is about protecting invariants; abstraction is about reducing the concept a client must understand.

- **Domain abstraction:** `PaymentGateway.charge()` — the client knows what it does, not that the vendor uses HTTP and JSON.
- **Parameteric/functional abstraction:** `map`, `filter`, `Repository<T>` — one implementation covering a family of cases.
- **Non-domain abstraction:** `Logger`, `Executor`, `Serializer` — infrastructure seams.

```ts
// The "right number of abstractions" heuristic: an abstraction earns its keep if it (a) has ≥2
// real implementations now or soon, (b) names a domain concept rather than a mechanism, or
// (c) shields a caller from a change the caller should not care about.
// Anything failing all three is a pass-through method — delete it and call the thing directly.
```

**Polymorphism.** One interface, many implementations, selected at runtime. Its value is *substitutability*: a client written against the interface never learns which implementation it got.

```ts
// The test for real polymorphism: can you replace the implementation without touching the client?
interface FreightQuote { quote(shipment: Shipment): Promise<Money>; }
class FedexQuote implements FreightQuote { /* ... */ }
class InHouseQuote implements FreightQuote { /* ... */ }

async function checkout(s: Shipment, quote: FreightQuote, pay: PaymentGateway): Promise<Order> {
  const fee = await quote.quote(s);                    // client knows NOTHING about Fedex
  await pay.charge(s.total().add(fee), s.id);
  return Order.from(s, fee);
}
// Substitutable, independently testable, independently deployable. That's polymorphism earning its keep.
```

**Inheritance.** "Is-a" plus code reuse. Use it for **genuine, stable taxonomies**, not for code sharing.

```ts
// GOOD: the hierarchy says something true about the domain and is stable.
abstract class AppError extends Error {
  constructor(message: string, readonly code: string, readonly cause?: unknown) { super(message); this.name = new.target.name; }
  toJSON() { return { error: this.code, message: this.message }; }
}
class NotFound extends AppError { constructor(what: string) { super(`${what} not found`, 'not_found'); } }
class ValidationError extends AppError { constructor(readonly fields: Record<string, string>) { super('invalid', 'validation'); } }
const e = new NotFound('order');
// Now: every catch can do `e instanceof AppError ? e.toJSON() : generic500(e)`.
// One iteration of that branch is closed by adding a subclass. That is Open/Closed in practice.

// BAD: reuse as the only justification. This is the "Fragile Base Class Problem" in its purest form.
class SqlRepository { /* 20 methods */ }
class UserService extends SqlRepository { /* wants 1 method */ }
// Now every SqlRepository change is a UserService change, and UserService exposes 19
// methods it should not have. Prefer composition: `class UserService { constructor(private repo: Repo) {} }`
```

**The framing to give in an interview.** These four are *means to an end*, and the end is **changeability**. Ask, for each: what change does this make cheap, and what does it make expensive? Encapsulation makes representation changes cheap and misuse hard. Abstraction makes dependency changes cheap. Polymorphism makes adding an implementation cheap. Inheritance makes adding a *subclass* cheap and adding a *field to the parent* expensive. That last sentence is the honest, memorable version of the whole debate.

**When to use vs not.**

- Encapsulation: **always**; the question is only how far.
- Abstraction: name a domain concept, not a mechanism. `PaymentGateway`, not `HttpClientWrapper`.
- Polymorphism: at every axis of variation you have identified. Not on classes with a single implementation and no seam.
- Inheritance: shallow, stable, semantic. Error hierarchies, collection hierarchies, framework extension points.

**Trade-offs.** All four reduce the amount of code that must change together, at the cost of indirection and of a runtime lookup you cannot see in the source. Encapsulation costs a bit of boilerplate and sometimes forces you to add a method for every question. Polymorphism costs a virtual call and a discoverability problem (grep does not find implementations).

**Production considerations.**

- Public mutable arrays on your domain objects (`order.lines.push`) break encapsulation and make invariants unmaintainable — expose `readonly Line[]`.
- **Don't leak DTOs as domain objects across the wire** — you will end up with a "domain object" whose fields came from JSON and were never validated.
- `Object.freeze` your value objects in development to make accidental mutation loud.
- Serialisation frameworks will happily construct your "immutable" entity through a no-arg constructor and setters, defeating the encapsulation. Configure strict hydration or use a factory.
- Deprecation is a design activity: if a public method cannot be changed without coordinating 12 call sites, you did not encapsulate.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Getter/setter pairs everywhere → no invariants.
2. Returning `readonly T[]` but the caller casts it away, or returning the internal array directly.
3. Deep inheritance trees (4+ levels) → every change ripples.
4. Inheritance for code reuse between siblings ("I need the helper method").
5. Interfaces named after implementations (`ISqlUserRepository`).
6. `protected` used as "I'll change it later" rather than for subclasses.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you encapsulate an invariant when the DB is the source of truth?"* — validate on hydration; if invalid, refuse to load and raise a data-integrity error. Never silently repair.
- *"Is composition always better than inheritance?"* — for code reuse, almost always; for genuine taxonomies, no. `Error` subclassing is fine.
- *"Give a getter you would delete."* — any getter that exposes a mutable internal collection or an invariant-bearing field.

---

### 11.2.2 Composition over inheritance, with worked examples

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**The rule.** Reuse behaviour by holding a reference to a collaborator (composition) rather than by extending a parent. Inheritance gives you *substitutability*; composition gives you *flexibility and independence*.

**Why, in one sentence: inheritance couples you to what your parent might change; composition couples you only to the small interface you declared.**

**Worked example 1: the notification hierarchy that had to be rewritten.**

```ts
// INHERITANCE: 2 notification types × 2 transports = 4 classes; add a "digest" notification
// and you need DigestSms — and DigestSms cannot be a subclass of both SmsNotifier and DigestNotifier.
abstract class Notifier {
  abstract send(msg: Message): Promise<void>;
  abstract retryPolicy: RetryPolicy;         // each subclass owns this; the caller has to know it
}
class EmailNotifier extends Notifier { /* … */ retryPolicy = none; }
class SmsNotifier   extends Notifier { /* … */ retryPolicy = aggressive; }
class DigestNotifier extends Notifier { /* … */ }   // can't be combined with transport

// COMPOSITION: orthogonal axes multiply without classes multiplying.
interface Transport { deliver(msg: WireMessage): Promise<Receipt>; }
interface MessageBuilder { build(m: Message): WireMessage; }

class NotificationService {
  constructor(private readonly transport: Transport,      // SMTP / SMS / push — one axis
              private readonly builder: MessageBuilder,    // plain / digest / templated — another axis
              private readonly policy: RetryPolicy) {}     // and another
  async send(m: Message): Promise<Receipt> {
    return this.policy.execute(() => this.transport.deliver(this.builder.build(m)));
  }
}
const svc = new NotificationService(new SmsTransport(smsClient), new DigestBuilder(userPrefs), new ExponentialRetry(3));
// Digest + SMS: exists. SMS + digest + exponential retry: exists. New transport: 1 class. New format: 1 class.
```

**Worked example 2: the shape hierarchy.**

```ts
// INHERITANCE: Square extends Rectangle, and the world breaks.
// Every client that accepts a Rectangle must now handle a Square, which violates LSP
// (a Square IS-A Rectangle only if you accept that changing width also changes height).
class Rectangle { constructor(public w: number, public h: number) {} area() { return this.w * this.h; } }
class Square extends Rectangle { setSide(s: number) { this.w = s; this.h = s; } }
// totalArea(shapes: Rectangle[]) calling shape.setWidth(5) on a Square silently breaks the others.

// COMPOSITION: shapes are data; the invariant lives where it belongs.
interface Shape { area(): number; }
const square   = (side: number): Shape => ({ area: () => side * side });
const rectangle = (w: number, h: number): Shape => ({ area: () => w * h });
// No LSP violation is possible, because there is no hierarchy to violate.
```

**Worked example 3: logging/metrics on a service — cross-cutting behaviour without the hierarchy.**

```ts
// INHERITANCE: LoggingOrderService extends OrderService. Now what about Metrics + Logging?
// (4 subclasses.) And the "don't forget to call super" trap.
class LoggingOrderService extends OrderService {
  async place(c: PlaceOrder) { log.start(); const r = await super.place(c); log.done(); return r; }
}
// COMPOSITION: decorate the collaborator once, regardless of the number of concerns.
class InstrumentedRepo implements OrderRepository {
  constructor(private readonly inner: OrderRepository, private readonly m: Metrics) {}
  async save(o: Order) { const t = performance.now(); try { return await this.inner.save(o); }
                        finally { this.m.timing('repo.save', performance.now() - t); } }
  async findById(id: string) { return this.inner.findById(id); }
  // Adding metrics later = wrapping again, no subclass, no `super` hazards.
}
```

**Worked example 4: the algorithm-as-collaborator (favour composition over a Template Method hierarchy).**

```ts
// INHERITANCE: Exporter subclassing Exporter; a new output format is a new subclass; the shared
// logging/validation lives in the parent and every subclass inherits it whether it wants it or not.
abstract class Exporter { protected abstract render(): string; /* shared: validate, log, compress */ }

// COMPOSITION: the shared algorithm is a function that receives the varying step.
interface Exporter { readonly ext: string; render(data: Report): Promise<Buffer>; }
function exporting(ext: string, render: (d: Report) => Promise<string>, hooks: Hooks = {}): Exporter {
  return {
    ext,
    async render(d: Report) {
      hooks.before?.(d);
      const body = await render(d);          // the variable step, injected
      hooks.after?.(body);
      return Buffer.from(body);
    },
  };
}
const pdf = exporting('pdf', (d) => renderPdf(d), { after: (b) => audit(b) });
const csv = exporting('csv', (d) => toCsv(d));
// A subclass that wanted to skip validation now can: just don't pass the hook.
```

**When inheritance IS the right answer.** Be able to say these without hedging:

- **Stable semantic taxonomies**: `AppError` → `NotFound` → `OrderNotFound`. The taxonomy is a fact about the domain, not a code-reuse device.
- **Framework extension points** where you *want* to constrain the flow: Spring's `Template`, JUnit lifecycle hooks, Django middleware chains.
- **Immutable value objects** with structural variation and shared invariants: `Shape` subclasses where every subclass is final and behaviour is overridden completely (no LSP hazard because there is no partially-overridden behaviour).
- **Sealed hierarchies you control**, exhaustively switched over, where adding a case must be a compile error at every use site.

**The honest cost of composition** (do not omit this, it shows you understand the trade):

- More objects and more indirection; the flow is spread across collaborators.
- Interface proliferation; you may write an interface with one implementation.
- Delegation boilerplate (every method forwarded).
- Runtime dispatch instead of a devirtualised call — irrelevant except in the hottest loops.
- Composition requires *knowing the axes of variation up front*; inheritance lets you hide them and discover them later. If the axis is not yet known, composition forces a premature guess.

**Production considerations.**

- Deep hierarchies break under framework magic: Jackson, Hibernate and Spring all assume default constructors and setters, which fights proper encapsulation.
- When you compose, define the interfaces at the *consumer* (the client that needs the behaviour), not at the provider. Consumer-owned interfaces are small, stable, and idiomatic.
- For plugins, use composition + a registry (Strategy/Factory), not a base class + `abstract` methods, because plugins live in separate assemblies.
- Watch for "composition explosion": 5 one-method collaborators where a base class would do. That is over-application, and it is the standard counter-argument to this rule.

**Common mistakes.**

1. `class CsvExporter extends Exporter` where `Exporter` exists solely to share logging.
2. Delegation that is pure pass-through (`getUser() { return this.repo.getUser(); }` with nothing else) — that is a middleman, delete it.
3. Composition used to avoid a small amount of duplication between two unrelated classes (the two classes should just both have the code, or share a util).
4. "Composition" that means `new` inside the method — that is not composition, that is hiding a dependency inside the class.
5. Replacing a legitimate taxonomy (error types) with string codes.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You have used composition your whole career and it bit you. When?"* — when the axis of variation was not known; you guessed an interface, guessed wrong, and now you have five collaborators to do what a `switch` would have.
- *"Show me a case where you deliberately chose inheritance."* — errors, framework extension points.
- *"How do you choose the interface location?"* — at the consumer.

---

### 11.2.3 The five SOLID principles, each with a before/after code example

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

Each entry below: one-line definition, the **before** (violating), the **after** (satisfying), the trade-off, and the probe.

#### Single Responsibility Principle (SRP)

**Definition.** A module should have one reason to change — one *axis* of change. Not "does one thing" (vague); "if this reason changes, only this file changes".

```ts
// BEFORE: three reasons to change in one class — pricing rules, persistence, notification.
// Any pricing change forces a recompile-and-review of the DB code, so unrelated changes collide.
class Order {
  place(items: Item[]): void { /* pricing rules live here */ this.save(); this.email(); }
  save(): void { /* schema lives here */ }
  email(): void { /* template lives here */ }
}

// AFTER: each reason to change is in exactly one place.
class OrderService {
  constructor(private readonly pricing: PricingRule,
              private readonly orders: OrderRepository,
              private readonly notifier: Notifier) {}
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder): Promise<Order> {
    const order = Order.from(cmd, this.pricing.quote(cmd.items));
    await this.orders.save(order);                 // persistence reason → repository
    await this.notifier.send(orderConfirmation(order));  // comms reason → notifier
    return order;
  }
}
```

*Trade-off:* more files, more constructor parameters, more indirection for a 20-line class. SRP is where "clean code" most often becomes over-fragmentation — a class with 3 private methods each delegating to a collaborator with one implementation is SRP-pedantry, not SRP.

#### Open/Closed Principle (OCP)

**Definition.** Software entities are open for extension, closed for modification. In practice: modules you should not need to edit are extended by adding code, not changing code.

```ts
// BEFORE: every new report format is an edit to this switch, in this file, forever.
class ReportWriter {
  write(report: Report): string {
    switch (report.format) {
      case 'csv':  return toCsv(report);
      case 'json': return JSON.stringify(report);
      case 'pdf':  return toPdf(report);
      case 'xml':  return toXml(report);   // and it keeps growing
    }
  }
}

// AFTER: add a writer, add a registration; never edit ReportWriter.
interface ReportWriter { readonly format: string; write(r: Report): Promise<string>; }
class CsvWriter implements ReportWriter { readonly format = 'csv'; async write(r) { return toCsv(r); } }
const WRITERS: ReportWriter[] = [new CsvWriter(), new JsonWriter(), new PdfWriter(), new XmlWriter()];
// OCP realised: the *registry* changes (data) while the *orchestrator* does not (code).
```

*Trade-off:* indirection and a runtime lookup for a lookup that used to be a `switch`. OCP is only worth paying where the number of variants is genuinely large or genuinely changing.

#### Liskov Substitution Principle (LSP)

**Definition.** If `S` can be used where `T` is expected, `S` must be usable *without the caller knowing*. Subtypes must honour the base contract: preconditions cannot be strengthened, postconditions cannot be weakened, and no new exceptions.

```ts
// BEFORE: a textbook violation. SquaredRectangle violates the setWidth contract because it
// must change BOTH dimensions — the caller's expectation is silently broken.
class Rectangle { setWidth(w: number) { this.w = w; } setHeight(h: number) { this.h = h; } get area() { return this.w * this.h; } }
class Square extends Rectangle {
  constructor(private side: number) { super(side, side); }
  setWidth(w: number) { super.setWidth(w); super.setHeight(w); }   // strengthens the side effect
}
function renderAll(shapes: Rectangle[]) { shapes.forEach(s => s.setWidth(10)); }
// renderAll([new Square(5)]) -> the "Rectangle" logic now sees an area of 100 instead of 50.
// Fix by composition:
//   const square = (side: number): Shape => ({ area: () => side * side });

// A subtler, very real violation: a collection that breaks its own contract.
class NoNullsList<T> implements List<T> {
  add(x: T) { this.items.push(x); }
  get(i: number): T { return this.items[i]; }   // contract said "throws RangeError"; returns undefined
}
// Any caller that used try/catch around index access now gets undefined and proceeds.
```

**The practical test:** *"Could a caller written against the base type be *surprised* by this subtype?"* Surprises are: new required arguments, extra exceptions, stricter validation, different side effects, non-substitutable return values (a `null` where the base promised a value), changed ordering.

*Trade-off:* LSP fights "clever" subclass specialisation; sometimes the honest answer is a separate class or a flag-free union type instead of a subtype.

#### Interface Segregation Principle (ISP)

**Definition.** No client should be forced to depend on methods it does not use. Prefer several small, client-specific interfaces over one fat one.

```ts
// BEFORE: any implementation must implement all 8 methods, including ones the client will never call.
interface Worker {
  execute(task: Task): void;
  stop(): void;
  getStatus(): Status;
  flushQueue(): void;
  setPriority(p: Priority): void;
  getMetrics(): Metrics;
  reloadConfig(): void;
  shutdown(): void;
}
class FakeWorker implements Worker { /* 5 methods throw new Error('not implemented') */ }  // the smell
// Testing `execute` requires implementing stop, flushQueue, reloadConfig... and if a method throws,
// the test breaks for an unrelated reason.

// AFTER: client-specific interfaces. Each implementation implements only what it actually does.
interface TaskRunner  { execute(task: Task): Promise<void>; }
interface Stoppable    { stop(): Promise<void>; }
interface Instrumented { getMetrics(): Metrics; }

class ScheduledExecutor implements TaskRunner, Stoppable, Instrumented { /* real */ }
class ImmediateExecutor implements TaskRunner { /* two methods, and that is ALL */ }
// Now a test fake for TaskRunner is four lines and never breaks.
```

*Trade-off:* more interfaces, and sometimes duplication of method signatures. That duplication is the cost of not being forced to implement `reloadConfig`.

#### Dependency Inversion Principle (DIP)

**Definition.** High-level modules must not depend on low-level modules; both must depend on abstractions. Abstractions must not depend on details; details should depend on abstractions.

```ts
// BEFORE: the domain service knows about the HTTP client, the SQL driver, and the file system.
class OrderService {
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder) {
    const r = await fetch('https://inventory.internal/stock', { method: 'POST', body: JSON.stringify(cmd) });  // low-level detail
    await this.pool.query('INSERT INTO orders …');                                                              // low-level detail
    this.fs.writeFileSync('/var/log/orders.log', …);                                                           // low-level detail
  }
}
// Now: business logic requires a network, a DB, and a disk to unit test.

// AFTER: the domain names what it needs; the composition root supplies the mechanism.
interface StockGateway { available(sku: string, qty: number): Promise<boolean>; }
interface OrderStore    { save(o: Order): Promise<void>; }
interface AuditLog      { record(entry: string): Promise<void>; }

class OrderService {                       // depends only on abstractions, all domain-named
  constructor(private readonly stock: StockGateway, private readonly orders: OrderStore, private readonly audit: AuditLog) {}
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder) {
    if (!await this.stock.available(cmd.sku, cmd.qty)) throw new OutOfStock(cmd.sku);
    const order = Order.from(cmd);
    await this.orders.save(order);
    await this.audit.record(`order placed ${order.id}`);
    return order;
  }
}
// Adapters implement StockGateway with fetch/grpc/whatever. Tests implement it with a map.
```

*Trade-off:* an interface per collaborator, and interfaces that can become "guess the seam" ceremony. DIP is the principle most often over-applied: apply it where the volatility is real.

**Cross-principle synthesis (this is the part that scores).** The five principles are one idea seen from five sides: **put the change in the right place.** SRP puts each axis of change in its own file. OCP means adding a variant is a new file. LSP keeps substitution safe so the abstraction is trustworthy. ISP keeps the abstraction small so it is implementable. DIP keeps the dependency pointing inward so the detail can be replaced. If you can state that in one sentence, you understand SOLID as a theory rather than a checklist.

**Production considerations.**

- Interface-per-class can explode into 20 files with 3-line interfaces; a codebase of pure abstractions is unreadable. Rule of thumb: an abstraction must either have ≥2 implementations (real or planned with a date) or shield a volatile dependency.
- Framework magic and DIP fight: Spring, Jackson, and Hibernate want to instantiate and mutate your classes. Accept DI on the framework edge, keep the domain pure.
- "Open for extension" without a closing mechanism is a lie — a registry that only one module can write to is not OCP; it is a `switch` with indirection.

**Common mistakes.**

1. ISP misapplied to create a 1-method interface per method (chatty interfaces: `ReadOrderId`, `ReadOrderTotal`). Merge by role, not by method.
2. DIP applied to your own value objects (`IOrder`) — absurd.
3. SRP applied at the-method level until every class is one method.
4. Liskov violation via "convenience" extra methods that only work on the subtype.
5. OCP via a giant registry file that everyone edits — which is the `switch` with extra steps.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which SOLID principle is most violated in practice, and what does it cost?"* — LSP, via base classes that subclasses "specialise" by adding required behaviour; cost: every `catch` block and every caller is wrong for one subtype.
- *"Show me ISP applied to an interface you own."* — the fat-interface-to-roles split above.
- *"How do you know when to stop applying SOLID?"* — when the abstraction has one implementation, no test double, and no volatility.

---

### 11.2.4 DRY, KISS, YAGNI applied honestly

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

These are the three heuristics people quote and misapply most. The honest version of each includes its failure mode.

#### DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself)

**The correct statement.** Knowledge should have a single authoritative representation. Note: *knowledge*, not *text*. Two identical-looking blocks of code can express different knowledge (coincidental duplication), and two different-looking pieces can express the same knowledge (a bug waiting to happen).

```ts
// GOOD duplication to remove: the same *decision* repeated in two places.
function vat(amount: Money, country: Country): Money {
  return amount.mul(VAT_RATES[country]);        // knowledge lives in VAT_RATES
}
function invoiceTotal(lines: Line[], country: Country): Money {
  const net = lines.reduce((t, l) => t.add(l.gross), Money.zero('USD'));
  return net.add(vat(net, country));            // reuses the decision — cannot drift
}
// If the tax rule changes, there is exactly one place to change it.

// BAD duplication to KEEP: coincidental duplication.
if (user.role === 'admin') { log.info(`[admin] ${msg}`); } else { log.info(`[user] ${msg}`); }
// The two lines LOOK alike. They are separate decisions that happen to coincide today.
// Merging them creates a function whose name cannot describe both, and when the admin case
// gains its own behaviour you have to unpick it. Coincidental duplication is CHEAP.
```

**The real question for DRY: what is the *unit* of duplication?** Three candidates, and they have very different answers:

| Duplicated thing | Verdict |
| --- | --- |
| A rule/decision ("tax is X", "the retry count is Y") | Extract — this is true duplication. |
| A stable algorithm with 3 call sites | Extract; the third site usually reveals a fourth parameter. |
| A 3-line block in 2 unrelated places | Leave it. Extraction costs more than duplication. |

**The DRY failure modes you must know:**

1. **Premature abstraction.** You DRY two things that will diverge. Now you have a function with a boolean flag and a name like `doThing(item, isSpecial)`. The flag is a hidden `if`, and you have made the code worse while claiming to improve it.
2. **Wrong altitude.** DRY-ing at the wrong level creates a "shared utility" module that everything imports — the exact coupling you were trying to remove. Shared utilities are the smell: a module with 40 unrelated functions has no cohesion.
3. **Coupling via constants.** `const RETRY = 3` in a shared file used by two subsystems for unrelated reasons. They now change together for no reason. That is *accidental* coupling, and it is worse than duplication.
4. **The fourth case.** Rule of three: at two occurrences, wait; at three, look hard. Most of the time the third occurrence shows you the right abstraction immediately.

**Test for a successful DRY:** "If the business changes rule X, how many files must I edit?" If the answer is 1, the extraction was right. If it is 3, you extracted text, not knowledge.

#### KISS (Keep It Simple)

**The correct statement.** Prefer the solution with the fewest *moving parts* (concepts, layers, states, configuration) that satisfies the requirement. Not the fewest lines.

**Moving parts, not lines — this is the key reframe.** A 300-line state machine with 6 states is simpler than 40 lines of reflection-based generics with 5 layers of indirection. Count concepts: classes, interfaces, registries, configuration keys, deployment units, background jobs, failure modes.

```ts
// SIMPLE: one obvious way.
export async function getOrder(id: string, db: Pool): Promise<OrderRow | null> {
  const { rows } = await db.query('SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = $1', [id]);
  return rows[0] ?? null;
}
// COMPLEX: the same thing, six moving parts, three configuration options, no failure tests.
class OrderQueryBuilder { constructor(private readonly dialect: Dialect, private readonly pool: Pool) {}
  where(f: Predicate) { /* … */ } join(…) orderBy(…) limit(…) cache(ttl) replica(…) build() { /* … */ } }
```

**KISS failure modes:**

1. **Under-engineering that becomes expensive.** The simple thing that works until the third requirement, at which point it must be *rewritten* (not refactored), and the rewrite is where the incidents happen.
2. **Confusing simple with *short*.** Removing the error handling to make a function 3 lines shorter.
3. **Confusing simple with *not automated*.** "Manual deploys are simpler" — until the 3am deploy.
4. **Confusing simple with *ignore the hard parts*.** Skipping idempotency because it's "extra".

**The KISS test:** *"Could a new engineer understand this in 5 minutes?"* and *"How many things must be understood simultaneously for this to work?"* If the second answer is high, you have complex code even if each piece is easy.

#### YAGNI (You Aren't Gonna Need It)

**The correct statement.** Don't build capability without a requirement — but distinguish *speculative generality* from *designing for known change*.

```ts
// YAGNI violation: a plugin architecture for one plugin.
interface Plugin { name: string; configure(cfg: Config): void; }
class PluginRegistry { register(p: Plugin) {} resolve(name: string) {} /* … 200 lines */ }
// One plugin exists. A new engineer must learn the whole registry to add a second feature.

// YAGNI violation: "make it generic" on a thing that has one shape.
class Cache<K, V> { /* … */ }
const cache: Cache<string, string> = new Cache();   // one type, always

// NOT a YAGNI violation — designing for change you KNOW is coming, with a date and a name:
// - money in minor units (you know currencies have 0 and 3 decimal places; you know float rounding bugs)
// - a merchant multi-tenant model (you know you are signing enterprise customers in Q3)
// - an event bus on your order domain (you know billing needs order events next quarter)
// - injected Clock/IdGenerator (you know you will want deterministic tests)
```

**The distinction that makes YAGNI usable: YAGNI is about *capability*, not about *interfaces*.** An interface with one implementation is cheap (12 lines) and buys you the ability to test. A speculative plugin framework is expensive (200 lines) and buys you nothing you can name. Use YAGNI against the second.

**YAGNI failure modes:**

1. **Applying it to testability.** "We'll add DI later if we need it" — you will not, because by then every dependency is `new`ed deep inside methods.
2. **Applying it to error handling and observability.** Those are not speculative generality; they are the minimum viable production.
3. **Treating it as an argument against refactoring.** Refactoring an existing mess is not YAGNI.
4. **Confusing "we don't need it" with "we don't need it *yet, and here is the date it will be needed*".**

**Production considerations.**

- **YAGNI has a cost you only pay on the day you need the thing**, and that day is the worst possible time. The mitigation is *documented seams*, not speculative implementations: put the interface where it belongs, write one implementation, note "second implementation when X is signed". That is cheap insurance, not YAGNI violation.
- **DRY cost**: shared modules are the highest-coupling files in any codebase. Give them one owner and treat changes to them as high-risk.
- **KISS cost**: undocumented complexity is invisible; simple-looking code with hidden failure modes is the most expensive code you can ship.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Extracting a 2-line helper used twice with a flag parameter.
2. A `utils.ts` with 200 functions that every file imports (the shared-code smell).
3. YAGNI as a reason not to add input validation or error handling.
4. KISS as a reason not to automate.
5. Using the three acronyms as rhetorical weapons instead of as decision rules.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You're told to DRY this code. What do you check first?"* — whether the two sites express the same *knowledge*, not the same text.
- *"When is a shared utility module a smell?"* — when its functions have no common theme; it is a dumping ground that everything imports.
- *"How do you apply YAGNI to a scaling concern?"* — you don't pre-optimise; you do add the metric and the cache behind an interface only when a profile says so.
- *"Give me a time you were wrong about YAGNI."* — the honest answer scores best.

---

### 11.2.5 Naming: the rules that make code readable

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Why naming is architecture.** A name is a design decision you publish to every future reader. The cost of a bad name is paid on every read, forever; the cost of a good one is paid once. Naming is also where you most often leak *intent* — a name tells the next person which of your three `getX` overloads they need.

**The eight rules that matter, with the smell each one prevents.**

1. **Names say what something *is*, not what it *does*.** "What it does" belongs in the body; "what it is" belongs in the name. `processOrderData()` is a smell — processing what, how, and why is data? Prefer `settleInvoice()`. Exception: commands/functions legitimately describe the action (`placeOrder`, `retryPendingCharges`).

```ts
// BAD
function process(data: any, flag: boolean) { /* … */ }
const list = [];          // list of what?
const flag = true;        // flag for what?
// GOOD
function settleOverdueInvoices(asOf: Date): Promise<SettlementReport> { /* … */ }
const pendingSettlements: Settlement[] = [];
const isRetryEligible: boolean = /* … */;
```

2. **Booleans read as predicates, and positive names are safer.** `isActive`, `hasCapacity`, `shouldRetry`, `canRefund`. Avoid double negatives (`isNotDisabled`) and avoid names that invite the wrong call: `deleteQuietly` (does it delete?), `close()` (does it free resources?).

3. **`is`/`has`/`can`/`should` are conventions, not rules.** Pick a convention for booleans in your codebase and apply it; the value is that a reader can scan a long boolean expression (`if (a && !b && c)`) without looking up each identifier.

4. **Units and formats are in the name or they are in the type.** `timeoutMs` not `timeout`, `retryCount` not `retries` (plural suggests a collection), `amountMinor` not `amount`, `placedAt`/`expiresAt` (timestamps as `At`, durations as a suffix: `durationMs`, `intervalSec`). This one rule prevents the most common class of production bug (seconds vs milliseconds).

5. **Collections are plural, singular is one item.** `order`, `orders`, `orderIds`. Distinguish `user` (the entity) from `userId` (the reference) from `userRepository`. The `Id` suffix is non-negotiable in TypeScript.

6. **Distinguish the entity from the DTO from the command from the view.** Same domain concept, four names, four layers: `Order` (domain), `OrderRow` (persistence), `PlaceOrder` (input command), `OrderView` (API output). If all four are `Order`, then every function that takes an `Order` must be checked for which one it really wanted. This is a real, common, expensive bug source.

7. **`get` means retrieval; `find` means retrieval that can fail to find; `load` means retrieval that does I/O; `create` means construction that may or may not persist.** These conventions are worth having because they answer "can this return null?" at the call site without reading the body:

```ts
findUser(id: string): Promise<User | null>      // may be absent
getUser(id: string): Promise<User>              // throws NotFound if absent
loadUser(id: string): Promise<User>              // hits the DB; the name says so
requireUser(id): User                           // sync, throws
```

8. **Domain language over technical language, consistently.** If the business says "fulfilment" and the code says `shipment`, every conversation requires a translation. If the code says "cancel" and the business says "void", same problem. Choose one and never mix — the biggest offender is a codebase with `delete`/`remove`/`destroy` meaning the same thing.

**Additional high-value rules.**

- **Name the unit test the same as the behaviour**: `rejects duplicate idempotency keys`, not `handles edge case 3`.
- **Avoid `get` + `set` pairs on domain objects** (encapsulation, 11.2.1). A `setX` that validates an invariant is a behaviour; name it `applyDiscount`, `markAsShipped`.
- **Prefix with the mechanism when the distinction matters**: `parseOrderRow` vs `loadOrderRow` vs `fetchOrderRow`.
- **Do not encode the type in the name redundantly** (`orderList: Order[]` → `orders`), but DO encode non-obvious representations (`ordersById: Map<OrderId, Order>`).
- **Version in the name only for external contracts** (`OrdersApiV2`), never for internal code.
- **Negations**: `!isEligible` is fine in a private boolean; `noRetry` in a public config is a bug factory — use `retry: boolean`.
- **Avoid an `Impl` suffix** except to distinguish an interface from its only production implementation in a codebase with genuine plugin variance; `InMemoryUserRepository` says more than `UserRepositoryImpl`.
- **Test names as documentation of intent** (`it('refunds idempotently when the gateway times out')`) — this is the highest-value naming practice in a codebase.

**The naming smells checklist** (grep-able patterns that mean someone needs to fix them):

`data`, `info`, `temp`, `tmp`, `obj`, `handler`, `manager`, `helper`, `util`, `utils`, `process`, `doIt`, `flag`, `flag2`, `val`, `res`, `result`, `item`, `thing`, `stuff`, `foo`, `bar`, `thing2`, `value`, `list`, `arr`, `x`, `i` (except in a 3-line loop), `e` (except in a catch).

**`Manager` and `Helper` deserve special mention.** They are names that admit they have no responsibility. A class called `PaymentManager` has not decided whether it is a service, a repository, a state machine, or a facade — and neither has the reader. Fix by finding the noun it actually manages and the verb it actually performs.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Abbreviations: `ordSvc`, `calcAmt`, `qty` (fine in an invoice line, not in an order API), `proc`.
2. Encoding implementation in the name (`arrayOfOrders`, `orderArray`, `fetchUserFromDbUsingHttp`).
3. `get2`, `newOrder`, `orderNew` — versioned-by-append names indicate unclear ownership.
4. Inconsistent synonyms for one concept across the codebase.
5. Boolean parameters: `send(to, html, true, false)` — four positional arguments and zero information. Use an options object with named booleans.

**Production considerations.**

- Names in **log lines and metrics** are also interfaces (dashboards and alerts depend on them). Renaming a log field breaks every query built on it.
- Names in **public API fields** and **event payloads** are permanent contracts; deprecate, never rename.
- Naming discipline erodes under deadline pressure; put a lint rule or a review checklist item in, otherwise it depends on memory.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Rename this method."* — you should ask what it does, then give a name that says intent.
- *"What's the most common naming mistake you see?"* — `get` that may return null, and `handler`/`manager` nouns that mean nothing.
- *"How do you handle naming collisions between the domain and the API?"* — deliberate suffix scheme: domain `Order`, DTO `OrderView`, command `PlaceOrder`; never reuse one name for two shapes.

---

### 11.2.6 Code smells and the refactoring that fixes each

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

A **code smell** (Fowler's catalogue) is a surface symptom suggesting a deeper problem. The useful thing about the catalogue is that most entries name the *refactoring* that fixes them — which is what interviewers want you to demonstrate.

**The core catalogue, mapped to the refactoring that fixes it.**

| Smell | What it looks like | Why it hurts | Refactoring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Duplicated Code** | Same logic in 2+ places | Change must be made in N places; drift | Extract Function/Class; but check it is *knowledge* duplication first |
| **Long Method** | > 50–80 lines, many branches | Hard to test, hard to name, hard to change | Extract Method; then Compose Methods |
| **Long Parameter List** | 6+ parameters, several same-typed | Positional blindness, call sites unreadable | Parameter Object (Builder if optional) |
| **Divergent Change** | One class edited for many unrelated reasons | No single reason to change; merge conflicts | Split the class (SRP) |
| **Feature Envy** | A method reaches into another object's data more than its own | Logic is in the wrong class | Move Method; or Extract the data into a value object |
| **Data Clumps** | Same 4 fields always travel together | Duplicated signatures; change them together | Introduce Parameter Object; then a value object |
| **Shotgun Surgery** | One logical change requires editing many files | Change cost is unpredictable | Move the behaviour to where the data is; centralise the switch/registry |
| **Primitive Obsession** | `String` for money/email/ID everywhere | Invalid states representable; parsing everywhere | Value Objects (`Money`, `OrderId`, `Email`) |
| **Speculative Generality** | Hooks, abstract classes, unused config | Reader cost, no current user | Remove it (YAGNI) |
| **Message Chains** | `a.getB().getC().getD()` | Leaky abstraction, brittle | Introduce a method on the object that has the data; return DTOs |
| **Middle Man** | Class that only forwards | Indirection with no value | Delete it; call the delegate |
| **Refused Bequest** | Subclass ignores most of the parent | LSP violation, dead code | Replace inheritance with delegation/composition |
| **Conditional Complexity** | Long `if/else if` chains | Untestable branches; grows forever | Replace with Strategy; **guard clauses**; table-driven logic |
| **Dead Code** | Unreachable branches, unused exports | Misleading; rots | Delete it (with a grep + a coverage check) |
| **Speculative Type Safety** | `as` casts everywhere | Types lie; runtime errors later | Parse at the boundary; make illegal states unrepresentable |

**The four worth writing code for**, because they are what people actually struggle with in a review.

**1. Primitive Obsession → value objects.** The single highest-leverage refactor in a codebase, especially around money.

```ts
// BEFORE: `number` for money. Every call site must know the currency, the unit, and the rounding rule.
function applyTax(amount: number, rate: number): number { return Math.round(amount * (1 + rate)); }
const total = applyTax(1000, 0.2);        // 1200 — correct?
// In yen (0 decimals) this is wrong. In kuruş (2 decimals) this is wrong. In USD cents this is also wrong.
// And `amount` in cents vs dollars is indistinguishable at every call site. Total: 0 units of information.

// AFTER: the unit is in the type.
class Money {
  private constructor(private readonly minor: number, readonly currency: Currency) {}
  static of(minor: number, c: Currency) { /* validate integer, currency known */ return new Money(minor, c); }
  static fromDecimal(dec: number, c: Currency) {
    const scale = CURRENCY_SCALE[c];                    // 2 for USD, 0 for JPY, 3 for BHD
    if (!Number.isFinite(dec)) throw new BadAmount(dec);
    return new Money(Math.round(dec * 10 ** scale), c); // ONE place knows the rounding rule
  }
  taxed(rate: number) { return new Money(Math.round(this.minor * (1 + rate)), this.currency); }
  toDecimal() { return this.minor / 10 ** CURRENCY_SCALE[this.currency]; }
}
function applyTax(m: Money, rate: number): Money { return m.taxed(rate); }  // cannot be misused
// Now `applyTax` cannot be called with dollars when the caller meant cents, and JPY is correct.
```

**2. Data Clumps → Parameter Object / Value Object.**

```ts
// BEFORE: (startDate, endDate, startTime, endTime, timezone, locale) travel together through 8 methods.
function formatReport(start: Date, end: Date, tz: string, locale: string, fmt: string, currency: string) { /* … */ }

// AFTER: one concept, one type, one place to add the next field.
interface DateRange { readonly start: Date; readonly end: Date; readonly timeZone: TimeZone }
function formatReport(range: DateRange, locale: Locale, currency: Currency, fmt: Format) { /* … */ }
```
The pay-off is not the shorter signature: it is that `DateRange` can validate `start <= end`, and the sixth parameter can no longer be passed in the wrong slot.

**3. Feature Envy → Move Method.**

```ts
// BEFORE: Order.total() is reaching deep into Line and Money. The pricing logic lives on Order,
// which does not own the pricing data.
class Order { total(): number { return this.lines.reduce((s, l) => s + l.unitPrice * l.qty * (1 - this.discountPct), 0); } }

// AFTER: the logic moves to where the data lives; Order asks.
class OrderLines { constructor(private readonly lines: readonly Line[], readonly discountPct: number) {}
  total(): Money { return this.lines.reduce((s, l) => s.add(l.lineTotal(this.discountPct)), Money.zero(this.currency)); } }
```
Rule of thumb for detecting envy: count references to `this` versus references to another object inside a method. If another object dominates, the method is in the wrong place.

**4. Shotgun Surgery → centralise the decision.** A "change the tax rate" change touching 14 files means the *decision* is scattered. Move it to one place:

```ts
// BEFORE: 14 files each hard-code a rate. Shotgun surgery.
// AFTER: one table + one resolver. Every file reads the decision from the same source.
export const TAX_RATES: Record<CountryCode, number> = { US: 0.07, DE: 0.19, IN: 0.18, JP: 0.10 };
export function taxFor(country: CountryCode, rate: import('./tax-versions').TaxVersion): number {
  return TAX_RATES[country] * (rate === 'reduced' ? 0.5 : 1);
}
```

**The refactoring safety rules** (interviewers love these):

1. **Tests before, tests during, tests after.** If you cannot get a characterisation test on the existing behaviour, you cannot refactor safely.
2. **One refactoring at a time.** Refactoring and behaviour change in the same commit is unreviewable and unrevertable independently.
3. **Small commits.** Each commit must leave the tests green.
4. **Refactoring is not a behaviour change.** If the semantics changed, that is a feature, and it needs its own test and its own note.
5. **Coverage is necessary, not sufficient.** Coverage tells you a line ran, not that an assertion exists.
6. **When in doubt, characterisation-test first.** (See 11.2.10.)

**Common mistakes.**
1. Long parameter list "solved" with an options bag of 12 optional fields (Builder problem).
2. Extracting a method that is used once, named `processData`.
3. "Removing duplication" of coincidental duplication — the classic over-refactoring mistake.
4. Splitting a class into 5 micro-classes and calling it SRP.
5. Deleting a long method's body without understanding it and shipping it.
6. Adding an abstraction during a bug fix (mixing concerns again).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Which smell would you look for first in a code review?"* — duplication of *knowledge*, because it causes drift; or shotgun surgery, because it makes change cost unpredictable.
- *"You have a 200-line method and no tests. What do you do?"* — write characterisation tests for the current behaviour first, then Extract Method bottom-up, one at a time.
- *"Refactor or rewrite?"* — refactor almost always; rewrite when the design is fundamentally wrong, and then extract-and-strangle rather than big-bang.

---

### 11.2.7 Clean architecture / hexagonal architecture: ports and adapters

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Clean Architecture (Uncle Bob, 2012) and Hexagonal Architecture (Alistair Cockburn, 2004) are the same idea: **the domain is at the centre and depends on nothing; everything else is an adapter plugged into a port.** The dependency arrow always points inward. Testability and technology independence come free.

**The rule.** *Source-code dependencies point inward. Details depend on abstractions; abstractions never depend on details.* Outer layers may know about inner layers; inner layers know nothing about outer layers.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Frameworks & Drivers  (HTTP handlers, CLI, DB drivers, UI,   │  ← OUTER  (adapters)
│  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  Interface Adapters (HTTP controllers, REST/GraphQL,    │  │
│  │                      event listeners, schedulers)        │  │
│  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │
│  │  │  Application Layer (use cases / application services,│ │  │  ← drives the domain
│  │  │   transactions, DTO mapping, orchestration, ports)   │  │  │
│  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  │  │
│  │  │  │  DOMAIN (entities, value objects, aggregates,  │  │  │  │  ← THE CENTRE
│  │  │  │   domain services, domain events, invariants)   │  │  │  │     zero framework imports
│  │  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │  │  │
│  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Ports and adapters, precisely.**

- A **port** is an interface owned by the *inside*, expressed in domain language, describing what the inside needs. `OrderRepository`, `PaymentGateway`, `Clock`, `NotificationSender`.
- An **adapter** is an implementation of a port that lives *outside* and knows the technology. `SqlOrderRepository`, `StripePaymentGateway`, `SystemClock`, `SmtpNotificationSender`.
- A **driving adapter** (primary/hexagon side) takes input from outside and calls the application: HTTP controller, CLI command, message consumer, cron job.
- A **driven adapter** (secondary side) is called by the application: database, message broker, HTTP client, filesystem.

**The one-line test to state in an interview:** *"The domain layer must compile with zero non-standard imports."* If `src/domain` imports `express`, `pg`, or `axios`, the boundary has leaked.

**Full worked example: place an order, hexagonal.**

```ts
// ───────────────────────────── DOMAIN (no imports except local) ─────────────────────────────
export type Currency = 'USD' | 'EUR' | 'JPY';
export class Money {
  private constructor(private readonly minor: number, readonly currency: Currency) {}
  static of(minor: number, c: Currency): Money {
    if (!Number.isSafeInteger(minor)) throw new NonIntegerAmount(minor);
    return new Money(minor, c);
  }
  static zero(c: Currency) { return new Money(0, c); }
  add(o: Money): Money {
    if (o.currency !== this.currency) throw new CurrencyMismatch(this.currency, o.currency);
    const s = this.minor + o.minor;
    if (!Number.isSafeInteger(s)) throw new AmountOverflow(s);
    return new Money(s, this.currency);
  }
  toMinor() { return this.minor; }
}
export class OrderId { constructor(readonly value: string) {
  if (!/^ord_[0-9a-f]{8}$/.test(value)) throw new InvalidOrderId(value); } }   // value object

export class Order {                                                        // aggregate root
  private constructor(readonly id: OrderId, readonly customerId: string,
                      readonly lines: readonly OrderLine[], readonly placedAt: Date) {}
  static place(id: OrderId, customerId: string, rawLines: RawLine[], now: Date, currency: Currency): Order {
    if (rawLines.length === 0) throw new EmptyOrder();                     // invariant
    if (rawLines.length > 100) throw new TooManyLines(rawLines.length);
    const lines = rawLines.map(l => OrderLine.of(l.sku, l.qty, l.unitPriceMinor, currency));
    const seen = new Set<string>();
    for (const l of lines) if (!seen.add(l.sku)) throw new DuplicateSku(l.sku);
    return new Order(id, customerId, lines, now);
  }
  get total(): Money { return this.lines.reduce((t, l) => t.add(l.total), Money.zero(this.lines[0]!.total.currency)); }
  // Order has NO save(), NO repository reference, NO id generation, NO timestamps of its own.
}
class OrderLine {
  private constructor(readonly sku: string, readonly qty: number, readonly unitPrice: Money) {}
  static of(sku: string, qty: number, minor: number, c: Currency) {
    if (!sku.trim()) throw new BlankSku();
    if (!Number.isInteger(qty) || qty <= 0) throw new BadQuantity(qty);     // invariant
    return new OrderLine(sku, qty, Money.of(minor, c));
  }
  get total(): Money { return this.unitPrice.mul(qtyOf(this.qty)); }
}

// ───────────────────────────── PORTS (owned by the inside) ─────────────────────────────
export interface OrderRepository {                        // a port
  save(o: Order): Promise<void>;
  findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null>;
  // Deliberately NOT: queryBuilder(), rawSql(), session(), entityManager()
}
export interface PaymentGateway { charge(amount: Money, key: string): Promise<PaymentReceipt>; }  // port
export interface IdGenerator { nextOrderId(): OrderId; }  // port
export interface Clock { now(): Date; }                    // port

// ───────────────────────────── APPLICATION (use cases) ─────────────────────────────
export class PlaceOrderUseCase {                         // NO transaction boundary knowledge of HTTP
  constructor(private readonly orders: OrderRepository, private readonly payments: PaymentGateway,
              private readonly ids: IdGenerator, private readonly clock: Clock,
              private readonly tx: TransactionRunner) {}    // port for atomicity
  async execute(cmd: PlaceOrderCommand): Promise<PlaceOrderResult> {
    return this.tx.run(async () => {
      const order = Order.place(this.ids.nextOrderId(), cmd.customerId, cmd.lines,
                                this.clock.now(), cmd.currency);
      const receipt = await this.payments.charge(order.total, cmd.idempotencyKey);
      if (receipt.status === 'declined') throw new PaymentDeclined(receipt.reason);
      await this.orders.save(order);
      return { orderId: order.id.value, total: order.total };
    });
  }
}
export interface TransactionRunner { run<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T>; }   // port
```

```ts
// ───────────────────────────── DRIVING ADAPTERS (HTTP) ─────────────────────────────
export function httpAdapter(app: PlaceOrderUseCase, auth: Auth) {
  return {
    post: async (req: IncomingMessage, res: ServerResponse) => {
      const body = await readJson(req);
      const parsed = PlaceOrderCommandSchema.safeParse(body);     // validation at the EDGE, not in the domain
      if (!parsed.success) return json(res, 422, { errors: parsed.error.flatten() });
      if (!auth.mayPlaceOrder(req)) return json(res, 403, { error: 'forbidden' });
      try {
        const r = await app.execute(parsed.data);                 // one call into the application
        return json(res, 201, r);
      } catch (e) {
        return e instanceof DomainError ? json(res, e.status, e.toJSON())
                                        : json(res, 500, { error: 'internal' });
      }
    },
  };
}
// Note: the controller knows nothing about SQL, nothing about Stripe, nothing about transactions.
// It translates HTTP -> command and result -> HTTP. That is the whole job. Maximum width, no depth.

// ───────────────────────────── DRIVEN ADAPTERS (Postgres, Stripe) ─────────────────────────────
class SqlOrderRepository implements OrderRepository {         // adapter
  constructor(private readonly db: Pool, private readonly mapper: OrderMapper) {}
  async save(o: Order): Promise<void> {
    await this.tx(() => this.db.query(
      `INSERT INTO orders (id, customer_id, placed_at, currency, total_minor)
       VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4,$5)
       ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE SET status = orders.status`,   // idempotent save
      [o.id.value, o.customerId, o.placedAt.toISOString(), o.currency, o.total.toMinor()]));
    await this.batchLines(o);
  }
  async findById(id: OrderId): Promise<Order | null> { /* explicit loading, no lazy proxies */ }
}
class PgTransactionRunner implements TransactionRunner {
  constructor(private readonly pool: Pool) {}
  async run<T>(fn: () => Promise<T>): Promise<T> {
    const c = await this.pool.connect();
    try { await c.query('BEGIN'); const v = await fn(); await c.query('COMMIT'); return v; }
    catch (e) { await c.query('ROLLBACK'); throw e; }            // always rollback
    finally { c.release(); }                                    // always release — else pool exhaustion
  }
}
class StripeGateway implements PaymentGateway { /* vendor quirks live ONLY here */ }

// ───────────────────────────── COMPOSITION ROOT ─────────────────────────────
function main() {
  const pool = new PgPool({ connectionString: process.env.DATABASE_URL, max: 10 });
  const usecase = new PlaceOrderUseCase(
    new SqlOrderRepository(pool, new OrderMapper()),
    new StripeGateway(new Stripe(process.env.STRIPE_KEY!)),
    new UlidOrderIdGenerator(),
    new SystemClock(),
    new PgTransactionRunner(pool),
  );
  http(createServer(httpAdapter(usecase, new JwtAuth(process.env.JWT_SECRET!)))).listen(8080);
}
```

**Why it is worth the cost (the actual benefits, not the aesthetics):**

1. **The domain is testable with zero infrastructure.** `Order.place(...)` is a pure function; the whole domain test suite runs in milliseconds with no DB. This is the single most concrete, measurable payoff.
2. **The use cases are testable with fake adapters** — a `FakePaymentGateway` that declines on the third call is 10 lines and lets you test the decline path, which you can never test against real Stripe.
3. **Framework churn stops at the edge.** Migrating Express → Fastify touches `httpAdapter` and nothing else. Migrating Postgres → DynamoDB touches `SqlOrderRepository` and nothing else. This is the argument that convinces sceptics.
4. **Team parallelism.** Two teams can work on two adapters without conflicts.
5. **Vendor risk containment.** A payment provider's API change is one file.

**The costs, stated honestly (an interviewer will push here):**

1. **Boilerplate and mapping.** DTO ⇄ command mapping, row ⇄ domain mapping, and a lot of small files. Real: 3–5× the code for the same behaviour on a CRUD app.
2. **Over-abstraction on simple things.** Not every `getUserById` needs a port. Ports should be justified by volatility or by the need to fake it in tests.
3. **Leakage still happens.** Three directions: (a) the ORM leaks through because the entity *is* the domain model (Active Record defeats the boundary); (b) transaction management leaks upward — "this use case must be atomic with that one" is an application-layer concern that ripples into domain signatures; (c) the domain needs to know *something* about time and identity, which you solve with `Clock`/`IdGenerator` ports, and then someone puts a UUID generator *inside* the domain entity (which is a leak, not a solution).
4. **Two models to keep in sync.** Domain and DTO drift is a real maintenance cost; mitigated by making the DTO a thin projection and testing the mapping.
5. **Performance**: an extra copy per boundary. Irrelevant except in the hottest paths.

**The dependency-rule violations to look for in a review:**

| Violation | Symptom |
| --- | --- |
| Domain imports a framework | `import express from 'express'` in `src/domain` |
| Domain does I/O | `fs.readFile` or `fetch` inside an entity method |
| Domain controls its own persistence | `order.save()` on the entity |
| Port has technology in its name | `SqlOrderRepository` as the *port*; `IHttpPaymentClient` |
| Port leaks the ORM | `findById` returning a `TypeORM` entity |
| Adapter leaks outward-inward | a controller importing a repository to do a join |
| Framework types in signatures | `Request`/`Response` in a use case |
| Transactions inside a domain entity | `@Transactional` on a domain method |
| Everything is a port with one implementation | `ILogger`, `IUuid`, `IClockFactory` for pure ceremony |

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you test a use case?"* — construct it with in-memory/fake adapters; no framework, no DB, no HTTP. Show a `FakePaymentGateway` that always declines.
- *"Where do you put validation?"* — at the boundary (parse into a typed command), and in the domain for invariants that matter regardless of caller (never trust a caller, even another service).
- *"What is a 'leaky' abstraction and how did you see it?"* — give the concrete example: a repository interface exposing a query builder.
- *"Does every entity need a repository interface?"* — no. Only where volatility or faking justifies it. Say the truth about your own code here.
- *"How do you handle a transaction that spans two aggregates?"* — it is still one local transaction in the application layer; you just do not pretend the domain manages it.

---

### 11.2.8 Domain-Driven Design basics: entities, value objects, aggregates, bounded contexts, domain events

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** DDD (Evans, 2003) is a modelling approach: put a domain model at the centre of the software and align the model with the business language. It is *not* a code style — you can practise DDD without any of the architecture in 11.2.7, and you can practise 11.2.7 with an anemic model (11.2.9).

**The five building blocks.**

**1. Entity.** Has **identity**, mutable state, and a lifecycle. Identity persists across all changes; two entities with identical attributes but different ids are different entities.

```ts
class Customer {
  readonly id: CustomerId;                    // identity: stable, compared by value of the id
  private email: Email; private status: CustomerStatus;
  constructor(id: CustomerId, email: Email) { this.id = id; this.email = email; this.status = 'active'; }
  changeEmail(e: Email) { this.email = e; }    // identity unchanged
  deactivate() { this.status = 'inactive'; }
  equals(other: Customer) { return this.id.equals(other.id); }  // never compare attributes
  toString() { return `Customer(${this.id})`; }   // debug output that does not leak PII
}
// Entities are mutable (unavoidable for lifecycle) but you control mutation through behaviour methods.
```

**2. Value Object.** No identity, immutable, compared by attribute value. **When in doubt, make it a value object** — you cannot regret a value object; you can regret an entity that should have been one.

```ts
class Money {
  private constructor(readonly minor: number, readonly currency: Currency) {}  // immutable, no id
  add(o: Money): Money { /* … */ return new Money(…) }
  equals(o: Money) { return this.minor === o.minor && this.currency === o.currency; }  // by value
}
// Consequences you get for free: safe to share, safe to use as a Map/Set key, safe to pass across
// layers, trivially testable, cacheable, and impossible to mutate by accident.
// Typical value objects: Money, EmailAddress, DateRange, ShippingAddress, Quantity, OrderId.
// A "data clump with rules" is almost always a value object waiting to happen.
```

**The rule of thumb that gets asked constantly: entity or value object?** Ask three questions: (a) does it have identity that survives change? (b) do you compare it by *what it is* or by *which one it is*? (c) can two instances with the same attributes be distinct? If (c) is "no", it is a value object.

**3. Aggregate.** A consistency boundary: a set of objects that must be consistent, with exactly one **aggregate root** as the only entry point. Everything else in the aggregate is reachable from the root and modified only through it.

```ts
// Order is the aggregate root. OrderLine and ShippingInstruction are inside it.
// Access rules: you can load a whole aggregate; you can only *save* the root.
// Only one repository per aggregate. References to other aggregates are by ID, never by object.
class Order {
  private lines: OrderLine[] = [];
  private shipping?: ShippingInstruction;
  static place(id: OrderId, customerId: CustomerId, now: Date): Order { return new Order(id, customerId, now); }
  addLine(sku: Sku, qty: Quantity, unitPrice: Money): void {
    if (this.status !== 'draft') throw new NotDraft(this.status);              // invariant
    if (this.lines.length >= 100) throw new TooManyLines();
    this.lines.push(OrderLine.of(sku, qty, unitPrice));
    this.version++;                                                            // optimistic concurrency
  }
}
// Smallest possible consistency boundary. The temptation to make Order+Customer+Payment one aggregate
// is the #1 DDD modelling error: it creates a huge write lock and a repository that loads half the DB.
```

**Aggregate design rules to state:** (a) one root, one repository, one transaction; (b) references across aggregates are by ID; (c) keep the aggregate small — a rule of thumb is that it should be loadable and savable in a couple of round trips; (d) an aggregate that is constantly "too big" is usually two aggregates that got merged because of a false "must be consistent" belief; (e) if two users edit different parts of the aggregate concurrently, the aggregate is too big and you want eventual consistency with a compensating action.

**4. Bounded Context.** A boundary within which a model has a specific meaning. The same word means different things in different contexts: "Customer" in Sales means a buyer with a credit limit; in Support it means a contact with tickets; in Billing it means a payer account. **A model is only valid inside its context.**

```ts
// The classic: the same concept, two representations, translated at the boundary.
// Support context's "Customer" has: id, displayName, preferredLanguage, openTicketCount.
// Billing context's "CustomerAccount" has: id, legalName, paymentMethods, creditLimit, dunningStage.
// They are different models. You do NOT share the class. You translate with an anti-corruption layer.
class SupportCustomer { constructor(readonly id: string, readonly displayName: string) {} }
class BillingAccount   { constructor(readonly id: string, readonly legalName: string) {} }

// The Anti-Corruption Layer: keeps the foreign model from leaking into yours.
class BillingAccountAcl {
  constructor(private readonly billingApi: BillingApi) {}
  toLocalModel(c: SupportCustomer): BillingAccountView {
    const remote = this.billingApi.getAccount(c.id);            // foreign model, quarantined here
    return { id: c.id, localDisplayName: c.displayName, dunningStage: remote.dunningStage };
  }
}
// If the legacy system (the "other" bounded context) is ugly and you cannot change it, the ACL
// is the layer that absorbs its ugliness so your model stays clean. This is one of DDD's most
// practically valuable patterns and it is a great interview answer.
```

Sub-contexts: **Core** (competitive advantage, invest here), **Supporting** (necessary but not differentiating), **Generic** (buy/commodity: auth, billing providers, notifications). Use this triage to decide where to spend modelling effort.

**5. Domain Event.** A statement of fact about the domain that already happened, in the past tense, with no intent ("do X"). The domain publishes it; the application layer reacts.

```ts
class OrderPlaced { constructor(readonly orderId: OrderId, readonly customerId: CustomerId,
                                 readonly total: Money, readonly occurredAt: Date) {} }
// Naming rules that make events good:
//  - past tense, specific: OrderPlaced, PaymentCaptured, CustomerUpgraded, ShipmentDispatched.
//  - one fact per event. "OrderProcessedAndShippedAndEmailed" is not an event, it is a workflow summary.
//  - carry the minimum useful payload plus IDs. Include a `version` and `occurredAt` for every consumer.
//  - NEVER "OrderShouldBeShipped" (that is a command: intent, imperative, one handler).
// The distinction to say out loud: an event is a fact no one can argue with; a command is a request
// that may be refused. Domain events are facts. Application services issue commands.
```

**Strategic design (the rest of DDD, worth naming).** **Context Map** (how contexts relate: customer/supplier, conformist, anti-corruption layer, published language, open-host service, shared kernel). **Ubiquitous Language** (one vocabulary, enforced in code names and in conversation; the most valuable and most-skipped practice). **Anti-Corruption Layer** (above). **Shared Kernel** (a small explicitly-shared model — keep it small, it couples everything). **Application Service** (the use-case orchestrator; thin, no domain rules). **Factory** (domain factories for creation with invariants). **Specification** (a composable business predicate: `isEligibleForRefund(o, now)`, composable with `and`/`or`/`not`).

```ts
// Specification: business rules as composable, named, testable predicates.
interface Spec<T> { isSatisfiedBy(t: T): boolean; and(o: Spec<T>): Spec<T>; or(o: Spec<T>): Spec<T>; not(): Spec<T>; }
const refundable = (r: RefundPolicy): Spec<Order> => ({
  isSatisfiedBy: (o) => o.status === 'delivered'
      && daysSince(o.deliveredAt) <= r.windowDays
      && o.total.minorUnits <= r.maxMinorUnits
      && !o.lines.some(l => l.isFinalSale),
  and: (o) => ({ ...this, isSatisfiedBy: (t) => this.isSatisfiedBy(t) && o.isSatisfiedBy(t) }) as Spec<Order>,
  // … or simply write the combinators once:
  or: (o) => and(this, o), not: () => ({ isSatisfiedBy: (t) => !this.isSatisfiedBy(t) }) as Spec<Order>,
});
if (refundable(policy).and(notFraudulent(o)).isSatisfiedBy(order)) approve(order);
```

**When to use DDD vs not.**

- **Use** when the domain is genuinely complex, the business language is contested, and you are the system of record. Payments, insurance, logistics, healthcare, marketplaces.
- **Use** when the business has experts who will disagree with your model if it is wrong — DDD's value is *conversations* as much as code.
- **Don't** use as ceremony for a CRUD admin panel. You will produce a beautiful model of nothing and pay the mapping tax for no benefit.
- **Don't** use bounded contexts as a licence for a distributed monolith: contexts are *modelling* boundaries; whether they are also *deployment* boundaries is a separate, infrastructure-driven decision (see 11.3.2).

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* a model that matches the business (so changes are cheap and discussions are efficient), invariants enforced in one place, aggregates that give you a real transaction boundary, events that decouple reactions, and explicit contexts that stop models being merged into a lowest-common-denominator mush.
*Cost:* **significant modelling effort up front and continuous maintenance of the model**; more ceremony; the risk of over-modelling; harder onboarding; and the fact that "the model is wrong" is discovered late.

**Production considerations.**

- **Version your domain model separately from the schema.** Migrations should be additive and backwards compatible for one release, so old and new code can run simultaneously during a deploy.
- **Events are contracts.** Same rules as 11.1.17: additive changes, versioned, tolerant readers, and an owner.
- **Aggregate size is a performance decision.** A large aggregate means a large write and a big lock. Watch row counts, and watch `UPDATE … WHERE version = ?` conflict rates.
- **Domain events in-process vs on a bus:** in-process is fine when the reaction is part of the same logical operation and cannot fail independently; a bus when the reaction is genuinely separate (and then you need the outbox).
- **Do not put the domain model in the DB** as jsonb blobs and call it DDD — you lose the constraints, the indexes, and the queries.
- **Refactoring the model is a breaking change to every consumer.** Plan model migrations as data migrations, with dual-read/write if needed.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Value objects with ids (now they are entities and you have lost immutability).
2. Entities with no behaviour (see 11.2.9).
3. Aggregates that span the whole business object graph.
4. Holding references to other aggregates rather than ids → loading cascades, accidental cycles.
5. Domain events named as commands, so nobody knows whether they may be refused.
6. Ubiquitous language abandoned — `Order` in the code means something different from `Order` in the business.
7. Modelling "the database" instead of "the business", because the DB was faster to design.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Entity or value object for a shipping address?"* — value object if two identical addresses are interchangeable; entity if each address has its own id and history (e.g. a saved address book entry).
- *"How do you decide aggregate boundaries?"* — by the invariant that must be enforced atomically, then by concurrency conflicts; start small.
- *"What's the most valuable DDD practice for a small team?"* — the ubiquitous language conversation and the anti-corruption layer at the legacy boundary.
- *"How does DDD relate to microservices?"* — bounded contexts *can* map to services, but the mapping is not mandatory, and forcing it is how you get a distributed monolith.

---

### 11.2.9 Anemic domain models and when they are fine

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** An anemic domain model consists of data-carrying classes with getters/setters and all behaviour in a separate service layer. It is the default outcome of naive layering, and it is the most common criticism of "clean architecture".

```ts
// THE ANEMIC VERSION — behaviourless entities, fat services, invariants scattered in services.
class Order {
  id!: string; customerId!: string; lines!: Line[]; status!: string; total!: number;
  shippingAddress?: Address;
}
class OrderService {
  async placeOrder(dto: PlaceOrderDto): Promise<string> {
    const order = new Order();
    order.id = uuid();
    order.status = 'draft';
    if (dto.lines.length === 0) throw new Error('empty');          // invariant, in a service
    order.lines = dto.lines.map(l => ({ ...l, total: l.qty * l.unitPrice }));  // logic, in a service
    order.total = order.lines.reduce((s, l) => s + l.total, 0);
    if (!dto.shippingAddress) throw new Error('no address');        // another invariant, same service
    await this.repo.save(order);
    return order.id;
  }
  async cancelOrder(id: string) {
    const order = await this.repo.find(id);
    if (order.status === 'shipped') throw new Error('cannot');     // the SAME invariant, a third time
    order.status = 'cancelled';
    await this.repo.save(order);
  }
  async applyDiscount(id: string, pct: number) {
    const order = await this.repo.find(id);
    order.total = order.total * (1 - pct);                         // no check that it's draft
  }
}
// Problems, concretely: (1) an Order with status='cancelled' and total>0 is representable,
// (2) the "cannot cancel a shipped order" rule is written 3+ times and will drift,
// (3) no method can be called without loading the whole thing, (4) `order.total = …` from anywhere.
```

**The four costs of anemic models, stated precisely.**

1. **Invalid states are representable.** Any combination of fields is assignable. The compiler will not stop you, so the check happens in some service you forgot about.
2. **Invariant duplication and drift.** The same rule appears in every service that touches the entity; one of them will be out of date.
3. **"Transaction script" sprawl.** Services become scripts that do many things each, and each service can be entered from a different starting state.
4. **The model cannot be reused.** The next use case (a CLI, a scheduled job, a webhook) re-implements the rules differently, and now the rules exist four times.

**But here is the honest counter-argument, which the interview is testing.**

**An anemic model is the right choice when:**

1. **The entity is mostly data with almost no invariants.** A `UserProfile`, an `AuditLogEntry`, a `Product` in a catalogue: behaviour is CRUD and any "logic" is a projection. A rich model here is ceremony.
2. **The logic is fundamentally about coordination**, not about a single entity's invariants: an ETL job, an import/export pipeline, an API gateway, an orchestrator.
3. **The domain is CRUD and the interesting complexity is in the UI or the queries.** Content management, an admin panel, a simple catalogue.
4. **The data is shared and write-heavy from many angles**, and the "rules" are actually access rules → then your rules belong in the schema (constraints, permissions) and a service, not in a domain class.
5. **You are serving a machine consumer**, not a human. Anemic DTOs + a validation library is exactly right.
6. **The framework owns the object.** In a JPA/Hibernate/Android context, Active Record plus services is the path of least resistance and the framework is not going to let you have a rich domain anyway.
7. **Time pressure / a small team.** An anemic model that ships is worth more than a rich model that gets stuck in modelling. This is a legitimate, defensible engineering trade.

**The diagnostic question that decides it.** *"Is there a rule about this entity that must hold no matter which code path modifies it?"* If yes → behaviour belongs on the entity. If all the rules are "which operations are allowed in this use case" → they belong in the use case (application service), and the entity can be data.

**The middle path, which is what good teams actually do.** Model *richly where the complexity is* and *anemically where it is not*. It is not a codebase-wide decision.

```ts
// Anemic: a projection DTO for an admin list. Correct and simple.
interface AdminUserView { id: string; email: string; createdAt: string; orderCount: number }

// Rich: the Order aggregate where the invariants genuinely matter and multiple code paths touch it.
class Order { /* as in 11.2.8 */ }

// An application service that orchestrates and holds *use-case* policy (not entity invariants):
class CheckoutService {
  constructor(private readonly orders: OrderRepository, private readonly payments: PaymentGateway) {}
  async checkout(cmd: CheckoutCommand) {
    // "A first-time buyer with a guest cart must be emailed the guest-checkout link" is a USE-CASE
    // rule, not an entity rule. Belongs here. Correct use of an application service.
    const order = Order.place(/* … */);
    if (order.total.minorUnits > LIMIT && !ctx.customer.hasPaymentMethod)
      throw new PaymentMethodRequired(order.total);
    // … entity invariants (non-empty, valid quantities) live inside Order.place, NOT here.
  }
}
```

**How to fix an anemic model incrementally (this is the most useful part of the answer).** Do not rewrite. Pick the entity with the most scattered rules, write a characterisation test of the rules, then:

1. Move each rule into a behaviour method on the entity (`order.applyDiscount(pct)` that checks `status === 'draft'`).
2. Delete the service code that duplicated the rule.
3. Make fields private with getters (or readonly) so the service *must* go through the method.
4. Introduce a value object where primitives were doing too much.
5. Repeat for the next entity.

Each step is independently shippable and independently revertible. You will end up with a model that is rich where it matters.
