**The Fowler nuance worth knowing.** "Anemic Domain Model" is a blog post by Martin Fowler (2003) and the criticism it draws is that people hear "rich model" and think every entity needs 20 behaviour methods. Fowler's own view: anemic is a legitimate choice for simple models, and the real problem is applying a heavyweight model to simple domains.

**Trade-offs.** *Rich models:* more code, higher initial cost, behaviour discoverable on the entity, invariants enforced once, reuse across entry points. *Anemic:* fast to write, trivially serialisable, ORM-friendly, trivially flat to reason about, but invariants are duplicated and can be violated by a new call path.

**Production considerations.**

- Anemic models are **easier to cache and to send over the wire** — no behaviour to worry about, no identity to preserve.
- Anemic models are **more dangerous in production** precisely because a new code path can set fields in an invalid combination, and the only guard is a service you may not have read.
- If you go rich, you need the discipline that **all** mutations go through the aggregate root. One public setter and the model is anemic *and* complicated.
- Watch for "rich" models that also contain I/O. Behaviour that talks to the network is not domain behaviour.

**Common mistakes.**

1. "Fixing" an anemic model for a read-only projection view.
2. Moving logic into entities *and* keeping it in services (now it exists twice).
3. Rich models that are `save()`-able, which makes them Active Records and untestable.
4. Believing the anemic/rich debate matters more than whether the invariants are enforced *somewhere reliable*.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Is your model anemic? Should you fix it?"* — the strong answer identifies where each choice is right and shows the incremental path; do not defend a universal position.
- *"Where does 'the customer must be logged in to check out' belong?"* — application service / boundary authorisation, not the entity; it is a use-case rule, not an invariant of an order.
- *"What's a rule that's an invariant vs a rule that's a use-case policy?"* — invariant: must hold for every code path that touches the object (order total equals the sum of its lines). Use-case policy: applies only in this scenario (guest checkout requires email first).

---

### 11.2.10 Working with a legacy codebase: seams, characterisation tests, strangler fig

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A legacy codebase is one without tests and without automated deployment safety, whose behaviour you depend on but do not fully understand. The strategy is to **never rewrite**, and instead add safety (tests), **structure** (seams), and **replace incrementally** (Strangler Fig).

**The four-step playbook.**

**Step 1 — Understand before touching.** Build a picture with tools, not memory:
- Map the entry points: HTTP routes, CLI commands, queue consumers, cron jobs, scheduled tasks.
- Map the data: every table, who writes it, who reads it, and the *implied* invariants (the ones in the code, not the ones in the schema).
- Map the side effects: emails, webhooks, third-party calls, file writes, prints.
- Identify the **hot spot**: the module that changes most often. That is where you should invest; the rest can stay ugly.
- Write it down. A one-page architecture note with a dependency diagram beats an hour of `grep`.

```bash
# The practical toolkit (any language; these are language-agnostic patterns)
#  - route inventory:  grep for route registrations / controller annotations
#  - fan-in analysis:   who imports/requires this module? (highest fan-in = highest risk to change)
#  - churn analysis:     git log --since="6 months" --format= --name-only | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -30
#  - test coverage of the hot spot: if it is 0%, that is your first project
#  - secret scanning / dynamic tracing: what actually runs in production?
```

**Step 2 — Create seams.** A **seam** is a place where you can alter behaviour without editing in that place — the definition from Michael Feathers' *Working Effectively with Legacy Code*. Every seam is a test seam or a substitution seam.

```ts
// The three ways to introduce a seam, in order of preference:

// (1) PARAMETERISE: pass the thing in. The best seam; always try this first.
const now = () => new Date();
function isExpired(session: Session, now: () => Date): boolean {
  return now() > session.expiresAt;
}
// Before: `new Date()` inside, untestable. After: inject the clock, testable.

// (2) WRAP: extract the call behind a function of your own, so you have a place to intercept.
class OrderService {
  private chargeGateway: PaymentGateway;
  async place(cmd: PlaceOrder) {
    // Before: await stripe.charges.create(...)  ← third-party SDK, no seam
    // After:
    return this.chargeGateway.charge(this.toCharge(cmd));   // you own this line; you can stub it
  }
}
// Wrapping one call is a seam. You can then stub the gateway in tests and delegate to the real
// one in production. This is the Adapter pattern from 11.1.5 doing a testing job.

// (3) BREAK UP: extract an overridable method, or introduce an interface + implementation.
// Use only if (1) and (2) cannot work (e.g. the call is 200 lines inline in a loop).
class ShippingCalculator {
  calculate(order: Order): Money { return this.baseRate(order).plus(this.remoteSurcharge(order)); }
  protected baseRate(o: Order): Money { /* 40 lines */ }
  protected remoteSurcharge(o: Order): Money { return Money.zero(o.currency); }
}
// Note: (3) is the only one that touches the legacy code's shape; prefer (1) and (2).
```

**Step 3 — Characterisation tests.** Michael Feathers' definition: a test that captures the **current** behaviour, including the bugs, so you can refactor safely. It is *not* a test of what the code should do.

```ts
// The methodology:
//  1. Pick a unit with observable behaviour (a pure-ish function, a command, an HTTP endpoint).
//  2. Write a test that captures the output for representative inputs — INCLUDING the surprising ones.
//  3. Do NOT fix the bug while writing the test. Mark it:
//     it('rounds 3 items to whole currency (looks like a bug, changing it is a separate PR)', …)
//  4. Get the suite green against the CURRENT code. That is your safety net.
//
// Example, characterising a pricing function you must not change yet:
describe('legacy pricing (characterisation — DO NOT FIX HERE, see PR #4412)', () => {
  it('gives VIPs 10% off but rounds down to the whole unit', () => {
    expect(legacyPrice({ segment: 'vip', cents: 1055 })).toEqual(1050);   // captures the rounding quirk
  });
  it('returns 0 for an empty cart instead of throwing (JIRA-3310)', () => {
    expect(legacyPrice({ segment: 'vip', cents: 0 })).toEqual(0);
  });
  it('charges full price for the segment "vip " (trailing space) — known bug', () => {
    expect(legacyPrice({ segment: 'vip ', cents: 1000 })).toEqual(1000);
  });
});
// Then: PR 1 changes behaviour deliberately, updating the test with a comment explaining why.
// That separation ("capture" PR then "change" PR) is the whole discipline.
```

**Where to put characterisation tests: the golden-master technique.** For a module that is hard to unit test (a report generator, a complex calculation), feed a large corpus of production-shaped inputs through the current implementation, record the outputs, and assert the new implementation matches. This is what you do to a parser, a scheduler, or a tax calculator.

```ts
// Golden master: capture now, compare later.
const CORPUS = loadFixtures('pricing-cases/*.json');       // 400 cases, incl. nasty ones
const golden = loadGolden('pricing-cases/golden.json');    // captured from the legacy impl
it('matches the legacy implementation on every case', () => {
  const mismatches = CORPUS.filter(c => newImpl(c) !== golden[hash(c)]);
  expect(mismatches).toEqual([]);   // empty diff = safe to cut over
});
// Golden-master caveats: the golden is only as good as the corpus (include edge cases!), and
// an empty diff on a poor corpus proves nothing. Build the corpus from PRODUCTION inputs.
```

**Step 4 — Strangler Fig.** Replace the system incrementally by putting a new implementation in front of the old one and migrating traffic/functionality over, until the old one is dead.

```ts
// (a) The routing seam: a facade/proxy in front of both.
class CheckoutFacade {
  constructor(private readonly legacy: LegacyCheckout, private readonly modern: ModernCheckout, private readonly flag: FeatureFlag) {}
  async checkout(req: Req): Promise<Res> {
    // 1% → legacy, 99% → modern. Compare their responses in shadow mode first.
    return this.flag.enabledFor(req.userId) ? this.modern.checkout(req) : this.legacy.checkout(req);
  }
}

// (b) Shadow/dual-run: run both, compare, report differences, do not use the result.
async function shadowCompare(req: Req) {
  const [legacyRes, modernRes] = await Promise.allSettled([legacy.checkout(req), modern.checkout(req)]);
  compare(legacyRes, modernRes);   // log mismatches; do NOT fail the request
}
// Shadow-mode rules: it must be side-effect free (use a sandbox for writes!), it must not be
// load-bearing (the modern path must not slow the legacy path), and mismatches must be actionable.

// (c) Per-capability strangling: migrate one endpoint at a time, not one layer at a time.
// (d) Data strangling: dual-write (old+new), backfill, verify, switch reads, stop old writes.
async function backfill() {
  for (const chunk of chunkById(orders, 500)) {
    await modernRepo.upsertAll(chunk.map(mapToModern));
    // verify before proceeding — do not blindly assume the backfill is correct
    const diff = await verifyCounts(chunk);
    if (diff !== 0) throw new BackfillMismatch(diff);
  }
}
```

**Strangler Fig rules that are learned the hard way.**
1. **Never big-bang.** Rewrite projects that try to replace everything at once have a very high failure rate and always miss the requirements that were undocumented.
2. **The new system must be a superset of the old one's observed behaviour**, including the bugs (until you deliberately fix them).
3. **Migrate by capability, not by technical layer.** Do not "rewrite the database first, then the API" — migrate the checkout flow, then the reporting flow.
4. **Data duplication is unavoidable and fine, temporarily.** Dual-write with a reconciliation job; pick a source of truth per field and write it down.
5. **The rollback must be one config change.** If rolling back requires a deploy, you do not have a rollback, you have a hope.
6. **Keep the legacy system runnable** until the new one has proved itself in production for a full business cycle (a month-end, a peak, a tax run).
7. **Budget the strangling explicitly.** A strangler with no end date becomes two systems forever. Set a kill criterion.

**The Legacy Code specific rules (Feathers' four "tricks" are worth naming).**

1. **Get the code into a test harness before changing it.** "Scratch that, I'm checking the tests" is the core discipline.
2. **Use the seams.** Never modify a method you cannot test; introduce a seam first.
3. **No tests, no refactoring, no new features in that method** — this is a hard rule and the most useful one.
4. **Learn the domain from the code** when the documentation is wrong; and go and *talk to the person who wrote it* if they are still available — the "pre-existing conditions" they describe are your requirements list.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Strangler Fig:** when the system is still changing (a live product) and you must ship value continuously.
- **Strangler Fig:** when the risk of a rewrite is unacceptable (payments, healthcare, anything with data you cannot regenerate).
- **Not:** when the system is dead (no traffic, no data you need) — delete it.
- **Not** when the system is small and you understand it completely — refactor in place.
- **Not** for a total rewrite when the business is stable and the old system is not changing — a rewrite with no new requirements is pure risk.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* continuous delivery of value, low blast radius, learnability, no downtime, the legacy system stays revenue-generating the whole time.
*Cost:* you run two systems for a long time; temporary data duplication; two teams' worth of operational complexity; and the real risk of *never finishing* — which must be managed with explicit milestones and a kill date.

**Production considerations.**

- **Characterisation tests must be deterministic.** Stub `Date.now`, `Math.random`, UUIDs, and the clock. A flaky characterisation test is worse than none because it destroys trust in the safety net.
- **Golden-master corpora come from production data — anonymised.** And they must include the weird real cases (huge carts, unicode addresses, DST boundaries, leap days, 32-bit boundaries, empty lists).
- **Feature flags become permanent.** Every flag needs an owner and an expiry date, or you will have 40 flags and no idea which combinations are live in production.
- **Observability before cutover:** compare metrics (latency, error rate, business outcomes) between legacy and modern for a full cycle.
- **Do not shadow-run writes to real third-party systems.** Use sandbox accounts or a "replay" mode.
- **Deprecation, not deletion:** keep the legacy path callable for a while; you will need it when a rare case appears in month 7.

**Common mistakes.**

1. Refactoring before the characterisation test exists.
2. Fixing the bug *inside* the refactoring commit — now you cannot tell whether the behaviour change was intended.
3. Strangling the technical layers instead of the business capabilities.
4. Assuming documentation is correct — legacy docs are usually aspirational.
5. No rollback plan.
6. Big-bang data migration on the weekend. (There is a famous, very well-known project of this kind. Do not plan another.)
7. Leaving the strangler with no end date.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you start on a codebase with zero tests?"* — identify the hot spot, add one characterisation test for the riskiest behaviour, then create a seam, then refactor that one method.
- *"When would you recommend a rewrite?"* — when the system is genuinely understood and small, or when it is dead and can be replaced by buying; almost never for a live, changing, revenue-producing system.
- *"What is a seam and how do you create one without a big refactor?"* — parameterise a global, or wrap the third-party call in one of your own functions.
- *"How do you know the strangler is finished?"* — a defined set of capabilities migrated, the legacy deploy deleted, and a kill date honoured.

---

## 11.3 Architectural Styles

### 11.3.1 Monolith: the default that is usually correct

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A single deployable unit containing all business logic, with internal layering (or none). "Monolith" describes the *deployment*, not the *code*: a well-structured monolith with hexagonal boundaries can be cleaner and more maintainable than a suite of microservices, and a tangled monolith is unmaintainable either way.

**The core claim to defend in an interview: the monolith is the default, and microservices are a cost you pay deliberately.** The overwhelming majority of successful systems are (or start as) monoliths. The failure rate of early microservices adoption is high — teams pay the distributed-systems tax for scaling they do not need.

**Why it is usually right (four reasons, each of which microservices does not fix).**

1. **Transactional integrity.** Inside one process, one database transaction spans everything. Across services you get sagas, eventual consistency, and compensation — all strictly more expensive and strictly weaker.
2. **Refactoring across the whole system.** Change a field, and in a monolith you change it and the compiler finds every usage. Across services you change a schema and orchestrate N deploys with backwards compatibility for weeks.
3. **Debugging.** One process, one stack trace, one debugger, one log stream. Across services you need distributed tracing and you will still be guessing half the time.
4. **Local development and deployment.** One clone, one command, one deploy. The cost of microservices here is not just time; it is *feedback loop length*, which compounds with everything else.

**What you actually gain by going to microservices.** Be honest and specific, because this is the whole justification:

- **Independent deployment** of components that change at different rates (a recommendation engine weekly vs. core billing monthly).
- **Independent scaling** of components with different load profiles (search receiving 90% of traffic; batch jobs needing CPU not IOPS).
- **Technology heterogeneity** where genuinely justified (a ML inference service in Python).
- **Fault isolation and blast-radius control** for genuinely risky work.
- **Team autonomy** and ownership alignment at scale (Conway's law in reverse).
- **Failure semantics you control** — you can choose what is unavailable.

Notice: every one of these is either *scale*, *team*, or *risk*. None of them is about code quality, and none of them applies until you have a scale or team problem. That is the key argument.

**What it costs (be able to state this list fluently — it is what shows you have been in production).**

| Cost | What it means in practice |
| --- | --- |
| **Network is the new RPC boundary** | Latency, partial failure, timeouts, retries, idempotency — every call site |
| **No shared transactions** | Sagas, eventual consistency, compensation, reconciliation jobs |
| **Distributed debugging** | Distributed tracing as a hard requirement; log correlation mandatory |
| **Deployment orchestration** | N pipelines, compatibility windows, coordinated releases for cross-service changes |
| **Local development** | Docker Compose with 12 services, seed data, emulators, or stubs that drift |
| **Data duplication** | Each service owns its data; a "join" becomes an API call or a duplicated read model |
| **Operational surface** | Service discovery, load balancing, config, secrets, mesh, dashboards, runbooks per service |
| **Testing pyramid inverts** | Many integration tests; contract tests become mandatory; end-to-end suites get slow and flaky |
| **Two-phase commit temptation** | Or, worse, an invented distributed transaction |
| **Observability cost** | You cannot correlate a single user action across 6 services without investment |

**Anatomy of a good monolith.** Not "one big ball of mud" — a monolith with internal structure:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  HTTP / CLI adapters        (routing, auth, serialisation)       │
│  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
│  │  Application layer      (use cases, transactions, DTOs)   │  │
│  │  ┌────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┬───────────┐  │  │
│  │  │ Ordering   │ Billing      │ Catalogue    │ Identity  │  │  │  ← modules
│  │  │ (module)   │ (module)     │ (module)     │ (module)  │  │  │    (enforced
│  │  └────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┴───────────┘  │  │     boundaries)
│  │  shared kernel: Money, Clock, Id, Result, DomainError        │  │
│  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
│  Persistence adapters      (SQL, HTTP clients, queue)          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```
Rules that make a monolith good: (a) modules with explicit public surfaces (only an `index.ts`/facade per module is importable); (b) a shared kernel that is small and boring; (c) each module owning its tables; (d) dependencies between modules allowed only through published interfaces; (e) no cross-module table reads.

**When to keep the monolith (most of the time).**

- Fewer than ~20–30 engineers. Below that, microservices cost more than they save.
- A product whose requirements are still moving. Microservices lock in your (wrong) current model.
- Low to moderate traffic with no component-specific bottleneck.
- Strong transactional requirements.
- Limited operational maturity or platform support.

**When to split (real triggers).**

- A specific component must scale/deploy independently and you can *name* it and *measure* the problem.
- Separate teams with independent roadmaps.
- A hard requirement: data isolation, regulatory boundary, or untrusted-code sandboxing.
- A component with a genuinely different technology/runtime need.

**Trade-offs, summarised as the sentence to say out loud:** *"The monolith optimises for changeability and correctness; microservices optimise for independent scaling and team autonomy, at the cost of correctness guarantees and operational complexity."*

**Production considerations.**

- **Modular monolith first, always.** You can extract a service from a well-modularised monolith relatively cleanly. You cannot merge microservices back cheaply. Get the boundaries right before you get the processes right.
- **One database for the monolith** is a feature, not a limitation, for a long time. Split it at the same time you split the code, not before.
- **Deployment frequency is not architecture.** You can deploy a monolith 50 times a day. That argument is often used incorrectly.
- **Anti-pattern: "we'll do microservices for scalability."** Scale the monolith vertically and cache; most read-heavy bottlenecks are solved with a read replica and a CDN.
- **Anti-pattern: "we'll do microservices so we can use different languages."** Almost never worth it.
- **Anti-pattern: "we need microservices for CI/CD."** Improve your pipeline instead.
- **Watch the God-object centre of a monolith.** It forms around the database schema and is the thing that later prevents extraction.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Using "monolith" as an insult, which makes it a rhetorical position rather than a technical one.
2. Refusing to split at all even when a real, measured trigger appears (then you build a custom platform instead — usually worse).
3. Splitting along technical lines (a "database service", a "validation service") instead of business capabilities.
4. Treating "monolith" as "no structure". Unstructured monoliths cannot be extracted from.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Monolith or microservices?"* — do not answer "it depends" and stop; answer with the specific trigger you would look for and how you would measure it.
- *"How do you know a monolith has grown too large?"* — build time, test time, deployment risk, merge conflicts across teams, and "how long does it take one person to understand the request flow". These are measurable; use them.
- *"What can you do in a monolith that you cannot do across services?"* — one transaction across the whole change, one deploy, one debugging session, one place to add a cross-cutting concern.
- *"How do you extract a service from a monolith?"* — Strangler Fig (11.2.10): seam the calls, dual-run, migrate data, cut traffic over, delete the old path.

---

### 11.3.2 Modular monolith: how to get the boundaries without the distribution cost

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A single deployable with **enforced internal module boundaries**: each module owns its data and exposes an explicit public interface; cross-module communication goes through published contracts only. You get microservices' *organisational* benefits without the network.

**Why this is the correct answer in 2026 for most teams.** If you will never be able to justify microservices, you should still get the boundary discipline. And if you later *do* extract a service, a modular monolith is the cheapest possible starting point.

**The four enforcement mechanisms, in increasing order of strength.**

```ts
// 1. CONVENTION ONLY — weakest. A README that says "don't import across modules".
//    Works for 5 people, fails at 25. Do not rely on this.

// 2. FILE/FOLDER STRUCTURE + public surface — the practical baseline.
//    Only a barrel file per module is importable; everything else is internal.
src/
  billing/
    index.ts          // the ONLY importable path: export { chargeInvoice } from './internal/api'
    internal/
      api.ts          // exported functions, typed, documented
      invoice.ts      // internal: not importable by anyone else
      repo.ts
  ordering/
    index.ts
    internal/…

// 3. ARCHITECTURE TESTS (the enforcement that actually works, and it is only ~30 lines).
//    Run them in CI. This is the highest-leverage thing you can add to a monolith.
import { globSync } from 'node:fs';
const modules = ['billing', 'ordering', 'catalogue', 'identity'];
const violations: string[] = [];
for (const file of globSync('src/**/*.ts')) {
  const from = modules.find(m => file.startsWith(`src/${m}/`));
  const imports = [...file.matchAll(/from\s+['"]([^'"]+)['"]/g)].map(m => m[1]!).filter(i => i.startsWith('@app/') || i.startsWith('.'));
  for (const imp of imports) {
    const to = modules.find(m => imp.includes(`/modules/${m}/`) || imp.includes(`/${m}/`));
    if (to && to !== from && !ALLOWED.includes(`${from}->${to}`)) violations.push(`${file} imports ${imp}`);
  }
}
it('no module reaches into another module except through its public surface', () => {
  expect(violations).toEqual([]);
});
// 4. SEPARATE PACKAGES/BUILD UNITS — compiles to independent artifacts; true build-time enforcement.
//    Cost: dependency management (workspace tooling, version pinning), slower builds, more ceremony.
```

**Data ownership per module.** This is where modularity actually lives, and where people cheat.

```sql
-- WRONG: modules read each other's tables. The schema becomes a shared API and nothing is modular.
SELECT o.*, c.name FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id;   -- cross-module table read

-- RIGHT: each module owns its schema; cross-module data comes through an interface or an event.
-- Ordering owns `orders`; Identity owns `customers`. Identity publishes CustomerRegistered.
-- Ordering keeps a local `customer_ref` projection (id + the few fields it needs).
CREATE TABLE customer_ref (customer_id uuid PRIMARY KEY, display_name text NOT NULL, synced_at timestamptz);
-- Projections are how you get read-side data in a modular monolith without a distributed join.
```

**Cross-module communication patterns, in order of preference:**

1. **Synchronous call through a published interface** (an ordinary function call to another module's `index.ts`). Same transaction, same process, still a boundary.
2. **In-process event/domain event** (a module-local emitter, or an event bus that happens to be in-process). Good for "this module does not need to know who reacts".
3. **Local projection** (module A's data cached in module B, updated by an event). The standard way to avoid a join across modules.
4. **Shared read model** (a read-only database or a dedicated projection module). Only when the query genuinely spans modules.

**The shared kernel: what belongs and what does not.**

```ts
// ALLOWED in the shared kernel (stable, generic, no business decisions):
export { Money } from './money';           // value object, no domain ownership
export { Result, DomainError, AppError } from './errors';
export { Clock, IdGenerator } from './ports';   // interfaces only
// FORBIDDEN in the shared kernel: anything where two modules would fight over the semantics.
//  - a "Customer" class that both Billing and Identity extend
//  - a generic "Repository<T>" everyone extends
//  - any module importing the shared kernel's "convenience" helpers that reach back into modules
// Rule of thumb: the shared kernel must be boring, small (< ~500 lines), and have no behaviour
// that encodes a business decision. If a module needs to change it, the boundary is not real.
```

**Monorepo layout options and the honest trade-off.**

| Approach | Enforcement | Build speed | Tooling cost |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single package + folders + arch tests | CI tests only | Fastest | Low |
| npm/pnpm workspaces, one package per module | Build-time | Medium | Medium (version pinning, publish) |
| Nx / Turborepo / Bazel with `project.json` deps | Build-time, graph-aware | Fast with caching | High (learning curve) |
| Separate `.sln`/Gradle modules / Go modules | Compile-time | Slower | High |

The pragmatic answer: **folders + barrel files + architecture tests in CI** for teams under ~30 engineers; workspace packages when you need build-time enforcement or independent teams; a full build system (Nx/Bazel) only if your build time is a real pain.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** almost always, as the *target* architecture regardless of deployment topology.
- **Use:** when you want microservices' boundaries but not microservices' costs; when you will extract a service in future; when a single deploy is a hard requirement.
- **Not:** if you will never have more than ~5 engineers (then a simple layered structure is enough) — though the arch tests are still cheap.
- **Not:** as an excuse to never consider services. If a measured trigger appears, the modular monolith is the *best possible starting point* for extraction.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* boundaries enforced with no network, one transaction where you want one, one deploy, easier refactoring across modules (compiler-checked), and a clean extraction path later.
*Cost:* architecture tests and discipline (they get disabled and then everyone crosses boundaries "just this once"); a shared kernel that grows into a blob; in-process events that hide coupling behind an emitter (the modules look decoupled but the timing is now invisible); memory pressure of loading everything; and you still have one deploy pipeline, so a broken change anywhere blocks everyone.

**Production considerations.**

- **Architecture tests must be fast and must fail the build.** An architecture rule nobody enforces is a comment.
- **Watch the shared kernel's git blame.** If two modules modified it in the same week, it is not a shared kernel, it is a contention point. Split it.
- **In-process events must not be used to hide a synchronous dependency.** If module B must respond before A returns, call B directly and let the compiler show the coupling.
- **Module init order becomes an issue** when modules register listeners at import time; use explicit `bootstrap()` with a declared order instead.
- **Runtime behaviour differences matter**: in a monolith, an in-memory call can be lazy where a remote call was eager. When you extract a service, "this was a function call, now it is a network call" changes error semantics, latency, and consistency. Audit for that before extracting.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Folder-based modules with no enforcement (cross-module imports creep in within a sprint).
2. Cross-module table reads "just for a report", then the report becomes a feature.
3. A shared kernel containing domain entities.
4. In-process events used as a substitute for explicit interfaces, producing untraceable coupling.
5. Making every module a "module" even the 50-line utility one.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you enforce module boundaries?"* — architecture tests in CI, public surfaces, and eventually build-level enforcement. Have the ~30-line arch test in mind.
- *"When do you take a module out to a service?"* — when it has an independent scaling/deployment/team/isolation requirement that you can measure.
- *"What's a shared kernel and when is it a smell?"* — the small boring set of value objects and interfaces every module uses; a smell when it contains business decisions or changes often.

---

### 11.3.3 Layered architecture and where it leaks

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Classic three-tier (Presentation → Business/Service → Data Access) or N-tier. Strictly ordered dependencies: each layer may call only the layer directly below it.

```
┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Presentation (controllers, UI, HTTP) │  ← knows about the service layer only
├──────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Business / Service layer             │  ← the logic; may call data layer
├──────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Data access (repositories, DAOs, ORM)│  ← knows about storage
└──────────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Why layered architecture is so widespread: it is a legal dependency graph.** You can enforce it with a linter, a build system, or code review, because the rule is *positional*: layer N imports layer N-1. That is the enormous, underrated advantage over hexagonal ("don't import upward") which requires knowing which layer you are in.

**What it gets right.**

- **Predictable navigation.** "Where does the logic live?" has one answer.
- **Testable in principle**: the service layer can be tested with a faked data layer.
- **Changeable in one direction**: you can replace the database without touching the service layer (in theory — see below).
- **Simple to teach and to enforce**, including with static analysis.

**The seven leaks. Every one of these is a real thing you will be asked about.**

1. **The service layer becomes anemic (11.2.9).** All the logic is in services, entities are data bags, invariants are duplicated across services. This is the classic outcome of "service layer" as "all the methods".

2. **The data layer leaks upward.** The moment a service imports `pg`, or calls `repo.queryBuilder()`, or knows a column name, the boundary is gone. Symptom: `import { db } from './data/db'` in a service file.

3. **The presentation layer leaks downward.** Business logic in a controller: `if (user.isVip) total *= 0.9`. Now the same rule is not applied by the background job, because the job does not go through the controller. This is the single most common real bug in layered systems.

4. **Transactions leak upward.** Services need "one transaction across these three repositories"; if each repository manages its own transaction, you cannot compose. The fix pushes transaction demarcation *up* to an application layer above the services.

5. **The presentation layer contains domain rules that are also duplicated elsewhere.** Same as 3 but for cross-cutting rules (validation, authorisation, status transitions).

6. **Shared-nothing layers become "shared-everything" layers.** When two services need the same helper, it goes into a `common` layer. Then everyone imports `common`, and `common` imports everything (it needed one thing from each layer). This is the "layer cake turned inside out" and it is the beginning of the death of the architecture.

7. **Feature slicing is impossible.** Everything about orders is spread across three layers, so one feature touches all three files and the deployment unit is the whole system. This is the structural reason teams move to vertical slices or modular monoliths.

**The fix: vertical slices / feature-first structure.**

```
# Layer-first (bad for change): all controllers | all services | all repositories
src/
  controllers/  userController.ts orderController.ts productController.ts
  services/     userService.ts    orderService.ts    productService.ts
  repositories/ userRepo.ts       orderRepo.ts       productRepo.ts

# Feature-first (good for change): one folder per capability, each with its own vertical slice
src/
  users/    { api.ts  application.ts  domain.ts  repo.ts }
  orders/   { api.ts  application.ts  domain.ts  repo.ts }
  products/ { api.ts  application.ts  domain.ts  repo.ts }
# Adding "gift cards" touches exactly one new folder. Deleting a feature is deleting a folder.
# This is a strict improvement over layer-first and it is what most teams should adopt.
```

**Layers vs hexagonal: the honest comparison.**

| | Layered | Hexagonal / Clean |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Dependency rule | "Import the layer below" (positional) | "Domain imports nothing" (semantic) |
| Where logic lives | Service layer (becomes anemic) | Domain entities + application services |
| Database | A data-access layer (leaks upward constantly) | A port + an adapter |
| Test doubles | Fake the data layer | Fake each port |
| Enforceability | Easy (static) | Harder (needs a domain folder rule) |
| Transaction placement | Pushes upward awkwardly | Application layer, explicitly |
| Fits complex domains | Poorly | Well |
| Fits CRUD | Well | Over-engineered |

The pragmatic recommendation you should give: **feature-first layering with a clear application/domain/repository split *within* each feature, plus a thin shared kernel.** You get most of the safety with a fraction of the ceremony. Hexagonal is worth its cost for the *core, complex* capabilities, not necessarily for every module.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** CRUD-ish systems, small teams, when you want simple enforceable rules, and as the structure *inside* each module of a modular monolith.
- **Use:** as the mental model for onboarding ("here is where things go").
- **Not:** for a complex domain where invariants must live with the data — that is what hexagonal/DDD is for.
- **Not:** as a reason to have a `common` layer.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* simple, enforceable, familiar, easy to test with a faked data layer, easy to navigate.
*Cost:* leaks (transactions, business logic in controllers, ORM types in services), anemic services, no per-feature ownership, and a `common` graveyard.

**Production considerations.**

- The biggest production bug class in layered systems is **logic in the presentation layer not being exercised by background jobs and API endpoints** (batch jobs bypass the controller). Fix by making controllers thin and putting the rule in the service, then testing the service.
- **Never put authorisation only in the controller.** Any other entry point (a job, a webhook, a CLI, a second API) bypasses it. Put it in the service/use case.
- **N+1 queries** are the data layer's fault but discovered in production: enforce a query budget.
- **Transaction demarcation**: decide one rule (per request/use case) and enforce it, or you will get half-committed business operations.

**Common mistakes.**
1. `common` / `utils` / `helpers` layer that everything imports.
2. Entities that are ORM models, so the data layer's types are everywhere.
3. Business rules in controllers (and nowhere else).
4. Layer-first folder structure in a codebase with many features.
5. "Strictly layered" enforced only by convention.
6. Anemic services (which is the *definition* of the naive service layer).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Where do transactions go in a layered architecture?"* — the application/service layer, one transaction per use case, with repositories participating in the caller's transaction. If each repository owns a transaction you cannot compose.
- *"How do you stop business logic ending up in controllers?"* — make controllers thin by review rule, and add a test that exercises the use case directly (so controller logic is visibly redundant).
- *"Layered or hexagonal for a new service?"* — ask about domain complexity; feature-first layering for CRUD, hexagonal for the complex core.

---

### 11.3.4 Microservices: what you actually gain and lose

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** An architecture where an application is decomposed into many small, independently deployable services, each owning its data, communicating over the network, usually organised around business capabilities.

**The honest framing, which you should open with in an interview.** Microservices is a *distributed systems* choice. You are choosing to give up in-process function calls and shared transactions in exchange for independent deployment, independent scaling, and team autonomy. The trade is only worth it at a certain organisational and product scale.

**The three things microservices actually give you (be specific, not vague).**

1. **Independent deployability of differently-changing components.** If the recommendation service changes weekly and billing changes monthly, a shared release train makes the weekly work wait for the monthly risk. This is the strongest argument and it is organisational, not technical.
2. **Independent scaling.** Different resource profiles: search is IO- and memory-heavy; inference is GPU-heavy; checkout is bursty. Scaling one without the others is the real financial argument.
3. **Failure isolation for risky work.** A new ML service can be wrong without endangering the payment path. Also regulatory/data-isolation boundaries (PCI scope, PII residency, tenant isolation).

**Everything else usually claimed is not a real benefit.** Be ready to push back on: "microservices are more scalable" (only for the specific component you scale; total system overhead goes up), "we can use different languages" (you now maintain a polyglot build/CI/deploy system), "we can deploy more often" (you can deploy a monolith more often too), "it's more maintainable" (it distributes complexity; it does not reduce it), "it scales with the team" (it requires Conway alignment; without it you get coordination overhead plus network latency).

**The cost list, in the order an interview will probe it.**

1. **Calls fail.** Partial failure is the *normal* case, not the exception. Every call site needs: timeout, retry policy with jitter, idempotency, error taxonomy, and a decision about what the user sees.
2. **No ACID across services.** Enter eventual consistency: sagas, compensation, reconciliation, and a user-visible intermediate state.
3. **Debugging requires infrastructure.** Distributed tracing (OpenTelemetry), log correlation with `traceparent`, and the discipline to propagate them. Without it you are guessing.
4. **Data duplication and cross-service queries.** "Show the order with its customer name and product names" becomes 3 API calls, a fan-out with a latency budget, or a denormalised read model (which then needs rebuilding).
5. **Contract management.** Every synchronous API is a distributed-system contract that must be versioned, backward compatible, tested (consumer-driven contract tests), and monitored.
6. **Deployment coordination.** A change that spans 3 services requires 3 deploys in a compatibility-safe order (expand/migrate/contract). This is where "deploy on Friday" becomes impossible.
7. **Local development.** You need a compose file, emulators, seeded data, or a service-virtualisation layer, and it will drift from production.
8. **Operational load.** Per-service: pipelines, dashboards, alerts, runbooks, on-call, capacity, versioning. This is the cost that most often causes a failed adoption.
9. **Testing.** Unit tests no longer cover integration. You need contract tests, a small number of integration tests, and an E2E suite that will be slow and flaky. Teams that skip this get untested microservices.
10. **Two Big Sides effects:** more network hops (tail latency compounds), more resource use for the same work, more failure surface.

**Design practices that materially reduce the cost.** Know these — they are what an experienced candidate adds:

- **Sync calls are the default; async is the exception.** Every synchronous hop adds p50 latency and a failure mode. Prefer a local call unless you have a reason.
- **Design for idempotency everywhere.** Every endpoint and every message consumer. Idempotency keys, stored and enforced, plus idempotent consumers.
- **Timeout on every call, always.** And a retry budget nested inside the caller's deadline.
- **Prefer one-way sync over request-response where possible.** Ask "could this be a message?" and "could the caller not need the answer?"
- **Avoid chatty interactions.** Design coarse-grained APIs (one call to fetch what the UI needs) — this is exactly the BFF pattern (11.3.10).
- **Own your data. No cross-service table reads, ever.**
- **Expand/migrate/contract** for schema changes. Never a breaking change in a single deploy.
- **Consumer-driven contract tests** (Pact-style) so a provider cannot break a consumer unnoticed.
- **Bulkheads + circuit breakers per dependency** (11.1.15).
- **Canary deploys and progressive rollout**, because your rollback is now cross-service.
- **Platform team as a product**: golden paths, service templates, default observability, and paved roads. Without this you get N bespoke pipelines.

**The failure modes to name from experience.**

1. **The distributed monolith.** N services that must always be deployed together and share a database anyway — the worst outcome: all the cost, none of the benefit.
2. **Chatty services.** Nano-services called 40 times per request; latency and failure probability compound (40 calls at 99.9% each = 96% chance all succeed).
3. **Shared database.** Defeats data ownership, makes extraction impossible, and couples deployability — the thing you wanted.
4. **God services.** A "core" service that everything depends on, which is a monolith with network latency.
5. **Premature decomposition** driven by technical layers or by org chart guesses rather than business capabilities.
6. **No platform investment**, so every service is bespoke, and the team spends all its time on pipelines.
7. **"Microservices" as an org-chart mapping** (one service per team) before the domains are understood.
8. **Ignoring the data.** Every service needs its own read model eventually; nobody planned for it.

**When to use vs not.** The list in 11.3.1 applies. Add: do not use them if you have fewer than ~20 engineers, or if your product requirements are still changing weekly, or if your team has no experience running distributed systems, or if your primary problem is code quality (fix that instead — modular monolith).

**Trade-offs, one sentence:** *Microservices trade transactional correctness and operational simplicity for independent deployment, independent scaling, and fault isolation — and the trade only pays at a certain team and product scale.*

**Production considerations.**

- **Observability is not optional tooling, it is a prerequisite.** OTel traces, structured logs with correlation ids, RED metrics per service, and a cross-service dashboard that shows a user journey.
- **Backward compatibility for one release minimum.** Two versions of a service run concurrently during any deploy.
- **Consumer-driven contract tests in CI** for every synchronous dependency.
- **Graceful shutdown.** Stop accepting traffic, finish in-flight requests, then exit. Rolling restarts are the norm.
- **Health checks with correct semantics**: liveness must not depend on downstream services (or a dependency outage restarts your entire fleet).
- **Per-service connection pools with real limits**, and bulkheads so one dependency's slowness does not consume the whole service.
- **Load balancing + retries must not amplify.** Client-side retries plus server-side retries plus a load balancer can produce a 10× amplification.
- **Data migration** is the hardest part of extraction, not code. Dual-write + backfill + verify, with a reconciliation job.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Extracting by technical layer (an "auth service", a "validation service") rather than business capability.
2. Sharing the database while claiming data ownership.
3. Sync-everything with no idempotency or timeout.
4. Assuming "we will add tracing next quarter".
5. Treating the service count as an architecture metric.
6. No platform/ops investment.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"You have 30 services and one is causing p99 latency spikes. How do you find the cause?"* — distributed tracing, per-dependency timing histograms, outbox/publish lag, then look for N+1 fan-out or retry amplification.
- *"When is a God service a problem, not a convenience?"* — when it is the only thing that can change core behaviour and every other service deploys with it: you have a distributed monolith.
- *"How do you test microservices?"* — unit + consumer-driven contract tests + a thin integration suite + E2E on the critical journeys only; plus chaos/dependency-failure tests for the resilience patterns.

---

### 11.3.5 Monolith-to-microservices decision criteria

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

This is the single most asked "give me a decision framework" question in this phase. You need **explicit, weighted, checkable criteria** and a defensible threshold. Vague answers ("it depends on scale") score poorly.

**The prerequisite gate — do not skip this.** If the answer to any of these is "yes", extract nothing and fix the problem instead:

- The monolith's problem is **code quality** (no module boundaries, no tests, a God object). → Do modular-monolith work (11.3.2).
- The monolith is **fine**; you just want to look modern. → Stop.
- Requirements are still moving and you do not yet know the domains. → Wait; premature decomposition locks in wrong boundaries and is very hard to undo.

**Criterion 1 — Change/deploy coupling.** *Do components with very different change rates currently block each other's releases?*

- Measure: commits per module per month, deploy frequency, and the number of releases that carry changes to unrelated modules.
- **Threshold:** ≥3 deployable components whose change rates differ by an order of magnitude, and a release train that couples them.
- **Evidence to gather:** "we batch deploys monthly because of the billing change freeze" is the strongest single sentence you can bring to this conversation.

**Criterion 2 — Scaling profile.** *Does one component need resources proportional to its own load, disproportionate to its value?*

- Measure: CPU/memory/IO per endpoint, and what the cost of scaling the whole system is.
- **Threshold:** one component consumes >50% of capacity while contributing <10% of revenue; or a component needs a different instance type/GPU/region than the rest.
- **Strong signal:** "to serve 2% of our traffic to a feature I need 8× the fleet because of a synchronous call chain inside it."

**Criterion 3 — Team topology (Conway).** *Do multiple teams need to change the same code at the same time?*

- Measure: merge conflicts, cross-team PR reviews, "who is allowed to change this?", on-call boundaries.
- **Threshold:** ≥3 teams whose release cadence is blocked by each other; or ≥2 teams with independent roadmaps for the same component.
- **Caveat to state:** teams per service is not a goal. If you have 4 teams, 4 services is a reasonable upper bound.

**Criterion 4 — Data/regulatory isolation.** *Do you need a hard data boundary?*

- **Threshold:** regulatory (PCI, HIPAA, GDPR residency), multi-tenant data isolation requirements, or a customer contractually requiring data segregation. This is the criterion that *overrides* all the others — it justifies extraction even without scale.

**Criterion 5 — Reliability/blast radius.** *Does a specific component's failure take down everything?*

- Measure: incidents caused by one component; shared failure modes (one pool, one deploy).
- **Threshold:** a component causing >25% of incidents, or a change to it requiring a fleet-wide deploy.

**Criterion 6 — Technology/runtime need.** *Does a component genuinely need a different runtime or a different specialised platform?*

- **Threshold:** ML inference (GPU), stream processing (specialised engine), embedded targets, extremely memory-dense workloads. Be honest: "we prefer Go in the search team" is not a threshold.

**The weighted scoring card.** Use this in the interview; it demonstrates you make decisions with criteria rather than enthusiasm.

| Criterion | Weight | Question | Extraction trigger |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Change coupling | 25% | Do components with different cadences block each other? | Order-of-magnitude difference in change rate + a shared release train |
| Scaling profile | 25% | Does one component need disproportionate resources? | >50% capacity for <10% value, or a different instance type |
| Team topology | 20% | Do teams block each other's releases? | ≥3 teams coupled, or ≥2 independent roadmaps |
| Data/regulatory | 15% | Do you need a hard data boundary? | Any regulatory/contractual isolation requirement (override) |
| Blast radius | 10% | Does one component cause most incidents? | >25% of incidents, or needs a fleet-wide deploy |
| Technology | 5% | Does it need a different runtime? | GPU, stream engine, embedded — nothing else |

**Interpretation:** score each 1–5, weight, and only extract for a component whose weighted score exceeds ~3.5 *and* which has at least one threshold met. Everything else stays in the monolith. State the number; it makes the decision auditable.

**The extraction sequence — the most valuable part of the answer.** Never "split everything". Extract **one** boundary, chosen by highest score, with these steps:

```text
0. FIRST: create a module boundary in the monolith (11.3.2) with enforced imports and data
   ownership. Stop here if the score was driven by code quality. This step is never wasted.
1. SEAM the call sites: every caller of the module now goes through its public interface.
   (Strangler Fig step 1 — 11.2.10)
2. EXTRACT the read path first, not the write path. Reads are idempotent, side-effect-light,
   and easy to shadow-compare. Order/list endpoints are the safest first extraction.
3. DUAL-RUN in shadow mode; compare responses; drive mismatches to zero.
4. EXTRACT THE DATA: give the new service its own schema. Dual-write from the monolith
   (via outbox) + backfill + verify + switch reads.
5. CUT OVER reads behind a flag (1% → 10% → 50% → 100%), monitoring error rate and business metrics.
6. Move writes: monolith becomes a client of the new service. Keep the old table in sync
   during the compatibility window.
7. DELETE the old code path and the old table. (This step is the one teams skip. Do not skip it.)
8. MEASURE for one full business cycle before extracting the next boundary.
```

**Why read path first (state this explicitly):** reads can be compared for equality, retried freely, shadowed safely, and reverted instantly; writes have side effects, cannot be compared trivially, and are the risky part. Extract reads, prove the pattern works, then do writes.

**The "when to stop" criteria.** Extraction is working when: each extracted service has an owner, a dashboard, an on-call rotation, a runbook, a SLO, and an independent pipeline. If those do not exist, extract a different one or stop extracting.

**The anti-decision-criteria (things that are NOT reasons).** These deserve their own list because they come up constantly:

- "We want to try microservices" — curiosity.
- "Our code is a mess and microservices will force us to decouple" — use modular monolith; you can create a distributed mess at the same price.
- "The industry uses microservices" — so does the industry running 4-person teams on Rails monoliths successfully.
- "We need to scale" — measure *what*, first. Often a cache or a query index solves it.
- "Netflix/Amazon do it" — they also run tens of thousands of services and have hundreds of engineers per service.
- "We want polyglot" — cost without a matching benefit.

**Common mistakes.**
1. No prerequisites check, so you extract a badly-modelled domain and get a distributed monolith.
2. Extracting by technical layer rather than business capability.
3. Extracting the write path first.
4. Skipping step 0 (module boundary) and therefore having nothing clean to extract.
5. Extracting 5 services at once and learning nothing from the first.
6. Never deleting the old path, so you now maintain two systems forever.
7. Using the scorecard as a checkbox rather than measuring.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Your monolith has 40 engineers and 2 deploys a month. What do you do first?"* — find out *why* deploys are rare: is it a release process, a change freeze, a fragile test suite, or one person who understands the billing code? The answer decides whether microservices help at all. (Usually: fix the pipeline first.)
- *"Which service would you extract first?"* — the one with the highest weighted score AND the fewest writes (a read-mostly catalogue or search) so the first extraction proves the pattern cheaply.
- *"How do you know your extraction succeeded?"* — the module's code is deleted, its deploys are independent, incidents are attributed to the new service, and no other service reads its tables.

---

### 11.3.6 Event-driven architecture: coupling, consistency, and failure handling

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** An architecture where components communicate by publishing and consuming events through a broker, so that the *timing and existence* of other components is not part of any component's knowledge. The unit of communication is a *fact that happened*, not a *request to do something*.

**Three coupling kinds, and EDA reduces only one of them.** This is the single most important thing to be able to say, because most claims about EDA overstate it.

| Coupling | What EDA does |
| --- | --- |
| **Temporal coupling** (A must run before B) | **Eliminated.** A publishes; B runs whenever. |
| **Spatial/deployment coupling** (A and B deploy together) | **Reduced.** B can deploy independently of A. |
| **Semantic coupling** (A and B agree on what "Order" means) | **NOT eliminated.** Arguably increased — the event schema is a contract between parties that do not know each other. |

So "event-driven is loosely coupled" is only true for temporal and spatial coupling. The semantic coupling remains and needs governance (schema registry, owners, compatibility tests). Say this — it is the mark of someone who has actually run one.

**What you gain.**

- **New consumers without producer changes.** The most concrete gain: adding an analytics consumer requires zero changes to the producer.
- **Fan-out with independent failure and retry.** A slow or broken consumer does not affect the producer (it affects only its own lag).
- **Independent scaling and deployment of consumers.**
- **A durable audit/replay log** — you can rebuild read models, debug from history, and implement "what if" analysis on real events.
- **Loose coupling in time**, which enables buffering for spikes.

**What you pay — and the honest list.**

1. **Eventual consistency becomes visible to users.** "Your order is processing" instead of "your order was placed". You now need a **status model with intermediate states** and a UI that communicates them.
2. **You lose the ability to read the flow.** In a choreographed design the sequence of steps lives in no single place. The flow *moves*, it does not disappear.
3. **Debugging requires correlation.** Every message needs `traceparent`, `correlationId`, `causationId`, and `eventId`; without them, incident response is archaeology.
4. **The dual-write problem** (11.3.6.1 below) is *the* hard problem of EDA; solving it needs the outbox pattern.
5. **Schema evolution becomes a first-class governance problem.** Every payload change is a breaking change for whoever is deployed.
6. **Operational surface**: brokers, partitions, consumer lag, DLQs, retention, rebalancing, and a new on-call.
7. **Harder local development**: you need a broker locally, or a good in-memory fake, and the fake will drift.
8. **Testing**: consumer-driven contract tests, event replay tests, and the fact that unit tests of a consumer do not exercise the broker's behaviour.

**The dual-write problem and the outbox pattern (implement this in the interview).**

```sql
-- THE BUG: write the state and publish the event. Two systems, no transaction between them.
BEGIN;
  UPDATE orders SET status = 'paid';      -- succeeds
COMMIT;
await broker.publish('order.paid', …);    -- the process crashes here
-- Result: the order is paid and NOBODY was ever told. Silent, permanent inconsistency.
```

```ts
// THE FIX: outbox pattern. Write the event to a table in the SAME transaction as the state.
interface OutboxRow {
  id: string;                  // event id (also the consumer idempotency key source)
  topic: string; payload: unknown;
  occurred_at: Date; published_at: Date | null; attempts: number; last_error: string | null;
}

class OrderService {
  constructor(private readonly tx: TransactionRunner, private readonly outbox: OutboxRepo) {}
  async markPaid(orderId: OrderId, receipt: Receipt): Promise<void> {
    await this.tx.run(async () => {
      await this.orders.updateStatus(orderId, 'paid');
      // (2) Same transaction → either both happen or neither does.
      await this.outbox.append({
        id: `evt_${orderId}_paid_${receipt.chargeId}`,   // deterministic id → dedupe-friendly
        topic: 'order.paid.v1',
        payload: { orderId: orderId.value, receiptId: receipt.id, totalMinor: receipt.totalMinor,
                   occurredAt: this.clock.now().toISOString(), traceparent: currentTraceparent() },
      });
    });
  }
}

// THE RELAY: publishes outbox rows to the broker. Separate process, at-least-once, idempotent downstream.
class OutboxRelay {
  private running = false;
  async runOnce(batchSize = 100): Promise<number> {
    const rows = await this.outbox.claimUnpublished(batchSize);
    let published = 0;
    for (const row of rows) {
      try {
        await this.broker.publish(row.topic, row.payload, {
          key: row.payload.orderId, headers: { 'traceparent': row.payload.traceparent }, messageId: row.id,
        });
        await this.outbox.markPublished(row.id);   // NOTE: not in a tx with publish → at-least-once
        published++;
      } catch (e) {
        await this.outbox.recordFailure(row.id, e);   // retry with backoff; DLQ after N attempts
      }
    }
    return published;
  }
}
// Consequence to state out loud: publishing and marking are not atomic → duplicates are possible →
// consumers MUST be idempotent. This is not a flaw; it is the design, and it is why the inbox exists.
```

```sql
-- THE CONSUMER SIDE: inbox table for effectively-once processing.
CREATE TABLE inbox (message_id text PRIMARY KEY, consumer text NOT NULL, processed_at timestamptz NOT NULL);
-- In ONE local transaction: insert into inbox (fails → duplicate → skip) AND apply the effect.
BEGIN;
  INSERT INTO inbox (message_id, consumer) VALUES ($1, 'search-indexer');   -- PK conflict = duplicate
  UPDATE search_index SET … ;
COMMIT;
-- The primary key does the deduplication. Atomicity does the rest. No magic required.
```

**Failure handling — the complete list you should be able to enumerate.**

1. **Poison messages.** Malformed, or failing permanently. → retries with backoff → DLQ with the payload and error → alert → operator replays after a fix. Never block a partition forever.
2. **Idempotent consumers** (the inbox above). Non-negotiable given at-least-once.
3. **Ordering.** Per partition key. Choose keys deliberately; document them as a schema.
4. **Consumer lag** is the primary SLI: alert on lag duration and count.
5. **Backpressure.** Bound consumer concurrency; shed or scale on lag; do not let every consumer degrade simultaneously.
6. **Partial failure in a workflow.** This is where sagas come in (11.1.16): a step fails, compensate the earlier ones.
7. **Unavailable broker.** Buffer on the producer side (which is what the outbox does) and retry the relay. Producers must not fail when the broker is down — that would make the broker part of your critical path.
8. **Deploying a consumer that breaks on old messages.** Never delete or repurpose an event type. Version it.
9. **Replay.** Because the log is durable, you can replay to rebuild a read model. Replay must be idempotent and must not re-trigger side effects (route replays to projections only, or use a `replay` flag in the handler).
10. **Clock and ordering assumptions.** Never depend on `occurredAt` ordering; depend on the broker's ordering within a partition.

**Event taxonomy (state these precisely, this is worth points).**

| Kind | Semantics | Naming | Handler count |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Domain event** | A fact about this bounded context, past tense | `OrderPaid` | 0..n in-context |
| **Integration event** | A fact published for others, past tense, with a published schema | `order.paid.v1` on a topic | 0..n |
| **Command (message)** | An imperative request; may be refused | `ProcessPayment` / `order.aggregate.reserve.v1` | Exactly 1 (must exist) |
| **Query (message)** | A question with a reply-to address | `GetOrderSummary` | Exactly 1 |

Rule to state: **if the handler does not exist, is that a bug?** Event: no (nobody may care). Command: yes (nobody did the work). That single question prevents most event-messaging mistakes.

**When to use vs not.**

- **Use:** for reactions that must not block or fail the caller (notifications, analytics, index updates, cache warming, audit), for fanning out to consumers that scale differently, for read-model building, and for integrating with external parties you do not control.
- **Use:** where the consumer genuinely might not exist yet or might be added later without touching the producer.
- **Don't use:** when the caller needs the answer immediately (that is a function call), or for the *core* correctness-critical flow if you value synchronous guarantees — or if you cannot afford the operational complexity.
- **Don't use** for a simple internal 3-listener fan-out (an in-process Observer, 11.1.8, is enough).
- **Don't use** as an excuse to avoid thinking about the workflow.

**Trade-offs.** One sentence: *EDA removes temporal and deployment coupling at the cost of immediate consistency, flow visibility, and operational and schema-governance complexity.*

**Production considerations.**

- Trace propagation across async boundaries is mandatory: `traceparent` in headers, `correlationId` and `causationId` in the payload.
- **Schema governance:** a registry with compatibility checks in CI; additive-only changes; version in the topic name; a deprecation window measured in releases, not days.
- **Broker choice consequences:** managed cloud brokers are operationally free and lock you in; self-hosted gives control and costs you a team. Consider exactly-once claims skeptically (Kafka EOS does not cover your side effects).
- **Retention + replay** is how you rebuild a read model; test the replay procedure regularly (it is a critical, rarely-exercised path).
- **DLQ replay tooling** is production infrastructure, not a script someone wrote once.
- **Message size limits** force you to send IDs + refs, and consumers then need an API call — which reintroduces coupling. Know this trade-off.
- **Order of business events and consistency:** if two events in one transaction, consumers may see them in either order across partitions. Put both on the same key, or use a per-aggregate sequence number.
- **Cost**: broker throughput, storage, retention; EDA designs are chatty and the bill reflects it.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Publishing outside the transaction (the dual-write bug).
2. Non-idempotent consumers.
3. Events named as commands ("SendWelcomeEmail") — then the flow is invisible and a missing handler is silent.
4. Renaming/repurposing an event type.
5. No DLQ, no lag alerting.
6. Assuming exactly-once.
7. Replaying events into side-effecting handlers (duplicate emails, duplicate charges).

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"What coupling does EDA actually remove?"* — temporal and spatial; **not** semantic.
- *"How do you guarantee an event is published for every state change?"* — outbox pattern with a relay; explain why at-least-once is acceptable and how consumers cope.
- *"A consumer is stuck at 500 errors. What happens?"* — retries with backoff, then DLQ, and the partition is blocked for messages after it (which is why per-message failures must be isolated from the partition).
- *"Do you need a broker for a monolith's internal events?"* — no; use an in-process emitter unless you need replay or independent scaling. Brokers inside a monolith is usually operational cost for no benefit.

---

### 11.3.7 CQRS: when a separate read model is worth it

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Command Query Responsibility Segregation: separate the **write model** (which enforces invariants and is optimised for consistency) from the **read model** (which is shaped for the query being served and optimised for read throughput). They may be the same process and the same database — the separation is logical before it is physical.

**Why anyone does it: the read and write workloads are genuinely different.**

| | Write model | Read model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Normalised, aggregate-oriented | Denormalised, query-oriented (can be a document/JSON, a search index, a cache) |
| Access | Point lookups and small transactions | Wide queries, joins, aggregations, filters, sorting, paging |
| Consistency | Strong within the aggregate; ACID | Eventual; may be seconds behind |
| Concurrency | Optimistic versioning / locks | Append + rebuild; rarely contended |
| Volume | Relatively few, expensive writes | Many reads; often 100:1 or worse |

**The three levels of CQRS, because people jump to level 3 and regret it.**

```ts
// LEVEL 1: SEPARATE INTERFACACES, SAME MODEL. The cheap, usually-correct starting point.
interface OrderCommands { place(cmd: PlaceOrder): Promise<OrderId>; cancel(id: OrderId): Promise<void>; }
interface OrderQueries  { byId(id: OrderId): Promise<OrderView | null>; search(f: OrderFilter): Promise<Page<OrderView>>; }
class OrderService implements OrderCommands { /* uses the domain model */ }
class OrderQueryService implements OrderQueries  { /* reads the same table, shapes the view */ }
// Value: query code no longer has to go through the aggregate to load 6 related entities,
//        and the read path can get its own indexes without compromising the write model.
//        Cost: near zero. This is the right default.

// LEVEL 2: SEPARATE MODELS IN THE SAME DATABASE/STORE, different tables.
interface OrderCommands { … }
class SqlOrderCommandStore implements OrderRepository { /* writes the normalised tables */ }
interface OrderQueries { … }
class SqlOrderReadStore implements OrderQueries {
  // Denormalised table shaped exactly for the query: no joins, no aggregation at read time.
  async search(f: OrderFilter): Promise<Page<OrderView>> {
    return this.db`SELECT customer_id, status, total_minor, currency, placed_at, item_count
                   FROM order_search_view
                   WHERE (${f.customerId}::uuid IS NULL OR customer_id = ${f.customerId})
                     AND (${f.status}::text IS NULL OR status = ${f.status})
                     AND placed_at > ${f.since}
                   ORDER BY placed_at DESC
                   LIMIT ${f.limit} OFFSET ${f.offset}`;
  }
}
// Value: the read query is a single indexed scan; you can add read-only replicas; you can shape
//        the view for the UI. Cost: a projection step, eventual consistency, and a projection to repair.

// LEVEL 3: SEPARATE STORES, EVENT-PROJECTED READ MODEL. The full version.
write store (Postgres, normalised)  --events-->  broker  --projectors-->  read store (Dynamo/ES/Mongo)
// Value: read models can be shaped completely differently per view, at any scale, with joins
//        pre-computed. Cost: you have now committed to event sourcing your write side (or adding
//        a change-data-capture mechanism), plus all of 11.3.6's costs.
```

**The honest threshold — when is a separate read model worth it?**

**Worth it when:**
- The read:write ratio is high (typically >10:1) and reads are expensive (multi-join aggregations, sorting, full-text search).
- The query shape differs fundamentally from the write shape (a dashboard aggregating 5 entities; a search over denormalised text).
- Reads must scale or be geographically distributed separately from writes.
- You are serving multiple distinct view requirements (mobile list vs admin detail vs export).
- Reads need a different storage technology (full-text search, graph, columnar analytics).
- You have a real CQRS framework or a project team that can operate the projection.

**Not worth it when:**
- The domain is CRUD and queries are 2-3 indexed lookups. You have added a projection to maintain, a lag to reason about, and a repair job to write.
- One team, small scale, low traffic. **Most systems never need CQRS.**
- You cannot articulate the read model you need. Do not build one speculatively.

**Costs you must be able to enumerate (this is where the interview really tests you).**

1. **Eventual consistency becomes user-visible.** A user places an order, navigates to the list, does not see it, and files a bug. You need: read-your-writes (a short-lived "just wrote" overlay or a token), "processing" states, and clear UI feedback.
2. **Projection lag as an SLO to monitor and alert on.** Not an occasional annoyance — an operational metric with a target and a page.
3. **Two models to evolve, and they drift.** A schema change requires two migrations and a projection backfill. This is the largest ongoing cost.
4. **Projection repair.** Projectors fail, restart from an older offset, or hit a poison message. You need: replay from the event log, a full-rebuild procedure, and a way to verify the projection matches the source of truth.
5. **More infrastructure.** A broker, a second store, a projection worker, dashboards for lag, and an on-call rotation for it.
6. **Cross-aggregate reads in commands become hard.** If a command needs a query-model read, you have a consistency question: do you read from the projection (stale) or the write model (expensive)? Rule: **commands read the write model.**
7. **Consistency rules get subtle.** Two users' simultaneous actions can be validated against a stale view and produce a violated invariant. Never enforce an invariant against a projection.
8. **Debugging spans two stores** and needs correlation ids.

**The read-your-writes solution, concretely.** This is the practical detail that makes CQRS usable and it is worth having in your answer:

```ts
// Option A: the client sends back a "since" version from the write response; the read path
// waits/overlays until the projection catches up.
const placed = await commands.place(cmd);            // returns { orderId, projectionWatermark: 412 }
const view = await queries.search({ since: placed.projectionWatermark });   // blocks up to ~200ms then overlays

// Option B: short-lived client-side optimism — apply the change locally, reconcile when the
// projection confirms. Simplest, and what most UIs should do.
const optimistic = { ...localPatch, _pending: true };
subscribeToServerState(orderId, server => replace(optimistic, server));

// Option C: read the write model directly for the just-created entity (bypass the projection for
// a single-entity read). Simple, correct, and the projection catches up behind you.
if (cache.has(id)) return cache.get(id)!;
const fresh = await writeStore.findById(id);
if (fresh) { cache.set(id, fresh); return fresh; }
return readStore.byId(id);                            // fall back to the projection for old data
```

**When to use vs not (the decision):** Start at level 1 (separate interfaces, same model) — it costs nothing. Move to level 2 when you *measure* a read bottleneck that indexes cannot fix and you own the projection. Move to level 3 when you need the read models to be fundamentally different (multiple views, different storage, global read scale) **and** you have the operational capacity. Most systems stop at level 1 or 2 forever, and that is a good outcome.

**Production considerations.**

- **Never validate invariants against the read model.** This is the #1 correctness bug in CQRS systems.
- **Version and stamp the read model** with the source offset/watermark so you can answer "is this view current?" — required for read-your-writes, for cache invalidation, and for debugging.
- **Full rebuild must be possible and tested.** Quarterly at minimum: rebuild into a new table, compare, swap.
- **Projection idempotency** — the same event may be projected twice.
- **Do not skip the event log.** The log is what makes replay possible.
- **Monitor:** projection lag (p50/p99), projection error rate, DLQ depth, and "read model row count vs source of truth count".
- **Cache invalidation:** if you add a cache in front of reads, it adds another consistency layer. Prefer a short TTL plus version-based invalidation from the projection.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Starting at level 3 because it is "proper CQRS".
2. Reading from the read model inside a command.
3. No rebuild/repair capability.
4. No read-your-writes strategy, so users see ghost bugs.
5. Schema changes that require coordinated, non-backwards-compatible deploys across both models.
6. Projection lag unmonitored — discovered by customers.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you keep the read model consistent with the write model?"* — honestly: it is eventually consistent, by design; here is the lag target, the monitoring, the rebuild procedure, and the read-your-writes strategy.
- *"When is CQRS wrong?"* — CRUD, low read volume, one team, and query shapes that match the write model.
- *"How do you handle a command that needs data from the projection?"* — do not; read the write model.
- *"CQRS or two caches?"* — if you only need a cache, use a cache; CQRS is about different *models*, not about caching.

---

### 11.3.8 Event sourcing: the honest trade-off

**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

**Definition.** Event sourcing persists the **sequence of domain events** as the source of truth, and derives current state by replaying them. The event log is the system of record; "state" is a projection/cache.

**The concept that makes it distinct from an audit log or event-driven architecture:** the events are not a side-effect; they *are* the data. Everything else follows from that.

**What you get (these are real and each is worth money).**

1. **Complete history.** You can answer "what was this order's state on 12 March, at 14:03, and why?" — trivially and exactly. No other design gives you this.
2. **Temporal queries.** "Reconstruct the portfolio as of any past date." This is a genuine capability for finance, insurance, gaming, and anything with reporting obligations.
3. **Rebuild any projection.** Fix a bug in a read model: replay the log and rebuild it correctly. Change the projection logic; no data migration.
4. **Event-driven integration.** The log *is* the event stream — no dual-write problem for consumers, no outbox needed (the log is already durable and ordered per aggregate).
5. **Debugging and support.** You can replay a customer's history exactly. Support and fraud teams genuinely use this.
6. **Fine-grained domain events** with no impedance mismatch to the storage schema.
7. **Natural optimistic concurrency** via the aggregate version/sequence number.

**What it costs (state these with equal fluency).**

1. **You must get the event schema right, and it is forever.** Event 1 in 2024 is immutable in 2044. Events are not rows in a table you can `UPDATE`; they are the historical record. Every event must be self-contained (no "the price was the old value" — include the value), and every field addition must be backwards compatible forever.
2. **Event upcasting is mandatory work.** Old events need to be interpreted by new code. You need an upcaster chain (`v1 → v2 → v3`) from day one, and you need to write them and test them. Most teams underestimate this by an order of magnitude.
3. **Snapshots for performance.** Replaying 500,000 events on every read is unacceptable; you need periodic snapshots plus replay-from-snapshot, which then requires snapshot validation and care.
4. **No ad-hoc SQL.** Every question about your data becomes "replay and ask" or "read a projection". Analysts who run `SELECT …` become your biggest internal stakeholder, and you cannot serve them directly. This is a large, underestimated organisational cost.
5. **Eventual consistency everywhere, including inside the aggregate** if you use projections for reads.
