6. **GDPR/right-to-erasure becomes a genuine conflict** with an immutable log. You need crypto-shredding or a deliberate (and legally defensible) exception. This is not a solvable engineering problem; it is a policy decision.
7. **Debugging is harder.** You must read the event log, not the data.
8. **Complex tooling.** Projectors, replay, snapshotting, upcasters, rebuild tooling, and a schema registry — none of which is standard in any framework.
9. **Higher write amplification** and storage: every state change is an append, and projections multiply that.
10. **Team learning curve.** Developers join expecting to query a database.

**The mechanics you need to be able to write.**

```ts
// THE AGGREGATE: decisions are made against a rehydrated state and recorded as events.
type OrderEvent =
  | { type: 'OrderPlaced';    v: 1; orderId: string; customerId: string; currency: string;
      lines: { sku: string; qty: number; unitPriceMinor: number }[]; occurredAt: string }
  | { type: 'PaymentCaptured'; v: 1; orderId: string; paymentId: string; amountMinor: number; occurredAt: string }
  | { type: 'OrderShipped';   v: 1; orderId: string; trackingId: string; occurredAt: string }
  | { type: 'OrderCancelled'; v: 1; orderId: string; reason: string; occurredAt: string; version: 1 }
  | { type: 'PriceAdjusted';  v: 2; orderId: string; lineSku: string; newUnitPriceMinor: number; occurredAt: string };

class Order {                                   // aggregate: behaviour + rehydration
  private state: OrderState = { status: 'none', lines: [], version: 0, totalMinor: 0, currency: '' };
  private readonly pending: OrderEvent[] = [];  // uncommitted events (decisions not yet persisted)

  static rehydrate(events: OrderEvent[]): Order {   // the ONLY way to get historical state
    const o = new Order();
    for (const e of events) o.apply(e);            // replay: pure state transition, no validation side effects
    o.pending.length = 0;                         // nothing is pending after a replay
    return o;
  }

  // Command methods: validate against current state, record an event. NO side effects here.
  capture(paymentId: string): OrderEvent {
    if (this.state.status !== 'placed') throw new InvalidTransition('capture', this.state.status);
    this.pending.push({ type: 'PaymentCaptured', v: 1, orderId: this.state.id!, paymentId,
                        amountMinor: this.state.totalMinor, occurredAt: this.now() });
    return this.pending[this.pending.length - 1]!;
  }
  ship(trackingId: string): OrderEvent { /* … */ }

  // Commit: expected version for optimistic concurrency. Append-only, never UPDATE.
  pendingEvents(): OrderEvent[] { return [...this.pending]; }
  expectedVersion(): number { return this.state.version; }

  private apply(e: OrderEvent): void {            // single code path for replay AND live application
    switch (e.type) {
      case 'OrderPlaced':    this.state = { status: 'placed', id: e.orderId, customerId: e.customerId,
                                             currency: e.currency, totalMinor: sum(e.lines), version: 1, lines: e.lines }; break;
      case 'PaymentCaptured': this.state = { ...this.state, status: 'paid', version: this.state.version + 1 }; break;
      case 'OrderShipped':   this.state = { ...this.state, status: 'shipped', version: this.state.version + 1 }; break;
      case 'OrderCancelled': this.state = { ...this.state, status: 'cancelled', version: this.state.version + 1 }; break;
      case 'PriceAdjusted':  this.state = { ...this.state, version: this.state.version + 1,
                                            lines: this.state.lines.map(l => l.sku === e.lineSku ? { ...l, unitPriceMinor: e.newUnitPriceMinor } : l) }; break;
    }
  }
}

// THE STORE: append with optimistic concurrency.
class EventStore {
  async append(streamId: string, events: OrderEvent[], expectedVersion: number): Promise<number> {
    return this.db.transaction(async tx => {
      const res = await tx.query(
        `INSERT INTO event_store (stream_id, seq, type, payload, occurred_at)
         SELECT $1, COALESCE(MAX(seq), 0) + 1, $2, $3, $4 FROM event_store WHERE stream_id = $1
         HAVING COALESCE(MAX(seq), 0) = $5`,                              // <-- the CAS check
        [streamId, events[0]!.type, JSON.stringify(events), nowIso, expectedVersion]);
      if (res.rowCount !== events.length) throw new ConcurrencyConflict(streamId, expectedVersion);
      // In practice: use a per-stream lock (SELECT … FOR UPDATE) or an atomic MAX(seq) upsert.
      return expectedVersion + events.length;
    });
  }
  async read(streamId: string, fromSeq = 0): Promise<OrderEvent[]> { /* … */ }
  async readAll(): Promise<{ streamId: string; events: OrderEvent[] }[]> { /* for full projection rebuild */ }
}
```

```ts
// THE UPCASTER: the part everyone forgets. Old events must remain readable forever.
type Upcaster = { from: number; to: number; name: string; upcast(e: any): any };
const UPCASTERS: Upcaster[] = [
  // v1 OrderPlaced had `price` in decimal dollars; v2 uses integer minor units. Fix at read time.
  { from: 1, to: 2, name: 'place-price-to-minor',
    upcast: (e) => e.type === 'OrderPlaced'
      ? { ...e, v: 2, lines: e.lines.map((l: any) => ({ ...l, unitPriceMinor: Math.round(l.price * 100), price: undefined })) }
      : { ...e, v: 2 } },
  { from: 2, to: 3, name: 'tracking-id-optional', upcast: (e) => ({ ...e, v: 3, trackingId: e.trackingId ?? null }) },
];
function upcast(raw: any): OrderEvent {
  let e = raw;
  while (e.v < CURRENT_VERSION) {
    const u = UPCASTERS.find(x => x.from === e.v);
    if (!u) throw new MissingUpcaster(`no upcaster from v${e.v} for ${raw.type}`);   // fail loudly, forever
    e = u.upcast(e);
  }
  return e as OrderEvent;
}
// The rule: upcasters must be PURE, DETERMINISTIC, and FOREVER. An upcaster that depends on
// anything but its input is a bug that appears years later. Write a test per upcaster with a
// frozen fixture of the real v1 event.
```

```ts
// THE PROJECTION: state derived from the log. Must be idempotent and rebuildable.
class OrderSummaryProjection {
  async handle(e: OrderEvent): Promise<void> {
    await this.db.transaction(async tx => {
      // The inbox row in the same transaction => effectively-once processing.
      const ins = await tx.query(`INSERT INTO inbox (message_id, consumer) VALUES ($1, 'order-summary')
                                 ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING message_id`, [`${e.orderId}:${e.seq}`]);
      if (ins.rowCount === 0) return;                       // already projected
      switch (e.type) {
        case 'OrderPlaced':
          await tx.query(`INSERT INTO order_summary (order_id, customer_id, status, total_minor, currency, placed_at, item_count, last_event_seq)
                          VALUES ($1,$2,'placed',$3,$4,$5,$6,$7)`, [ /* … */ ]); break;
        case 'PaymentCaptured':
          await tx.query(`UPDATE order_summary SET status='paid', paid_at=$2, last_event_seq=$3
                          WHERE order_id=$1`, [e.orderId, e.occurredAt, e.seq]); break;
        case 'OrderShipped':
          await tx.query(`UPDATE order_summary SET status='shipped', tracking_id=$2, shipped_at=$3, last_event_seq=$4
                          WHERE order_id=$1`, [/* … */]); break;
        case 'OrderCancelled':
          await tx.query(`UPDATE order_summary SET status='cancelled', cancel_reason=$2, last_event_seq=$3 WHERE order_id=$1`, [/* … */]); break;
        case 'PriceAdjusted': /* … */ break;
      }
    });
  }
  // FULL REBUILD: stream every event through handle() from offset 0 into a fresh table, verify,
  // swap. This must be runnable in production without downtime, and it must be TESTED — it is the
  // escape hatch for every projection bug.
  async rebuild(target: string): Promise<void> { /* stream → project → verify → swap */ }
}
```

**Snapshots, because full replay is not viable in production.**

```ts
class SnapshotStore {
  async save(order: Order): Promise<void> {
    // Version-tag the snapshot with the event sequence it represents; validate on load.
    await this.db.query(`INSERT INTO snapshots (stream_id, seq, state, created_at)
                         VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4) ON CONFLICT (stream_id) DO UPDATE SET seq=$2, state=$3`,
      [order.id(), order.expectedVersion(), JSON.stringify(order.exportState()), nowIso]);
  }
  async load(streamId: string): Promise<{ seq: number; events: OrderEvent[] }> {
    const snap = await this.maybeLoadSnapshot(streamId);
    const events = await this.store.read(streamId, snap?.seq ?? 0);   // replay the tail only
    return { seq: snap?.seq ?? 0, events };
  }
}
// Rules: snapshot every N events (e.g. 100) or every T seconds; snapshots must include the seq;
// never trust a snapshot's contents without its seq (a stale snapshot with a wrong seq = corruption);
// validate a checksum; be ready to delete all snapshots (they are a cache, never authoritative).
```

**The decision framework — when should you event source?**

**Use it when** (at least two, ideally three):
1. **You have a genuine, business-critical need for the history** — audit, dispute, replay, regulatory reconstruction, "what happened to my order".
2. **Your domain is event-shaped**: state transitions are meaningful facts (finance, trading, insurance, logistics, healthcare, games, workflow engines).
3. **Temporal queries or counterfactuals** are required ("portfolio value on any past date", "replay with a modified fee rule to see what would have happened").
4. **You must support third parties extending the model** — event sourcing plus plugins/micro-frontends is the natural extension mechanism.
5. **You have the engineering capacity** to build and maintain projections, snapshots, upcasters, and replay tooling.

**Do not use it when:**
- The domain is CRUD with no meaningful history requirement.
- Analysts and support staff need ad-hoc SQL and you are not prepared to serve them a warehouse/dbt pipeline on top of projections.
- Your team will not maintain upcasters. This is the #1 cause of failure.
- Erasure requirements conflict with the immutable log and you have no policy.
- You are early and the domain model is still changing. **This is the strongest argument against**: event schemas encode today's understanding of the domain, and if today's understanding is wrong, you have encoded the wrong thing forever.

**Trade-offs in one sentence.** *Event sourcing buys perfect history, replay, and temporal queries at the cost of immutable forever-schemas, mandatory upcasting, projection infrastructure, lost ad-hoc querying, and a real conflict with erasure requirements.*

**Production considerations.**

- **Event immutability is a discipline, not a permission.** Deleting or updating an event is data loss. Put a DB trigger or permissions rule that makes `UPDATE`/`DELETE` on the event store impossible for the application role. Teams really do run `UPDATE event_store` in production.
- **Determinism**: handlers must be pure functions of events. No `Date.now()`, no `Math.random()`, no I/O inside `apply`. Inject a clock for anything time-derived, or store the timestamp in the event.
- **Snapshot validation**: checksum them; detect corruption by replaying and comparing.
- **Replay is a production operation**, not a dev tool. It needs progress reporting, rate limiting (do not melt the database), and the ability to abort.
- **Concurrency**: append-with-expected-version; on conflict, reload and retry with the user's re-applied intent (or reject and ask the user).
- **Privacy**: never put secrets/PII you will need to delete directly in events. Store an id + encrypted blob with a deletable key (crypto-shredding), or accept the risk consciously.
- **Storage**: events grow forever; partitioning by aggregate id or by time; plan retention for the log separately from projections.
- **Schema registry**: version events, document semantics, and enforce compatibility in CI. EventStorming sessions (the DDD workshop) are worth knowing by name.

**Common mistakes.**
1. No upcaster chain from day one — then year two is a rewrite.
2. Non-deterministic handlers; replay produces different state.
3. Deleting the projection and replaying during an incident, discovering the replay does not reproduce the current state (because someone mutated data directly). Prevention: projections are rebuildable by construction; never hand-edit them.
4. Putting side effects in the event handler (sending emails during replay → customers get 500 emails).
5. Underestimating the reporting/analytics requirement.
6. Treating events as a log you can edit.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you change the meaning of an old event?"* — never; you add an upcaster and keep the old event untouched. Show the chain.
- *"How do you handle a bug in a projection?"* — fix the handler and rebuild from offset 0 into a new projection, verify, swap. No data migration.
- *"What breaks when you replay?"* — non-deterministic code, external side effects, and changed business rules applied to history (which is a feature if you want counterfactuals and a bug if you want historical accuracy — this is the sharpest question here).
- *"Why not just an audit log?"* — because in an audit log the log is not the source of truth; state is, so the two can disagree and you have no recovery path.

---

### 11.3.9 Serverless architecture: constraints and the function-per-request trap

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition.** Run your application as small, stateless, short-lived units ("functions") invoked by events, with all state and durable coordination in managed services. The *operational* model (you manage the code; the platform manages the infrastructure) is the actual essence; "no servers" is marketing.

**The four real constraints — know these and the design follows.**

1. **Cold starts and connection reuse.** A cold start means: allocate a sandbox, download code, initialise the runtime, run your module-level code. Your module-level code (a DB pool, an HTTP agent, a big require) runs on *every* invocation in some runtimes and *occasionally* in others. Mitigations: minimise module-level work, keep the package small, use provisioned concurrency, keep the handler at the top level of the file, reuse clients via module scope (or a global, which survives warm invocations).
2. **Execution timeouts** (15 min hard cap on major platforms; far less for your HTTP timeout). This is the hard boundary on any long-running work.
3. **Concurrency and burst limits.** Thousands of concurrent invocations; you will hit account concurrency limits and per-function limits. This means **every connection pool must be sized for the worst-case concurrent invocation count**, and a DB will see a thundering herd.
4. **State must be external.** No in-memory state survives reliably; no sticky sessions; no local disk. Everything durable in DynamoDB/S3/Postgres/etc. Ephemeral `/tmp` may exist but is not durable and is not shared.

**The function-per-request trap.** Writing one function per HTTP route, each importing a shared "utils" that opens connections, means: cold start multiplied by route count, no shared connection reuse, duplicated initialisation, and a request path that cannot hold anything in memory. You end up with a stateless-at-all-costs design that pays latency on every request.

**The three serverless architectures, and which trap each avoids.**

```ts
// (A) FUNCTION PER REQUEST — the anti-pattern. Everything cold, nothing shared.
exports.createOrder = async (event: APIGatewayEvent) => {
  const pool = new Pool({ connectionString: process.env.DB });   // (1) new pool PER INVOCATION
  const user = await jwt.verify(event.headers.Authorization);      // (2) re-verified every time
  const rows = await pool.query('SELECT …');                       // (3) N+1 into the DB
  await pool.end();
  return { statusCode: 201, body: JSON.stringify(await buildOrder(rows)) };
};
// Problems: cold start × routes; a new pool per invocation; no connection reuse; no batching;
// no way to keep a warm cache; impossible to test the composition once.

// (B) THIN ADAPTER + CORE — the shape most teams should adopt.
// The function is a translation layer only. The core is a normal, testable, reusable module.
src/
  handlers/createOrder.ts     // 15 lines: parse, call core, map errors to HTTP
  core/placeOrder.ts          // pure-ish: ports in, result out. Fully unit tested.
  adapters/pgRepo.ts          // pooled, reused via module scope, sized correctly
const pool = new Pool({ connectionString: env.DB_URL, max: env.POOL_MAX });  // module scope
exports.handler = async (event: APIGatewayEvent) => {
  const cmd = parse(event);
  const result = await placeOrder(cmd, { orders: new PgOrderRepository(pool),
                                        payments: new StripeGateway(stripe),
                                        clock: new SystemClock() });
  return toHttp(result);
};

// (C) CHUNKED / BATCHED WORK — for the work that is embarrassingly parallel or large.
// DynamoDB/Kinesis streams, SQS batch (up to 10 messages), S3 batch, Step Functions for
// orchestration with retries and state. Costs: idempotency per message, partial batch handling,
// and DLQ semantics per message.
```

**Cold start anatomy and the optimisations that actually matter.**

```ts
// 1. TOP-LEVEL handler export (not a closure created per invocation) — enables the runtime to
//    snapshot the initialisation.
exports.handler = async (event) => handle(event);

// 2. Module-scope clients, created once per warm container:
const pg = new Pool({ /* … */, max: 10 });            // reuse, do NOT create per invocation
const httpAgent = new https.Agent({ keepAlive: true, maxSockets: 50 });

// 3. Lazy + cached for expensive things you do not always need (the model, a big table):
let model: Promise<Model> | undefined;
const getModel = () => (model ??= loadModel());      // memoised; only loads when first needed

// 4. Avoid top-level work you do not need: don't compile the whole rules engine at import.
```

**Concurrency model: the under-appreciated constraint.** With 1,000 concurrent invocations of one function and `max: 10` per pool, you have 10,000 connections against a database with a 200-connection limit. Real answers:
- **Reduce concurrency** (reserved concurrency per function, SQS with a low `maxReceiveCount`/visibility, or a serialising queue).
- **Use a proxy/pooler** (RDS Proxy, PgBouncer transaction pooling).
- **Batch** (SQS batch, DynamoDB batch writes) so one invocation does 10 items.
- **Sharded connection pools** or a global rate limiter on DB connections.
- **Front the function with a queue** so concurrency is bounded by the queue's visibility and consumer settings rather than by incoming traffic.

**Connection pool sizing rule.** `pool_max ≈ database_max_connections × (fraction for this function)` and each function that touches the DB counts once. Instrument connection counts.

**When serverless is the right choice.**

- **Event-driven, spiky, or unpredictable load.** Batch jobs, scheduled jobs, file processing, queue consumers, webhooks, API backends with extreme traffic variability.
- **Short-lived, stateless tasks.** Thumbnail generation, PDF rendering, image transforms, format conversion.
- **Per-request cost is low and traffic is intermittent** (idle cost dominates in the other direction).
- **You do not want to operate infrastructure** and the platform's constraints fit your problem.
- **Glue between managed services** (DynamoDB stream → Lambda → SNS).

**When serverless is the wrong choice (say these plainly).**

- **Long-running work** beyond the timeout (batch ETL, big reports, ML training). Use a job queue with a long-running worker.
- **Anything needing a persistent, hot, in-process cache or a long-lived connection** where cold starts dominate.
- **High sustained, predictable load.** You pay per invocation, runtimes are slower for pure compute, and cold-start-sensitive code will suffer. Dedicated compute is often cheaper and faster.
- **Sub-10ms p99 latency requirements** — impossible with cold starts.
- **WebSockets/long-lived connections**, or heavy per-connection state (API Gateway now supports some of this, but it is not free).
- **Complex, multi-step transactional workflows** where saga state must be durable and queryable.
- **Environments where the platform's networking/security model conflicts with your compliance requirements.**
- **Cost opacity at high volume.** Lambda pricing is famously hard to predict; "the bill went up 10× because of downstream retries" is a real story.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* no server management, elastic scaling, per-invocation cost, fine-grained deploys, and pay-for-idle near zero for spiky workloads.
*Cost:* cold starts, execution ceilings, external state, no in-process sharing, harder local debugging, unfamiliar tooling, tighter networking, and cost predictability problems. And you trade away the ability to run a long-lived process with a warm cache — which is exactly what a lot of software relies on.

**Production considerations.**

- **Idempotency on every trigger source.** SQS delivers at-least-once; S3 events repeat; API Gateway retries. Dedupe by message id.
- **Partial batch failures**: handle each record in the batch independently and return `batchItemFailures`; do not fail the whole batch for one poison message.
- **Graceful shutdown** / not holding the invocation past the response.
- **Env vars are config, not secrets.** Use a secrets manager; do not use env vars for anything you rotate.
- **Bundle size and dependencies** dominate cold start. Avoid huge dependency trees; prefer the platform SDK over a full AWS SDK client if the runtime provides one.
- **Structured logging to stdout** with correlation ids, plus traces; the platform collects them.
- **Concurrency limits** must be set deliberately (reserved concurrency) to protect downstream dependencies — this is the bulkhead pattern in the serverless world.
- **Local development** needs a simulator or a real dev account; the drift between local and deployed behaviour is a genuine tax (test with real events locally where possible).

**Common mistakes.**
1. New connection pool per invocation → connection exhaustion at exactly the moment traffic spikes.
2. Function per route with a shared "utils" that opens everything.
3. Retrying an SQS message 5 times with no DLQ and no idempotency.
4. Failing an entire batch because one record failed.
5. No concurrency limit on a function that hammers a fragile dependency.
6. Long-running work in a function.
7. Assuming the function is stateless-and-therefore-fast: cold start is not free.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"How do you avoid the N+1 in a Lambda?"* — batch the DB access, or move the join into a projection; also warm the pool by reusing module scope and by pre-warming.
- *"Your function has a 3-second p99. Where does it go?"* — cold start (measure with a warm/cold metric), then init work, then downstream, then the response payload size; add `Init Duration` to the platform's telemetry.
- *"How do you test serverless code?"* — the handlers are thin translators (pure unit tests); the core is tested normally; plus a handful of deployed smoke tests against real events.
- *"Lambda or containers?"* — cold start, duration, and ecosystem vs. runtime flexibility, long-running support, and cost predictability at sustained load.

---

### 11.3.10 BFF (backend for frontend)

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition.** A Backend For Frontend is a service layer **per client channel** (web, mobile, TV, watch) that aggregates, reshapes, and adapts backend APIs into exactly the payload and endpoint shape that client needs. It is owned by the client team.

**The problem it solves.** Three things, all of which come from the "microservices + N clients" world:

1. **API aggregation / fan-out.** A product page needs product + price + inventory + reviews + recommendations. The client would make 5 calls, each with latency, each needing its own error handling, each exposing the internal API shape. A BFF makes 1 call.
2. **Client-specific payload shaping.** A web page needs 40 fields; a mobile app needs 8 for the same screen; a smart TV needs 4 in a different shape. One "universal" API forces every client to over-fetch and then ignore fields.
3. **Encapsulation of service topology.** When the pricing service is split in two, only the BFF changes — clients never learn about internal service decomposition.

**A real BFF.**

```ts
// The BFF is a thin, client-shaped orchestrator. It contains NO domain rules.
// Ownership: the web team owns web-BFF. The mobile team owns mobile-BFF.
export function createProductPageBFF(deps: {
  catalog: CatalogApi; pricing: PricingApi; inventory: InventoryApi;
  reviews: ReviewsApi; http: HttpClient; circuit: CircuitBreaker; cache: Cache;
}) {
  return {
    async getProductPage(req: ProductPageRequest): Promise<ProductPageView> {
      const sku = parseSku(req.sku);                       // validate at the edge
      const cacheKey = `pp:${sku}:${req.locale}:${req.currency}`;

      // 1. CACHE the aggregate: the page is read-mostly and expensive to compute.
      const hit = deps.cache.get<ProductPageView>(cacheKey);
      if (hit && !isStale(hit)) return hit;

      // 2. FAN OUT in parallel, each behind a circuit breaker + timeout, each degrading independently.
      const [product, price, stock, reviews] = await Promise.all([
        deps.circuit.call('catalog',   () => withTimeout(deps.catalog.get(sku), 150),  () => null),
        deps.circuit.call('pricing',   () => withTimeout(deps.pricing.quote(sku, req.currency), 120), () => null),
        deps.circuit.call('inventory', () => withTimeout(deps.inventory.level(sku), 120),       () => null),
        deps.circuit.call('reviews',   () => withTimeout(deps.reviews.summary(sku), 250),       () => ({ average: null, count: 0 })),
      ]);
      // 3. DEGRADE per-field. This is the heart of a BFF: each field has its own fallback so one
      //    broken dependency cannot blank the page.
      if (!product) throw new UpstreamUnavailable('catalog');      // product is non-negotiable → error
      const view: ProductPageView = {
        sku, name: product.name, description: product.description, images: product.images,
        price: price ? { amount: price.amount, currency: price.currency, was: price.wasPrice }
                     : { unavailable: true },                                       // honest degradation
        availability: stock ? { inStock: stock.inStock, quantity: stock.quantity } : { unknown: true },
        rating: reviews.average, reviewCount: reviews.count,
        recommendations: null,                                                   // filled below, optional
        // A "stale price" is a legal, correct answer. A fabricated price is not.
      };
      deps.cache.set(cacheKey, view, { ttlSec: 60 });
      return view;
    },
  };
}
```

**The four design rules for a BFF that stay clean.**

1. **No business rules.** A BFF composes and reshapes; it does not decide prices, eligibility, or entitlements. Business rules live in the owning services. (The one exception: *presentation* rules — currency formatting, label selection, sort order for this channel — which are legitimately the BFF's job.)
2. **It is per channel, not per screen.** A BFF per screen produces dozens of tiny deployables with duplicated logic. A BFF per channel (web / mobile) produces 2–3 things.
3. **It may cache aggressively; the backing services may not know about it.** The BFF is the right place for a short-TTL aggregate cache and for request coalescing (join concurrent identical requests — `single-flight`).
4. **It owns the error-to-UI mapping.** An upstream 500 becomes a field-level "unavailable" plus a metric, not a blank page.

```ts
// Request coalescing (single-flight): 40 concurrent requests for the same SKU trigger ONE upstream call.
function singleFlight<K, V>(fn: (k: K) => Promise<V>): (k: K) => Promise<V> {
  const inflight = new Map<K, Promise<V>>();
  return (k) => {
    const existing = inflight.get(k);
    if (existing) return existing;
    const p = fn(k).finally(() => inflight.delete(k));   // always delete, even on rejection
    inflight.set(k, p);
    return p;
  };
}
// Also: request deadlines. Pass the remaining budget down so slow upstreams are abandoned early.
const budget = deadlineFrom(req, { totalMs: 1500 });
await withTimeout(deps.pricing.quote(sku), budget.remaining('pricing'));
```

**When a BFF is worth it (and when it is one service too many).**

**Worth it:**
- Multiple client channels with genuinely different payload needs (web + mobile + a partner API).
- Page-level aggregation across 3+ services (otherwise client fan-out burns mobile battery and latency budget).
- A high fan-out of mobile clients where every request round trip matters.
- Client teams that need to iterate independently of the backend service graph.
- When you want to expose internal services to third parties safely (a BFF is a natural API gateway-with-logic).

**Not worth it:**
- One client, one channel, and one page's worth of data from one or two services. You have added a network hop and a deployment.
- The backends are already shaped for the UI (well-designed APIs), or there is a proper API gateway doing aggregation.
- If you end up with 12 BFFs and duplicated logic in each — the failure mode, and the reason "one per channel" is the rule.

**Trade-offs.** *Gain:* fewer round trips, smaller payloads, per-channel agility, topology encapsulation, clean degradation, and independent client-team deploys.
*Cost:* **one more network hop** (usually fine, sometimes on the critical path), **duplicated logic across BFFs** (mitigate with shared generated clients, not shared logic), **another thing to deploy and monitor**, and a temptation to put business logic in it, which duplicates the services' rules and creates two sources of truth.

**Production considerations.**

- **Latency budget per page.** If the page must render in 1500ms and there are four upstream calls, each needs a budget with 20–30% slack; fan out in parallel, never sequentially.
- **Per-field circuit breakers + fallbacks** — the degradation story is the whole value proposition.
- **Cache the aggregate** with a short TTL, and make the TTL per-field (prices 10s, reviews 5min, catalog 30min) — a common trick is to build the response from separately cached pieces.
- **Never fabricate data.** `price: unavailable` is honest; `price: 0` is a business incident.
- **Metrics per upstream per BFF:** latency, error rate, circuit state, fallback rate, cache hit rate. The fallback rate is your product-health signal.
- **Security:** the BFF is the public surface. Authorise here (fine-grained, channel-specific) as well as in the services (coarse). The BFF must never trust the client-supplied user id.
- **Do not duplicate business logic for "performance".** If the BFF recalculates a price, you now have two price algorithms.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Business rules creeping into the BFF (discount logic, eligibility).
2. A BFF per screen.
3. Sequential fan-out ("I need the product before I know which reviews to fetch") — usually avoidable.
4. No per-field fallback, so one slow service blanks the page.
5. Duplicated logic across web and mobile BFFs, then the two disagree.
6. Inventing data on failure.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"BFF or API gateway?"* — a gateway does generic routing/auth/rate limiting; a BFF does client-shaped aggregation. They compose (gateway in front, BFF behind).
- *"How do you handle a slow dependency?"* — per-upstream timeout + circuit breaker + per-field fallback + a page-level degraded state; and metric the fallback rate.
- *"How do you stop the BFF becoming a God service?"* — one per channel, no business rules, per-field concerns only; move domain work back.

---

### 11.3.11 Service mesh awareness

**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

**Definition.** A service mesh is a dedicated infrastructure layer (data plane + control plane) that handles service-to-service communication: mutual TLS, retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, rate limiting, traffic splitting/shifting, telemetry, and authorisation policies — configured as configuration rather than embedded in code.

**The two planes, precisely.**

- **Data plane:** a sidecar proxy (Envoy, Linkerd2-proxy, Cilium) or a node-level/gateway proxy that sits on every request path and applies the policy. It is in the hot path of every request, which is why its performance and failure modes matter.
- **Control plane:** pushes configuration to the data plane (xDS), manages certificates, aggregates telemetry, and provides the API you configure policies through.

**What it gives you (this is the honest value list).**

1. **Uniform mTLS everywhere** with automated certificate rotation. This is the single most-cited real benefit: mTLS between every service, with identity-based authorisation, without touching application code.
2. **Retries, timeouts, circuit breaking, outlier detection, rate limits as declarative config** — consistent across all services, including services written in languages you would not want to implement this in.
3. **Traffic management:** canary by percentage, header-based routing, mirroring (shadow traffic!), fault injection (inject delays/errors between two services on purpose). Canary without redeploys is a real, hard-won capability.
4. **Uniform telemetry:** consistent `traceparent` propagation, RED metrics (rate, errors, duration) per hop, and topology graphs that come for free.
5. **Language/framework independence** for cross-cutting concerns — a Node service and a Go service get identical retry behaviour.
6. **Authorisation moved to infrastructure:** identity-based policies (`only service A may call B`), reducing the need for bespoke auth code.

**The costs and the reasons teams try it and give up (all of these are real).**

1. **Latency and resource cost.** Two hops per request (source sidecar → destination sidecar), extra CPU, extra memory per pod. For small packets the overhead is a real percentage of your p99; teams measure 1–3ms added p99 and extra node memory.
2. **The sidecar is a deployment and upgrade tax.** You now manage a proxy in every workload: resource limits, sidecar injection, version skew, readiness (a sidecar can delay or break startup even when the app is fine), and debugging "is this my app or my proxy?".
3. **A new distributed system that can be the single point of failure.** If the control plane is unreachable, existing proxies keep working with the last config (this is why sidecars do not need the control plane on the request path — a well-designed mesh does not put the CP in the data path). This is a great interview point: the control plane must be **out of the data path**.
4. **Debugging becomes two-layered.** A failure may be in the app, the proxy, the mTLS handshake, or the config. You need to know how to read proxy access logs and metrics (`istio_requests_total`, outlier detection counters, config dump).
5. **Configuration sprawl.** Hundreds of `VirtualService`/`DestinationRule`/retry policies, and no one who knows which are still needed. Merging mesh config with app config is a known mess; the recommendation is a clear ownership split.
6. **You have moved your resilience logic out of code, which means it is harder to test and harder to reason about locally.** A retry configured in the mesh cannot be exercised in a unit test, and local development no longer reproduces production behaviour.
7. **The "does not apply in-process" problem.** A mesh cannot protect a function call inside a process. Some resilience concerns are better done in code (idempotency, fallback logic, business degradation), so you end up with *both*, and now the retry counts can multiply (mesh retry × client retry).
8. **Cost of the control plane and the team** to run it. Mesh is an infrastructure project with a real operational learning curve.

**When to adopt a mesh, and when not to.**

**Adopt when:** you have many services (typically >10–15, in some organisations >30); uniform mTLS and identity is a compliance requirement; you need traffic management (canary/header routing/mirroring) at scale; you have multiple languages and want consistent cross-cutting behaviour; and — critically — **you have a platform team** to own it.

**Do not adopt when:** you have few services; your team has no capacity to run a control plane; your real problem is application design (a mesh will not fix a God service or a chatty call graph); you need request-level programmability that config cannot express (in which case code-level resilience is clearer); or you are on a platform where the mesh adds cost without compliance value.

**Alternatives to know (for a balanced answer).** Sidecar-less eBPF/dataplane approaches (Cilium) which reduce the hop count; a lighter managed service-mesh product; **library-based mesh** (service-to-service SDKs: Istio's "gRPC mesh" proposal, or Envoy-based libraries embedded in the process — no sidecar, language-specific); or simply doing resilience in code (which is what most small teams should do, and what this phase's resilience section assumes).

**What an interviewer wants to hear.** Not a list of features; the *trade-off*: the mesh centralises cross-cutting communication concerns in infrastructure and gets uniform mTLS/telemetry/traffic management cheaply at scale, at the cost of latency, operational complexity, reduced programmability, and a new thing that can be down at 3am. And that **some** concerns must stay in code — idempotency, fallback semantics, and domain degradation are application decisions.

**Production considerations.**

- Sidecar resources (CPU/memory limits) must be set explicitly; an unbounded sidecar is a noisy-neighbour problem.
- **Readiness/liveness**: a pod is not ready until the sidecar has upstream endpoints; configure `holdApplicationUntilProxyStarts`.
- **Outlier detection and connection draining** during deploys, or you will get 502s on every rolling deploy.
- **Retry amplification**: mesh retries × client retries × LB retries. Cap total attempts; only retry idempotent methods; never retry non-idempotent writes at the mesh level.
- **mTLS mode**: `PERMISSIVE` for migration, `STRICT` once all workloads are injected. Never leave it permissive by accident.
- **Config management**: GitOps for mesh config with change review and progressive rollout — mesh config changes are production changes.
- **Observe the mesh**: a proxy that is itself failing produces errors that look like app errors. Dashboards must separate proxy-originated 5xx from app 5xx.
- **Know the escape hatch:** if the mesh is degraded, can you deliver requests without it? Sidecars with cached config can; a CP-outage-tolerant design matters.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Adopting a mesh with 6 services and one platform engineer.
2. Retrying in both the mesh and the client.
3. Retrying POSTs in the mesh.
4. Forgetting to configure `holdApplicationUntilProxyStarts` and wondering why pods get no traffic.
5. Mesh config edited by hand in the console with no review.
6. Treating the mesh as a security boundary for authorisation of *business* permissions (it authorises *service identity*, not "can this user refund").

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Does the control plane sit in the request path?"* — it must not; sidecars hold the last-known-good config and continue when the CP is down.
- *"Where should retries live, in the mesh or in the app?"* — coarse, uniform, infrastructure-level behaviour in the mesh; application-specific semantics (idempotency, which errors are retryable, fallback payloads) in the app; and never double-retry.
- *"What breaks when the mesh is down?"* — if the sidecar needs the CP to serve, everything is down. Design for cached config.

---

### 11.3.12 Peer-to-peer architectures awareness

**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

**Definition.** Systems where nodes are peers with no central authority: they discover each other, coordinate, and replicate state directly. The two families that matter are **content-addressable / decentralised data** (blockchains, IPFS, Dynamo-style tables) and **content-addressable systems more broadly** (Fluentd, Bazel remote caching, Merkle trees, Merkle-DAGs, CRDT libraries).

**Two families, distinguished by what is decentralised.**

**A. Coordination/P2P messaging and state.** Nodes gossip; state is eventually consistent; conflicts are resolved by rules (last-write-wins, vector clocks, CRDTs).
- **Gossip protocol:** each node periodically exchanges state with a few random peers; information spreads in O(log n) rounds. Used by Cassandra, HashiCorp's memberlist, service discovery.
- **CRDTs (Conflict-free Replicated Data Types):** data structures whose merge is commutative, associative, and idempotent, so replicas converge without coordination. Types you should know: G-Counter (grow-only counter), PN-Counter, G-Set, OR-Set (observed-remove), LWW-Register, RGA/Logoot for sequences.
- **Vector clocks / hybrid logical clocks:** per-replica versions to detect whether two writes are concurrent (conflict) or ordered (no conflict). Used by Dynamo.
- **Timestamps/ordering problem:** there is no global clock. Solutions: physical clocks (unreliable), vector clocks (accurate, grows), hybrid logical clocks (drift-bounded, compact), Lamport clocks (causal order only).

**B. Consensus and shared ledgers.**
- **The Byzantine Generals Problem:** nodes may be malicious or faulty, so consensus must tolerate *arbitrary* faults, not just crashes. Practical Byzantine fault tolerance (pBFT) and its descendants.
- **Permissioned vs permissionless:** whether nodes are known/allowed (permissioned: BFT with a known set, high throughput) or open (permissionless: probabilistic finality via Nakamoto consensus, open membership).
- **Probabilistic vs absolute finality:** Bitcoin/Bitcoin-like chains give high-probability finality with a fork risk; BFT systems give deterministic finality with a known validator set.
- **The blockchain trilemma:** decentralisation, security, and scalability — at most two, with Layer 2/off-chain channels/rollups as the practical escape.
- **State:** replicated, append-only, consensus-ordered, with each node verifying every block. Cost: throughput limited by the slowest node, storage growth, and privacy problems.

```ts
// CRDT demo: an OR-Set (observed-remove set) with tombstones, so add and remove are both commutable.
type Tag = { actor: string; counter: number; value: string };
interface OrSetState { adds: Map<string, Tag[]>; removes: Map<string, Set<Tag>> }   // per-element tombstones

class OrSet {
  constructor(private readonly state: OrSetState, private readonly actor: string, private ctr = 0) {}
  add(value: string): OrSet {                                     // O-Set add: unique tag
    const tag = { actor: this.actor, counter: ++this.ctr, value };
    const list = this.state.adds.get(value) ?? [];
    list.push(tag);
    this.state.adds.set(value, list);
    return this;
  }
  remove(value: string): OrSet {                                  // O-Set remove: record observed tags
    const observed = this.state.adds.get(value) ?? [];
    this.state.removes.set(value, new Set([...(this.state.removes.get(value) ?? []), ...observed]));
    return this;
  }
  merge(other: OrSetState): OrSet {                               // UNION => commutes, associates, idempotents
    const s: OrSetState = { adds: new Map(), removes: new Map() };
    for (const [v, tags] of [...this.state.adds, ...other.adds]) {
      s.adds.set(v, dedupe([...(s.adds.get(v) ?? []), ...tags]));
    }
    for (const [v, set] of [...this.state.removes, ...other.removes]) {
      s.removes.set(v, new Set([...(s.removes.get(v) ?? []), ...set]));
    }
    return new OrSet(s, this.actor, Math.max(this.ctr, other.ctr));
  }
  contains(value: string): boolean {                              // has an add with no matching tombstone
    const adds = this.state.adds.get(value) ?? [];
    const rems = this.state.removes.get(value) ?? new Set();
    return adds.some(t => !rems.has(t));
  }
  toArray(): string[] { return [...this.state.adds.keys()].filter(v => this.contains(v)); }
}
// Property that makes CRDTs work: any replica's merge order reaches the same state.
// The cost: tombstones and metadata GROW without bound (garbage collection requires knowing when
// all replicas have seen a tombstone — a coordination problem CRDTs deliberately avoid, hence
// causal stability tracking).
```

```ts
// Vector clock: detect concurrent writes without a central coordinator.
type VC = Record<string, number>;
const tick  = (vc: VC, me: string): VC => ({ ...vc, [me]: (vc[me] ?? 0) + 1 });
const merge = (a: VC, b: VC): VC => { const out: VC = { ...a };
  for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(b)) out[k] = Math.max(out[k] ?? 0, v); return out; };
type Relation = 'equal' | 'before' | 'after' | 'concurrent';
function compare(a: VC, b: VC): Relation {
  let aGreater = false, bGreater = false;
  for (const k of new Set([...Object.keys(a), ...Object.keys(b)])) {
    const av = a[k] ?? 0, bv = b[k] ?? 0;
    if (av > bv) aGreater = true; if (bv > av) bGreater = true;
  }
  if (!aGreater && !bGreater) return 'equal';
  if (aGreater && !bGreater) return 'after';
  if (bGreater && !aGreater) return 'before';
  return 'concurrent';                       // <-- CONFLICT: the coordinator (or the user) must resolve
}
// The classic Dynamo behaviour: concurrent writes go back to the client for resolution. Or, in a
// shop, a shopping cart: LWW-Register per field so the cart converges without user interaction.
```

**Where this actually shows up in application engineering (the part that makes this answer strong).**

- **Local-first / offline-capable apps** (Linear-style, Figma-style, Google Docs-style) rely on CRDTs or OT for concurrent editing and sync.
- **Collaborative editing:** operational transformation (OT, centralised server, Google Docs) vs CRDTs (decentralised, Yjs/Automerge). CRDTs give offline-first and peer-to-peer merge at the cost of metadata and subtle bugs.
- **Databases with quorum reads/writes** (Dynamo, Cassandra, Riak, FoundationDB): N, W, R with `W + R > N` for consistency, and hinted handoff/read repair for the inconsistency window. This is the practical P2P database model and it is *not* blockchain.
- **Service discovery and membership** via gossip (Consul, etcd membership, Kubernetes headless services + gossip).
- **Build systems and caches** using content-addressable storage and CAS (Bazel remote cache, Nix, Docker registry content digests, Git object model).
- **Supply chain and provenance**: Merkle trees and signed attestations (Sigstore, in-toto), which are content-addressed and verifiable without a central authority.
- **Decentralised identity, payments, and settlement** — blockchain's actual niche, plus its costs.

**Trade-offs, since this section is mostly awareness but must be defensible.**

| Property | Centralised | P2P / CRDT | Blockchain |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Consistency | Strong, immediate | Eventual, converges | Deterministic consensus |
| Latency | Lowest | Varies (offline is free) | Highest (consensus + block time) |
| Throughput | Highest | High | Low relative to central systems |
| Availability | Depends on the centre | High (works offline) | High, if the chain runs |
| Trust | Centralised trust | No single authority | Consensus over a validator set |
| Conflicts | Impossible | Handled by CRDT rules or app resolution | Ordered by consensus |
| Operational cost | Low for users | Peer management, NAT, discovery | Validator cost, chain bloat, irreversibility |
| Privacy | Centralised copies | Data on every peer | Public by default (unless zero-knowledge) |

**Why most applications should not go decentralised:** you lose strong consistency, you gain complexity, and you gain nothing unless you have a specific requirement — no trusted centre, offline-first operation, or multi-party trust.

**Production considerations.**

- **CRDT metadata growth** and garbage collection need causal-stability tracking; ignoring this produces unbounded storage.
- **P2P and NAT/traversal** (relays, hole punching, STUN/TURN) are a real operational concern for consumer-facing P2P.
- **Consensus systems need careful key management and rotation**; irreversibility means a bad key is unrecoverable.
- **Blockchain finality semantics must be understood by the product**: "the payment is submitted" vs "the payment is final" is a user-facing distinction.
- **Determinism**: anything that depends on wall-clock time or iteration order breaks replay in replicated systems.
- **Byzantine faults in BFT:** you can tolerate f faults with 3f+1 replicas, and you pay latency for it.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Confusing Dynamo-style quorum replication (not blockchain) with a ledger.
2. Assuming a CRDT is free — metadata, growth, and subtle semantics bugs are the price.
3. Choosing a blockchain where an audit log and a signature would do.
4. Ignoring conflict *semantics*: CRDTs converge the data structure; they cannot resolve "which of these two business decisions is correct".
5. Assuming availability without dealing with peer discovery and connectivity.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"Where have you needed eventual consistency and how did you resolve conflicts?"* — CRDTs (converge the structure), version vectors (detect conflicts), quorum, or LWW with explicit precedence rules. Give a concrete one.
- *"What is the difference between eventual consistency and consensus?"* — eventual consistency converges without agreement on order; consensus agrees on a total order.
- *"Do you need a blockchain here?"* — almost never; say so with a reason (an audit log plus signatures achieves the verifiable-history requirement at a fraction of the cost).

---

### 11.3.13 Choosing a style: the decision framework and how to defend it

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

This is the single most-likely-to-be-asked artifact in Phase 11. You need the framework, the table, and the arguments.

**The framework — six questions, in order. Ask them before naming a style.**

```
1. What are the invariants, and do they span more than one store?
   → If yes: transaction boundaries matter. Lean toward fewer processes (monolith).
2. What is the read:write ratio, and do reads need shapes different from what writes produce?
   → If yes and measurably expensive: consider a read model (CQRS level 2).
3. What must scale independently, and can you MEASURE it?
   → If you can name a component with a disproportionate load profile: consider extracting it.
4. What changes at what rate, and what must deploy independently?
   → If components have order-of-magnitude-different change rates: consider services.
5. How many teams, and how do they coordinate today?
   → <5 teams: modular monolith. >5-8 with independent roadmaps: consider services.
6. What are the failure/consistency requirements, and what is your operational capacity?
   → High transactional consistency + low ops capacity: monolith. Isolated blast radius +
     strong ops capacity: services.
```

**The decision matrix.** This is the table to memorise; every row is defensible.

| Situation | Recommended style | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New product, requirements still moving, <20 engineers | **Modular monolith** (feature-first, ports where useful) | Cheapest to change; you do not yet know the domains; no distribution tax |
| Established monolith, CRUD, low-moderate traffic | **Modular monolith**, maybe extract nothing | No trigger has fired |
| One component is 60% of load and 5% of value | **Extract that component** (Strangler Fig) | Measured, targeted; costs are contained |
| Complex domain (finance/insurance/healthcare) with heavy invariants | **Modular monolith + rich domain, hexagonal inside modules; extract only the parts with independent scale/team needs** | The complexity is in the *model*, not in the process count |
| >8 teams, independent roadmaps, blocked deploys | **Microservices along bounded contexts** | Independent deployment is the actual benefit |
| Hard regulatory/data-isolation boundary | **Extract that boundary into a service** | This criterion overrides scale and team criteria |
| Heavy event fan-out, independent consumer scaling, replay needed | **Event-driven, probably inside a monolith first** | Replay + fan-out are the wins; broker inside a monolith is acceptable when you need replay |
| Reads massively dominate and are complex (dashboards, search) | **CQRS level 2 (separate read store, projection)** | Read/write shapes genuinely differ |
| Need full history, temporal queries, audit/regulatory replay | **Event sourcing for that aggregate only** | Scoped event sourcing is a legitimate, common choice |
| Spiky, event-driven, short tasks; no long-running processes | **Serverless** | Cost model and ops model fit |
| Long-running work, persistent connections, predictable high load | **Containers / dedicated compute** | Cold starts and timeouts do not fit |
| Multiple client channels, page-level fan-out | **BFF per channel** | Payload shaping and degradation |
| Many services, need uniform mTLS/telemetry/canaries, have a platform team | **Add a service mesh** | Cross-cutting communication concerns in infra |
| Offline-first or multi-writer collaboration | **Local-first + CRDTs for the conflicting parts; server for authority** | Only for the parts that genuinely need multi-writer |

**The synthesis answer, and the one to give if asked "so what would you actually do?"**

```
Start: a feature-first modular monolith with a thin shared kernel, ports at volatile boundaries,
      and a rich domain model where the complexity is.
Add:  a CQRS read projection for the specific read bottleneck you can measure.
Add:  in-process domain events; a broker only when you need replay or independent consumer scaling.
Extract: at most the one or two components with a measured scaling/deployment/team/isolation trigger.
Never: split because the requirements are unclear, because the code is messy, or because
       microservices are the fashionable answer.
```

**How to defend it — the eight arguments that land.**

1. **"Start with the monolith and split later" is a trap; start with the *modular* monolith."** The extraction cost is determined by the quality of the internal boundaries. If you have enforced module boundaries and data ownership, extraction is weeks; without them, it is months.

2. **"Splitting a system along business capabilities is the only defensible split."** Splitting by technical layer (an auth service, a validation service, a logging service) creates the distributed monolith: everything must deploy together anyway, and you have all the costs of distribution.

3. **"The real cost of microservices is the loss of a transaction and a debugger, not the network."** One ACID transaction spanning a change is worth more than most read-traffic arguments. Sagas are strictly weaker: partial states, compensation logic, reconciliation jobs, and a support burden.

4. **"Team size is the strongest predictor, and the rule of thumb is that a service needs 2+ teams' worth of ownership to be worth the operational cost."** Below one team per service you get all the costs and none of the benefit.

5. **"Read and write patterns are the second most common justification, and CQRS addresses it without microservices."** You can get a separate read model inside a monolith. Do that before you distribute.

6. **"Operational maturity is a hard prerequisite."** Distributed tracing, on-call, automated deployment with rollback, and a platform team. Without these, microservices convert application bugs into platform incidents.

7. **"Every service boundary you add creates a distributed systems problem you now own forever."** Backward compatibility, contract tests, idempotency, retries, timeouts, and schema evolution — per boundary. Count them.

8. **"You can reverse most of this; you cannot reverse a data model split."** Data ownership is the irreversible part. Code extraction can be undone; a split database with cross-service joins cannot.

**When microservices IS the right answer (state this with the same confidence, or you look dogmatic).**

- You have ≥8 teams with independent roadmaps blocked by a shared release train.
- You have a component with a 100× load asymmetry or a hard technical runtime requirement.
- You have a regulatory or contractual data-isolation requirement.
- You have a high-frequency-deploy component and a low-frequency one, and the coupling is genuinely costing you.
- You have an organisation that can and does operate distributed systems as a platform product.

**The failure modes of the decision process itself (a good closing answer).**

1. **Distribution as an identity signal.** "We are advanced, therefore microservices." This is the most common and most expensive.
2. **Service count as a maturity metric.** Twelve services and nobody can tell you the request flow.
3. **Splitting by org chart** before the domains are understood.
4. **Extracting the write path first** (11.3.5).
5. **Never deleting the old code.** Two systems forever.
6. **Distributed monolith.** All costs, no benefits.
7. **Ignoring the data.** Splitting code and then sharing the database.

**Common mistakes.**
1. Answering "it depends" with no criteria. Have the six questions ready.
2. Naming a style without a trigger metric.
3. Confusing deployment frequency with architecture.
4. Recommending microservices for a code-quality problem.
5. Forgetting to ask about operational maturity.

**Interviewer probes.**
- *"We have 12 engineers and a monolith that deploys twice a month. What do you recommend?"* — diagnose why deploys are rare (pipeline, test suite, coupling, risk). Then: feature-first modular monolith, faster pipeline. Microservices will not make the pipeline faster.
- *"Your startup has 3 services and 40 engineers. Is that right?"* — probably fine early, but check for a distributed monolith (shared DB, always deployed together) and for whether the boundaries match real business capabilities.
- *"Convince me you are right."* — have the counter-argument ready: "if the team triples and we get blocked by release coupling, I would extract the components with independent change rates and scale profiles, in that order, using a Strangler Fig, and I would re-evaluate this decision every quarter."

---

## 11.4 Low-Level Design Case Studies

> How to read this section: for each problem, the format is **Requirements → Constraints → API surface → Class/diagram description → State and invariants → The tricky part → Extension points → What the interviewer scores.** The walkthroughs after this section expand each one in full.

### 11.4.1 Parking lot: the canonical multi-resource reservation problem

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Requirements.**
- Given a parking lot with fixed numbered slots, support `park(vehicle)`, `unpark(vehicle)`, `findFreeSlots(count)`, `findVehiclesByColor(color)`, `findSlotsBySize(Size)`.
- Sizes: MOTORBIKE, COMPACT, LARGE. Free-slot search must respect vehicle size compatibility.
- Multiple floors with rows and slots per row. Nearest-first or cheapest-first selection.
- Fees by vehicle type, with time-based billing and a payment step.
- Tickets: issue on entry, settle on exit, with a grace period and a lost-ticket rule.

**Constraints.**
- Slots are **fixed capacity** (unlike a warehouse problem): cannot be split, cannot exceed slot size, cannot assign a slot larger than the vehicle.
- Vehicle-type → slot-size compatibility: MOTORBIKE and COMPACT can occupy any slot; LARGE needs a LARGE slot. (The classic problem statement has exactly this rule.)
- Entry must be **atomic**: two cars must not be assigned the same slot.
- Selection should be "first available by size match" by default, with strategies layered on top (nearest to entrance, cheapest, EV-only, accessible-only).

**API surface.**
```ts
interface ParkingLot {
  park(v: Vehicle): Ticket;                        // throws NoSlotAvailable / VehicleTooLarge
  unpark(ticket: Ticket): FeeReceipt;              // calculates fee; throws TicketNotOpen
  findFreeSlots(count: number, size?: SlotSize): readonly SlotRef[];
  findVehicleByTicket(t: Ticket): Vehicle | null;
  findSlotsBySize(size: SlotSize): readonly SlotRef[];
  findVehiclesByColor(color: Color): readonly Vehicle[];
  availability(): ReadonlyMap<SlotSize, number>;   // counts per size
}

class Ticket { constructor(readonly id: string, readonly slot: SlotRef, readonly issuedAt: Date) {} }
class Vehicle { constructor(readonly plate: string, readonly type: VehicleType, readonly color: Color) {}
                static from(input: VehicleInput): Vehicle { /* validate plate format, known type */ } }
class Slot { readonly ref: SlotRef;                 // { floor, row, number }
             private occupant: Vehicle | null;
             constructor(ref: SlotRef, readonly size: SlotSize, readonly accessible: boolean, readonly ev: boolean) {}
             get isFree() { return this.occupant === null; }
             get occupant() { return this.occupant; }
             canFit(v: Vehicle) { return this.size >= requiredSize(v.type); }
             occupy(v: Vehicle) { if (!this.isFree) throw new SlotOccupied(this.ref);
                                   if (!this.canFit(v)) throw new VehicleTooLarge(v.type, this.size);
                                   this.occupant = v; }        // private set: the only mutation path
             vacate(): Vehicle { const v = this.occupant; if (!v) throw new SlotAlreadyFree(this.ref);
                                 this.occupant = null; return v; } }
```

**Diagram description (narrate this aloud in 90 seconds).**

```
                 ParkingLot  (1)
                    │  owns
                    ▼
                  Floor (1..n) ──────────────┐
                    │  owns rows              │
                    ▼                         │
              Row (1..m per floor)            │
                    │  owns slots             │
                    ▼                         │
              Slot ─────────── occupies ─────▶ Vehicle
                    ▲                          ▲
                    │  allocated by           │  issued as
                  ParkingSpotFinder           Ticket
                 (Strategy: FirstFit |         │
                  Nearest | Cheapest | EVOnly) └─ used at exit to
                    ▲                                compute the fee
              RateStrategy (per-type hourly rate, dynamic pricing)
```

**State and invariants.**

```
Slot state:      FREE ──occupy(v)──▶ OCCUPIED(v) ──vacate()──▶ FREE
Invariants:
  I1  At most one vehicle per slot (enforced by Slot.occupy, not by the caller).
  I2  A vehicle occupies only a slot whose size >= requiredSize(vehicle.type).
  I3  A ticket is unique; a ticket maps to exactly one occupied slot.
  I4  park() is atomic: find-slot and occupy-slot happen under one critical section / one DB tx.
  I5  unpark() transitions the slot to FREE before the fee is settled (never hold a slot on a payment failure,
      unless the design explicitly says a grace period holds it).
  I6  Every parked vehicle has exactly one open ticket; an open ticket has exactly one occupied slot.
```

**The tricky part: the allocation strategy, and atomicity.**
The naive `park` does `findFreeSlot()` then `occupy()`. Under concurrency, two threads find the same slot. The correct answer has two parts: (a) encapsulate the check-and-set inside `Slot.occupy` so the slot itself is the lock; (b) introduce an explicit **allocation strategy** so the *choice* of slot is swappable (11.1.10 Strategy) while the *atomicity* is guaranteed by the slot.

```ts
interface SlotAllocator { allocate(vehicle: Vehicle): Slot; }

class FirstFitAllocator implements SlotAllocator {
  constructor(private readonly floor: Floor, private readonly size: SlotSize) {}
  allocate(v: Vehicle): Slot {
    for (const row of this.floor.rows) for (const slot of row.slots) {
      if (slot.isFree && slot.canFit(v)) return slot;      // the check happens here and again in occupy()
    }
    throw new NoSlotAvailable(this.floor.id, this.size);
  }
}
class NearestToEntranceAllocator extends FirstFitAllocator { /* … */ }
class CheapestAllocator extends FirstFitAllocator          { /* lowest rate floor/zone */ }
class AccessibleOnlyAllocator extends FirstFitAllocator     { /* slot.accessible only */ }

// ParkingLot composes an allocator per floor so each floor can use a different policy.
class ParkingLot {
  private readonly byTicket = new Map<string, Ticket>();
  constructor(private readonly floors: Floor[], private readonly allocators: Map<FloorId, SlotAllocator>,
              private readonly rates: RateStrategy, private readonly clock: Clock,
              private readonly nextTicketId: IdGenerator, private readonly tx: TxRunner) {}
  park(v: Vehicle): Ticket {
    return this.tx.run(() => {
      // (1) Try each floor; allocate() throws NoSlotAvailable which we catch per floor.
      for (const floor of this.floors) {
        const slot = this.allocators.get(floor.id)!.allocate(v);
        slot.occupy(v);                       // (2) ATOMIC check-and-set inside the slot
        const ticket = new Ticket(this.nextTicketId.next(), slot.ref, this.clock.now());
        this.byTicket.set(ticket.id, ticket);
        return ticket;                        // (3) commit (DB tx) => slot + ticket are consistent
      }
      throw new NoSlotAvailable(v.type);
    });
  }
  unpark(t: Ticket): FeeReceipt {
    return this.tx.run(() => {
      const floor = this.floorOf(t.slot);
      const slot = floor.slotAt(t.slot);
      const v = slot.vacate();                                 // FREE first
      this.byTicket.delete(t.id);
      const hours = this.clock.now().getTime() - t.issuedAt.getTime();
      return new FeeReceipt(t, v, this.rates.rateFor(v.type, hours), hours);
    });
  }
  availability() {
    return this.floors.reduce((acc, f) => { for (const [size, n] of f.freeCountBySize()) acc.set(size, (acc.get(size) ?? 0) + n); return acc; },
                              new Map<SlotSize, number>());
  }
}
```

Fees with a strategy, and the graceful state machine for a ticket — the part most candidates skip and interviewers reward:

```ts
type TicketState = 'issued' | 'settling' | 'settled' | 'disputed';   // see 11.1.11
class FeeCalculator {                     // Strategy: HourlyRate | FirstHourFree | WeekendRate | DynamicRate
  constructor(private readonly strategies: Record<VehicleType, RateStrategy>) {}
  compute(v: Vehicle, from: Date, to: Date): Money {
    const hours = Math.max(1, Math.ceil((to.getTime() - from.getTime()) / 3_600_000));   // round UP
    return this.strategies[v.type].feeFor(hours);
  }
}
```

**Extension points.**
1. **Slot allocation strategy** (FirstFit / Nearest / Cheapest / EV / Accessible / `PriorityAllocator` that honours a membership tier).
2. **Pricing strategy** per vehicle type and time of day.
3. **Vehicle sizing rule** (`requiredSize`) — add tricycles, bicycles-with-sidecars, trailers.
4. **Reservation system** — a third resource type (a future booking) competing for the same slots; adds a `Reserved` slot state and a hold-with-expiry.
5. **Multi-lot network** — a `ParkingNetwork` that queries sibling lots when local is full.
6. **Payment integration** behind a `PaymentGateway` port (11.2.7), with idempotency keyed on the ticket id.
7. **Occupancy analytics** — an event emitter on `SlotOccupied`/`SlotVacated` for dashboards.
8. **Valet / valet-by-plate** — a different allocation entry point, not a change to the core.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- Finding the **atomicity problem** unprompted (the #1 discriminator: 80% of candidates write `find` then `occupy` with no lock).
- Naming the **variation axes** (slot selection, pricing) and putting each behind an interface instead of a `switch`.
- Modelling the slot as an object that enforces its own invariants, rather than a `Map<SlotId, Vehicle>` in the lot.
- Knowing **big-O and the scan cost** (`findFreeSlots` is O(slots); you should offer an index — a `Set` of free slots per size per floor — and note the update cost on occupy/vacate).
- Noticing that **`findVehiclesByColor` is an index problem, not a scan**, and offering a `Map<Color, Set<SlotRef>>`.
- Mentioning persistence and that the "in-memory" version is a simplification of a transactional DB version.

---

### 11.4.2 LRU cache

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** `get(key)`, `put(key, value)`, capacity bound, `size`, optional TTL, and (production) statistics. Eviction policy: LRU by default.

**Constraints.**
- **O(1) get and put.** This is the constraint that eliminates most wrong answers.
- Capacity is measured in entries *or* total size of values.
- Thread/task safety; in JS, single-threaded but async interleaving still matters.
- No unbounded memory; capacity is a hard limit.

**API surface.**
```ts
interface Cache<K, V> {
  get(key: K): V | undefined;
  peek(key: K): V | undefined;      // does NOT count as a usage — this distinction is tested
  put(key: K, value: V): void;
  delete(key: K): boolean;
  clear(): void;
  readonly size: number;
  readonly capacity: number;
  keys(): K[];                       // least-recently-used first
}
```

**The class diagram and the data structure choice.**

```
                ┌──────────────────────────┐
                │        LRUCache          │
                │  capacity: number         │
                │  map: Map<K, Node<K,V>>   │   ← insertion order == recency order
                └───────────┬──────────────┘
                            │ one node per entry
                            ▼
     ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
     │  Node { key: K, value: V,                    │
     │         prev?: Node, next?: Node }           │
     └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```
The critical insight: **a JavaScript `Map` preserves insertion order, and has O(1) `get`/`set`/`delete`.** That makes it the ideal backing store: recency order *is* insertion order, if you always re-insert on access.

```ts
class LRUCache<K, V> implements Cache<K, V> {
  private readonly map = new Map<K, V>();
  constructor(readonly capacity: number) {
    if (capacity < 1) throw new InvalidCapacity(capacity);
  }
  get(key: K): V | undefined {
    if (!this.map.has(key)) { this.stats.misses++; return undefined; }
    const v = this.map.get(key)!;
    this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, v);    // move to the END = most recently used
    this.stats.hits++;
    return v;
  }
  peek(key: K): V | undefined { return this.map.get(key); }   // no reorder
  put(key: K, value: V): void {
    if (this.map.has(key)) this.map.delete(key);
    else if (this.map.size >= this.capacity) {
      const oldest = this.map.keys().next().value as K;         // O(1)
      this.map.delete(oldest);                                  // evict LRU
      this.stats.evictions++;
    }
    this.map.set(key, value);
  }
  get size() { return this.map.size; }
  keys(): K[] { return [...this.map.keys()]; }
  clear() { this.map.clear(); }
}
// Complexity: get O(1), put O(1), delete O(1), keys O(n). Space O(capacity).
```

**The alternative implementations and why you should name them.**

```ts
// (a) Doubly-linked list + HashMap: the textbook structure, O(1), and the standard answer in
//     languages without ordered maps. In JS this is strictly WORSE than the Map version: more
//     code, more invariants (pointer maintenance), same complexity.
class Node<K, V> { key!: K; value!: V; prev?: Node<K,V>; next?: Node<K,V> }
class LRUCacheList<K, V> {
  private head?: Node<K,V>; private tail?: Node<K,V>;
  private readonly map = new Map<K, Node<K,V>>();
  private unlink(n: Node<K,V>) { n.prev!.next = n.next; n.next!.prev = n.prev; }
  pushFront(n: Node<K,V>) { n.prev = undefined; n.next = this.head; this.head!.prev = n; this.head = n; }
  get(k: K) { const n = this.map.get(k); if (!n) return undefined;
              this.unlink(n); this.pushFront(n); return n.value; }
  put(k: K, v: V) { /* … O(1) … */ }
}

// (b) Two maps (recent + old) — the "2Q" approximation used by Redis: amortises the recency update
//     by only promoting from old→recent on the second access. Cheaper writes, worse hit ratio.
class TwoQueueCache<K, V> {
  private recent = new Map<K, V>();     // FIFO, capacity = capacity
  private old    = new Map<K, V>();     // FIFO, capacity = capacity
  get(k: K) { return this.recent.get(k) ?? this.touchOld(k); }
  private touchOld(k: K) { const v = this.old.get(k); if (v === undefined) return undefined;
                            this.old.delete(k); this.recent.set(k, v); return v; }
  put(k: K, v: V) { if (this.recent.size >= this.capacity) this.old = shiftIntoOld(this.recent, this.capacity);
                     this.recent.set(k, v); }
}

// (c) TTL/LRU hybrid — the variant real systems use: evict by TTL first, then LRU.
class TtlLruCache<K, V> {
  private readonly map = new Map<K, { value: V; expiresAt: number }>();
  get(k: K) {
    const e = this.map.get(k); if (!e) return undefined;
    if (Date.now() > e.expiresAt) { this.map.delete(k); return undefined; }
    this.map.delete(k); this.map.set(k, e); return e.value;
  }
  put(k: K, v: V, ttlMs: number) { /* evict-expired sweep, then LRU evict */ }
  // Also: sweeping all entries is O(n) — production caches use a timing wheel or a min-heap of
  // expiry times so expired-entry removal is O(log n) amortised. Mention this; it is the detail
  // that separates "I built it" from "I ran it".
}
```

**State and invariants.**
```
Invariant 1: map.size <= capacity, at all times.
Invariant 2: iteration order of the map == recency order, least-recent first.
Invariant 3: every key in the map has exactly one entry (no duplicates from a Map).
Invariant 4: put(existingKey) does not grow the size.
Invariant 5: get() on a miss leaves the order unchanged.
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **`peek` must not reorder.** This is the classic test case ("get then peek then evict — which goes?"). Many implementations break it.
2. **JS `Map` order guarantee.** `Map` iterates in insertion order, and re-inserting an existing key moves it to the end *only if you delete it first* — actually, re-setting an existing key preserves the original position, so `delete` then `set` is required. Say this explicitly; it is a real subtlety.
3. **Capacity by size, not count** (`maxWeight` with a `weigh(value)` function), and eviction of *multiple* entries until under budget.
4. **Async concurrency.** A cache that calls a loader: 20 concurrent misses on the same key trigger 20 loader calls. You need **single-flight / request coalescing**:
```ts
class AsyncCache<K, V> {
  private inflight = new Map<K, Promise<V>>();
  async getOrLoad(k: K, load: (k: K) => Promise<V>): Promise<V> {
    const hit = this.get(k); if (hit !== undefined) return hit;
    const existing = this.inflight.get(k);
    if (existing) return existing;                       // coalesce: one load for N callers
    const p = load(k).then(v => { this.put(k, v); return v; })
                    .finally(() => this.inflight.delete(k));   // always clear, even on rejection
    this.inflight.set(k, p);
    return p;
  }
}
```
5. **Stampede protection on expiry** — jittered TTLs so 10,000 keys do not all expire at the same instant.
6. **Negative caching** — caching "not found" with a short TTL to protect against penetration (a stream of requests for keys that never exist).

**Extension points.**
1. `EvictionPolicy` interface: `LRU`, `LFU`, `TTL`, `Random`, `SLRU` (segmented: probationary/protected), `ARC`, `TinyLFU` (what Caffeine/Redis use).
2. `Loader` function injected (so the cache is testable and works with the single-flight pattern).
3. `StatsProvider` — hits, misses, hit ratio, evictions, size, estimated memory.
4. **Distributed tiering**: in-process LRU (L1) → Redis (L2) → origin (L3), with a coherence policy and single-flight at each tier.
5. **Compression** of values above a size threshold.
6. **Persistent/overflow tier** — LRU + a size-bounded overflow map (like Caffeine's).
7. **Admission policy** — do not admit a value on first miss (TinyLFU frequency sketch idea) to avoid polluting with one-hit-wonders.
8. **Read-through vs write-through vs write-behind** loaders.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **O(1) on both get and put**, and the reason why (Map insertion order; or list+map).
- Knowing that `peek` must not promote, and the delete-then-set subtlety.
- Volunteers the async problems (coalescing, stampede, negative caching) without being asked — this is the strongest signal.
- Offers **LFU/SLRU and the tiered distributed** variant when asked "how would this look in production".
- Can state the **complexity of every method** and the space bound.

---

### 11.4.3 Rate limiter

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** Limit requests per key (IP, user, API key, tenant) over a window. Reject over-limit requests. Configurable policy. Distributed (multiple server instances).

**Constraints.**
- Must be O(1) memory per key and O(1) time per request (no unbounded storage).
- Must work across instances (a single-process limiter is wrong behind a load balancer).
- Must be *atomic* under concurrency (check-then-increment races).
- Approximation is acceptable; exactness usually is not worth it.

**The five algorithms — know all of them, they are the heart of the question.**

```ts
// 1. FIXED WINDOW COUNTER — simplest, worst. Boundary burst: 10 at 10:59:59 and 10 at 11:00:00
//    = 20 requests in 2 seconds against a limit of 10/min.
class FixedWindowCounter implements RateLimiter {
  private readonly windows = new Map<string, { window: number; count: number }>();
  constructor(private readonly limit: number, private readonly windowMs: number, private readonly now: Clock) {}
  async tryAcquire(key: string): Promise<Decision> {
    const w = Math.floor(this.now.now().getTime() / this.windowMs);
    const e = this.windows.get(key) ?? { window: w, count: 0 };
    if (e.window !== w) { e.window = w; e.count = 0; }              // reset on window change
    if (e.count >= this.limit) return { allowed: false, retryAfterMs: (w + 1) * this.windowMs - this.now.now().getTime() };
    e.count++; this.windows.set(key, e);
    return { allowed: true, remaining: this.limit - e.count };
  }
}
// Cost: O(1). Memory: O(keys) — MUST be evicted, or it leaks. Failure mode: 2× burst at boundaries.

// 2. SLIDING WINDOW LOG — exact. Store every request timestamp; drop those older than the window.
//    Perfect accuracy, O(window) memory per key. Use only when the limit is small.
class SlidingWindowLog implements RateLimiter {
  private readonly hits = new Map<string, number[]>();
  async tryAcquire(key: string): Promise<Decision> {
    const t = this.now.now().getTime();
    const cutoff = t - this.windowMs;
    const list = (this.hits.get(key) ?? []).filter(ts => ts > cutoff);   // prune
    if (list.length >= this.limit) { this.hits.set(key, list);
      return { allowed: false, retryAfterMs: list[0]! - cutoff }; }
    list.push(t); this.hits.set(key, list);
    return { allowed: true, remaining: this.limit - list.length };
  }
}

// 3. SLIDING WINDOW COUNTER — the standard production choice. Exact, O(1) memory per key.
//    Weight the current window's count by the fraction of it that overlaps the window.
class SlidingWindowCounter implements RateLimiter {
  private readonly state = new Map<string, { curCount: number; curWin: number; prevCount: number }>();
```
