```ts
  async tryAcquire(key: string): Promise<Decision> {
    const t = this.now.now().getTime();
    const w = Math.floor(t / this.windowMs);
    let s = this.state.get(key);
    if (!s) { s = { curCount: 0, curWin: w, prevCount: 0 }; this.state.set(key, s); }
    if (s.curWin !== w) {                       // rotate; the previous window's data is now "prev"
      const elapsedInWindow = t - w * this.windowMs;
      s.prevCount = elapsedInWindow >= this.windowMs ? s.curCount
                   : s.curCount * Math.ceil((this.windowMs - elapsedInWindow) / this.windowMs);
      s.curCount = 0; s.curWin = w;
    }
    const weight = (t - w * this.windowMs) / this.windowMs;          // 0..1 position in the window
    const estimate = s.prevCount * (1 - weight) + s.curCount;
    if (estimate >= this.limit)
      return { allowed: false, retryAfterMs: Math.ceil((1 - weight) * this.windowMs) };
    s.curCount++;
    return { allowed: true, remaining: Math.floor(this.limit - estimate) - 1 };
  }
  // This is what most API gateways actually use. O(1) time and memory. Approximate but never
  // allows more than the limit by more than one request.
// ALSO: opportunistic cleanup of `state` — keys idle for > windowMs must be deleted, or memory leaks.

// 4. TOKEN BUCKET — allows bursts up to `capacity`, refills continuously at `rate`/s.
//    The right default for APIs: it expresses both a sustained rate and a burst allowance.
class TokenBucket implements RateLimiter {
  private readonly buckets = new Map<string, { tokens: number; lastRefillMs: number }>();
  constructor(private readonly capacity: number, private readonly refillPerSec: number, private readonly now: Clock) {}
  async tryAcquire(key: string, cost = 1): Promise<Decision> {
    const t = this.now.now().getTime();
    const b = this.buckets.get(key) ?? { tokens: this.capacity, lastRefillMs: t };
    const refilled = b.tokens + ((t - b.lastRefillMs) / 1000) * this.refillPerSec;
    const tokens = Math.min(this.capacity, refilled);            // never exceed capacity
    if (tokens < cost) { this.buckets.set(key, { tokens, lastRefillMs: t });
      return { allowed: false, retryAfterMs: Math.ceil(((cost - tokens) / this.refillPerSec) * 1000) }; }
    this.buckets.set(key, { tokens: tokens - cost, lastRefillMs: t });
    return { allowed: true, remaining: Math.floor(tokens - cost), limit: this.capacity };
  }
}
// capacity = burst size; refillPerSec = sustained rate. Cost O(1). Memory O(keys).

// 5. LEAKY BUCKET — output rate is strictly constant; the queue absorbs bursts.
//    Use when you need a smooth downstream rate (e.g. calling a partner API with a hard quota).
//    In serverless: the "bucket" is a queue; if it is full you drop/reject. That is the "leaky".
```

**Distributed rate limiting.** This is where the interview separates candidates.

```ts
// Option A: CENTRALISED store (Redis). The correct default.
class RedisSlidingWindow implements RateLimiter {
  constructor(private readonly redis: Redis, private readonly windowMs: number) {}
  // Atomic via a Lua script: prune, count, compare, increment. All in one round trip, no race.
  async tryAcquire(key: string, limit: number): Promise<Decision> {
    const lua = `
      local key = KEYS[1]
      local now = tonumber(ARGV[1])
      local window = tonumber(ARGV[2])
      local limit = tonumber(ARGV[3])
      redis.call('ZREMRANGEBYSCORE', key, 0, now - window)
      local count = redis.call('ZCARD', key)
      if count < limit then
        redis.call('ZADD', key, now, now .. math.random())
        redis.call('PEXPIRE', key, window)
        return {1, limit - count - 1}
      end
      local oldest = redis.call('ZRANGE', key, 0, 0, 'WITHSCORES')
      return {0, limit, tonumber(oldest[2]) + window - now}
    `;
    return (await this.redis.eval(lua, { keys: [`rl:${key}`], arguments: [String(Date.now()), String(this.windowMs), String(limit)] })) as any;
  }
}
// Why Lua and not a read-then-write: the check and the increment must be one atomic operation, or
// N concurrent requests all pass the check. This is the single most important implementation detail.

// Option B: LOCAL limiter + periodic sync (cheap approximation, what Cloudflare/NGINX often do).
class LocalPlusSyncLimiter implements RateLimiter {
  private local = new TokenBucket(100, 50, this.clock);
  private budget = 10_000;                            // cluster-wide budget
  async tryAcquire(key: string): Promise<Decision> {
    if (!this.local.tryAcquire(key).allowed) return REJECT;          // fast local path
    if (this.budget <= 0) return REJECT;                             // cluster budget exhausted
    this.budget -= 1; return ALLOW;
  }
  startSync(intervalMs: number) { setInterval(async () => {
    const taken = await this.central.consumeBudget(this.local.pendingCount);
    this.budget = taken; this.local.resetPending();
  }, intervalMs); }
}
// Trade-off: a cluster can over-admit by up to (instances × burst) between syncs. Fine for abuse
// prevention, wrong for billing. Say which is which.

// Option C: QUOTA at the edge (API gateway / CDN) so the app never sees over-limit requests.
// Often the right answer: reject at the edge to save capacity, AND keep a local limiter as a defence.

// Option D: leaky-bucket-queue in serverless (API Gateway + SQS): the queue is the bucket, DLQ is
// the overflow, and a consumer throttles. This is the natural serverless shape (11.3.9).
```

**Keying strategy (an interview favourite).** `key = tenant + ':' + userId` for per-user; `ip + ':' + route` for abuse; **hierarchical**: check both the user limit and the tenant limit, reject if either fails. And: **fail open vs fail closed** when Redis is unavailable — for a public API you usually fail *open* (or fall back to a local limiter) so a Redis outage does not take down the site; for billing/regulatory quotas you fail *closed*. State this decision explicitly; it shows production awareness.

**State and invariants.**
```
Invariant 1: for a key, no more than `limit` acquisitions succeed in any window (fixed window: per window;
              sliding/token bucket: over any window-length period, within approximation error).
Invariant 2: state per key is O(1) memory; state total is bounded (evict idle keys).
Invariant 3: the acquire operation is atomic per key (no over-admission under concurrency).
Invariant 4: `retryAfterMs` is never negative and is an upper bound on when a retry could succeed.
```

**Extension points.**
1. `RateLimiter` interface with `tryAcquire(key, cost)` → `Decision { allowed, limit, remaining, retryAfterMs, resetAt }`.
2. Pluggable **policy**: fixed window / sliding window / token bucket / leaky bucket / concurrency limiter (semaphore — limits *in-flight*, not rate; this is what you want for expensive endpoints).
3. **Cost-weighted** acquisition (a search costs 5, a ping costs 1).
4. **Hierarchical limits** (per-IP, per-user, per-tenant, global) checked in order.
5. **Distributed** via Redis Lua / central budget sync / gateway-level quota.
6. **Adaptive limits** (adjust the limit from observed latency/error rate — token bucket with dynamic refill).
7. **Exemptions and overrides** (internal traffic, admin, allow-listed IPs) — and the security warning that they must be explicit.
8. **Rejection semantics**: `429` with `Retry-After` and `X-RateLimit-*` headers; include the limit so clients can back off intelligently.
9. **Metrics**: allowed/rejected counts, rejection rate per key class, and an alert on a spike (that is a DDoS signal).

**What the interviewer scores.**
- Knows **at least three algorithms** and can state the boundary-burst problem with a concrete example.
- Puts **token bucket** forward as the sensible default and justifies it (burst + sustained).
- Identifies the **atomicity race** in the distributed case and knows that Redis needs a Lua script (or an atomic `INCR`+`EXPIRE` for fixed windows).
- Asks about **fail-open vs fail-closed**.
- Notes the **memory leak** in per-key state and how to prevent it (idle eviction / TTL).
- Distinguishes **rate** limiting from **concurrency** limiting and from **quota** limiting.

### 11.4.4 Elevator

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** Model `request(floor)`, `move(direction)`, `next()`, and the dispatch policy. Handle: multiple requests, current direction, doors, capacity, idle/overloaded states.

**Constraints.**
- The car must not reverse direction while passengers are inside (or with pending requests behind it).
- Doors must not open on an intermediate floor while travelling.
- Request coalescing: multiple requests for the same floor → one stop.
- Fairness: no starvation (a request far in the travel direction keeps being overtaken).

**The design that gets full marks: separate the *mechanics* from the *policy*.**

```ts
type Direction = 'up' | 'down';
type ElevatorState = 'idle' | 'moving' | 'doors-open' | 'maintenance' | 'fire-alarm';
type Request = { floor: number; direction: Direction; source: 'hall' | 'cab' };

// ── The CAR: a state machine (11.1.11). Behaviour differs per state; illegal ops don't exist.
class ElevatorCar {
  private state: ElevatorState = 'idle';
  private direction: Direction = 'up';
  private currentFloor: number;
  private passengers = new Set<Passenger>();
  private readonly stops = new Set<number>();          // coalesced floor requests, per direction

  constructor(readonly id: string, private readonly floors: number, private readonly capacity: number,
              private readonly floorsPerSecond: number, private readonly doorOpenMs: number) {
    this.currentFloor = floors;                          // parked at the top
  }
  get status() { return { state: this.state, floor: this.currentFloor, direction: this.direction,
                          load: this.passengers.size, capacity: this.capacity }; }

  requestFromHall(floor: number, dir: Direction): void {
    if (floor < 1 || floor > this.floors) throw new BadFloor(floor, this.floors);
    this.stops.add(floor);                              // O(1) coalescing: a Set, not a list
    if (this.state === 'idle') this.direction = this.decideDirection(floor);
    this.pump();                                        // drive toward a stop if we can move
  }
  requestFromCab(floor: number): void {
    if (this.state === 'moving') throw new Unsafe('cab button while moving');
    if (floor < 1 || floor > this.floors) throw new BadFloor(floor, this.floors);
    this.stops.add(floor);
    this.pump();
  }

  private decideDirection(target: number): Direction {
    if (target > this.currentFloor) return 'up';
    if (target < this.currentFloor) return 'down';
    return this.direction;
  }
  private pump(): void {
    if (this.state === 'idle' && this.stops.size > 0) {
      const target = this.stops.values().next().value as number;
      this.direction = this.decideDirection(target);
      this.state = 'moving';
    }
  }
  // The movement tick. In production this is driven by the motor's feedback, not a timer.
  async tick(): Promise<void> {
    if (this.state !== 'moving') return;
    const stepDir: Direction = this.currentFloor + this.step <= this.floors ? 'up' : 'down';
    if (this.stops.has(this.currentFloor)) { await this.openDoors(); return; }   // service a stop
    this.currentFloor += this.stepFor(stepDir);
  }
  // The SAFETY RULES, which is what the interview is really about:
  private canReverse(): boolean {
    return this.passengers.size === 0 && !this.hasPendingRequestBehind();
  }
  private async openDoors(): Promise<void> {
    this.state = 'doors-open';
    this.stops.delete(this.currentFloor);
    await sleep(this.doorOpenMs);
    this.state = 'idle';
    this.maybeReverse();                                // reverse only when legal
    this.pump();
  }
  private maybeReverse(): void {
    const wantUp = [...this.stops].some(f => f > this.currentFloor);
    const wantDown = [...this.stops].some(f => f < this.currentFloor);
    if (this.direction === 'up' && !wantUp && wantDown && this.canReverse()) this.direction = 'down';
    if (this.direction === 'down' && !wantDown && wantUp && this.canReverse()) this.direction = 'up';
  }
  private hasPendingRequestBehind(): boolean {
    return this.direction === 'up' ? [...this.stops].some(f => f < this.currentFloor)
                                   : [...this.stops].some(f => f > this.currentFloor);
  }
  enter(p: Passenger): void {
    if (this.state !== 'doors-open') throw new Unsafe('enter while doors closed');
    if (this.passengers.size >= this.capacity) throw new Overloaded(this.capacity);
    this.passengers.add(p);
  }
  exit(p: Passenger): void { if (this.state !== 'doors-open') throw new Unsafe('exit while doors closed');
                             this.passengers.delete(p); }
}

// ── The POLICY: which elevator handles a request. This is where Strategy lives (11.1.10).
interface DispatchStrategy {
  select(car: ElevatorCar, req: Request, all: readonly ElevatorCar[]): ElevatorCar | null;
}
class RoundRobinStrategy implements DispatchStrategy { /* simple, fair */ }
class FirstComeFirstServedStrategy implements DispatchStrategy { /* honours arrival order */ }
class LeastWaitingStrategy implements DispatchStrategy {
  select(car: ElevatorCar, _req: Request, all: readonly ElevatorCar[]): ElevatorCar | null {
    return all.filter(c => c.canServe(_req) && !c.isMovingAwayFrom(_req))
               .sort((a, b) => a.waitingSince(_req) - b.waitingSince(_req))[0] ?? null;
  }
}
class SCANStrategy implements DispatchStrategy { /* classic: always sweep to the top, then back down */ }
class DestinationControlStrategy implements DispatchStrategy { /* passenger picks a floor before boarding */ }

// ── The SYSTEM: owns the cars and the policy.
class ElevatorSystem {
  private readonly cars: ElevatorCar[];
  constructor(cars: ElevatorCar[], private readonly policy: DispatchStrategy) { this.cars = cars; }
  call(floor: number, dir: Direction): void {
    const req: Request = { floor, direction: dir, source: 'hall' };
    const car = this.policy.select(this.cars[0]!, req, this.cars);
    if (!car) throw new NoCarAvailable(req);
    car.requestFromHall(floor, dir);
  }
  step(): void { this.cars.forEach(c => void c.tick()); }
  status() { return this.cars.map(c => c.status); }
}
```

**State and invariants.**
```
Elevator states: idle ⇄ moving ⇄ doors-open; plus maintenance and fire-alarm (terminal-ish).
Invariant 1: doors never open while state === 'moving'.
Invariant 2: direction never reverses with passengers inside.
Invariant 3: direction never reverses while a request exists behind the car (no starvation).
Invariant 4: |passengers| <= capacity, and boarding is only legal in 'doors-open'.
Invariant 5: the car never moves past the top/bottom floor (bounds checked at every step).
Invariant 6: at most one stop per floor (Set coalescing).
Invariant 7: a request is never dropped: every request in `stops` is eventually served or fails explicitly.
```
Transitions table (state machine in data form — see 11.1.11):
```
idle + hallRequest    → moving     (direction chosen by target position)
moving + atStopFloor  → doors-open
doors-open + timer    → idle       (then reverse if legal, then pump)
any + maintenance     → maintenance (drain + stop)
any + fireAlarm       → doors-open at nearest floor, then to ground, then halt  ← the safety case
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **Fairness vs efficiency.** SCAN is simple and efficient but starves far requests if traffic is asymmetric; a "collective control" with look-ahead (a scheduling algorithm that plans the whole set of trips) is the sophisticated answer. Name both, implement one.
2. **Direction reversal rules.** The interesting rule is: the car continues in its direction if there are passengers going that way, OR if there is a hall request in that direction; only then does it reverse. Getting this exactly right is worth more than anything else here.
3. **The destination-dispatch (destination control) design** is a genuinely different architecture: passengers enter and press a floor *before* the doors open, so the car knows its full manifest and can be scheduled globally (a group-controller / BFS-based scheduler). This is the "real" elevator algorithm and mentioning it shows depth.
4. **Failure and safety**: fire alarm overrides everything; a stuck door, a sensor fault, an over-speed governor. These belong in the state machine as transitions with priority, not in `if` statements in the tick loop.
5. **Concurrency**: two calls arriving for the same floor from both directions, and a call arriving while the car is reversing.

**Extension points.**
1. `DispatchStrategy`: round-robin, FCFS, least-waiting, SCAN, destination-control, zone-based, ETA-based (predictive with historical traffic).
2. Multiple cars with a **shaft conflict** constraint (opposite direction in one shaft must be resolved).
3. **Express/local** elevators (a subset of floors served) — a routing constraint, which makes it a graph problem.
4. **Fire/safety mode** as a priority transition.
5. **Accessibility**: door width, braille buttons, voice announcements, longer dwell time for wheelchairs.
6. **Energy**: destination dispatch with a "regenerative trip" table — a strong differentiator to mention.
7. **Telemetry**: per-trip distance, stops, wait time, energy, and dispatch decisions for analytics.
8. **Simulator**: discrete-event simulation for testing dispatch policies — the right way to compare strategies.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Separating mechanics from policy.** A candidate who writes everything in one `Elevator` class with a `switch` on the dispatch algorithm loses points here.
- Knowing the **direction-reversal safety rules** precisely.
- **Coalescing** requests with a `Set`.
- Recognising this as a **state machine** and being able to draw the transition table.
- Offering **destination control** when asked how a real building works.
- Noticing that fairness (no starvation) is an explicit requirement, not an accident.

---

### 11.4.5 Splitwise / expense sharing

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** `addExpense(payer, amount, participants, splitMode)`, `settleUp(userId)`, `getBalances(groupId)`, `simplifyDebts(groupId)`, and settlement recording.
Split modes: equally, exact amounts, percentages, shares/weights. Support multiple currencies.

**Constraints.**
- Balances must be computable **incrementally** (O(1) per expense) — recomputing from scratch is O(expenses × participants) and does not scale.
- **Rounding**: 100 cents split 3 ways is 33.33 — you must define who gets the remainder and be consistent.
- Money is never a float.
- Simplification is a graph problem, not a pairwise loop.

```ts
type Currency = string;
class Money {                                        // value object (11.2.8)
  private constructor(readonly minor: number, readonly currency: Currency) {}
  static of(minor: number, c: Currency) {
    if (!Number.isSafeInteger(minor)) throw new NonIntegerAmount(minor);
    return new Money(minor, c);
  }
  add(o: Money): Money {
    if (o.currency !== this.currency) throw new CurrencyMismatch(this.currency, o.currency);
    const s = this.minor + o.minor; if (!Number.isSafeInteger(s)) throw new AmountOverflow(s);
    return new Money(s, this.currency);
  }
  negate() { return new Money(-this.minor, this.currency); }
  compare(o: Money) { return this.minor - o.minor; }
  get isZero() { return this.minor === 0; }
}

// ── SPLIT STRATEGIES (11.1.10): each knows how to turn an expense into per-user shares.
interface SplitStrategy {
  readonly name: string;
  shares(amount: Money, participants: readonly Participant[], input?: SplitInput): Map<UserId, Money>;
}
// Exact amounts: validated to sum exactly to the total — throw on mismatch. Never silently adjust.
class ExactSplit implements SplitStrategy {
  shares(amount: Money, _p: readonly Participant[], input?: SplitInput): Map<UserId, Money> {
    const m = new Map<UserId, Money>();
    for (const [u, mny] of input!.exact!) m.set(u, mny);
    const sum = [...m.values()].reduce((t, v) => t.add(v), Money.zero(amount.currency));
    if (sum.compare(amount) !== 0) throw new SplitDoesNotSum(sum, amount);   // strict, correct
    return m;
  }
}
// Equal: distribute the remainder deterministically (largest id first, or by join order) and DOCUMENT it.
class EqualSplit implements SplitStrategy {
  shares(amount: Money, participants: readonly Participant[]): Map<UserId, Money> {
    const n = participants.length;
    if (n === 0) throw new NoParticipants();
    const base = Math.floor(amount.minor / n);                  // deterministic floor
    let remainder = amount.minor - base * n;                     // 0..n-1 cents
    const m = new Map<UserId, Money>();
    const ordered = [...participants].sort((a, b) => a.userId.localeCompare(b.userId));  // STABLE tie-break
    for (const p of ordered) {
      const extra = remainder > 0 ? 1 : 0;                      // one cent each until gone
      if (extra) remainder--;
      m.set(p.userId, Money.of(base + extra, amount.currency));
    }
    if ([...m.values()].reduce((t, v) => t.add(v), Money.zero(amount.currency)).compare(amount) !== 0)
      throw new SplitInvariantBroken();                          // assert the invariant in code
    return m;
  }
}
class PercentageSplit implements SplitStrategy { /* validate sum == 100, then allocate with the same
                                                   remainder rule to guarantee the sum is exact */ }
class SharesSplit implements SplitStrategy      { /* weights; remainder to the largest share */ }

// ── EXPENSE + the incremental balance ledger, which is the key insight.
class Expense {
  constructor(readonly id: ExpenseId, readonly groupId: GroupId, readonly payer: UserId,
              readonly amount: Money, readonly participants: readonly UserId[],
              readonly shares: ReadonlyMap<UserId, Money>, readonly createdAt: Date) {}
  static create(id: ExpenseId, groupId: GroupId, payer: UserId, amount: Money,
                participants: readonly UserId[], strategy: SplitStrategy, createdAt: Date,
                input?: SplitInput): Expense {
    if (participants.length === 0) throw new NoParticipants();
    return new Expense(id, groupId, payer, amount, participants, strategy.shares(amount, participants, input), createdAt);
  }
}

class Ledger {                       // <-- the incremental balance model
  // net[user] > 0  => user is owed money;  net[user] < 0 => user owes money.
  private readonly net = new Map<UserId, Money>();
  constructor(private readonly currency: Currency) {}
  record(e: Expense): void {         // O(participants); no replay of history
    // The payer is credited the FULL amount; each participant is debited their share.
    this.credit(e.payer, e.amount);
    for (const [u, share] of e.shares) this.debit(u, share);
  }
  unrecord(e: Expense): void {       // credit/debit reversed — needed for expense deletion
    this.debit(e.payer, e.amount);
    for (const [u, share] of e.shares) this.credit(u, share);
  }
  balanceOf(u: UserId): Money { return this.net.get(u) ?? Money.zero(this.currency); }
  private credit(u: UserId, m: Money) { this.net.set(u, this.balanceOf(u).add(m)); }
  private debit(u: UserId, m: Money)  { this.net.set(u, this.balanceOf(u).sub(m)); }
  // The invariant: sum of all balances == 0. ALWAYS. Assert it; it is the single best sanity check.
  get total(): Money { return [...this.net.values()].reduce((t, v) => t.add(v), Money.zero(this.currency)); }
}

class Splitwise {
  private readonly ledgers = new Map<GroupId, Ledger>();
  constructor(private readonly splits: { equal: SplitStrategy; exact: SplitStrategy;
              percentage: SplitStrategy; shares: SplitStrategy }) {}
  addExpense(cmd: AddExpense): Expense {
    const strategy = this.splits[cmd.mode];
    const e = Expense.create(cmd.id, cmd.groupId, cmd.payer, cmd.amount, cmd.participants,
                             strategy, cmd.createdAt, cmd.input);
    this.ledgerFor(cmd.groupId).record(e);
    return e;
  }
  settle(cmd: Settle): void {
    const l = this.ledgerFor(cmd.groupId);
    l.record(Expense.create(cmd.id, cmd.groupId, cmd.from, cmd.amount.minor === 0 ? cmd.amount : cmd.amount,
                            [cmd.to], new ExactSplit(), cmd.at, { exact: new Map([[cmd.to, cmd.amount]]) }));
    // A settlement is modelled as an expense: from → to. This is the elegant trick; note it.
  }
  balances(groupId: GroupId): Map<UserId, Money> { return new Map(this.ledgerFor(groupId).entries()); }

  /** Greedy debt simplification: turn the balance vector into the MINIMAL number of transfers. */
  settleUp(groupId: GroupId): Settlement[] {
    const debts = [...this.ledgerFor(groupId).entries()]
      .map(([user, m]) => ({ user, amount: m.minorUnits }))
      .filter(d => d.amount < 0)                       // people who owe
      .map(d => ({ ...d, amount: -d.amount }))
      .sort((a, b) => b.amount - a.amount);             // largest debtor first
    const credits = [...this.ledgerFor(groupId).entries()]
      .filter(([, m]) => m.minorUnits > 0)
      .map(([user, m]) => ({ user, amount: m.minorUnits }))
      .sort((a, b) => b.amount - a.amount);
    const out: Settlement[] = [];
    let i = 0, j = 0;
    while (i < debts.length && j < credits.length) {
      const pay = Math.min(debts[i]!.amount, credits[j]!.amount);
      out.push({ from: debts[i]!.user, to: credits[j]!.user, amount: Money.of(pay, this.currencyOf(groupId)) });
      debts[i]!.amount -= pay; credits[j]!.amount -= pay;
      if (debts[i]!.amount === 0) i++;
      if (credits[j]!.amount === 0) j++;
    }
    return out;    // at most (n-1) transfers for n participants. Greedy, not optimal, but minimal-ish.
  }
}
```

**State and invariants.**
```
Ledger invariants:
  I1  sum(net.values()) == 0, always.            <-- the master invariant; assert it in code/tests
  I2  net[user] > 0 means "user is owed"; < 0 means "user owes". Sign convention documented.
  I3  Every expense's shares sum EXACTLY to its amount (no lost or created cents).
  I4  All amounts in a group share one currency, or an explicit FX conversion is stored per expense.
Settlement invariants:
  I5  Applying all suggested settlements makes every balance zero.
  I6  The number of transfers is at most n-1 for n participants (greedy guarantees this).
  I7  Settlements do not change the ledger's total (they only move net positions between users).
Expense invariants:
  I8  payer ∈ participants? NO — a payer may legitimately not participate (paying for others).
  I9  No duplicate participant ids (otherwise they get charged twice).
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **Rounding and remainders.** Must be deterministic and documented, and must sum exactly. The classic failure is `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3` compounded across 100 expenses; the answer is integer minor units with a single documented remainder rule. Bonus: state that "always give the remainder to the same participant" can be exploited, so use a stable, non-collusion-prone tie-break.
2. **Simplification minimality.** Greedy (largest-first matching) is O(n log n) and produces at most n−1 transfers; it is *not* always the absolute minimum, and saying that shows depth. The exact minimum is a subset-sum problem; for small n, exhaustive search is feasible.
3. **Multi-currency.** You cannot convert balances without an FX rate, and the rate changes. The correct model: store each expense in the currency it was incurred in, and either (a) require a group currency and store the FX rate + timestamp **per expense**, or (b) compute per-currency balances separately and settle per currency. Do not silently use the current rate for a 6-month-old expense.
4. **Deletion and correction.** Editing an expense must be a `unrecord` + `record`, not an in-place mutation, or the ledger drifts. This is a great thing to volunteer.
5. **Settlement semantics.** Modelling a settlement as an expense means settlement history is auditable and reversible.

**Extension points.**
1. `SplitStrategy`: equal / exact / percentage / shares / weighted-by-meal / "payer covers X%".
2. **Currency support**: `FxRateProvider` port with a stored rate per expense; per-currency ledgers.
3. **Recurring expenses** (monthly rent) with idempotent generation per period.
4. **Category and analytics** (who spends most on what), via events on `ExpenseAdded`.
5. **Settlement optimisation**: exhaustive minimum for small groups; a matching algorithm (Hungarian/max-flow) for larger ones.
6. **Multi-payer expenses** (split the payment itself between several payers).
7. **Group membership changes mid-history** (do we retroactively include them? almost never — model membership as effective-dated and exclude them from past expenses).
8. **Notifications** on settlement; **receipt upload**; **integrations** (bank transfer initiation).

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Integer minor units and a documented remainder rule**, and the `sum == 0` invariant.
- Incremental balances (a `Map<UserId, Money>`), not recomputation.
- Recognising debt simplification as a **greedy matching problem** with a complexity statement, plus the honest note that it is not provably minimal.
- Modelling settlement as an expense (or otherwise explaining how the ledger stays consistent).
- Volunteers the multi-currency problem before being asked.

### 11.4.6 Tic-tac-toe with AI (minimax)

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** (see §4.3.7 for the algorithmic treatment)

**Requirements.** Board, rules, win detection, minimax with alpha-beta pruning, optional depth limit with heuristic evaluation, perfect play unbeatable.

**Constraints.** 3×3 board (9 cells), two players, alternate turns, first player wins on three in a row, full board with no winner = draw. AI must be **unbeatable** when searching the full tree.

**API surface + full implementation.**

```ts
type Cell = -1 | 0 | 1;                     // empty, X, O
type Board = readonly (readonly Cell[])[];   // 9 cells, immutable

const SIZE = 3, CELLS = 9;
const WIN_LINES: readonly number[][] = [
  [0,1,2],[3,4,5],[6,7,8],       // rows
  [0,3,6],[1,4,7],[2,5,8],       // columns
  [0,4,8],[2,4,6],               // diagonals
];

class TicTacToe {
  readonly board: Board; readonly nextPlayer: Cell; readonly winner: Cell | 0; readonly history: Board[];
  private constructor(board: Board, nextPlayer: Cell, winner: Cell | 0, history: Board[]) {
    this.board = board; this.nextPlayer = nextPlayer; this.winner = winner; this.history = history;
  }
  static initial(): TicTacToe { return new TicTacToe(Array(9).fill(-1) as Cell[], 0, 0, []); }
  play(cell: number): TicTacToe {                            // IMMUTABLE: returns a new game
    if (this.winner !== 0) throw new GameOver(this.winner);
    if (this.board[cell] !== -1) throw new CellOccupied(cell);
    if (cell < 0 || cell >= CELLS) throw new BadCell(cell);
    const next = [...this.board] as Cell[]; next[cell] = this.nextPlayer;
    const b: Board = next;
    const w = TicTacToe.winnerOf(b);
    return new TicTacToe(b, w !== 0 ? this.nextPlayer : (this.nextPlayer === 0 ? 1 : 0),
                         w, [...this.history, b]);
  }
  static winnerOf(b: Board): Cell | 0 {
    for (const [a, bb, c] of WIN_LINES) if (b[a] !== -1 && b[a] === b[bb] && b[a] === b[c]) return b[a] as Cell;
    return b.every(c => c !== -1) ? 0 : 0;                    // draw returns 0 with no moves left
  }
  isDraw(): boolean { return this.winner === 0 && this.availableMoves().length === 0; }
  availableMoves(): number[] { return this.board.map((c, i) => (c === -1 ? i : -1)).filter(i => i >= 0); }
  undo(): TicTacToe {                                           // history-based undo (see 11.1.9)
    if (!this.history.length) throw new NothingToUndo();
    const boards = [...this.history]; boards.pop();
    return new TicTacToe(this.board, this.nextPlayer, 0, boards);
  }
}

// ── The AI: minimax + alpha-beta, as a Strategy so difficulty is swappable (11.1.10).
interface Ai { suggest(game: TicTacToe): number; readonly name: string; readonly depth: number; }
class MinimaxAi implements Ai {
  constructor(readonly name: string, readonly depth: number, private readonly evaluate: (g: TicTacToe) => number) {}
  suggest(game: TicTacToe): number {
    const scored = this.search(game, this.depth, Number.NEGATIVE_INFINITY, Number.POSITIVE_INFINITY,
                               game.nextPlayer);
    const best = Math.max(...scored.map(s => s.score));
    // Deterministic tie-break: prefer the lowest index so the AI plays reproducibly.
    return scored.filter(s => s.score === best).sort((a, b) => a.move - b.move)[0]!.move;
  }
  private search(g: TicTacToe, depth: number, alpha: number, beta: number, forPlayer: Cell)
    : { move: number; score: number }[] {
    if (g.winner !== 0)   return [{ move: -1, score: g.winner === forPlayer ? 10 - (9 - depth) : -10 + (9 - depth) }];
    if (g.isDraw() || depth === 0) return [{ move: -1, score: this.evaluate(g) }];
    const moves = g.availableMoves();
    const out = moves.map(move => {
      const child = g.play(move);
      const s = this.search(child, depth - 1, alpha, beta, forPlayer);
      // Negamax-style sign flip: maximise for the player, minimise for the opponent.
      return { move, score: s[0]!.score === Number.NEGATIVE_INFINITY ? Number.NEGATIVE_INFINITY : s[0]!.score };
    });
    if (forPlayer === g.nextPlayer) {
      const best = Math.max(...out.map(o => o.score));
      const filtered = out.filter(o => o.score === best);
      alpha = Math.max(alpha, best);
      const pruned = filtered.length ? filtered : out.slice(0, 1);
      return beta <= alpha ? pruned : out;                     // CUTOFF
    } else {
      const worst = Math.min(...out.map(o => o.score));
      const filtered = out.filter(o => o.score === worst);
      beta = Math.min(beta, worst);
      const pruned = filtered.length ? filtered : out.slice(0, 1);
      return alpha >= beta ? pruned : out;                     // CUTOFF
    }
  }
}

// Heuristic evaluation for limited depth: reward lines that are one move away, punish blocks.
class HeuristicEvaluator {
  constructor(private readonly winScore = 10, private readonly blockScore = 8,
              private readonly centerScore = 5, private readonly oppositeCenterScore = 3,
              private readonly openLineScore = 1) {}
  evaluate(g: TicTacToe): number {
    let score = 0;
    for (const [a, b, c] of WIN_LINES) {
      const line = [g.board[a]!, g.board[b]!, g.board[c]!];
      const marks = (p: Cell) => line.filter(x => x === p).length;
      const empties = line.filter(x => x === -1).length;
      for (const p of [0, 1] as Cell[]) {
        const sign = p === g.nextPlayer ? 1 : -1;
        if (marks(p) === 2 && empties === 1) score += sign * (p === g.nextPlayer ? this.winScore : -this.winScore);
        if (marks(p) === 1 && empties === 2) score += sign * this.openLineScore;
      }
    }
    if (g.board[4] === g.nextPlayer) score += this.centerScore;
    if (g.board[4] !== -1 && g.board[4] !== g.nextPlayer) score -= this.oppositeCenterScore;
    return score;
  }
}
class FirstMoveAi implements Ai { name = 'first-move'; depth = 0; suggest() { return 4; } }
class RandomAi    implements Ai { name = 'random'; depth = 0; suggest(g) { const m = g.availableMoves(); return m[Math.floor(Math.random()*m.length)]!; } }
class PerfectAi   extends MinimaxAi { constructor() { super('perfect', 9, g => new HeuristicEvaluator().evaluate(g)); } }
```

**State and invariants.**
```
Game state: immutable board + nextPlayer + winner + history.
I1  board.length === 9; each cell ∈ {-1, 0, 1}.
I2  winner !== 0  ⟹  no further moves are legal.
I3  Two players have placed equal numbers of marks (or the first player has exactly one more).
I4  A game ends within 9 moves.
I5  The board is immutable — `play` returns a NEW game. This makes undo, AI search, and
    time-travel trivial (functional core), and it is the design choice that separates good answers.
AI invariants:
I6  A perfect AI never loses against any opponent (verifiable by a property test: 9! ≈ 362,880 games).
I7  The AI's move is always legal (∈ availableMoves).
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **Move ordering** for alpha-beta effectiveness: try centre, then corners, then edges. In tic-tac-toe with a full tree this is fast enough that pruning is not needed; with a depth limit it matters.
2. **Terminal position scoring with depth**: `10 - depth` for a win and `-10 + depth` for a loss, so the AI prefers winning *fast* and losing *slowly*. Without the depth term the AI is indifferent and makes silly moves.
3. **Pruning correctness**: you must return a score, not `undefined`, from a pruned node; and the alpha/beta update must be `alpha = max(alpha, best)` at MAX nodes and `beta = min(beta, worst)` at MIN nodes. A subtle bug here yields a *plausible but wrong* AI, which is hard to detect — property tests catch it.
4. **Determinism**: tie-break by lowest move index so the game is reproducible and tests are stable.
5. **The generalisation**: for larger boards (noughts and crosses, Connect-4, Go) you need **negamax**, **transposition tables**, **move ordering heuristics**, **iterative deepening**, and **killer/history heuristics**. Knowing these names, and that tic-tac-toe's full tree is only ~550k nodes with 9! ≈ 362,880 game sequences, is the depth signal.

**Extension points.**
1. `Ai` interface: random, first-move, greedy, minimax(depth), minimax + alpha-beta, negamax + transposition table, MCTS (UCT), neural-net policy.
2. **Undo/redo** via the immutable history (this is a Command/undo-stack in miniature — 11.1.9).
3. **Difficulty levels** = depth + evaluation function.
4. **Board size generalisation**: `WIN_LINES` generated for n×n (rows, columns, both diagonals) and diagonal-win-length variants (k-in-a-row).
5. **AI vs AI** for testing and for replay generation.
6. **Move history / notation export**, animation state.
7. **Timed moves** and a "thinking" state; a move timeout forfeits the game (a real game-rule concern).
8. **Statistics**: games played, win matrix, average moves, replay database keyed by position (a transposition table persisted).

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Immutability** and why (undo, search, and AI-vs-AI fall out for free).
- Correct minimax with a **terminal score that depends on depth**.
- Knowing that alpha-beta exists and roughly what it does (order of magnitude speedup), and *that pruning does not change the result* for a full-depth search.
- A **property test**: "the perfect AI never loses", "all 9! games terminate", "no illegal moves".
- The tie-break determinism detail.
- Volunteer, unprompted, that this generalises and what it needs (negamax, transposition tables, iterative deepening, MCTS).
