### 11.4.7 Notification service (fan-out, retries, preferences)

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Requirements.**
- `notify(userId, event)` where event is a domain fact (`order.placed`, `payment.failed`).
- Choose channels per user from **preferences**: per-event opt-in/opt-out, per-channel (email, SMS, push), quiet hours, per-channel overrides, locale, fallback order.
- Fan out to N channels; each delivery has its own status.
- **Retries** with backoff, **rate limits** per channel and per user, and a **DLQ**.
- Idempotency: the same (user, event, channel) must not be delivered twice.
- Templates per channel and locale; delivery receipts.

**Constraints.**
- A channel failure must not affect other channels or the caller.
- Never send twice for the same logical notification.
- A user with all channels disabled must not break the pipeline.
- Compliance: quiet hours, opt-out (including a hard global opt-out), unsubscribe links, and an audit trail of what was sent.

**API surface.**
```ts
type Channel = 'email' | 'sms' | 'push';
type NotificationEvent = 'order.placed' | 'payment.failed' | 'password.reset' | 'weekly.digest';

interface NotificationPreferences {
  isChannelEnabled(userId: UserId, event: NotificationEvent, channel: Channel): boolean;
  isQuietHours(userId: UserId, at: Date): boolean;
  fallbackOrder(event: NotificationEvent): readonly Channel[];     // e.g. push → email → sms
  localeFor(userId: UserId): Locale;
  rateLimitFor(channel: Channel, userId: UserId): RatePolicy;
}

interface ChannelSender {
  readonly channel: Channel;
  readonly idempotent: boolean;                 // can we re-send safely? (SMS/email mostly yes via provider keys)
  send(req: DeliveryRequest): Promise<DeliveryResult>;
}
type DeliveryResult = { status: 'sent'; providerId: string } | { status: 'suppressed'; reason: string }
                    | { status: 'failed'; code: string; retryable: boolean; message: string };

class NotificationService {
  constructor(
    private readonly prefs: NotificationPreferences,
    private readonly senders: Readonly<Record<Channel, ChannelSender>>,
    private readonly templates: TemplateRenderer,
    private readonly store: NotificationStore,        // delivery records + idempotency + audit
    private readonly rateLimiter: RateLimiter,       // 11.4.3 per channel and per user
    private readonly clock: Clock,
    private readonly metrics: Metrics,
  ) {}

  async notify(userId: UserId, event: NotificationEvent, payload: unknown): Promise<DispatchReport> {
    const at = this.clock.now();
    const channels = this.prefs.fallbackOrder(event).filter(c => this.prefs.isChannelEnabled(userId, event, c));
    if (channels.length === 0) return { userId, event, attempted: 0, sent: [], reason: 'all-channels-disabled' };
    // (1) The first channel that respects quiet hours and rate limits wins; the rest are the fallback.
    const attempts: DeliveryAttempt[] = [];
    for (const channel of channels) {
      const dedupeKey = `${userId}:${event}:${channel}:${eventIdOf(payload)}`;
      if (await this.store.alreadyHandled(dedupeKey)) { attempts.push({ channel, status: 'deduped' }); continue; }
      if (this.prefs.isQuietHours(userId, at)) {
        // Defer rather than drop — unless the event is critical (password reset: allow with a
        // different template that does not mention marketing content).
        if (!CRITICAL_EVENTS.has(event)) { attempts.push({ channel, status: 'deferred', until: endOfQuietHours(at) }); continue; }
      }
      const limit = await this.rateLimiter.tryAcquire(`notif:${channel}:${userId}`);
      if (!limit.allowed) { attempts.push({ channel, status: 'throttled', retryAfterMs: limit.retryAfterMs }); continue; }
      try {
        const body = this.templates.render(channel, event, payload, this.prefs.localeFor(userId));
        const res = await this.senders[channel].send({ to: await this.resolveAddress(userId, channel),
                                                       body, dedupeKey, idempotencyKey: dedupeKey });
        await this.store.record(dedupeKey, channel, res, at);        // SAME transaction as the send record
        attempts.push({ channel, ...res });
        if (res.status === 'sent') break;                             // first success wins; stop the cascade
      } catch (e) {
        const retryable = this.isRetryable(e, channel);
        await this.store.recordFailure(dedupeKey, channel, e, at, retryable);
        attempts.push({ channel, status: 'failed', retryable });
      }
    }
    this.metrics.count('notification.attempts', attempts.length, { event, status: attempts.at(-1)?.status ?? 'none' });
    return { userId, event, attempted: attempts.length, sent: attempts.filter(a => a.status === 'sent'), attempts };
  }
}
```

**Retry and delivery as a state machine + queue (the production-grade version).**

```ts
type DeliveryState = 'pending' | 'sent' | 'suppressed' | 'deferred' | 'failed' | 'dead';
// (11.1.11 State, expressed as data since the transitions are simple and enumerable.)
const DELIVERY_TRANSITIONS: Record<DeliveryState, Partial<Record<DeliveryEvent, DeliveryState>>> = {
  pending:    { attempt: 'pending', succeed: 'sent', fail: 'pending', expire: 'dead', suppress: 'suppressed' },
  sent:       {},                                                     // terminal
  suppressed: {},                                                     // terminal
  deferred:   { expire: 'pending', attempt: 'pending' },
  failed:     { attempt: 'pending', expire: 'dead' },                 // backoff-gated
  dead:       { replay: 'pending' },                                   // operator intervention
};

interface DeliveryAttempt { at: Date; channel: Channel; outcome: DeliveryResult | 'throttled' | 'deferred'; }

class DeliveryWorker {
  constructor(private readonly store: NotificationStore, private readonly senders: …,
              private readonly clock: Clock, private readonly queue: Queue, private readonly metrics: Metrics) {}
  async process(job: DeliveryJob): Promise<void> {
    const record = await this.store.lock(job.dedupeKey);            // SELECT … FOR UPDATE, or an atomic claim
    if (!record || record.state === 'sent' || record.state === 'dead') return;    // idempotent skip
    if (record.nextAttemptAt > this.clock.now()) return;              // backoff not elapsed
    if (record.attempts >= MAX_ATTEMPTS[record.channel]) {
      await this.store.transition(job.dedupeKey, 'expire', 'dead', this.clock.now());
      this.metrics.count('notification.dead', 1, { channel: record.channel, reason: 'attempts-exhausted' });
      await this.dlq.publish({ ...record, failure: record.lastError });      // human-visible
      return;
    }
    try {
      const res = await this.senders[record.channel].send(record.request);
      if (res.status === 'sent')   await this.store.transition(job.dedupeKey, 'succeed', 'sent', this.clock.now());
      else if (!res.retryable)     await this.store.transition(job.dedupeKey, 'suppress', 'suppressed', this.clock.now());
      else                         await this.store.transition(job.dedupeKey, 'fail', 'failed', this.clock.now());
    } catch (e) {
      await this.store.transition(job.dedupeKey, 'fail', 'failed', this.clock.now());
    }
  }
  backoffMs(attempt: number, channel: Channel): number {
    // Exponential backoff + FULL jitter, channel-aware (SMS should retry aggressively, email gently).
    const base = CHANNEL_BACKOFF[channel];
    return Math.random() * Math.min(base.maxMs, base.firstMs * 2 ** attempt);
  }
}
```

**State and invariants.**
```
Delivery states: pending → sent | suppressed | deferred | failed → (retry) pending → dead.
I1  At most ONE 'sent' per (user, event, channel, eventId).   <-- enforced by a UNIQUE index on dedupeKey
I2  Every transition is recorded with a timestamp and an attempt number (audit).
I3  A 'sent' delivery is never re-sent (the worker skips it).
I4  The cascade stops at the first successful channel.
I5  Opt-out is honoured for every channel and every event; a global opt-out suppresses everything
    except events legally required (e.g. a password reset).
I6  Quiet hours defer, they do not drop — except when deferral exceeds the event's TTL, in which case drop.
I7  Per-user and per-channel rate limits are enforced before every attempt.
I8  A channel failure never propagates to the caller of notify() (unless it was the ONLY channel and
    the event is critical, in which case the caller gets a report, not an exception).
I9  Templates are rendered per channel and locale; a missing template is a hard error in dev and a
    suppressed delivery with a metric in prod (never a crash in the worker loop).
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **Idempotency across retries AND cascades.** The dedupe key must include the channel (so a cascade to SMS after email is allowed) and the event id (so two distinct `order.placed` events are not deduped).
2. **The outbox/inbox problem** (11.3.6): `notify` is often called inside a transaction; the send must happen after commit. Enqueue in the outbox.
3. **Non-idempotent side effects.** Retrying a push notification is safe; retrying a webhook-style side effect may not be. Ask each `ChannelSender` whether it is idempotent, and use the provider's idempotency key where available.
4. **Preference resolution is a precedence problem**: global account setting > per-event > per-channel > per-event-channel > quiet hours > rate limit. Get the precedence order right and document it, or you will never explain why a user received something.
5. **The cascade vs broadcast decision**: does a user get *all* enabled channels or *the first successful* one? Most products want "the best one that works", some want "all"; make it a policy (11.1.10).
6. **Backpressure**: a burst of `order.placed` for a flash sale. Per-user rate limit + a global queue with bounded concurrency + a DLQ.
7. **Compliance**: unsubscribe must be immediate and effective on the next attempt; suppression records must persist; a deleted user's data must be purged from the audit trail.

**Extension points.**
1. `ChannelSender` — Slack, Teams, WhatsApp, push (FCM/APNs), in-app, webhooks, voice.
2. `TemplateRenderer` — per locale, per channel, with typed payloads, versioned templates, and A/B variants.
3. **Preference model** — extend with per-event importance, digest batching ("bundle all marketing into a daily digest"), frequency caps.
4. **Delivery policy**: cascade vs broadcast; priority queue (password reset jumps the queue); batching.
5. **Digest/batching mode** — aggregate events over a window and send one message (this changes the whole pipeline and is worth mentioning).
6. **Store**: outbox + inbox + delivery audit + dedupe unique index + dead-letter table.
7. **Delivery-status webhooks** from providers (delivered / bounced / complained) — bouncing addresses must be suppressed automatically.
8. **Per-tenant configuration** (multi-tenant: different senders, templates, and limits).
9. **Observability**: send rate, success/failure/suppressed/deferred/throttled counts per channel and event, latency per channel, dead-letter depth, fallback rate.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Isolation of channel failures** (one `try/catch` per channel, never a shared promise that fails together).
- **Idempotency** with a concrete dedupe key and where it is enforced (a unique index, not a check).
- The **cascade vs broadcast** decision, stated explicitly, with quiet-hours and preference precedence.
- **Retries with backoff + jitter** and a DLQ, and the distinction between retryable and permanent failures.
- Recognising the **outbox** requirement (you cannot send inside a transaction).
- Knowing this is an **Observer/pub-sub consumer** (11.1.8/11.1.17) and that the handler must be idempotent because it will receive duplicates.

---

### 11.4.8 Logger

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** `log(level, message, fields)`. Levels (trace/debug/info/warn/error/fatal). Structured logging with arbitrary fields. Async writing. Multiple sinks (console, file, remote aggregator). Level filtering at runtime without restart. Request correlation. Hot-path performance (logging must not dominate a hot function).

**Constraints.**
- Logging is on the hot path; it must be cheap and must never throw.
- Output must be machine-parseable (JSON) — humans can read JSON.
- Must not lose critical (error+) messages when a process dies.
- Log volume control: sampling, rate limiting for repeated errors, and level control per namespace.

**API surface.**
```ts
type Level = 'trace' | 'debug' | 'info' | 'warn' | 'error' | 'fatal';
const LEVELS: Record<Level, number> = { trace: 10, debug: 20, info: 30, warn: 40, error: 50, fatal: 60 };

interface LogRecord {
  ts: string; level: Level; msg: string; ns: string;
  fields: Record<string, unknown>; traceId?: string; spanId?: string; env: string; version: string;
}

interface LogSink { write(r: LogRecord): void; flush?(): Promise<void>; close?(): Promise<void>; }

// FLUENT API: the builder lets you attach fields once and reuse them (child loggers).
interface Logger {
  readonly ns: string;
  trace(m: string, f?: Fields): void; debug(m: string, f?: Fields): void;
  info(m: string, f?: Fields): void;  warn(m: string, f?: Fields): void;
  error(m: string, f?: Fields): void; fatal(m: string, f?: Fields): void;
  child(ns: string, f?: Fields): Logger;          // "request-scoped" logger with correlation ids
  isLevelEnabled(l: Level): boolean;
}

class LoggerFactory implements Logger {                                        // Logger = LogRecord + threshold
  constructor(private readonly ns: string, private readonly base: Fields, private readonly threshold: () => number) {}
  info(m: string, f?: Fields): void {
    if (LEVELS.info < this.threshold()) return;               // (1) EARLY EXIT: no allocation when disabled
    this.emit({ ts: nowIso(), level: 'info', msg: m, ns: this.ns, fields: { ...this.base, ...f },
                traceId: this.base.traceId as string, env: envName(), version: appVersion() });
  }
  child(ns: string, f?: Fields): Logger { return new LoggerFactory(`${this.ns}.${ns}`, { ...this.base, ...f }, this.threshold); }
  private emit(r: LogRecord): void {
    try { this.buffer.push(r); this.maybeDrain(); }            // (2) NEVER let logging throw
    catch { /* a logger that throws is worse than a logger that loses a line */ }
  }
}
```

**The pipeline, in one class.** This is the part that shows you have thought about production.

```ts
class LogPipeline {
  private buffer: LogRecord[] = [];
  private draining = false;
  constructor(
    private readonly threshold: () => number,                          // dynamic: reads process.env.LOG_LEVEL each time
    private readonly sinks: LogSink[],
    private readonly batchSize = 200,
    private readonly flushMs = 1000,
    private readonly maxBuffer = 10_000,
    private readonly samplers: Sampler[] = [],
    private readonly deduper: RepeatDedupe = new RepeatDedupe(),
  ) {}

  log(r: LogRecord): void {
    if (LEVELS[r.level] < this.threshold()) return;
    if (this.buffer.length >= this.maxBuffer) {
      // (1) BUFFER OVERFLOW POLICY: drop the LOWEST severity records, count the drops, and keep going.
      //     Never block the application thread on a slow sink.
      this.evictLowest();
      this.droppedCount++;
    }
    const deduped = this.deduper.apply(r);            // (2) "error repeated 5000 times in 3s" -> one record + a count
    if (!deduped) return;
    for (const s of this.samplers) if (!s.shouldSample(r)) return;   // (3) sampling for high-volume, low-value logs
    this.buffer.push(r);
    if (this.buffer.length >= this.batchSize) void this.drain();
    else if (!this.draining) this.scheduleDrain();
  }

  async drain(): Promise<void> {                        // SINGLE-FLIGHT: one drain at a time
    if (this.draining) return;
    this.draining = true;
    const batch = this.buffer.splice(0, this.buffer.length);
    try {
      await Promise.allSettled(this.sinks.map(async s => { s.writeBatch(batch); await s.flush?.(); }));
    } finally {
      this.draining = false;
      if (this.buffer.length) this.scheduleDrain();    // more arrived while draining: go again
    }
  }
  private scheduleDrain() { setTimeout(() => void this.drain(), this.flushMs).unref?.(); }
  private evictLowest() {
    let worstIdx = 0;
    for (let i = 1; i < this.buffer.length; i++) if (LEVELS[this.buffer[i]!.level] < LEVELS[this.buffer[worstIdx]!.level]) worstIdx = i;
    this.buffer.splice(worstIdx, 1);
  }
}
```

**The sinks and the decisions they force.**

```ts
class JsonLineSink implements LogSink {                       // NDJSON to stdout (12-factor)
  writeBatch(records: LogRecord[]): void {
    for (const r of records) process.stdout.write(JSON.stringify(r) + '\n');   // never console.log a string
  }
}
class AggregatorSink implements LogSink {                     // Datadog / Loki / Elasticsearch
  constructor(private readonly client: MetricsClient, private readonly apiKey: string) {}
  writeBatch(records: LogRecord[]): void {
    this.client.submit(records.map(r => ({ metric: `log.${r.level}`, tags: [`env:${r.env}`, `ns:${r.ns}`],
      points: [{ value: 1, timestamp: r.ts }], message: r.msg, fields: r.fields }));
  }
}
class FileSink implements LogSink { /* buffered writes, rotation by size/time, retention */ }
class RingBufferSink implements LogSink {                    // keep the last N records in memory so an error
  constructor(private readonly cap = 1000) {}               // report can include the preceding context
  recent(): LogRecord[] { return this.buf.slice(); }
}

// SENSITIVE DATA: the most important production concern in this whole design.
class RedactingSink implements LogSink {
  constructor(private readonly inner: LogSink, private readonly rules: RedactionRule[]) {}
  writeBatch(records: LogRecord[]): void {
    this.inner.writeBatch(records.map(r => ({ ...r, msg: redact(r.msg, this.rules),
                                              fields: redactFields(r.fields, this.rules) })));
  }
}
// Rules: keys matching /password|token|secret|authorization|cookie|card|cvv|ssn/i -> '[redacted]';
// emails/phones/IBANs -> hashed or masked; never log a full card or a raw token, even "in dev".
```

**Context propagation and correlation.**

```ts
// (a) AsyncLocalStorage is the correct answer in Node: the request-scoped logger is available
//     anywhere in the async call tree without threading it through signatures.
import { AsyncLocalStorage } from 'node:async_hooks';
const als = new AsyncLocalStorage<{ traceId: string; userId: string; logger: Logger }>();

// HTTP middleware:
export function requestContext(req: IncomingMessage, res: ServerResponse, next: () => void) {
  const traceId = (req.headers['x-btrace'] as string) ?? randomUUID();
  als.run({ traceId, userId: (req as any).userId ?? 'anon',
            logger: root.child('http', { traceId, method: req.method, path: req.url! }) }, next);
}

// Anywhere in the code, deeply nested, in a completely unrelated module:
const logger = als.getStore()?.logger ?? rootLogger;
logger.info('charge attempted', { amountMinor: m.minorUnits });
// (b) The honest alternative: explicit context parameters (AsyncLocalStorage / OpenTelemetry context).
```

**State and invariants.**
```
I1  A log call never throws (wrapped in try/catch; the application must not fail because of logging).
I2  Level filtering happens BEFORE record construction (early exit) so disabled levels are ~free.
I3  At most one drain in flight (single-flight) → no interleaved batch corruption.
I4  The buffer is bounded; overflow drops the lowest severity and increments a dropped counter.
I5  Records are immutable once created (never mutate a record already handed to a sink).
I6  Every record carries: timestamp (UTC ISO), level, namespace, message, env, version, and traceId
    where a request context exists.
I7  Critical levels (error+) are flushed on process exit AND flushed eagerly (not just on the timer),
    because a crash is exactly when you need the log.
I8  No secret ever reaches a sink (redaction is applied to every sink, not per call site).
I9  Repeated identical errors are deduplicated with a count, not silently dropped.
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **Performance.** The cost of a disabled log line must be ~0: check the threshold before building anything, never allocate fields objects eagerly, and keep the hot path free of `JSON.stringify` (that happens at drain time).
2. **Backpressure and loss.** A slow remote aggregator must not block or crash the app. Bounded buffer + drop policy + a dropped counter + an alert.
3. **Crash safety.** `process.on('exit')`/`uncaughtException` handlers must flush synchronously; async flush in an exit handler is unreliable.
4. **Ordering.** Multiple sinks and multiple processes: logs are not globally ordered. Never depend on log order in a test; assert on fields instead.
5. **Redaction.** Doing it at the call site fails the first time someone logs a raw request body. Do it in the sink layer.
6. **Volume control.** A log line in a per-request loop at `info` will cost you money. Samplers and dedupe are the tools.
7. **The "which level?" problem.** A convention (and enforcement) is worth stating: `info` = business-significant state change, `warn` = recoverable anomaly, `error` = this operation failed and a human may need to look, `fatal` = the process cannot continue.

**Extension points.**
1. `LogSink`: stdout, file (rotation), syslog, Loki/ELK, Datadog, Sentry, OpenTelemetry log backend, and a test sink that captures records in an array.
2. **Level filtering per namespace** (`{"order": "debug", "payment": "warn"}`) and dynamic reload — useful because paying for a debug flag in production is how you diagnose an incident.
3. **Structured tracing**: auto-attach span/trace IDs from the OTel context.
4. **Sampling policies**: by level, by namespace, by rate (10% of debug), by "only for these request IDs".
5. **Error deduplication and aggregation** ("this error occurred 12,043 times in the last hour") — cheap and extremely high value.
6. **PII redaction rules** per data class, with an allowlist of safe fields.
7. **Child loggers** with inherited fields (the fluent `child()` above).
8. **Log-based metrics** — emit a counter alongside a record when a message represents a business event.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Async batching with a bounded buffer and a single-flight drain** — this is the difference between "I have used pino" and "I understand logging".
- **Early exit before allocation** for performance.
- **Never throws**, and crash-time flushing.
- **Redaction in the sink layer**, and knowing what must never be logged.
- **Correlation IDs propagated via AsyncLocalStorage / OTel context**, not by threading a parameter.
- Awareness of **volume control** (sampling, dedupe) and of **level discipline**.
- Recognises this as **an Observer/Singleton-adjacent problem** and can defend a single shared pipeline with injected threshold and sinks, rather than a global mutable singleton.

---

### 11.4.9 Library management system

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirements.** Search the catalogue (by title, author, ISBN, subject); add/remove a copy; borrow; return; renew; reserve; list a member's loans; apply late fees; notify on due dates. Multiple copies per title. Membership types with limits.

**Constraints.**
- A **title** (bibliographic record) vs a **copy** (physical item): one title, N copies; only a copy is borrowed.
- Reservation queue ordering (FIFO, with an expiry window).
- Borrowing limits per membership type and no-reservation-if-unavailable rules.
- Overdue tracking and fee calculation; fines can block borrowing.
- Concurrency: two members reserving the last copy simultaneously.

```ts
// ── The aggregate. Title vs Copy is the modelling insight everyone must get right.
class BookTitle {
  private constructor(readonly id: TitleId, readonly isbn: string, readonly title: string,
                      readonly authors: readonly string[], readonly subjects: readonly string[],
                      private copies: Map<CopyId, BookCopy>) {}
  static register(id: TitleId, isbn: string, title: string, authors: string[], subjects: string[]): BookTitle {
    if (!isbn.trim()) throw new MissingIsbn();
    return new BookTitle(id, isbn, title, authors, subjects, new Map());
  }
  addCopy(copyId: CopyId): void {
    if (this.copies.has(copyId)) throw new DuplicateCopy(copyId);
    this.copies.set(copyId, BookCopy.at(copyId));
  }
  removeCopy(copyId: CopyId): BookCopy {
    const c = this.copies.get(copyId); if (!c) throw new NoSuchCopy(copyId);
    if (c.status === 'on-loan') throw new CopyOnLoan(copyId);      // invariant: cannot remove a borrowed copy
    this.copies.delete(copyId); return c;
  }
  availableCopies(): BookCopy[] {
    return [...this.copies.values()].filter(c => c.status === 'available');
  }
  reservationQueue(): readonly Reservation[] { /* per-title FIFO with expiry */ }
}

type CopyStatus = 'available' | 'on-loan' | 'reserved' | 'lost' | 'in-repair';
class BookCopy {
  private constructor(readonly id: CopyId, private _status: CopyStatus,
                      private loan?: Loan, private reservation?: Reservation) {}
  static at(id: CopyId) { return new BookCopy(id, 'available'); }
  get status() { return this._status; }
  get currentLoan() { return this.loan; }
  borrowBy(member: MemberId, policy: LoanPolicy, at: Date): Loan {
    if (this._status !== 'available') throw new CopyNotAvailable(this.id, this._status);
    this.loan = Loan.open(this.id, member, at, policy.loanPeriodDays(at), at, policy.renewLimit);
    this._status = 'on-loan'; this.reservation = undefined;
    return this.loan;
  }
  closeLoan(at: Date): ReturnOutcome {                                   // (11.1.11 State)
    if (!this.loan) throw new NotOnLoan(this.id);
    const days = daysBetween(this.loan.borrowedAt, at);
    const overdueDays = Math.max(0, days - this.loan.dueAt.getTime() / 86_400_000 + this.loan.dueAt.getTime() / 86_400_000);
    const lateDays = Math.max(0, daysBetween(this.loan.dueAt, at));
    const outcome = { loan: this.loan, lateDays, fine: Money.of(lateDays * FINE_PER_DAY, 'USD') };
    this.loan = undefined;
    this._status = this.reservation ? 'reserved' : 'available';
    return outcome;
  }
  renew(at: Date, today: Date): Loan {
    if (!this.loan) throw new NotOnLoan(this.id);
    if (this.loan.renewalsUsed >= this.loan.renewLimit) throw new RenewalLimitReached(this.loan.renewLimit);
    if (this.reservation) throw new CopyReservedForAnotherMember();      // fairness: the queue is absolute
    const extended = this.loan.extend(at, this.loan.periodDays);
    if (extended.dueAt > today) throw new CannotRenewWithoutReservation(this.loan.dueAt);
    return this.loan;
  }
  reserveFor(member: MemberId, at: Date, expiresAfterDays: number): Reservation {
    if (this._status === 'on-loan' && this.loan && this.loan.memberId === member)
      throw new AlreadyHasThisCopy(this.id);
    this.reservation = { copyId: this.id, memberId: member, at, expiresAt: addDays(at, expiresAfterDays) };
    this._status = 'reserved';
    return this.reservation;
  }
}

class Member {                                     // aggregate root
  constructor(readonly id: MemberId, readonly tier: MembershipTier, private fines: Money = Money.zero('USD'),
              private currentLoans: Loan[] = [], readonly blockedUntil?: Date) {}
  borrow(copy: BookCopy, title: BookTitle, policy: LoanPolicy, at: Date): Loan {
    if (this.blockedUntil && this.blockedUntil > at) throw new MemberBlocked(this.id, this.blockedUntil);
    if (this.currentLoans.length >= policy.maxLoans(this.tier)) throw new LoanLimitReached(policy.maxLoans(this.tier));
    if (this.fines.compare(policy.blockThreshold(this.tier)) > 0) throw class FinesOwed extends Error {}
    if (this.currentLoans.some(l => l.titleId === title.id)) throw new AlreadyBorrowedThisTitle(title.id);
    const loan = copy.borrowBy(this.id, policy, at);
    this.currentLoans.push(loan);
    return loan;
  }
  returnBook(loanId: LoanId, copy: BookCopy, at: Date): ReturnOutcome {
    const loan = this.currentLoans.find(l => l.id === loanId);
    if (!loan) throw new NoSuchLoan(loanId);
    const outcome = copy.closeLoan(at);
    if (outcome.fine.compare(Money.zero('USD')) > 0) this.fines = this.fines.add(outcome.fine);
    this.currentLoans = this.currentLoans.filter(l => l.id !== loanId);
    return outcome;
  }
}
```

**Search as its own concern (this is the part most candidates under-design).**

```ts
interface CatalogueSearch {                          // a PORT, so the backing store can change
  search(q: SearchQuery): Promise<Page<TitleSummary>>;
  byIsbn(isbn: string): Promise<TitleSummary | null>;
  suggestions(prefix: string, limit: number): Promise<TitleSummary[]>;
}
class PostgresCatalogueSearch implements CatalogueSearch {
  // Full-text search + trigram indexes + a denormalised searchable view. Relevance ranking,
  // faceting by subject, pagination with a cursor (not OFFSET for deep pages).
}
class SqlSuggestionIndex implements CatalogueSearch { /* normalised keys, prefix search, popularity ranking */ }
class ElasticCatalogueSearch implements CatalogueSearch { /* analyzers, synonyms, fuzzy matching, facets */ }
```

**State and invariants.**
```
BookCopy: available → on-loan → available | reserved
                  ↘ lost / in-repair (out of circulation; not lendable, not reservable)
Loan:     open → returned | overdue (a view over open + dueAt < now) ; renewalsUsed <= renewLimit
Member:   active → blocked (fines over threshold or admin action) → active
Reservation: pending → fulfilled | expired | cancelled
Invariants:
I1  A copy has at most ONE open loan and at most ONE active reservation.
I2  Only an 'available' copy can be borrowed.
I3  A copy cannot be removed from a title while on loan.
I4  A member never holds two loans of the same title.
I5  concurrentLimit(tier) is never exceeded; fines above blockThreshold(tier) block borrowing.
I6  Fines accumulate exactly once per return (not per day while outstanding) and are computed from
    dueAt to returnedAt — never rounded to a day incorrectly.
I7  Reservation queues are FIFO and expire after a fixed window, releasing the copy back to 'available'.
I8  A renewal is refused if the copy is reserved by someone else.
```

**The tricky part.**
1. **The availability/reservation race.** Two members reserve the last copy. Solution: a unique constraint per (title, queue position) or a serialisable transaction / `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on the title row, plus a uniqueness constraint on (copyId) for the active reservation. Interviewers like this because it combines a domain rule with a concurrency answer.
2. **The reservation queue expiry vs fulfilment.** On return, if there is a queue, the copy goes to 'reserved' and the next member is notified with a **hold window**; if they do not collect in time, the copy is released to the next. This is a second state machine inside a method — a common place to accidentally lose copies.
3. **Renewal rules.** Refuse renewal if reserved; refuse if there are no available copies of that title (an "awaiting pickup" nuance); cap renewals; and extend from the *current due date*, not from today (a classic bug).
4. **Fines.** Decide: fine at return time vs accruing daily. Decide a cap. Decide whether a payment partially clears a block. Decide whether fines block borrowing at all or just borrowing of certain titles.
5. **Search relevance.** Title/author ordering, transliteration, "Smith, John" vs "John Smith", ISBN normalisation (hyphens), and suggesting a search index (Elasticsearch/Meilisearch/Postgres FTS) rather than `LIKE '%x%'` — and knowing the trade-off (index staleness, operational cost).

**Extension points.**
1. **Membership policies** as a `LoanPolicy` strategy: standard loan period, max loans, max renewals, fine rate, fine cap, block threshold — all data, per tier.
2. **Reservation policies**: queue mode (FIFO / priority by membership tier / hold-first-come), hold window length, max active reservations.
3. **Search backends** behind the port; a **suggestion index**; **faceting** by subject/author/language/availability.
4. **Fines and payments**: online payment, waiving, and an audit trail.
5. **Inter-library loan** (a different aggregate boundary with an external system and an anti-corruption layer).
6. **Overdue workflow**: reminders before due date, at due date, and while overdue; each one an event-driven notification (11.4.7) — and it must be **idempotent**.
7. **Acquisitions and inventory**: purchase orders, MARC records, copies as assets with locations.
8. **Events for integration**: `CopyBorrowed`, `CopyReturned`, `ReservationFulfilled`, `FineAccrued` — consumed by notifications, analytics, and audit.

**What the interviewer scores.**
- **Title vs Copy** modelling (the single biggest point in this problem).
- Knowing that **search is a separate concern** behind a port, and that `LIKE` is not a search engine.
- The **reservation queue and the hold window** as a state machine, including the expiry path.
- **Renewal refusal when reserved** and extending from the correct date.
- Fines: when they accrue, the cap, and the blocking rule.
- Concurrent reservation of the last copy — a real answer (constraint + serialisable tx), not "use a lock everywhere".
- Membership tiers as **policy data**, not `if` statements.

---

---

# 11.4 LLD WALKTHROUGHS

> How to run each walkthrough: spend **35–40 minutes** and narrate in this exact order. State requirements before you draw anything; ask clarifying questions; propose the shape *before* the code; then name the tricky part and the concurrency story; then the extension points; then what you are optimising for. Never open with a class diagram.

---

## Walkthrough 1 — Parking lot

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements (2 min).** Read the prompt out loud, then confirm: (a) multiple floors, rows, numbered slots; (b) three vehicle types and the size-compatibility rule (motorbike/compact fit anything, large needs a large slot); (c) "first available" search with a size filter; (d) a ticket on entry; (e) dynamic pricing per vehicle type and time. **Ask:** "Do you need reservations, valet, or multiple lots?" Getting a "no" saves you 20 minutes; getting a "yes" changes the slot states.

**Step 2: Constraints (3 min).** Slots have fixed capacity; you cannot split a slot. Slot size is a *minimum* requirement. Entry must be atomic. Fees are computed at exit, so you must remember the entry time (the clock is injected). Say aloud: **"the two hard problems are (1) which slot and (2) making that choice safe under concurrency."**

**Step 3: API surface (4 min).**
```ts
interface ParkingLot {
  park(v: Vehicle): Ticket;
  unpark(t: Ticket): FeeReceipt;
  findFreeSlots(count: number, size?: SlotSize): readonly SlotRef[];
  findVehicleByTicket(t: Ticket): Vehicle | null;
  findSlotsBySize(size: SlotSize): readonly SlotRef[];
  findVehiclesByColor(c: Color): readonly Vehicle[];
  availability(): ReadonlyMap<SlotSize, number>;
}
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration (5 min).**
```
ParkingLot 1 ──owns──▶ Floor 1..n ──owns──▶ Row 1..m ──owns──▶ Slot
    │  allocates via                                    │ occupies
    ▼                                                   ▼
SlotAllocator (Strategy)                            Vehicle
    └ implementations: FirstFit | Nearest | Cheapest | EV | Accessible
RateStrategy (Strategy) ─ per-vehicle-type / per-time-window pricing
Ticket ─────────────── ties a Vehicle to a SlotRef + issuedAt (the ONLY bridge between entry and exit)
```
Then explain the direction of the dependency: the allocator *asks* the lot's floors for slots; the slot is what enforces the invariant. The allocator never mutates anything — it only *selects*.

**Step 5: State and invariants (5 min).** Draw the one state machine that matters:
```
Slot:  FREE ──occupy(v)──▶ OCCUPIED(v) ──vacate()──▶ FREE
Ticket: ISSUED ──settle──▶ CLOSED        (and ISSUED ──void──▶ CLOSED if payment fails)
```
Invariants you must say out loud: at most one occupant per slot; `slot.size >= requiredSize(vehicle.type)`; a ticket maps to exactly one occupied slot; **find-slot and occupy-slot are one atomic unit**; unpark frees the slot before settlement so a payment failure never holds a bay hostage.

**Step 6: The tricky part (10 min).**
1. **Atomicity.** The naive `findFreeSlot()` then `occupy()` races. Answer: the check-and-set belongs *inside* the slot (`Slot.occupy` throws if occupied), plus a transaction around slot+index+ticket. Give the DB version too: `UPDATE slots SET vehicle=? WHERE ref=? AND vehicle IS NULL` and check the affected-row count.
2. **Slot selection is a strategy, not a hard-coded loop.** Show `SlotAllocator` with `FirstFit` and one alternative, and explain that "first available" is a *requirement* while "nearest to the lift" is a *preference*.
3. **Index the searches.** `findFreeSlots` is O(slots) if you scan; keep a `Set<SlotRef>` of free slots partitioned by size per floor and update it on occupy/vacate — O(1) thereafter. `findVehiclesByColor` needs a `Map<Color, Set<SlotRef>>`. An interviewer asking about complexity here is a good sign.
4. **Fee rounding** — round *up* to the started hour and document it; someone will ask.

**Step 7: Extension points (5 min).** Reservation with a hold window (adds a `RESERVED` slot state and an expiry); multi-lot network; valet; tier-priority allocation; occupancy analytics via domain events; payment gateway behind a port with the ticket id as the idempotency key.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** You found the race without prompting. You put the two variation axes behind interfaces. You modelled `Slot` as the invariant owner rather than a `Map<SlotId, Vehicle>` in the lot. You gave complexity for the queries and offered an index. You can answer "what would you store in the database and where is the transaction boundary?" — because a purely in-memory answer is the standard giveaway of an unprepared candidate.

---

## Walkthrough 2 — LRU cache

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** `get`, `put`, `delete`, capacity bound, and capacity measured in entries or in total value size. Ask: "concurrent access? TTL? distributed?" A TTL request changes the design materially.

**Step 2: Constraints.** **O(1) get and O(1) put.** That single constraint eliminates naive array scanning. Bounded memory. In production: hit ratio is the metric that decides whether the cache earns its keep.

**Step 3: API surface.** As in 11.4.2. Include `peek` deliberately and explain *why you added it* — "so a monitoring read does not change eviction order."

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.** One class, one backing store:
```
LRUCache { capacity, map: Map<K,V> }
  get(k)    → has? (delete+set to promote) : return value | miss
  put(k,v)  → exists? delete : (size ≥ capacity ? evict map.keys().next() : —) ; set
  peek(k)   → map.get(k)                // NO promotion
  keys()    → [...map.keys()]           // LRU → MRU
```
Explain the invariant: **`Map`'s iteration order is insertion order, so recency order *is* insertion order provided you `delete` before `set`.** Note that re-`set`ting an existing key does *not* move it to the end in JS — that is the detail that separates a working implementation from a subtly broken one.

**Step 5: State and invariants.** (1) `size <= capacity` always; (2) iteration order == recency, least-recent first; (3) a miss does not reorder; (4) re-putting an existing key does not grow the size; (5) capacity-by-weight evicts repeatedly until under budget.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
- `peek` must not promote.
- **Async loader coalescing**: N concurrent misses for the same key must produce one load (single-flight with a `finally` that always clears the inflight map — otherwise a rejected load is cached as a permanent rejection).
- **Stampede on expiry**: identical TTLs on many keys means they all expire together. Jitter the TTL.
- **Negative caching** to stop key-proliferation attacks from filling the cache with misses.
- **Capacity by weight**, and evicting more than one entry to get under budget.
- **Memory estimate**: a 10,000-entry cache of 1 KB values is 10 MB plus Map overhead; if `maxWeight` is a concern, weigh on insertion.

**Step 7: Extension points.** Pluggable `EvictionPolicy` (LRU/LFU/SLRU/TinyLFU/TTL); a `Loader` for read-through; stats provider; multi-tier (L1 process, L2 Redis, L3 origin) with a coherence policy; compression of large values; an admission policy so one-hit-wonders do not evict useful entries.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** O(1) on both operations with the reason stated. The `delete`-before-`set` subtlety. Volunteers async coalescing and stampede protection. Offers LFU/SLRU and the tiered variant when asked about production. States complexity per method.

---

## Walkthrough 3 — Rate limiter

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** Limit requests per key over a window. Ask four questions: *what is the key?* (IP / user / tenant+route), *is it per-instance or cluster-wide?*, *fail open or closed when the store is down?*, *do you need burst tolerance?* These four answers determine the design.

**Step 2: Constraints.** O(1) memory per key and O(1) time per request. Atomicity across processes. Exactness is usually not worth the cost.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
interface RateLimiter {
  tryAcquire(key: string, cost?: number): Promise<Decision>;
}
interface Decision {
  allowed: boolean; limit: number; remaining: number;
  retryAfterMs: number; resetAt?: number; policy: string;
}
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
RateLimiter (interface)
  ├─ FixedWindowCounter      map<key,{window,count}>            O(1)  boundary burst 2×  ← naive
  ├─ SlidingWindowLog         map<key, number[]>                 exact  O(window) memory/key
  ├─ SlidingWindowCounter    map<key,{cur,prev,win}>            O(1)  ← production default
  ├─ TokenBucket             map<key,{tokens,lastRefill}>       O(1)  ← best fit for APIs
  └─ LeakyBucketQueue        queue with a bounded drain rate    ← partner APIs / serverless
RedisLimiter: implements RateLimiter over a Lua script (atomic)  ← distributed
```
Narrate the progression as *increasing fidelity*: the fixed window is simple and wrong at boundaries; the sliding log is right and expensive; the sliding window counter and the token bucket are the industry compromise.

**Step 5: State and invariants.** Per key, O(1) state. Never more than `limit` in any window (within ±1 for the sliding counter). The acquire must be atomic per key. `retryAfterMs` must be a valid upper bound. **Idle keys must be evicted** or state grows without bound.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **The boundary burst**, with a concrete example: 10 requests at 10:59:59 and 10 at 11:00:00 = 20 in 2 seconds.
2. **The distributed atomicity race** — a read-then-write in Redis lets N concurrent requests all pass the check. Fix: a **Lua script** (or a single atomic `INCR` + conditional `EXPIRE` for the fixed window). This is the single most important implementation detail in the problem.
3. **Fail-open vs fail-closed** — public abuse protection fails open (or falls back to a local limiter); billing/quota fails closed. State which you chose and why.
4. **Hierarchical limits** — check per-user *and* per-tenant; reject if either fails.
5. **Rate vs concurrency vs quota.** A rate limiter caps requests/second; a **concurrency limiter** (semaphore) caps *in-flight* work; a **quota** caps a long-window total. For expensive endpoints and for serverless, concurrency limiting is often the right one.
6. **Memory growth** from per-key state — evict on idle, or use Redis TTLs.

**Step 7: Extension points.** Cost-weighted acquisition (a search costs 5); adaptive refill based on observed latency/error rate; reserved exemptions (explicitly, with audit); rejection semantics with `Retry-After` and `X-RateLimit-*`; metrics per key class and an alert on rejection spikes (a DDoS signal); edge-level quota via API gateway plus a local defence-in-depth limiter.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Three-plus algorithms with the trade-off between them. Token bucket advocated as the sensible default. The Redis Lua atomicity insight. The fail-open/closed decision. Awareness of the memory leak. Rate vs concurrency vs quota distinction.

---

## Walkthrough 4 — Elevator

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** `request(floor)`, `move(direction)`, `next()`, plus the system-level questions: how many cars, how many floors, and **what dispatch policy**? The policy question is the interview in disguise — candidates who only build one car miss the point.

**Step 2: Constraints.** No reversal with passengers inside; doors only open when stopped at a stop floor; request coalescing; **no starvation** of far requests; capacity limits.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
class ElevatorCar { status(): CarStatus; requestFromHall(floor: number, dir: Direction): void;
                    requestFromCab(floor: number): void; tick(): Promise<void>;
                    enter(p: Passenger): void; exit(p: Passenger): void; }
class ElevatorSystem { call(floor: number, dir: Direction): void; step(): void; status(): CarStatus[]; }
interface DispatchStrategy { select(req: Request, cars: readonly ElevatorCar[]): ElevatorCar | null; }
// RoundRobin | FirstComeFirstServed | LeastWaiting | SCAN | DestinationControl | ZoneBased
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.** Deliberately split into three parts and say so:
```
ElevatorSystem ──owns──▶ ElevatorCar (1..n)         MECHANICS: motion, doors, capacity, safety
       │                    └── uses ──▶ DispatchStrategy   POLICY: who answers this hall call
       └── (a DispatchStrategy is a Strategy because the algorithm changes by deployment,
            not by state; that is the difference from State)
ElevatorCar ──▶ a State machine (idle | moving | doors-open | maintenance | fire-alarm)
```
The car exposes only legal operations: `requestFromCab` throws while moving; `enter`/`exit` throw unless the doors are open. **The illegal operations do not exist** — that is the payoff of the state pattern.

**Step 5: State and invariants.**
```
idle ⇄ moving ⇄ doors-open        (+ maintenance, fire-alarm as overriding states)
I1 doors never open while moving
I2 no reversal with passengers inside
I3 no reversal while a request exists behind the car
I4 |passengers| <= capacity; boarding only in doors-open
I5 never moves past the top/bottom floor
I6 at most one pending stop per floor (a Set, not a list)
I7 every request is eventually served or explicitly rejected (no silent drops)
```
Draw the transition table — it is the clearest artifact you can produce in this problem.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **The reversal rule**, stated precisely: the car continues while there are passengers heading that way *or* a hall call exists in that direction; only when neither holds, and the car is empty, does it reverse. This is the answer that gets the offer.
2. **Fairness vs efficiency** — SCAN is efficient but asymmetric; a look-ahead (collective-control) scheduler that plans all trips is the sophisticated answer.
3. **Destination control** — passengers choose their floor *before* boarding, which changes the problem from "which floor next" to "group trips into a minimum number of shafts", and enables energy-aware scheduling.
4. **Safety overrides** (fire alarm, maintenance, door sensor fault) as **prioritised transitions in the state machine**, not `if` statements in the tick loop.
5. **Shaft conflict** with multiple cars: two cars cannot pass each other in one shaft.

**Step 7: Extension points.** More `DispatchStrategy` implementations; express/local elevators (a routing constraint); fire/safety mode; accessibility (door width, dwell time, announcements); energy optimisation (regenerative trip pairing); telemetry for ETA prediction; a discrete-event simulator to compare dispatch policies.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Mechanics separated from policy. The reversal rules exactly right. A state machine with a transition table. `Set`-based coalescing. Destination control mentioned as how real buildings work. Fairness treated as a requirement.

---

## Walkthrough 5 — Splitwise / expense sharing

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** `addExpense(payer, amount, participants, splitMode)`, `settleUp(user)`, `balances(group)`, and record settlements. Split modes: equal, exact, percentage, shares. Ask: **multi-currency? recurring expenses? mid-history group changes?** Multi-currency changes the model from a single `Money` to a rate-stamped conversion per expense.

**Step 2: Constraints.** Money is integer minor units, never float. Balances must be **incremental** (a recompute from history is O(n·m) and does not survive interview scrutiny). Every split must sum *exactly* to the expense total — no lost cents.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
class Splitwise {
  addExpense(cmd: AddExpense): Expense;
  deleteExpense(id: ExpenseId): void;                 // requires unrecord — a real requirement
  balances(groupId: GroupId): Map<UserId, Money>;
  settleUp(groupId: GroupId): Settlement[];           // minimal-ish transfers
  settle(cmd: Settle): void;
}
interface SplitStrategy { shares(amount: Money, participants: readonly UserId[], input?: SplitInput): Map<UserId, Money>; }
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
Splitwise (application service)
  ├─ Ledger  ── net: Map<UserId, Money>          incremental balances; sum(net) === 0
  ├─ SplitStrategy: Equal | Exact | Percentage | Shares
  ├─ Expense (entity, identity = id)  ── shares: ReadonlyMap<UserId, Money>
  └─ Money (value object: minor units + currency)
Settlement is modelled as an Expense (from → to) — so settlement history is auditable and reversible.
```
Point out the design choice that matters: **the ledger is a derived projection maintained incrementally**, and the invariant `Σ balances = 0` is the check that proves the whole model is coherent.

**Step 5: State and invariants.** (1) `Σ net = 0` always; (2) sign convention documented (positive = owed); (3) every expense's shares sum exactly to the amount; (4) all amounts in a group share a currency, or an FX rate + timestamp is stored per expense; (5) applying all suggested settlements zeroes every balance; (6) transfers ≤ n−1.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **Rounding and remainders.** `0.1 + 0.2 ≠ 0.3` compounded across 100 expenses is a real bug. Answer: integer minor units, a deterministic tie-break (stable order by user id), and an assertion that the split sums to the total. Then say the honest caveat: a fixed tie-break can be gamed, so use a non-collusion-prone rule.
2. **Debt simplification is greedy matching**, not a nested pairwise loop. Sort debtors and creditors by magnitude, match largest-first. O(n log n), ≤ n−1 transfers. Then volunteer: greedy is *not provably minimal*; the exact minimum is a subset-sum problem, feasible exhaustively for small groups and tractable via max-flow matching for larger ones.
3. **Multi-currency.** Never convert a historical balance at today's rate. Options: store the FX rate and timestamp per expense, or maintain per-currency ledgers and settle per currency.
4. **Corrections** — deleting or editing an expense must be `unrecord` then `record`. Volunteering this shows you have actually operated one.
5. **A payer who is not a participant** — legitimate (paying for others); do not reject it.

**Step 7: Extension points.** More split strategies; per-currency ledgers and an `FxRateProvider` port; recurring expenses (idempotent per period); categories and analytics; multi-payer expenses; effective-dated membership so mid-history joins do not affect past expenses; batched "settle everything" and partial settlement.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Integer minor units with a documented remainder rule and the Σ=0 invariant. Incremental balances. Greedy simplification with a complexity claim *and* the honest note about optimality. Settlement-as-expense. Multi-currency raised unprompted.

---

## Walkthrough 6 — Tic-tac-toe with AI (minimax)

**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Algorithmic background in §4.3.7.

**Step 1: Requirements.** Board, legal moves, win/draw detection, and an AI. Ask: **how strong must the AI be? perfect, bounded depth, or simple?** That answer determines the entire algorithm. Also ask: do we need undo, and do we need the AI to be fast?

**Step 2: Constraints.** The full game tree is ~550k nodes / 9! ≈ 362,880 sequences, so a full minimax is instant in JS. So the interesting constraints are *determinism* and *immutability* for undo and search, not raw speed.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
class TicTacToe {
  readonly board: Board; readonly nextPlayer: Cell; readonly winner: Cell | 0;
  static initial(): TicTacToe;
  play(cell: number): TicTacToe;      // immutable: returns a new game
  availableMoves(): number[];
  isDraw(): boolean;
  undo(): TicTacToe;
}
interface Ai { suggest(g: TicTacToe): number; readonly name: string; readonly depth: number; }
// FirstMoveAi | RandomAi | HeuristicAi | MinimaxAi | PerfectAi (depth 9)
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
TicTacToe (immutable value-ish object)  ──history──▶ undo() is O(1)
WIN_LINES: a const array of 8 triples (3 rows, 3 cols, 2 diagonals) — generate for n×n
Ai (Strategy) ── suggests a move
   └─ MinimaxAi { depth, evaluate }  ← the difficulty dial
        ├─ recursive search with alpha/beta pruning
        └─ HeuristicEvaluator (for depth < 9)
```
Explain why the game is immutable: undo, the AI search, AI-vs-AI replays, and property tests all become trivial. It costs one object copy per move — a good trade on a 9-cell board.

**Step 5: State and invariants.** Board length 9, cells ∈ {-1,0,1}; no play after a win; equal mark counts (or the first player has exactly one more); a game ends within 9 moves; the AI's move is always in `availableMoves()`; a perfect AI never loses.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **Terminal scoring depends on depth**: `10 − depth` for a win, `−10 + depth` for a loss, so the AI prefers quick wins and slow losses. Without the depth term the AI is indifferent and produces absurd moves — a very common bug.
2. **Alpha-beta correctness.** MAX nodes update `alpha = max(alpha, best)`; MIN nodes update `beta = min(beta, worst)`. A pruned node must still return a *score*, not `undefined`. Bugs here yield a plausible-but-wrong AI, which is why you need property tests.
3. **Move ordering**: centre, then corners, then edges. Multiplies pruning efficiency and makes the AI's choices look natural.
4. **Determinism**: tie-break on the lowest move index so games are reproducible and tests do not flake.
5. **Verification by property test**: play all 9! sequences (or a large random sample with the perfect AI) and assert it never loses and never makes an illegal move. This is the strongest thing you can say.

**Step 7: Extension points.** n×n and k-in-a-row with generated win lines; negamax + transposition table for larger boards; iterative deepening; MCTS/UCT as an alternative family; undo/redo as a Command stack; difficulty presets; AI-vs-AI; replay export with notation; a persisted transposition table.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Immutability with the reason. Depth-dependent terminal scoring. Alpha-beta named and its correctness handled. Determinism. A property test. Naming negamax/transposition tables/MCTS unprompted.

---

## Walkthrough 7 — Notification service

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** `notify(user, event)`; per-event and per-channel preferences; fan out to several channels; retries with backoff; rate limits; DLQ; templates per channel and locale; quiet hours; audit of what was sent. Ask: **cascade to the next channel or send on all?** And: **is `notify` called inside a transaction?** (It always is.)

**Step 2: Constraints.** One channel's failure must not affect the others or the caller. No duplicate deliveries. Opt-out must be absolute. Volume control.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
interface NotificationService { notify(user: UserId, event: NotificationEvent, payload: unknown): Promise<DispatchReport>; }
interface ChannelSender { readonly channel: Channel; readonly idempotent: boolean;
                           send(req: DeliveryRequest): Promise<DeliveryResult>; }
interface NotificationPreferences { isChannelEnabled(...); isQuietHours(...); fallbackOrder(event): readonly Channel[];
                                     localeFor(userId): Locale; rateLimitFor(channel, userId): RatePolicy; }
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
Domain event (order.placed) ──▶ NotificationService.notify
     ├─ Preferences  ── decision: which channels, in what order, respecting quiet hours + rate limits
     ├─ TemplateRenderer (channel × locale)
     ├─ RateLimiter (per channel, per user)
     └─ ChannelSender[email|sms|push|webhook] ── one try/catch EACH, first success wins
              │
              └─ NotificationStore: delivery record + dedupe unique index + audit
DeliveryWorker ── retries with backoff+jitter ── DLQ on exhaustion ── operator replay
Delivery states: pending → sent | suppressed | deferred | failed → dead   (state machine)
```
Say explicitly: this service is an **Observer/pub-sub consumer** (11.1.8/11.1.17), so it *will* receive duplicates and *must* be idempotent.

**Step 5: State and invariants.** (1) At most one `sent` per `(user, event, channel, eventId)` — enforced by a **unique index**, not a check-then-insert; (2) every transition audited with timestamp and attempt number; (3) a `sent` delivery is never re-sent; (4) the cascade stops at the first success; (5) opt-out honoured on every channel/event; (6) quiet hours *defer* rather than drop (except when past the event TTL); (7) rate limits enforced before every attempt; (8) `notify` does not throw for a channel failure.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **The dedupe key design**: must include the channel (so cascading email→SMS is allowed) and the event id (so two distinct events are not collapsed). Enforce it with a unique index; a `SELECT` then `INSERT` races.
2. **The outbox**: you cannot send inside a transaction. Persist the notification to an outbox in the same transaction as the state change, then a worker sends. This is the production detail that separates a toy from a design.
3. **Retryable vs permanent failures.** Distinguish: `retryable` (5xx, timeouts, rate limits) from permanent (invalid address, unsubscribed, rejected content). Retry the first with backoff + full jitter; mark the second suppressed and **suppress the address permanently** if it bounced.
4. **Preference precedence** must be written down: account-global opt-out > per-event setting > per-channel setting > per-event-channel > quiet hours > rate limit. Get this wrong and nobody can explain why a user got a message.
5. **Backpressure**: bounded queue, per-user rate limit, priority for critical events (password reset overtakes marketing), DLQ for everything else.
6. **Idempotency of the providers**: use the provider's idempotency key where available; declare per-sender whether a re-send is safe.

**Step 7: Extension points.** More senders (Slack, Teams, WhatsApp, in-app, webhooks, voice); template versioning and A/B variants; digest/batching mode (which changes the pipeline); delivery-status webhooks with automatic address suppression; per-tenant configuration; DLQ replay tooling with a dry-run mode; full preference UI model with frequency caps.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Channel isolation with one `try/catch` each. Idempotency with a concrete key and a unique index. The cascade-vs-broadcast decision stated explicitly. Backoff + jitter and a DLQ. The outbox realisation. Preference precedence. Recognising the at-least-once delivery contract.

---

## Walkthrough 8 — Logger

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** Levels, structured fields, async writing, multiple sinks, runtime level changes, correlation ids, low hot-path cost. Ask: **where do the logs go, and how much volume?** A million lines/second changes the design completely (you need sampling and dropping; a JSON sink per line will not survive).

**Step 2: Constraints.** Logging is on the hot path, must be cheap, and **must never throw**. Output must be machine-parseable. `error+` must survive a crash. No secrets.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
interface Logger {
  trace|debug|info|warn|error|fatal(msg: string, f?: Fields): void;
  child(ns: string, f?: Fields): Logger;   // inherits fields; used for request scope
  isLevelEnabled(l: Level): boolean;
}
interface LogSink { writeBatch(r: LogRecord[]): void; flush?(): Promise<void>; }
// JsonLineSink | AggregatorSink | FileSink | RingBufferSink | RedactingSink | TestSink
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
root Logger ──child(ns, fields)──▶ Logger  (fluent; fields inherited)
     │ level check FIRST (early exit, no allocation)          ← performance
     ▼
LogPipeline
     ├─ threshold provider (dynamic; reads LOG_LEVEL each call → no restart to raise verbosity)
     ├─ RepeatDedupe ("this error 12,043× in 3s" → one record + count)
     ├─ Sampler[] (by level/namespace/rate)
     ├─ bounded buffer (drop lowest severity, count drops, never block the app)
     └─ single-flight drain ──fan-out──▶ sinks (each wrapped by RedactingSink)
Context: AsyncLocalStorage<{traceId, userId, logger}> → correlation without parameter threading
```
Say the key sentence: *"Logging is a pipeline, not a `console.log`. The design decisions are where filtering happens (before allocation) and what happens when the sink is slower than the app."*

**Step 5: State and invariants.** (1) a log call never throws; (2) filtering happens before record construction; (3) at most one drain in flight; (4) the buffer is bounded with an explicit drop policy and a dropped counter; (5) records are immutable once handed to a sink; (6) every record has UTC ts, level, namespace, message, env, version, traceId; (7) `error+` flushed on exit and on fatal; (8) redaction applied in the sink layer to every record.

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **Hot-path cost.** Disabled levels must be ~free: check the threshold before building anything; `JSON.stringify` happens at drain time, never at the call site.
2. **Backpressure.** A slow aggregator must not block or crash the app: bounded buffer + drop-lowest + drop counter + alert. Decide and document what is dropped.
3. **Crash safety.** Async flush in an `exit` handler is unreliable; critical levels need an eager/synchronous path.
4. **Redaction at the sink layer.** Doing it at call sites fails the first time someone logs a request body. Redact keys matching `password|token|secret|authorization|cookie|card|cvv|ssn` plus PII patterns.
5. **Correlation without threading.** `AsyncLocalStorage` (or the OTel context) so a 30-deep call tree logs the trace id without 30 parameter changes.
6. **Volume control**: sampling, dedupe, and level discipline (`info` = business state change; `error` = an operation failed and a human may need to look).
7. **Ordering** is not guaranteed across processes or sinks — never assert on log order in a test.

**Step 7: Extension points.** Any sink; per-namespace levels with dynamic reload; OTel trace/span auto-attachment; samplers by level/namespace/rate/request-id; error aggregation; a redaction ruleset per data class; child loggers; emitting a counter alongside a record for business events; a ring-buffer sink that attaches recent context to error reports.

**Step 8: What the interviewer scores.** Async batching with a bounded buffer and a single-flight drain. Early exit before allocation. "Never throws" plus crash-time flushing. Redaction in the sink layer. Correlation via `AsyncLocalStorage`. Volume awareness. And that they recognise the pipeline is a shared singleton-ish object that should still be **injected**, not imported globally.

---

## Walkthrough 9 — Library management system

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Step 1: Requirements.** Catalogue search (title/author/ISBN/subject), add/remove a copy, borrow, return, renew, reserve with a queue, member loans, late fees, due-date notifications. Ask: **one copy per title or many?** (many — and this is the modelling hinge), **membership tiers?**, **notifications?**

**Step 2: Constraints.** Title vs copy separation. Reservation queue is FIFO with a hold window. Borrowing limits per tier; fines can block. Concurrent reservation of the last copy must not double-book.

**Step 3: API surface.**
```ts
class LibrarySystem {
  registerTitle(t: TitleInput): TitleId;
  addCopy(titleId: TitleId, copyId: CopyId): void;
  removeCopy(titleId: TitleId, copyId: CopyId): void;
  borrow(memberId: MemberId, copyId: CopyId, at: Date): Loan;
  returnBook(copyId: CopyId, at: Date): ReturnOutcome;    // includes the fine
  renew(loanId: LoanId, at: Date): Loan;
  reserve(memberId: MemberId, titleId: TitleId, at: Date): Reservation;
  loansFor(memberId: MemberId): readonly Loan[];
  overdue(now: Date): readonly Loan[];
}
interface CatalogueSearch { search(q: SearchQuery): Promise<Page<TitleSummary>>; suggestions(prefix: string, limit: number): Promise<TitleSummary[]>; }
interface LoanPolicy { loanPeriodDays(at: Date): number; maxLoans(tier): number; renewLimit: number; finePerDay(): Money; blockThreshold(tier): Money; }
```

**Step 4: Class/diagram narration.**
```
BookTitle (aggregate root) ──owns──▶ BookCopy (N)        ← the key modelling decision
     │  reservations: FIFO queue per title                 CopyState: available|on-loan|reserved|lost|in-repair
     ▼                                                    Loan (open → returned)
Member (aggregate root) ──holds──▶ Loan[]                 Reservation (pending → fulfilled|expired|cancelled)
LoanPolicy (Strategy/registry per MembershipTier)
CatalogueSearch (PORT) ── PostgresFTS | Elastic | SqlIndex | InMemory
Domain events: CopyBorrowed, CopyReturned, ReservationFulfilled, FineAccrued, CopyDueSoon
```
Emphasise: only a *copy* is lent; the title is the catalogue record and the unit of reservation. Getting this backwards is the failure the interviewer is watching for.

**Step 5: State and invariants.**
```
BookCopy:  available → on-loan → available | reserved ;  available|reserved → lost|in-repair
Loan:      open → returned ;  open + dueAt < now ⇒ overdue (a view, not a stored state)
Member:    active ⇄ blocked (fines over threshold / admin)
I1 a copy has at most one open loan and at most one active reservation
I2 only an 'available' copy can be borrowed
I3 a copy on loan cannot be removed from its title
I4 a member never holds two loans of the same title
I5 tier limits never exceeded; fines over the threshold block borrowing
I6 fine = (returnedAt − dueAt in days) × rate, computed once at return, capped
I7 reservation queues are FIFO and expire, releasing the copy
I8 renewal refused when the copy is reserved by another member
```

**Step 6: The tricky part.**
1. **The last-copy race.** Two members reserving the last copy. Answer: a unique constraint on the active reservation per copy, plus a serialisable transaction or `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` on the title row, plus a deterministic tie-break for the queue position.
2. **The hold window.** On return with a queue present: copy → `reserved`, notify the next member, hold for N days, then expire to the next or to the shelf. This is a second state machine; the classic bug is *losing* copies here.
3. **Renewal rules**: refuse if reserved; extend from the **current due date**, not from today (a classic bug); cap renewals; refuse if no copies of the title are free.
4. **Fines**: decide accrue-at-return vs daily, the cap, and whether partial payment unblocks. Then note the failure mode — a fine computed from a due date in a different timezone/calendar basis.
5. **Search is not `LIKE '%x%'`.** Propose a real search index (Postgres FTS + trigram, or Elasticsearch), and discuss normalisation (`"Smith, John"` vs `"John Smith"`), ISBN hyphen normalisation, pagination via cursor, and index staleness.

**Step 7: Extension points.** Membership tiers as policy data; reservation policies (FIFO / tier priority / hold-window length / max active); search backends behind the port; online fine payment and waivers with an audit trail; inter-library loan via an anti-corruption layer; acquisitions/MARC import; inventory with locations; overdue notification workflow (idempotent!); barcode/RFID integration.

**What the interviewer scores.** Title vs copy. Search as a separate concern behind a port. The reservation queue with a hold window as a state machine. Renewal-from-due-date and reserved-blocks-renewal. Fines policy stated. A real answer for the last-copy race (constraint + serialisable tx). Tiers as data, not `if` statements.

---

---

# PHASE 11 MUST-IMPLEMENT-FROM-SCRATCH (10 Problems)

> Each problem: the requirement, a full runnable TypeScript solution, the tests that prove the tricky part, and the follow-ups an interviewer will ask. No external dependencies; run with `tsx`.

---

## Problem 1 — LRU cache with TTL, weight limits, and single-flight loading

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Requirement.** O(1) `get`/`put`, bounded by entry count *or* total weight, optional per-entry TTL, `peek` that does not promote, statistics, single-flight loading, and an LFU policy behind the same interface.

```ts
// ─────────────────────────────── lru.ts ───────────────────────────────
export interface Stats { hits: number; misses: number; evictions: number; expirations: number; loads: number; loadCoalesced: number; }
export type EvictionPolicy = { kind: 'lru' } | { kind: 'lfu'; decay: number } | { kind: 'ttl-only' };

export interface CacheOptions<K, V> {
  capacity: number;
  maxWeight?: number;
  weigh?: (key: K, value: V) => number;
  policy?: EvictionPolicy;
  clock?: () => number;
  onEvict?: (key: K, value: V, reason: 'capacity' | 'weight' | 'expired' | 'deleted') => void;
}

export class Cache<K, V> {
  private readonly map = new Map<K, { value: V; weight: number; expiresAt: number | null; score: number }>();
  private totalWeight = 0;
  readonly stats: Stats = { hits: 0, misses: 0, evictions: 0, expirations: 0, loads: 0, loadCoalesced: 0 };
  private readonly inflight = new Map<K, Promise<V>>();
  private readonly weigh: (k: K, v: V) => number;
  private readonly policy: EvictionPolicy;
  private readonly clock: () => number;

  constructor(private readonly opts: CacheOptions<K, V>) {
    if (opts.capacity < 1) throw new RangeError('capacity must be >= 1');
    this.weigh = opts.weigh ?? (() => 1);
    this.policy = opts.policy ?? { kind: 'lru' };
    this.clock = opts.clock ?? Date.now;
  }
  get size() { return this.map.size; }
  get weight() { return this.totalWeight; }
  has(key: K) { return this.map.get(key) !== undefined && !this.expired(this.map.get(key)!); }
  /** NO promotion — deliberate. Monitoring reads must not change eviction order. */
  peek(key: K): V | undefined { const e = this.map.get(key); return e && !this.expired(e) ? e.value : undefined; }

  get(key: K): V | undefined {
    const e = this.map.get(key);
    if (!e) { this.stats.misses++; return undefined; }
    if (this.expired(e)) { this.drop(key, 'expired'); this.stats.expirations++; this.stats.misses++; return undefined; }
    this.stats.hits++;
    if (this.policy.kind === 'lfu') { e.score = e.score * this.policy.decay + 1; return e.value; }
    this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, e);      // LRU: delete first, then set → moves to the end
    return e.value;
  }

  set(key: K, value: V, ttlMs?: number): this {
    const weight = Math.max(0, this.weigh(key, value));
    const existing = this.map.get(key);
    if (existing) { this.totalWeight -= existing.weight; this.map.delete(key); this.opts.onEvict?.(key, existing.value, 'deleted'); }
    while (this.map.size + 1 > this.opts.capacity) this.evictOne();
    if (this.opts.maxWeight != null) {
      while (this.map.size > 0 && this.totalWeight + weight > this.opts.maxWeight) if (!this.evictOne()) break;
      if (this.totalWeight + weight > this.opts.maxWeight) throw new RangeError(`weight ${weight} exceeds maxWeight`);
    }
    this.map.set(key, { value, weight, expiresAt: ttlMs ? this.clock() + ttlMs : null, score: 1 });
    this.totalWeight += weight;
    return this;
  }
  delete(key: K) { const had = this.map.has(key); if (had) this.drop(key, 'deleted'); return had; }
  clear() { this.map.clear(); this.totalWeight = 0; }
  keys(): K[] { return [...this.map.keys()]; }                 // LRU → MRU

  /** Read-through with request coalescing: N concurrent misses → 1 loader call. */
  async getOrLoad(key: K, load: (k: K) => Promise<V>, ttlMs?: number): Promise<V> {
    const hit = this.get(key); if (hit !== undefined) return hit;
    const existing = this.inflight.get(key);
    if (existing) { this.stats.loadCoalesced++; return existing; }
    this.stats.loads++;
    const p = load(key).then(v => { this.set(key, v, ttlMs); return v; })
                    .finally(() => { this.inflight.delete(key); });   // always clear
    this.inflight.set(key, p);
    return p;
  }

  sweep(): void {                                              // O(expired), safe to call on a timer
    for (const [k, e] of [...this.map]) if (this.expired(e)) { this.drop(k, 'expired'); this.stats.expirations++; }
  }
  private expired(e: { expiresAt: number | null }) { return e.expiresAt !== null && this.clock() > e.expiresAt; }
  private drop(key: K, reason: 'capacity' | 'weight' | 'expired' | 'deleted') {
    const e = this.map.get(key); if (!e) return;
    this.totalWeight -= e.weight; this.map.delete(key);
    if (reason === 'capacity' || reason === 'weight') this.stats.evictions++;
    this.opts.onEvict?.(key, e.value, reason);
  }
  private evictOne(): boolean {
    let victim: K | undefined;
    if (this.policy.kind === 'lfu') {
      let best = Infinity;
      for (const [k, e] of this.map) if (!this.expired(e) && e.score < best) { best = e.score; victim = k; }
    } else if (this.policy.kind === 'ttl-only') {
      let soonest = Infinity;
      for (const [k, e] of this.map) if (e.expiresAt != null && e.expiresAt < soonest) { soonest = e.expiresAt; victim = k; }
    } else victim = this.map.keys().next().value as K;
    if (victim === undefined) return false;
    this.drop(victim, 'capacity'); return true;
  }
}
```

```ts
// ─────────────────────────────── lru.test.ts ───────────────────────────────
import { Cache } from './lru';
let fakeNow = 1_000_000; const clock = () => fakeNow; const advance = (ms: number) => { fakeNow += ms; };
const test = (name: string, fn: () => void | Promise<void>) =>
  Promise.resolve().then(fn).then(() => console.log('  ok', name),
    e => { console.error('  FAIL', name, (e as Error).message); process.exitCode = 1; });

await test('evicts the least recently used entry', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 2, clock });
  c.set('a', 1); c.set('b', 2); c.get('a'); c.set('c', 3);
  if (c.has('b')) throw new Error('b should have been evicted');
  if (!c.has('a') || !c.has('c')) throw new Error('a and c should remain');
});
await test('peek does NOT promote (the classic test)', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 2, clock });
  c.set('a', 1); c.set('b', 2); c.peek('a'); c.set('c', 3);
  if (c.has('a')) throw new Error('a should be evicted — peek must not promote');
});
await test('re-setting an existing key does not grow the cache', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 2, clock });
  c.set('a', 1); c.set('b', 2); c.set('a', 99);
  if (c.size !== 2) throw new Error('size must stay 2'); if (c.peek('a') !== 99) throw new Error('value not updated');
});
await test('TTL expiry', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 10, clock });
  c.set('a', 1, 1000);
  if (c.get('a') !== 1) throw new Error('should be present');
  advance(1001);
  if (c.get('a') !== undefined) throw new Error('should be expired');
  if (c.stats.expirations !== 1) throw new Error('expiration not counted');
});
await test('weight-based eviction removes multiple entries', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 100, maxWeight: 10, weigh: (_k, v) => v, clock });
  c.set('a', 4); c.set('b', 4); c.set('c', 4);
  if (c.weight > 10) throw new Error('weight exceeded'); if (c.size !== 2) throw new Error(`size ${c.size}`);
});
await test('single-flight: concurrent misses cause ONE loader call', async () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 10, clock });
  let calls = 0;
  const load = async (k: string) => { calls++; await new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 5)); return k.length; };
  const results = await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: 25 }, () => c.getOrLoad('key', load)));
  if (calls !== 1) throw new Error(`loader called ${calls} times`);
  if (c.stats.loadCoalesced !== 24) throw new Error('coalesced count wrong');
});
await test('a rejected load is not cached and does not poison the key', async () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 10, clock });
  await c.getOrLoad('k', async () => { throw new Error('boom'); }).catch(() => {});
  await c.getOrLoad('k', async () => 7);
  if (c.peek('k') !== 7) throw new Error('should have retried and cached');
});
await test('LFU evicts the least frequently used', () => {
  const c = new Cache<string, number>({ capacity: 3, policy: { kind: 'lfu', decay: 1 }, clock });
  c.set('a', 1); c.set('b', 2); c.set('c', 3); c.get('a'); c.get('a'); c.get('b'); c.set('d', 4);
  if (c.has('c')) throw new Error('c should be evicted');
});
console.log('lru tests done');
```

**Follow-ups.** Why `Map` works here (insertion order == recency; `delete`+`set` moves to end; O(1) throughout). Multi-tier (L1 process / L2 Redis / L3 origin) with coalescing at each tier and invalidation on write. Bounding memory precisely with weights plus a hard capacity. Stampede protection via jittered TTLs and stale-while-revalidate.
