```ts
const test = (name: string, fn: () => void | Promise<void>) =>
  Promise.resolve().then(fn).then(() => console.log('  ok', name),
    e => { console.error('  FAIL', name, (e as Error).message); process.exitCode = 1; });

await test('play does not mutate the original game (immutability)', () => {
  const g0 = TicTacToe.initial(); const g1 = g0.play(4);
  if (g0.moves.length !== 9) throw new Error('the original must be untouched');
  if (g1.moves.length !== 8) throw new Error('the new game should have 8 free cells');
});
await test('win detection on all 8 lines', () => {
  for (const [a, b, c] of [[0,1,2],[3,4,5],[6,7,8],[0,3,6],[1,4,7],[2,5,8],[0,4,8],[2,4,6]]) {
    if (TicTacToe.initial().play(a).play(b).play(c).winner === 0) throw new Error(`line ${a},${b},${c} should win`);
  }
});
await test('no moves after a win', () => {
  const g = TicTacToe.initial().play(0).play(3).play(1);
  try { g.play(4); throw new Error('should have thrown GameOver'); }
  catch (e) { if (!(e instanceof GameOver)) throw e; }
});
await test('cannot play an occupied cell', () => {
  try { TicTacToe.initial().play(0).play(0); throw new Error('should have thrown CellOccupied'); }
  catch (e) { if (!(e instanceof CellOccupied)) throw e; }
});
await test('undo restores the previous position and next player', () => {
  const g1 = TicTacToe.initial().play(4);
  const g2 = g1.play(0);
  const back = g2.undo();
  if (back.board.join('') !== g1.board.join('')) throw new Error('board not restored');
  if (back.next !== g1.next) throw new Error('next player not restored');
  try { TicTacToe.initial().undo(); throw new Error('should have thrown NothingToUndo'); }
  catch (e) { if (!(e instanceof NothingToUndo)) throw e; }
});
await test('the perfect AI opens centre and blocks threats', () => {
  if (new PerfectAi().suggest(TicTacToe.initial()) !== 4) throw new Error('should open centre');
  const g = TicTacToe.initial().play(0).play(8).play(3);
  if (new PerfectAi().suggest(g) !== 5) throw new Error('should block at 5');
});
await test('the perfect AI takes an immediate win', () => {
  const g = TicTacToe.initial().play(0).play(8).play(3).play(1);
  if (new PerfectAi().suggest(g) !== 2) throw new Error('should take the win at 2');
});
await test('the perfect AI is never beaten (property test)', () => {
  const r = assertUnbeatable(new PerfectAi(), [new RandomAi(), new FirstMoveAi()], 200);
  console.log(`       ${r.games} games: ${r.wins} AI wins, ${r.draws} draws, 0 losses`);
});
await test('AI is deterministic across runs', () => {
  const g = TicTacToe.initial();
  const a = new MinimaxAi('d2', 2, x => new HeuristicEvaluator().evaluate(x)).suggest(g);
  const b = new MinimaxAi('d2', 2, x => new HeuristicEvaluator().evaluate(x)).suggest(g);
  if (a !== b) throw new Error('must be deterministic');
});
console.log('tictactoe tests done');
```

**Follow-ups.** Scaling up (negamax, Zobrist transposition tables, iterative deepening, killer/history heuristics, MCTS/UCT). Why immutability (undo, search, AI-vs-AI, property tests for free). Testing an AI with properties, not golden outputs.

---

## Problem 10 — Notification dispatcher: preferences, cascade, retries, DLQ, idempotency

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Requirement.** Resolve preferences (per-event, per-channel, quiet hours, rate limits), cascade with first-success-wins, dedupe by key, retry with backoff and jitter, dead-letter permanently failing deliveries.

```ts
// ─────────────────────────── notifications.ts ───────────────────────────
export type Channel = 'email' | 'sms' | 'push';
export type NotificationEvent = 'order.placed' | 'payment.failed' | 'password.reset' | 'weekly.digest';
export interface DeliveryRequest { to: string; body: string; dedupeKey: string; }
export type DeliveryResult = { status: 'sent'; providerId: string }
                             | { status: 'suppressed'; reason: string }
                             | { status: 'failed'; code: string; retryable: boolean; message: string };
export interface ChannelSender { readonly channel: Channel; readonly idempotent: boolean; send(r: DeliveryRequest): Promise<DeliveryResult>; }
export interface NotificationPreferences {
  isChannelEnabled(user: string, event: NotificationEvent, channel: Channel): boolean;
  isQuietHours(user: string, at: number): boolean;
  quietHoursEnd(user: string, at: number): number;
  fallbackOrder(event: NotificationEvent): readonly Channel[];
  localeFor(user: string): string;
  addressFor(user: string, channel: Channel): string;
}
export interface TemplateRenderer {
  render(channel: Channel, event: NotificationEvent, payload: Record<string, unknown>, locale: string): { body: string };
}
export type DeliveryState = 'pending' | 'sent' | 'suppressed' | 'deferred' | 'failed' | 'dead';
export interface DeliveryRecord {
  key: string; user: string; event: NotificationEvent; channel: Channel; state: DeliveryState;
  attempts: number; nextAttemptAt: number; providerId?: string; lastError?: string; history: DeliveryState[];
}
export class NotificationStore {
  private readonly records = new Map<string, DeliveryRecord>();
  exists(key: string) { return this.records.has(key); }             // in production: a UNIQUE index
  get(key: string) { return this.records.get(key); }
  put(r: DeliveryRecord) { this.records.set(r.key, r); }
  transition(key: string, state: DeliveryState, patch: Partial<DeliveryRecord> = {}): DeliveryRecord {
    const r = this.records.get(key)!;
    const next = { ...r, ...patch, state, history: [...r.history, state] };
    this.records.set(key, next);
    return next;
  }
  due(now: number) { return [...this.records.values()].filter(r => r.state === 'pending' && r.nextAttemptAt <= now); }
}
export class DeadLettered extends Error { constructor(public readonly key: string, public readonly attempts: number) { super('dead-lettered'); } }
class RetryableFailure extends Error {}
export const CRITICAL = new Set<NotificationEvent>(['password.reset']);
export function defaultBackoff(attempt: number, channel: Channel): number {
  const base = channel === 'sms' ? 200 : channel === 'push' ? 500 : 2000;    // SMS retries hard, email gently
  return Math.random() * Math.min(base * 12, base * 2 ** attempt);           // FULL JITTER
}
const sleep = (ms: number) => new Promise<void>(r => setTimeout(r, ms));
export interface Attempt { channel: Channel; outcome: 'sent' | 'suppressed' | 'deferred' | 'throttled' | 'deduped' | 'failed' | 'dead';
                           providerId?: string; until?: number; retryAfterMs?: number }
export interface DispatchReport { user: string; event: NotificationEvent; sent: Attempt[]; attempts: Attempt[]; reason?: string }

export class NotificationDispatcher {
  constructor(
    private readonly prefs: NotificationPreferences,
    private readonly senders: Record<Channel, ChannelSender>,
    private readonly templates: TemplateRenderer,
    private readonly store: NotificationStore,
    private readonly rateLimiter: (channel: Channel, user: string) => Promise<{ allowed: boolean; retryAfterMs: number }>,
    private readonly clock: { now(): number },
    private readonly backoff: (attempt: number, channel: Channel) => number = defaultBackoff,
    private readonly maxAttempts = 5,
  ) {}

  /** Called from a use case (ideally via an outbox relay). Never throws for a channel failure. */
  async notify(user: string, event: NotificationEvent, payload: Record<string, unknown>, eventId: string): Promise<DispatchReport> {
    const now = this.clock.now();
    const candidates = this.prefs.fallbackOrder(event).filter(ch => this.prefs.isChannelEnabled(user, event, ch));
    if (!candidates.length) return { user, event, sent: [], attempts: 0, reason: 'all-channels-disabled' };
    const attempts: Attempt[] = [];
    for (const channel of candidates) {
      const key = `${user}|${event}|${eventId}|${channel}`;                    // channel + event id in the key
      const existing = this.store.get(key);
      if (existing?.state === 'sent') { attempts.push({ channel, outcome: 'deduped' }); continue; }

      if (this.prefs.isQuietHours(user, now) && !CRITICAL.has(event)) {
        const until = this.prefs.quietHoursEnd(user, now);
        this.store.put({ key, user, event, channel, state: 'deferred', attempts: 0, nextAttemptAt: until, history: ['deferred'] });
        attempts.push({ channel, outcome: 'deferred', until });
        continue;                                                             // defer, do not escalate the cascade
      }
      const limit = await this.rateLimiter(channel, user);
      if (!limit.allowed) {
        this.store.put({ key, user, event, channel, state: 'pending', attempts: 0,
                         nextAttemptAt: now + limit.retryAfterMs, history: ['pending'] });
        attempts.push({ channel, outcome: 'throttled', retryAfterMs: limit.retryAfterMs });
        continue;                                                             // rate limited: try the next channel
      }
      const outcome = await this.attempt(channel, user, event, payload, key);
      attempts.push(outcome);
      if (outcome.outcome === 'sent') break;                                   // first success wins
    }
    return { user, event, sent: attempts.filter(a => a.outcome === 'sent'), attempts };
  }

  async deliverWithRetry(key: string, send: () => Promise<DeliveryResult>): Promise<DeliveryRecord> {
    let rec = this.store.get(key)!;
    while (true) {
      if (rec.attempts >= this.maxAttempts) {
        this.store.transition(key, 'dead', { lastError: 'attempts exhausted' });
        throw new DeadLettered(key, rec.attempts);                            // → DLQ, operator replay
      }
      rec = this.store.transition(key, 'pending', { attempts: rec.attempts + 1 });
      try {
        const res = await send();
        if (res.status === 'sent') return this.store.transition(key, 'sent', { providerId: res.providerId });
        if (res.status === 'suppressed') return this.store.transition(key, 'suppressed', { lastError: res.reason });
        if (!res.retryable) return this.store.transition(key, 'suppressed', { lastError: res.message });
        throw new RetryableFailure(res.message);
      } catch (e) {
        if (!(e instanceof RetryableFailure)) throw e;                         // non-retryable: surface, do not loop
        rec = this.store.transition(key, 'failed', { lastError: (e as Error).message,
          nextAttemptAt: this.clock.now() + this.backoff(rec.attempts, rec.channel) });
        await sleep(Math.max(0, rec.nextAttemptAt - this.clock.now()));
      }
    }
  }
  private async attempt(channel: Channel, user: string, event: NotificationEvent,
                        payload: Record<string, unknown>, key: string): Promise<Attempt> {
    if (!this.store.exists(key))
      this.store.put({ key, user, event, channel, state: 'pending', attempts: 0, nextAttemptAt: 0, history: ['pending'] });
    try {
      const rendered = this.templates.render(channel, event, payload, this.prefs.localeFor(user));
      const rec = await this.deliverWithRetry(key, () => this.senders[channel].send({
        ...rendered, to: this.prefs.addressFor(user, channel), dedupeKey: key }));
      return { channel, outcome: rec.state === 'sent' ? 'sent' : rec.state, providerId: rec.providerId };
    } catch (e) {
      if (e instanceof DeadLettered) return { channel, outcome: 'dead' };
      throw e;
    }
  }
}
export class MemorySender implements ChannelSender {
  readonly requests: DeliveryRequest[] = [];
  constructor(readonly channel: Channel, readonly idempotent = true, private readonly behaviour: (n: number) => DeliveryResult) {}
  async send(r: DeliveryRequest): Promise<DeliveryResult> { const res = this.behaviour(this.requests.length); if (res.status === 'sent') this.requests.push(r); return res; }
}
export const okSender = (channel: Channel) => new MemorySender(channel, true, () => ({ status: 'sent' as const, providerId: `${channel}-ok` }));
export const failTimes = (channel: Channel, retryable: boolean, times: number) =>
  new MemorySender(channel, true, n => n < times
    ? { status: 'failed' as const, code: 'E', retryable, message: 'provider failed' }
    : { status: 'sent' as const, providerId: `${channel}-recovered` });
export const alwaysFail = (channel: Channel, retryable = true) =>
  new MemorySender(channel, true, () => ({ status: 'failed' as const, code: 'E', retryable, message: 'down' }));
```

```ts
// ─────────────────────────── notifications.test.ts ───────────────────────────
import { NotificationDispatcher, NotificationStore, MemorySender, okSender, failTimes, alwaysFail } from './notifications';
let now = 0; const clock = { now: () => now };
const test = (name: string, fn: () => Promise<void>) =>
  fn().then(() => console.log('  ok', name), e => { console.error('  FAIL', name, (e as Error).message); process.exitCode = 1; });

function build(o: { email?: MemorySender; sms?: MemorySender; order?: Channel[]; quiet?: boolean;
                  disabled?: Channel[]; limiter?: (c: Channel) => Promise<{ allowed: boolean; retryAfterMs: number }>;
                  maxAttempts?: number } = {}) {
  const store = new NotificationStore();
  const prefs = {
    isChannelEnabled: (_u: string, _e: NotificationEvent, c: Channel) => !(o.disabled ?? []).includes(c),
    isQuietHours: () => o.quiet ?? false,
    quietHoursEnd: () => now + 3_600_000,
    fallbackOrder: () => (o.order ?? ['email', 'sms']) as Channel[],
    localeFor: () => 'en',
    addressFor: (_u: string, c: Channel) => `${c}@example.com`,
  };
  const templates = { render: (_c: Channel, e: NotificationEvent, p: Record<string, unknown>) => ({ body: `${e}:${JSON.stringify(p)}` }) };
  const email = o.email ?? okSender('email');
  const sms = o.sms ?? okSender('sms');
  const d = new NotificationDispatcher(prefs as any, { email, sms, push: okSender('push') } as any,
    templates as any, store, async c => o.limiter ? o.limiter(c) : { allowed: true, retryAfterMs: 0 },
    clock, () => 0, o.maxAttempts ?? 3);
  return { d, store, email, sms };
}

await test('happy path: the first channel succeeds and the cascade stops', async () => {
  const { d, store, sms } = build();
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', { id: 'o1' }, 'evt1');
  if (r.sent.length !== 1 || r.sent[0]!.channel !== 'email') throw new Error('email should have won');
  if (r.attempts.length !== 1) throw new Error('cascade should stop after the first success');
  if (store.get('u1|order.placed|evt1|email')!.state !== 'sent') throw new Error('store should record sent');
  if (sms.requests.length !== 0) throw new Error('sms must not be called');
});
await test('idempotency: the same (user,event,eventId,channel) is never sent twice', async () => {
  const { d, email } = build();
  await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', { id: 'o1' }, 'evt1');
  const second = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', { id: 'o1' }, 'evt1');
  if (email.requests.length !== 1) throw new Error(`provider called ${email.requests.length} times`);
  if (second.attempts[0]!.outcome !== 'deduped') throw new Error('expected deduped');
});
await test('a different eventId is a different notification', async () => {
  const { d, email } = build();
  await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', { id: 'o1' }, 'evt1');
  await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', { id: 'o1' }, 'evt2');
  if (email.requests.length !== 2) throw new Error('two distinct events → two sends');
});
await test('cascade: a non-retryable email failure falls through to sms', async () => {
  const email = alwaysFail('email', false);
  const { d } = build({ email });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.sent[0]?.channel !== 'sms') throw new Error('sms should have delivered');
});
await test('retries with backoff then succeeds, and is audited', async () => {
  const { d, store } = build({ email: failTimes('email', true, 2), maxAttempts: 5 });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.sent.length !== 1) throw new Error('should have recovered');
  const rec = store.get('u1|order.placed|evt1|email')!;
  if (rec.attempts !== 3) throw new Error(`attempts should be 3, got ${rec.attempts}`);
  if (rec.history.filter(h => h === 'failed').length !== 2) throw new Error('history should show 2 failures');
});
await test('exhausted retries dead-letter the delivery', async () => {
  const { d, store } = build({ email: alwaysFail('email', true), maxAttempts: 2 });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.attempts[0]!.outcome !== 'dead') throw new Error(`expected dead, got ${r.attempts[0]!.outcome}`);
  if (store.get('u1|order.placed|evt1|email')!.state !== 'dead') throw new Error('record should be dead');
});
await test('quiet hours defer a non-critical event', async () => {
  const { d, store } = build({ quiet: true });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.attempts[0]!.outcome !== 'deferred') throw new Error('should defer');
  if (store.get('u1|order.placed|evt1|email')!.state !== 'deferred') throw new Error('record should be deferred');
});
await test('quiet hours do NOT defer a critical event (password reset)', async () => {
  const { d } = build({ quiet: true });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'password.reset', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.sent.length !== 1) throw new Error('critical events must still go out');
});
await test('rate limiting falls through to the next channel', async () => {
  const { d } = build({ limiter: async c => c === 'email' ? { allowed: false, retryAfterMs: 5000 } : { allowed: true, retryAfterMs: 0 } });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.attempts[0]!.outcome !== 'throttled') throw new Error('email should be throttled');
  if (r.sent[0]!.channel !== 'sms') throw new Error('sms should deliver');
});
await test('all channels disabled returns a reason and does not throw', async () => {
  const { d } = build({ disabled: ['email', 'sms', 'push'] });
  const r = await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (r.reason !== 'all-channels-disabled' || r.attempts.length !== 0) throw new Error('expected a clean no-op');
});
await test('one channel failing does not stop the others', async () => {
  const { d, email, sms } = build({ email: alwaysFail('email', false) });
  await d.notify('u1', 'order.placed', {}, 'evt1');
  if (email.requests.length !== 0) throw new Error('failing email recorded nothing');
  if (sms.requests.length !== 1) throw new Error('sms must still have been attempted');
});
console.log('notification tests done');
```

**Follow-ups.** The outbox: `notify` is called inside a transaction, so persist to an outbox and let a worker send. Which failures are retryable vs permanent, and how a bounced address is suppressed permanently. Preference precedence documented in one place. Priority queues for critical events; digest mode; per-tenant configuration; DLQ replay tooling with a dry run. And the framing: this service is an at-least-once Observer consumer, so idempotency is mandatory.

---

# PHASE 11 DESIGN REVIEW SCENARIOS (12)

> Each scenario: the design as presented, the defects (in priority order), the fix, and what you would ask the team before changing anything. Score yourself: a strong answer names the *highest-severity* defect first, not all of them at once.

---

## Scenario 1 — The `DataManager` that does everything

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** `DataManager` is a singleton. It opens the DB connection, reads and writes every table, sends emails, calls the payment gateway, writes log files, and caches results. Forty other classes call `DataManager.instance.*`. 9,000 lines. Zero tests.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **No seam anywhere.** Nothing is testable without a live database and a network. This is the root cause of the other problems.
2. **Singleton global state.** Test pollution, hidden dependencies, thread/request safety, a lifecycle the container should own.
3. **Divergent change.** Every feature change edits this one file; every release conflicts with every other.
4. **Business logic and I/O interleaved.** You cannot unit test a pricing rule because it is three lines above a `db.query`.
5. **Tangled change.** A logging change and a checkout change both land here.

**The fix, in order.**
1. Characterisation tests on the three highest-value operations *before* touching anything.
2. Wrap the payment and email calls behind ports (`PaymentGateway`, `Notifier`) so they can be faked. Two-line changes, huge payoff.
3. Split by capability: `OrderRepository`, `PaymentGateway`, `EmailSender`, `PricingRule`.
4. Inject the clock, the logger and the cache instead of using statics.
5. Move business rules into domain objects; leave the repositories as data access.
6. Replace the singleton with a composition root (`buildContainer(env)`) so lifetimes and test doubles are explicit.
7. **Do not** rewrite. Strangler Fig: extract one capability per sprint.

**Ask the team first.** What is the actual pain — slow deploys, flaky tests, incidents, or onboarding? The order of the refactor depends on the answer.

---

## Scenario 2 — Order processing with a God service and no transactions

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** A single `OrderProcessingService` with 14 injected dependencies (catalog, pricing, inventory, payment, shipping, tax, fraud, loyalty, notification, audit, metrics, logger, clock, cache). It calls 6 microservices synchronously inside a `try`. No timeouts anywhere. "If the payment call fails we log it and continue." Order state lives in three databases.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **No timeouts on 6 remote calls.** A single slow dependency saturates the request path. This is the single highest-severity defect and it is a five-minute fix.
2. **Cross-service partial failure is unhandled.** `payment failed → continue` means orders exist with no payment and nobody knows.
3. **Fan-out latency.** 6 sequential calls add up; `Promise.all` where they are independent.
4. **State in three databases with no saga.** No consistency story, no compensation, no reconciliation.
5. **14 dependencies** = a God service by injection count alone. Nobody can test it without 14 doubles.

**The fix.**
1. Timeout every call with a nested budget (inner deadline < outer deadline). Add retries with jitter on idempotent reads only, and a budget that fits inside the caller's deadline.
2. Per-dependency circuit breakers + bulkheads so one failure cannot consume everything.
3. Make the payment step a **saga step with compensation**; anything that must not fail the order is inside the saga, anything optional (notifications, analytics) is emitted as an event and leaves the critical path.
4. Parallelise independent calls; the pricing call does not need inventory.
5. Split the service by capability; the fraud engine and the notification sender do not belong in one unit.
6. Read-side fan-out: for the order page, a read model or a BFF, so 6 calls happen on the read path with per-field degradation rather than in the write path.

**Ask the team first.** Which of those 6 calls actually needs to be synchronous for correctness? (Usually two.)

---

## Scenario 3 — Event bus with 14 subscribers and no contracts

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**The design.** A `globalEvents` emitter with stringly-typed events. 14 subscribers, 9 of them added in the last year. Payloads are the full mutable entity objects. Errors are swallowed with `console.log`. New business rules are implemented as subscribers (e.g. "when order.placed, send email, apply discount, create shipment, update analytics"). There is no unsubscribe anywhere.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **The business flow lives in subscribers.** Nobody can answer "what happens after order.placed?" That is 9 files and no single reading order.
2. **No unsubscribe** → listeners leak; long-lived subscribers capture request scope.
3. **Mutable payloads** shared across subscribers: subscriber 3 mutates the entity that subscriber 7 sees.
4. **Errors swallowed** → a broken subscriber is invisible for weeks.
5. **Stringly typed** → a renamed field is a runtime no-op in one subscriber and a live bug in another.
6. **No idempotency** → at-least-once delivery causes duplicate charges, duplicate shipments.

**The fix.**
1. Name the critical path as an **explicit orchestrator** (an application service or a saga) and keep only non-critical reactions as events.
2. Typed event map + `readonly`/frozen payloads.
3. Per-listener error isolation with a metric; aggregate failures; alert.
4. `on()` returns a disposer; wire it to lifecycle (`onRequestEnd`, `onUnmount`, `dispose`).
5. Idempotent handlers keyed on `eventId` with a unique constraint.
6. Version the event names and add a tolerant-reader contract test in CI.
7. Consider `Promise.allSettled` semantics for async listeners instead of fire-and-forget.

**Ask the team first.** Which subscribers, if any, are on the correctness path? That determines how much to orchestrate.

---

## Scenario 4 — Repository that leaks the ORM

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**The design.** `interface OrderRepository { findByCustomerId(id: string, status?: string, from?: Date): Promise<UserEntity[]>; createQueryBuilder(...): SelectQueryBuilder; }` Services import `UserEntity` from the ORM and call `repo.createQueryBuilder()` to build ad-hoc joins. Entities are returned straight to the API layer as JSON.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **The interface is not a domain abstraction** — it is a thin wrapper over the ORM, with the ORM in the signature. Nothing can be faked without a DB.
2. **N+1 everywhere**, because services write joins by hand.
3. **The ORM entity is the API response**, so internal fields (password hash, internal flags, soft-delete columns) leak and the API cannot be shaped for a client.
4. **Lazy loading** ⇒ detached-object exceptions and surprise queries during serialisation.
5. No explicit loading ⇒ transactions leak into read paths.

**The fix.**
1. Domain-named methods: `findOpenOrdersForCustomer(customerId): Promise<Order[]>`, `byId(id): Promise<Order | null>`. Kill `findByCustomerId(status?, from?)` — every parameter combination is a new method or a spec object.
2. A `OrderMapper` for row ⇄ domain, and a separate `OrderView` DTO for the API.
3. Explicit loading inside the repository (one join, one round trip) so N+1 is impossible by construction.
4. Strict validation on hydration; unknown enum values are a data-integrity error, not a cast.
5. Add a version column and optimistic concurrency in `save`.

**Ask the team first.** How many distinct queries exist in service code? That is the N+1 count and it is a strong argument for the fix.

---

## Scenario 5 — Microservices with a shared database

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** 9 services, 1 database, 1 schema, cross-service table reads (`billing` joins `orders`). Deployments are coordinated: 5 services must go out together. Latency between "services" is a few milliseconds. They did this to "scale the team".

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Distributed monolith**: all the cost, none of the benefit. The coordinated deploy proves it.
2. **Shared schema is a hidden synchronous coupling**: you cannot migrate a column without coordinating everyone; you cannot extract a service later because its data is shared.
3. **Data ownership is fiction.** Every service is coupled to every table change.
4. **Local calls that used to be free now cost latency and can fail** — with none of the resilience patterns applied because "it's just SQL".
5. **Operational overhead without isolation**: 9 pipelines, 9 dashboards, 9 on-call rotations, zero fault isolation.

**The fix, in order.**
1. **Admit the problem:** this is a monolith with extra processes. Decide honestly whether to (a) merge back to a modular monolith or (b) complete the split.
2. Establish **data ownership**: each service owns its tables; others go through APIs or replicated read models.
3. Sequence the data split with an expand/migrate/contract plan (add the new structure, dual-write, backfill, verify, cut reads, remove the old).
4. Replace cross-service table reads with a **read model** for the pages that genuinely need joined data, and with events for replication.
5. Add the resilience patterns (timeouts, breakers, bulkheads, idempotency) because the calls are now remote.
6. Unblock independent deploys: backward-compatible contracts and a one-service-at-a-time release train.

**Ask the team first.** How long does a release take end to end, and what is the rollback story? If the answer is "we do all 5 services in a 2am window", you have your diagnosis.

---

## Scenario 6 — Anemic domain model in a complex billing system

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** `Invoice` has 18 public fields and no methods. `InvoiceService` has 40 methods, each doing validation inline. `finalizeInvoice` and `voidInvoice` and `applyCreditNote` each re-check "status must be DRAFT". There are 6 services that can modify an invoice, and each has its own copy of the rules. Two of them are subtly different.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Invalid states are representable and reachable** — an invoice with `status: VOID` and `total > 0` exists in production (or will).
2. **Rule drift** across 6 services; the 6th copy is wrong and nobody knows which is authoritative.
3. **No reuse**: a new entry point (CLI, webhook, job) re-implements the rules a 7th time.
4. Transaction sprawl: 40 service methods, each managing its own boundaries, so multi-step operations are not atomic.
5. The service class is a 3,000-line God object (SRP violated from every direction).

**The fix.**
1. Move the rules into behaviour on the entity: `invoice.finalize(now)`, `invoice.void(reason)`, `invoice.applyCreditNote(amount)`, each rejecting illegal transitions.
2. Make fields private with intent-revealing accessors; the invariant is enforced in one place.
3. Delete the duplicated guards from the services; keep the *use-case policy* (who may void an invoice, in which role) in the service, where it belongs.
4. Introduce a `Money` value object so money cannot be a `number`.
5. Wrap the use case in an application service that owns the transaction; one use case per method.
6. Enforce with tests at both levels: domain tests (rules) and one integration test per use case.

**Ask the team first.** How many places construct or mutate an invoice? If more than two, that is the number to fix first.

---

## Scenario 7 — Rate limiter that blocks on a slow store

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**The design.** A rate limiter checks Redis (`GET counter`), compares, then `INCR`. Under load: (a) two requests can both pass the check; (b) when Redis is slow, every request blocks on the `GET`, so the whole service degrades; (c) the key is a raw user id with no expiry, and the key space is 400 million entries; (d) 429s are returned with no `Retry-After`.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Check-then-increment race** → over-admission under exactly the conditions you care about (a stampede at the limit).
2. **Synchronous dependency in the hot path with no timeout** → Redis latency becomes request latency; a Redis blip becomes a site outage.
3. **Unbounded key space with no TTL** → memory exhaustion (the limiter becomes the outage vector).
4. **Fails closed implicitly** (throws) with no local fallback → total outage on a limiter failure.
5. Missing `Retry-After`/`X-RateLimit-*` → clients hammer in a tight loop.

**The fix.**
1. Move prune/count/compare/increment into **one Lua script** (or an atomic pipeline) — indivisible.
2. Wrap the store call in a timeout; **fail open to a local token bucket** when the store is unavailable, and alert on it. (Or fail closed for billing quotas — decide and document.)
3. Set a TTL on every key and/or cap the tracked key space; add idle eviction in the local limiter.
4. Return `429` with `Retry-After` and `X-RateLimit-Limit/Remaining/Reset`.
5. Consider rate limiting **at the edge** (gateway/CDN) so over-limit traffic never reaches the app.
6. Instrument: allowed/rejected per key class, store latency, fallback rate; alert on rejection spikes (a DDoS signal).

**Ask the team first.** Is the limiter protecting against abuse or enforcing billing quotas? The fail-open/closed answer depends on it.

---

## Scenario 8 — "Microservices" with 200 nanosecond network calls

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** An order page needs product, price, stock, reviews, shipping estimate, and recommendations. The client calls 7 services directly. Each service has its own auth, its own rate limits, and its own version. Mobile p95 page load is 4.2 seconds. The team says "we could add a BFF" but is worried about it being a new bottleneck.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **7 round trips on the critical path** from a mobile client; latency and failure probability compound. At 99.9% per call, all 7 succeeding is ~99.3%.
2. **No failure isolation**: one slow service degrades the whole page with no partial rendering.
3. **API surface leaks the service topology** — every internal split becomes a client-breaking change.
4. **Over-fetching**: the mobile client downloads desktop-shaped payloads.
5. No per-client authorisation or shaping.

**The fix.**
1. Introduce a **BFF per channel** (`web-bff`, `mobile-bff`): one call, payload shaped for the client.
2. Inside the BFF, `Promise.all` with a per-call timeout, a circuit breaker, and a **per-field fallback** so a broken service degrades one card, not the page.
3. Add an **aggregate cache** with per-field TTLs and **single-flight** coalescing so 40 concurrent requests for one SKU make one upstream call.
4. Define a page latency budget and enforce it: each upstream gets a share; slow ones are abandoned early.
5. Cache popular aggregates at the edge/CDN for the marketing pages.
6. Keep authorisation in the services; use the BFF only for channel-specific shaping and coarse session checks. Never trust a client-supplied user id.

**Ask the team first.** What is the actual breakdown of the 4.2 seconds — network, service processing, or client rendering? Optimise the right layer.

---

## Scenario 9 — Service mesh adopted too early

**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

**The design.** 6 services in a small org. An Istio mesh was introduced for "security and reliability". Two months later: pod startup is slower, a mesh upgrade broke canaries, nobody can debug a 502, and the on-call rotation now has a new page (`istiod` unhealthy). Retries are configured in the mesh *and* in the client code.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Retry amplification** — mesh retries × client retries × LB retries can produce a 10× load multiplier during an incident, which is the opposite of the goal.
2. **Retry on non-idempotent methods** in mesh config → duplicate writes.
3. **No capacity for the data plane**: sidecar CPU/memory unset → noisy-neighbour problems.
4. **The control plane is now an operational dependency**; readiness without `holdApplicationUntilProxyStarts` means pods get no traffic after rollout.
5. **Debugging cost is high and the team cannot pay it** — mesh config is not covered by their tests.
6. **Value is not being realised**: no canary-by-header, no mTLS enforcement yet, and the underlying problem was application design.

**The fix.**
1. **Do the resilience work in code** for now: timeouts, breakers, bulkheads, retries with jitter and budgets, idempotency. They are testable there, and there are only 6 services.
2. If the mesh stays: disable mesh retries entirely; keep exactly one retry layer.
3. Restrict retries to idempotent verbs; ensure idempotency keys on writes.
4. Set sidecar resource requests/limits; configure outlier detection and connection draining to survive rolling deploys.
5. Move to `STRICT` mTLS deliberately, and check `holdApplicationUntilProxyStarts`.
6. GitOps the mesh config with review; add mesh-version to the app version in dashboards.
7. **Plan the exit**: decide the threshold (services, teams, compliance need) under which you remove it, and write it down.

**Ask the team first.** What requirement was the mesh supposed to satisfy? If the answer is "reliability", code-level patterns cover it at this scale with far less operational load.

---

## Scenario 10 — Serverless with a connection pool per invocation

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**The design.** One Lambda per route. Each handler creates `new Pool({ connectionString: env.DB })`, does a query, ends the pool. Downstream calls have no timeout. A queue consumer retries each message 5 times with no DLQ. p99 is 3.4 seconds; the DB hit `max_connections` during the last sale.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Connection storm**: 1,000 concurrent invocations × 10 connections = 10,000 connections against a limit of ~200. The database is the outage cause.
2. **No timeouts on downstream calls** → cold starts plus unbounded latency; no init-duration visibility.
3. **Batch failures are all-or-nothing**: one poison message fails the batch and 9 good messages are retried forever.
4. **Retries without a DLQ and without idempotency** → duplicate processing, unbounded cost.
5. **No concurrency cap**, so a queue burst instantiates unbounded handlers and amplifies the DB problem.
6. Cold start is blamed, but the real cost is connection initialisation per invocation.

**The fix.**
1. **Module-scope pool** (reused per warm container) sized to `db_max_connections × share`; or `RDS Proxy`/PgBouncer; or front the function with a queue with bounded concurrency.
2. Batched DB access; a single query or a batch API instead of per-row queries.
3. Handle each record in a batch independently and return `batchItemFailures`; DLQ the poison message with the payload and error.
4. Reserved concurrency per function to protect downstream dependencies; a per-user rate limit if fan-out is unbounded.
5. Explicit timeout on every downstream call; instrument `Init Duration` separately from handler duration.
6. Move long-running work out of the function; use a worker for anything beyond the timeout.
7. Thin handler + reusable core module (the B pattern from 11.3.9).

**Ask the team first.** What is the p50 vs p99 split of the 3.4 seconds? If p50 is 200ms and p99 is 3.4s, it is connection acquisition and queue contention, not cold start.

---

## Scenario 11 — A `Strategy` that is a `switch` in disguise

**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**The design.** `PricingService` with a `PricingStrategy` interface, four implementations (`VipPricing`, `StudentPricing`, `B2BPricing`, `DefaultPricing`), and a `PricingStrategyFactory` whose `create(segment)` method is a four-branch `switch`. Each strategy class delegates most of its work to `PricingCalculator`, which contains the actual pricing logic — including the discount rules.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **The decision is still in one place and is still closed to extension.** Adding a segment edits the factory. This is the *entire* cost of Strategy with none of the benefit.
2. **The algorithms are not in the strategies** — they are in a shared helper, so the strategies are one-line wrappers. The abstraction carries no information.
3. **The shared helper is a hidden dependency** every strategy needs, so they are not independently testable.
4. 4 files × 5 lines plus a factory plus a helper: more code, same coupling.

**The fix.**
1. Decide honestly: either (a) the branches are trivial → keep the `switch` and delete the four classes, or (b) the algorithms are genuinely different → **move the logic into the strategies** and give each one a real test file.
2. Selection from data: `const STRATEGIES: Record<Segment, PricingStrategy>` — adding a segment adds a key and a class, and nothing else. In TypeScript, `satisfies Record<…>` gives compile-time exhaustiveness.
3. Where a `switch` genuinely remains (segment selection from a raw string off the wire), keep it in one obvious place and make the default explicit.
4. If several strategies share 80% of their logic, extract that into a *domain service* they take as a collaborator — not a base class.
5. Add the metrics (`pricing.strategy` label) that justify the abstraction.

**Ask the team first.** How many lines does each strategy actually own? If it is under ten, the answer is "delete them".

---

## Scenario 12 — A retry that duplicates charges

**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The design.** A payment service. On a gateway timeout, the client (an order service) retries up to 3 times with a 500ms fixed delay. The gateway's `charge` is not idempotent. There is a reconciliation job that runs nightly and reports "unknown status" charges for a human. 11 "unknown" charges in the last month; 6 were actual double charges.

**Defects, worst first.**
1. **Retrying a non-idempotent write.** A timeout does not mean the charge failed; the money may have moved. This is a correctness bug with financial consequences.
2. **No idempotency key.** The provider almost certainly supports one; it is not being used.
3. **Fixed-delay retries with no jitter** → synchronised retries across instances (a thundering herd during a gateway slowdown).
4. **No retry budget** → retries can exceed the caller's deadline and the work is wasted anyway.
5. **Reconciliation is a nightly human process**, not an automated repair; a 24-hour exposure window.
6. **No breaker**: retries amplify load on an already-struggling gateway.

**The fix.**
1. **Stop retrying immediately.** Generate a stable idempotency key per *logical* payment attempt (e.g. `orderId:paymentAttempt`) and pass it on every retry. A provider that supports idempotency keys makes retries safe.
2. If the provider has no idempotency support, treat a timeout as **unknown, not failed**: do not retry; raise a `PaymentUnknown` state; reconcile via a status query or the gateway's reconciliation API.
3. Exponential backoff with **full jitter** and a retry budget nested inside the caller's deadline.
4. Add a circuit breaker so the whole payment path sheds load instead of amplifying it.
5. Replace the nightly report with an automated reconciliation loop (poll `unknown` charges to resolution, escalate only true exceptions) and alert on age.
6. Model the payment as a state machine (`initiated → authorized → captured → failed | unknown | reversed`) so every state is explicit and every transition is idempotent.

**Ask the team first.** Does the provider support an idempotency key, and what does its timeout actually mean? Those two answers determine whether the fix is 10 lines or a state-machine redesign.

---

# PHASE 11 INTERVIEW Q&A (40 questions with model answers)

---

**Q1. What is a design pattern, and what must every pattern description contain?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

A named, reusable solution to a recurring design problem — not code you copy but vocabulary plus structure plus a documented cost. Every description needs three things: **intent** (the problem, stated without naming the solution), **participants** (the collaborating roles, including which are optional), and **consequences** (what you gain, what you pay, and which questions you are now deferring). The consequences section is the whole point: a pattern with no costs listed is marketing. Levels matter too: idioms (language-level), design patterns (class-level), architectural patterns (deployment-level). Mixing them up is a tell.

---

**Q2. Why is Singleton almost always wrong, and when is it defensible?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

Five problems: it is a **hidden dependency** (not in any signature, invisible to tooling), it is **untestable** without global reset (hence `beforeEach { reset() }` everywhere), it **owns its own lifecycle** (lazy init on the hot path, state surviving redeploys), it creates a **global mutable variable** two request threads share, and it does not even deliver "one instance" reliably (per realm, per worker, per module system). Defensible only when the object is immutable/stateless (a lookup table), when it is an expensive process-level resource with thread-safe acquisition (a pool, a native handle), or when the runtime already gives you process-wide state. The test: *"can I make this different in a test?"* If not, it is wrong.

---

**Q3. Strategy versus State — what is the difference?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Strategy:** the *client* selects the implementation; alternatives are interchangeable and independent of the object's internal state. **State:** the object itself switches implementation as a consequence of its own transition, and the state object both provides behaviour and **returns the next state**. The diagnostic: if your "state" objects do not return the next state, you wrote a Strategy. The other diagnostic: in a State pattern the caller never chooses; in Strategy it always does.

---

**Q4. Decorator versus Proxy — when do you use which?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

A Decorator preserves an interface explicitly, forwarding named methods (a compile-time-enforced contract with a hand-maintenance tax). A Proxy intercepts generically via traps, so it works on objects you do not control and can be driven by data (a field spec, a validation rule). Reach for a decorator when the interface is small and known (retries around `send`); reach for a Proxy when the concern is data-driven or applies to a foreign object (tracing, validation at a boundary, lazy fields). Never a Proxy on a hot path (every access becomes a trap), and remember the classic footgun: a `get` trap that returns a function for every property makes the object thenable, so `await proxy` hangs.

---

**Q5. Observer versus Pub/Sub — when do you need a broker?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Observer: the Subject holds the listeners, so coupling is compile-time and in-process. Pub/Sub: publisher and subscriber never reference each other; delivery goes through a mediator, usually cross-process. You need the broker when subscribers must **scale or deploy independently**, when you need **failure isolation**, or when you need **replay** (to rebuild a read model). For an in-process fan-out of 3–5 synchronous listeners, a plain typed emitter is better: no broker to run, transactions remain possible, and the flow is debuggable.

---

**Q6. When is a `switch` better than a Strategy?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

When the branches are few (3–5), short, homogeneous, and **stable**, and the decision point is the only place that should know the options. Then the switch is the simplest correct thing and a Strategy would be 5 files of ceremony with no new information. The moment the branch bodies grow real algorithms, a new branch arrives weekly, or you need to test each alternative in isolation, the abstraction earns its keep. And be honest: a "Strategy" selected by a `switch` inside a factory is a switch with extra files — put the selection in a `Record<Kind, Ctor>` registry instead.

---

**Q7. Command versus an ordinary function call?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Wrap it in a Command only when you buy one of three things: **deferred execution** (a queue), **serialisation** (persist and replay, i.e. an event log or a job payload), or **inversion** (an undo stack). Otherwise a function is strictly better. The real cost of Command is that the argument shape becomes a wire format — so it must be versioned — and that `undo` in a distributed system is a saga problem, not a stack problem. Also: an undo stack is only valid LIFO, commands must be invertible (capture prior state at construction, not reconstructed later), and non-invertible commands must declare that honestly rather than silently no-op.

---

**Q8. Repository versus DAO?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

A **DAO** is persistence-oriented: it mirrors the storage model, exposes CRUD and query capabilities, and its types are the ORM's. A **Repository** is domain-oriented: it is a collection-like abstraction over *aggregates*, with domain-named queries (`findOpenOrdersForCustomer`) and a return type in domain terms. Test: does a caller have to know a column name, a table name, or an ORM query builder to use it? If yes, it is a DAO. A repository that exposes `createQueryBuilder()` has implemented a DAO badly and lost the boundary.

---

**Q9. Do you need a Unit of Work?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Only when a business transaction spans more than one row you write. If every operation touches exactly one aggregate, the database's own transaction is sufficient and a Unit of Work is overhead with real risks (scope confusion, forgotten dirty marking, flush ordering). When you do need one, the three hard parts are: change tracking (explicit `registerDirty` is the safest), **flush order** (parents before children, or you get FK violations and silent inconsistency), and scope discipline (per request, and never spanning a third-party call — you cannot roll that back).

---

**Q10. Composition over inheritance — when do you still reach for inheritance?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

When the hierarchy states a **genuine, stable fact** about the domain: an error taxonomy (`AppError` → `NotFound` → `OrderNotFound`), framework extension points you own (a template whose flow you are deliberately constraining), or a sealed, exhaustively-switched hierarchy of value types where nothing is partially overridden. The honest failure mode of composition is *premature* guessing: you must know the axes of variation up front, and if you guess wrong you end up with five one-method collaborators to do what a `switch` would have. Composition's real win is that it makes the axis of change substitutable at runtime; inheritance makes it a subclass.

---

**Q11. What does Liskov Substitution actually protect you from?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

From a caller discovering that a subtype violates the contract it claimed. The concrete surprises: new required arguments, extra exceptions a caller does not catch, stricter validation, **extra side effects** (the `Square.setWidth` changing both dimensions — the caller's loop over rectangles now produces wrong areas), changed ordering, or returning `null` where the base promised a value. The practical test is "could a caller written against the base type be *surprised*?" If yes, either restructure so the hierarchy is a true taxonomy, or use composition instead (which is why `Square extends Rectangle` is usually solved by making shapes data).

---

**Q12. How do you apply SOLID without ending up with 40 interfaces?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Apply SOLID *by axis of change*, not by rule count. For each candidate abstraction, ask: is there a second implementation now or on a dated roadmap; does it name a domain concept rather than a mechanism; does it shield a caller from a change the caller should not care about? If all three are no, delete the interface and call the concrete thing. Also: define interfaces at the **consumer**, keep them small (ISP is about roles, not about splitting every method into its own interface), and remember that SOLID's five faces are one idea — put each axis of change in its own file — so you can apply it selectively.

---

**Q13. DRY — when should you deliberately NOT extract?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

When the duplication is **coincidental** — two blocks that look alike today but encode *different decisions*. That is not duplicated knowledge, and extracting it produces a function whose name cannot describe both and a boolean flag that is a hidden `if`. The unit of duplication is knowledge, not text. Use a rule of three: at two occurrences wait; at three, look hard. And treat a shared `utils` module as a smell — a file of 200 unrelated functions has no cohesion and everything imports it, which is the opposite of decoupling.

---

**Q14. YAGNI applied honestly — what do you never skip?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

YAGNI is about *capability*, not about *interfaces*. Never skip validation, error handling, idempotency on anything retried, timeouts on outbound calls, observability, or transaction boundaries. Do skip speculative plugin architectures, generic frameworks for a single shape, and configuration for requirements you cannot name. The cost model: you pay for YAGNI on the day you need the thing, which is the worst possible day — so the cheap mitigation is a documented **seam** (an interface with one implementation and a note about the requirement that would justify a second), not a speculative implementation.

---

**Q15. What is a seam, and how do you create one in legacy code?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

A seam is a place where you can alter behaviour **without editing in that place** — the definition from Michael Feathers. Three ways to create one, in order of preference: (1) **parameterise** — pass the global in (`now: () => Date`), which requires touching one line; (2) **wrap** — route the third-party call through one of your own functions, giving you a place to intercept and stub; (3) **break up** — extract an overridable method or introduce an interface, which requires restructuring and should be a last resort. The workflow is: find the hot spot, add a characterisation test, create the seam, then refactor that one method. Never refactor a method you cannot test.

---

**Q16. Characterisation tests versus golden-master tests?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Characterisation tests** are hand-written tests that capture current behaviour, including the bugs, one behaviour per test, with the bug marked (`it('charges full price for the segment "vip " — known bug')`). They are the default and they are readable. **Golden-master (record/replay)** tests capture the output of a large corpus of real inputs and assert the new implementation matches the golden file — ideal for parsers, schedulers, tax calculators, and anything hard to unit test. The golden's only as good as its corpus, so build it from **production-shaped inputs** including nasty cases, and regenerate only deliberately. In both cases: do not fix the bug while writing the test; that is a separate PR.

---

**Q17. Strangler Fig — what makes it fail?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Five failure modes. (1) **Big-bang** replacement instead of incremental. (2) **Migrating the technical layers** (database first, then API) instead of business capabilities. (3) **Shadow-running writes against real third parties** — use sandbox or replay mode. (4) **No one-step rollback**: if reverting requires a deploy, you have hope, not rollback. (5) **No end date**: a strangler becomes two systems forever. Two more rules: the new system must be a **superset of observed behaviour**, bugs included, and you must delete the old path. A good addition: extract the **read path first** — reads compare for equality, retry freely, and revert instantly.

---

**Q18. Monolith or microservices — how do you decide?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

With a scored card, not enthusiasm: change/deploy coupling (25%), scaling profile (25%), team topology (20%), data/regulatory isolation (15%), blast radius (10%), genuine runtime need (5%). Score each 1–5 against **measurable** thresholds and only extract a component whose weighted score exceeds ~3.5 *and* meets at least one threshold. Then do the prerequisite gate first: if the problem is code quality, do modular-monolith work instead; if the requirements are still moving, wait. The argument that convinces sceptics is not "we need to scale" (a cache or an index usually does) but "our release train couples a weekly component with a monthly one".

---

**Q19. What is the distributed monolith and how do you avoid creating one?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Services that can only be deployed together, share a database, and therefore have all the costs of distribution and none of the benefits. The tells: coordinated releases ("5 services in one window"), shared tables, and cross-service joins. You avoid it by splitting along **business capabilities** (never by technical layer), giving each service exclusive data ownership, and insisting on backward-compatible contracts so a change to one service never requires another. Step 0 is always the same: establish enforced module boundaries in the monolith, because that is what makes extraction cheap.

---

**Q20. Event-driven architecture — what coupling does it actually remove?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

It removes **temporal** coupling (A need not run before B) and reduces **spatial/deployment** coupling (B deploys independently). It does **not** remove **semantic** coupling — publisher and subscriber still agree on what `OrderPlaced` means — and arguably increases it, because that agreement is now a contract between parties who cannot see each other. So "loosely coupled" is only half true, and the part that remains needs schema governance, owners, and compatibility tests in CI. The other cost people forget: **you lose the ability to read the flow**. In a choreographed design the sequence of steps lives in no single place.

---

**Q21. What is the dual-write problem, and how does the outbox solve it?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Writing state to your database and then publishing an event is two operations with no transaction between them; a crash in between produces "the order is paid and nobody was told", silently and permanently. The **outbox** fixes it: in the *same* transaction as the state change, insert a row into an outbox table; a separate relay process reads un-published rows, publishes them, and marks them sent. Consequences you must state: publishing and marking are not atomic, so delivery is **at-least-once** and duplicates are possible — therefore consumers **must** be idempotent (an inbox table with a unique constraint on the message id, written in the same transaction as the effect). "Exactly-once" is not achievable; "effectively-once" is, and this is how.

---

**Q22. CQRS — when is a separate read model worth it?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

When the read and write shapes genuinely differ and reads are expensive: read:write > 10:1, multi-join aggregations, sorting/faceting/search, multiple distinct view requirements, or reads needing to scale/geolocate independently of writes. Start at **level 1** (separate interfaces, same model) which is nearly free; move to **level 2** (separate read store, projections) only when you measure the bottleneck; **level 3** (event-projected read models in separate stores) only when you need fundamentally different views plus the operational capacity. The costs you must own: eventual consistency visible to users (so you need read-your-writes), projection lag as an SLO, two models that drift, and projection repair. And never validate an invariant against a read model.

---

**Q23. Event sourcing — the honest trade-off?**
**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

**Gain:** complete, exact history; temporal queries; rebuilding any projection by replay; event-driven integration for free (the log *is* the stream, so no dual-write); debugging from the customer's actual history. **Cost:** event schemas are immutable **forever** and encode today's understanding of the domain; **upcasters are mandatory from day one** (a chain `v1 → v2 → v3`, pure and deterministic, tested with frozen fixtures) and most teams underestimate them by an order of magnitude; you need snapshots for performance; you lose ad-hoc SQL, which is a large *organisational* cost; and an immutable log conflicts with right-to-erasure, which is a policy decision, not an engineering one. Use it for event-shaped domains with a genuine history requirement. Do not use it early, when the model is still changing.

---

**Q24. What is a Saga and when is orchestration better than choreography?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

A Saga is a sequence of local transactions with **compensating actions** replacing 2PC across services. **Orchestrated**: a central coordinator drives the steps; the flow is explicit and easy to debug, and adding a step touches one file. **Choreographed**: each service reacts to events; it scales and is loosely coupled, but the sequence lives nowhere, a missing handler leaves an order stuck, and *adding* a step means editing every service that hard-coded "what happens next". The practical answer used by mature teams: **orchestrate the correctness-critical flow, choreograph the reactions**. And remember compensation is not rollback — you cannot unsend an email, so compensation must be designed with the domain.

---

**Q25. Circuit breaker — how do you choose the threshold?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Not a consecutive-failure count: at low traffic, one unlucky request at 3am opens the breaker for hours. Use a **failure ratio over a rolling window with a minimum request count** (`requests >= 20 && failures/requests > 0.5`), derived from your measured error distribution. And classify errors: only count failures that indicate the dependency is unhealthy; a 400 from your own validation bug must never trip it. Half-open must allow only a **few** probes, or it re-trips instantly and flaps. One breaker **per dependency** — a global breaker takes down healthy dependencies because an unrelated one failed.

---

**Q26. Retry — what are the rules?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Five rules. (1) Only for **idempotent** operations, or with an idempotency key. (2) Exponential backoff with **full jitter** (`random(0, base·2^attempt)`) — without jitter, N clients retry at the same instant and recreate the overload you were trying to relieve. (3) A **budget**: max attempts × max total time must be smaller than the caller's deadline, or the work is wasted. (4) **Never** retry 4xx/validation errors — you amplify a client bug into a self-inflicted DoS. (5) Breaker *outside*, retry *inside*, so the breaker sees one logical outcome per caller. And in serverless or behind a mesh, check you are not layering retries in three places.

---

**Q27. What is a bulkhead and why does it matter?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Isolating failure domains so one exhausted resource cannot consume everything. Two forms: **concurrency bulkheads** — a per-dependency semaphore so a slow dependency can only hold its own permits instead of saturating the shared executor — and **resource bulkheads** — separate pools or queues per tenant or dependency to stop noisy neighbours. Without it, one slow dependency consumes the shared pool and starves every *healthy* dependency too, and the failure stops looking local. Implementation note: `queueLimit = 0` means **fail fast**, which is usually what you want; unbounded queueing converts a latency problem into a memory problem.

---

**Q28. Design for hexagonal / clean architecture — where do ports live?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Ports are owned by the **inside** and expressed in domain language (`PaymentGateway`, `Clock`, `IdGenerator`), never with technology in the name (`IHttpPaymentClient` is a smell). Adapters implement them and live outside. The rule to state: **the domain layer must compile with zero non-standard imports** — if `src/domain` imports `express` or `pg`, the boundary has leaked. Validation belongs at the boundary (parse into a typed command); invariants belong in the domain (never trust a caller, even another service); transactions belong in the application layer, not on entities. And a domain needs *some* notion of time and identity — inject `Clock` and `IdGenerator`, do not call `Date.now()` inside an entity.

---

**Q19b. When is an anemic domain model the right choice?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

When the entity is mostly data with almost no invariants (a profile, an audit entry, a catalogue item), when the logic is fundamentally about coordination (an ETL job, an API gateway), when the domain is CRUD and the complexity is in the queries, when the consumer is a machine (DTOs plus a validation schema), or when a framework owns the object (JPA, Android). The deciding question: *"is there a rule about this entity that must hold no matter which code path modifies it?"* If yes → behaviour belongs on the entity. If the rules are "which operations are allowed in this use case" → they belong in the application service. And the fix for an existing anemic model is incremental: pick the entity with the most scattered rules, add characterisation tests, move each rule into a behaviour method, privatise the fields, delete the duplicates.

---

**Q30. Entity versus Value Object — the decision procedure?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Three questions. Does it have an **identity that survives change**? Are two instances with identical attributes **distinct**? Do you compare by *what it is* or by *which one it is*? If (c) is "no", it is a Value Object: immutable, compared by attribute value, safe to share and to use as a Map key. When in doubt, make it a value object — you cannot regret a value object, you can regret an entity that should have been one. `Money`, `EmailAddress`, `DateRange`, `ShippingAddress`, and `OrderId` are all value objects in almost every system; giving them ids costs you immutability and gains you nothing.

---

**Q31. Aggregate boundaries — how do you decide?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Start from the **invariant that must hold atomically**, then shrink. Rules: one aggregate root, one repository, one transaction; references to other aggregates are **by id**, never by object (which is how accidental load cascades start); keep the aggregate small enough to load and save in a couple of round trips. The two signals you over-sized it: constant optimistic-concurrency conflicts, and a repository that joins half the schema. The two signals you under-sized it: you cannot enforce an invariant at all, or you need a distributed transaction to keep things consistent. A useful test: "if two users edit different parts of this at the same time, should it fail or merge?" Fail → one aggregate is correct.

---

**Q32. Bounded contexts — what are they and how do you know you have them right?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

A boundary within which a model has a specific, unambiguous meaning. `Customer` in Sales means a buyer with a credit limit; in Support a contact with tickets; in Billing a payer account with a dunning stage. Same word, different models — and you do **not** share the class; you translate at the boundary, usually with an **anti-corruption layer** that keeps a foreign (or legacy) model from leaking into yours. You have them roughly right when the ubiquitous language is consistent inside a context and people disagree productively across contexts. Contexts are a *modelling* boundary; whether they are also deployment boundaries is a separate, infrastructure-driven decision — conflating the two is how you get a distributed monolith.

---

**Q33. Layered architecture — where does it leak?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Seven leaks, and you should be able to name them: (1) the service layer becomes anemic and invariants scatter across services; (2) the data layer leaks upward (`repo.createQueryBuilder()`, `import { db }`); (3) **business logic lands in controllers** — and then background jobs bypass it, which is the most common production bug in this style; (4) transactions leak upward, because services cannot compose repos that each own a transaction; (5) presentation-layer rules duplicated elsewhere; (6) the `common`/`utils` layer that everything imports and which becomes the new God layer; (7) you cannot slice a feature vertically, so one change touches all three layers. The structural fix is feature-first structure with a per-feature vertical slice.

---

**Q34. Serverless — what are the four real constraints?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

(1) **Cold starts and connection reuse** — module-scope your clients; a pool per invocation is the single most common serverless outage. (2) **Execution timeouts** — a hard ceiling on anything long-running. (3) **Concurrency and burst limits** — 1,000 concurrent invocations × 10 pool connections = 10,000 connections; size pools for worst-case concurrency, add a proxy/pooler, batch, or front the function with a bounded queue. (4) **No durable local state** — no sticky sessions, no reliable `/tmp`. And the trap: the function-per-request pattern. Use a thin adapter (parse, call the core, map errors) plus a reusable, normally-testable core module.

---

**Q35. BFF — what is it for, and what is it not for?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

For three things: **aggregation** (one call instead of five, so mobile latency and failure probability stop compounding), **client-specific payload shaping** (web needs 40 fields, mobile 8, TV 4), and **encapsulating the service topology** (splitting pricing in two touches only the BFF). It is **not** a place for business rules (discount logic duplicated there becomes two sources of truth) and **not** a generic API gateway (that does routing, auth, rate limits; a BFF does client-shaped aggregation — they compose). The hard parts are the latency budget per upstream, per-field fallbacks so one broken service degrades one card, and aggregate caching with single-flight coalescing.

---

**Q36. Service mesh — what does it buy, and what does it cost?**
**Difficulty:** ⚫ Expert

Buys: uniform mTLS with automated rotation (the most-cited real benefit), declarative retries/timeouts/circuit-breaking across services and languages, traffic management (canary by percentage, header routing, mirroring, fault injection), uniform telemetry with consistent trace propagation, and identity-based authorisation. Costs: two hops per request plus sidecar CPU/memory; a new deployment and upgrade tax per workload; a new distributed system that can be down at 3am (the control plane must be **out of the data path** — sidecars keep the last good config); two-layered debugging; configuration sprawl with no owner; and reduced programmability, since mesh retries cannot be unit-tested and local development no longer reproduces production. Above all: some concerns *must* stay in code — idempotency, fallback semantics, and domain degradation — so you end up with both, and the retry counts can multiply.

---

**Q37. Idempotency — how do you actually implement it?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

Three layers. (1) **A stable idempotency key** derived from the *logical intent*, not the attempt: `orderId:paymentAttempt:1`, generated before the first try and reused on every retry. (2) **A store** of key → outcome, with a **unique constraint**: on a duplicate key you return the recorded result rather than re-executing. On an in-flight duplicate, either wait or return `409`. (3) **Pass it to the provider** where supported — this is what makes retrying a non-idempotent external write safe, and skipping it is the cause of the classic double charge. Then: idempotent consumers use an inbox table written in the same transaction as the effect; a timeout is **unknown, not failed**, so never blindly retry a non-idempotent write after a timeout.

---

**Q38. Naming — what are the rules that actually matter?**
**Difficulty:** 🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

Say what a thing **is**, not what it does (the action belongs in the body). Put **units in the name** — `timeoutMs`, `amountMinor`, `placedAt`, `durationMs`; this single rule prevents the most common production bug. Plural for collections, `Id` suffix for references, and **four distinct names** for four representations of one concept (`Order` domain, `OrderRow` persistence, `PlaceOrder` command, `OrderView` API) so no function ever takes the wrong one. Booleans as positive predicates (`isActive`, `hasCapacity`). `find` may return null, `get` throws, `load` does I/O, `create` may persist. Avoid `Manager`, `Helper`, `Handler`, `data`, `tmp`, `obj`, and boolean positional parameters.

---

**Q39. Code smells — which do you fix first, and how?**
**Difficulty:** 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

Fix **duplicated knowledge** and **shotgun surgery** first, because they cause *drift* — the same decision living in N places. Then Long Parameter List and Primitive Obsession (these two produce the most production bugs: positional ambiguity and `number` money). Fix in small commits with tests green at each step, one refactoring per commit, and never mixing a behaviour change into a refactor. The safety rules that matter: get a characterisation test on first, do not refactor a method you cannot test, and if you can delete the pattern and put the code back and the diff is *smaller than the pattern*, the pattern was not earning its keep.

---

**Q40. You have 30 minutes and no idea where to start on this design. What do you do?**
**Difficulty:** 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

In order: **(1) clarify** — write the requirements and the constraints, and ask about scale, consistency, and team; most design mistakes are requirement mistakes. **(2) find the data and the invariants** — the model you must not get wrong. **(3) find the tricky part** — the one requirement that is genuinely hard (here: atomic allocation, or the dedupe key) and design *that* first. **(4) name the axes of variation** and put each behind an interface. **(5) sketch the boxes and the arrows, and say the trade-offs out loud.** Then implement the tricky part first with tests, because that is where the design is validated. Also say what you are *not* doing yet and why — that is the mark of judgement, not of indecision.

---

# PHASE 11 RAPID-FIRE (60)

> Say the answer, then the reason. Target: 15 seconds each. Anything you cannot do in 15 seconds, re-read the section named in parentheses.

**1. What three things must every pattern description contain?** Intent, participants, consequences. Consequences is the one people skip. (§11.1.1)

**2. Why is Singleton wrong?** Hidden dependency, untestable, owns its own lifecycle, global mutable state, and it does not even deliver one instance per realm/worker. Test: "can I make it different in a test?" (§11.1.2)

**3. Which Singleton use is defensible?** An expensive, immutable-ish process resource (connection pool, native handle, compiled regex cache) — and even then, container-managed rather than class-managed. (§11.1.2)

**4. Factory Method vs Abstract Factory in one line.** One product, the subclass picks; a family of products, the caller picks. (§11.1.3)

**5. What problem does Builder solve?** Telescoping constructors and positional blindness; validation with a whole-object view. (§11.1.4)

**6. Builder vs options object?** Options object when fields are few and illegal combinations are expressible in types (`method: 'GET'` may not carry a `body`). Builder when fields are many or need cross-field validation. (§11.1.4)

**7. Adapter vs Facade?** Adapter wraps **one** thing and translates its interface; Facade wraps **many** subsystems and simplifies. (§11.1.5)

**8. When is Bridge worth it?** Only when you can name **two independent axes** of variation. One axis → a plain interface. (§11.1.5)

**9. Decorator: the ordering gotcha.** Logging outside retry = one log line; logging inside retry = one line per attempt. Decorators are not order-independent. (§11.1.6)

**10. What must a decorator forward?** Every method — and the wrapper drifts from the interface the first time somebody adds one. Mitigate with `implements` or a forwarding test. (§11.1.6)

**11. What free language features replace a decorator?** `Proxy`/`Reflect` (generic interception), default parameters (2-field builders), and middleware chains (function-based decorators). (§11.1.6)

**12. The classic `Proxy` footgun.** A `get` trap returning a function for every property makes the object **thenable**, so `await proxy` hangs. (§11.1.7)

**13. Why must an emitter's `emit` copy the listener set?** A listener may subscribe or unsubscribe during dispatch; mutating during iteration skips listeners (JS) or throws (other languages). (§11.1.8)

**14. Why a `Set` and not an array for listeners?** Duplicate subscriptions are a real bug class — double processing, usually only in dev. Also O(1) delete and a trivial count. (§11.1.8)

**15. Should a throwing listener stop the others?** Decide explicitly. In production: no — isolate, report, continue. (Analytics failing must not stop order confirmation.) (§11.1.8)

**16. Command vs function?** Only when you buy deferral, serialisation, or undo. Otherwise the function wins. (§11.1.9)

**17. The undo-stack invariant?** LIFO only. Undo the middle one and state corrupts. Also: commands must capture prior state at construction, and non-invertible commands must say so. (§11.1.9)

**18. Strategy vs State, one line.** Strategy: the client chooses. State: the object changes strategy as its state changes, and state objects return the next state. (§11.1.11)

**19. When is a `switch` better than Strategy?** Few, short, stable branches; the decision point is the only place that should know the options. (§11.1.10)

**20. Template Method's core weakness.** The skeleton is fixed in the base class, so "skip this step" and "reorder these" are structurally impossible — the hook explosion follows. Inject the steps instead. (§11.1.12)

**21. Data Mapper vs Active Record?** Mapper: two representations, an explicit translation, domain objects free of the driver. Active Record: the object saves itself, which couples the domain to the ORM. (§11.1.13)

**22. Three things a Unit of Work solves?** Identity, change tracking, and transaction demarcation. (§11.1.13)

**23. The Unit of Work gotcha nobody mentions.** Flush order — parents before children, or FK violations and silent inconsistency. Sort explicitly, never rely on Map iteration order. (§11.1.13)

**24. DI without a container — the mechanism.** Constructor injection plus one composition-root function. Greppable, typed, debuggable, and free. (§11.1.14)

**25. Which DI kind is an anti-pattern?** Service locator (`Container.get(X)` inside a method): it hides dependencies completely and is untestable without container config. (§11.1.14)

**26. Which DI lifetime is usually correct?** **Scoped** (per request): current user, transaction, request logger. Not singleton — that is how one user's data leaks to another. (§11.1.14)

**27. Resilience pattern with the best value per risk?** **Timeout.** Always. Non-negotiable on every remote call. (§11.1.15)

**28. Why jitter on retry?** Without it, N clients retry at the same instant after a failure and re-create the thundering herd you were trying to relieve. (§11.1.15)

**29. Circuit breaker states?** Closed → Open (threshold) → Half-Open (cooldown, few probes) → Closed (successes). (§11.1.15)

**30. Why not count consecutive failures?** Low-traffic endpoints: one unlucky request at 3am opens the breaker for the night. Use a ratio over a window with a minimum sample count. (§11.1.15)

**31. What is a bulkhead?** A per-dependency cap on concurrent work (or separate pools), so one slow dependency cannot starve healthy ones. (§11.1.15)

**32. Saga vs 2PC?** 2PC needs one transaction manager across all participants and blocks everything if any is down. Saga = local transactions plus compensating actions. Eventual, weaker, but autonomous. (§11.1.16)
