**33. Orchestrated vs choreographed Saga?** Orchestration puts the sequence in one file (easy to debug, easy to extend). Choreography puts it nowhere (scales, but "add a step" means editing every service). (§11.1.16)

**34. Compensation vs rollback?** You cannot undo an observable side effect — an email cannot be unsent. Compensation is a *semantic* undo designed with the domain, and it can itself fail and must be retried idempotently. (§11.1.16)

**35. Event vs command — one test.** "If the handler does not exist, is that a bug?" Event: no. Command: yes. Also: events are past tense, commands are imperative. (§11.1.17)

**36. What does EDA remove, and what does it keep?** Removes temporal and deployment coupling; **keeps** semantic coupling (and needs governance for it). (§11.1.17)

**37. "Exactly-once delivery" — the honest answer.** It does not exist. You get **effectively-once processing**: an inbox row with a unique message id, written in the same local transaction as the effect. (§11.1.17)

**38. LRU cache: why O(1)?** A JS `Map` preserves insertion order, so recency order *is* insertion order if you `delete` then `set` on access. Re-`set`ting alone does **not** move the key. (§11.4.2)

**39. `peek` vs `get` on a cache?** `peek` must not reorder. A monitoring read that promotes changes eviction — a classic failing test. (§11.4.2)

**40. Token bucket vs fixed window?** Fixed window allows a 2× burst at the boundary (10 at :59 + 10 at :00). Token bucket expresses a sustained rate **and** a burst allowance; it is the sensible default for APIs. (§11.4.3)

**41. Why Lua for a Redis rate limiter?** Check and increment must be one atomic operation. A read-then-write lets N concurrent requests all pass. (§11.4.3)

**42. Fail open or closed on a limiter outage?** Public abuse protection: fail open (or fall back to a local limiter). Billing/quota: fail closed. Say which, and metric it. (§11.4.3)

**43. Parking lot: where is the concurrency fix?** Inside `Slot.occupy` — the check-and-set is owned by the slot, not by the allocator, which only *selects*. (§11.4.1)

**44. Parking lot: how do you make the queries cheap?** Index free slots as a `Set<SlotRef>` per size per floor, updated on occupy/vacate, and `Map<Color, Set<SlotRef>>` for colour queries. (§11.4.1)

**45. Elevator: when may the car reverse?** Only when it is empty **and** there is no pending request behind it. Continue if passengers want that direction or a hall call exists there. (§11.4.4)

**46. Elevator: separate what from what?** **Mechanics** (the car: motion, doors, capacity, safety) from **policy** (which car answers a call). Policy is a Strategy because it varies by deployment, not by state. (§11.4.4)

**47. Elevator: how does a real building work?** Destination control — the passenger picks a floor before boarding, so the scheduler groups trips and plans the whole set. Different problem, better energy profile. (§11.4.4)

**48. Splitwise: the master invariant.** Sum of all balances is **zero**, always. Assert it in code — it is the cheapest possible corruption detector. (§11.4.5)

**49. Splitwise: rounding.** Integer minor units only. One documented, deterministic remainder rule, and an assertion that the shares sum exactly to the total. (§11.4.5)

**50. Debt simplification: algorithm and complexity.** Greedy: sort debtors and creditors by magnitude, match largest-first. O(n log n), at most n−1 transfers. It is **not** provably minimal — the exact minimum is a subset-sum problem. (§11.4.5)

**51. Tic-tac-toe: why terminal score depends on depth?** `10 − depth` for a win, `−10 + depth` for a loss, so the AI prefers fast wins and slow losses. Without it the AI is indifferent and plays absurdly. (§11.4.6)

**52. Tic-tac-toe: what does alpha-beta change?** Only the nodes visited, never the result — for a full-depth search it is exactly minimax. Bugs produce a *plausible but wrong* AI, which is why you property-test it. (§11.4.6)

**53. Notification service: the dedupe key.** `${user}|${event}|${eventId}|${channel}` — it must include the channel (so cascading email→SMS is allowed) and the event id (so two distinct events are not collapsed). Enforce with a unique index. (§11.4.7)

**54. Notification service: cascade or broadcast?** Decide explicitly. "First successful channel wins" is most common; "all enabled channels" is for some products. Either way, state it — it is a product decision disguised as a technical one. (§11.4.7)

**55. Logger: what is the hot-path rule?** Filter on level **before** building anything. A disabled log line must cost ~0; `JSON.stringify` happens at drain time. (§11.4.8)

**56. Logger: what happens when the aggregator is slow?** Bounded buffer, drop the **lowest** severity, count the drops, never block the application. Decide and publish the policy. (§11.4.8)

**57. Logger: where does redaction happen?** In the sink layer, on every record, driven by key patterns and PII regexes. At call sites it fails the first time somebody logs a request body. (§11.4.8)

**58. Logger: how do you get a trace id into a 30-deep call tree without threading a parameter?** `AsyncLocalStorage` (Node) or the OpenTelemetry context, attached once in middleware and read from a child logger. (§11.4.8)

**59. Library system: title vs copy?** The title is the catalogue record and the unit of **reservation**; the copy is the physical item and the unit of **borrowing**. Only copies are lent; reservations queue on the title. (§11.4.9)

**60. Library system: two members, one copy, both reserve.** Not a `synchronized` block — a unique constraint on the active reservation per copy plus a serialisable transaction (or `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`) on the title row, and a deterministic queue-position tie-break. (§11.4.9)

---

# PHASE 11 FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

Use this as a self-audit. Anything you cannot tick, re-read the section in parentheses. Tick honestly — the goal is to find the three gaps worth spending the next evening on, not to collect 100 ticks.

## A. Patterns — can I explain, not just recognise?

- [ ] I can state intent, participants, and consequences for any GoF pattern I name. (§11.1.1)
- [ ] I can argue **against** Singleton on five named grounds, and name the one defensible use. (§11.1.2)
- [ ] I can say Factory Method vs Abstract Factory in one sentence each, with the axis-count rule. (§11.1.3)
- [ ] I can write a Builder with real validation, and explain when an options object is better. (§11.1.4)
- [ ] I can distinguish Adapter (one), Facade (many), Bridge (two axes) without hesitating. (§11.1.5)
- [ ] I can explain decorator ordering, the forwarding tax, and the `Proxy` thenable trap. (§11.1.6, §11.1.7)
- [ ] I can enumerate the six design decisions in an event emitter and defend each. (§11.1.8)
- [ ] I know the undo-stack invariant and why compensation is not rollback. (§11.1.9, §11.1.16)
- [ ] I can distinguish Strategy from State without relying on a mnemonic. (§11.1.10, §11.1.11)
- [ ] I can name Template Method's hook-explosion failure and its composition replacement. (§11.1.12)
- [ ] I can say what Repository/UoW/Data Mapper each solve, and where Active Record is fine. (§11.1.13)
- [ ] I can argue manual DI over a container, and name the two DI lifetimes that matter. (§11.1.14)
- [ ] I can name the resilience family and give the one-line rule for each. (§11.1.15)
- [ ] I can state what event-driven architecture **does not** decouple. (§11.1.17)
- [ ] I can name at least six pattern anti-patterns with their fixes. (§11.1.18)

## B. OOP, SOLID, clean code

- [ ] I can give a before/after that makes an invalid state unrepresentable. (§11.2.1)
- [ ] I can show composition replacing a combinatorial hierarchy, **and** an inheritance case I would keep. (§11.2.2)
- [ ] I can write the before/after for all five SOLID principles. (§11.2.3)
- [ ] I can apply DRY to *knowledge* rather than text, and name the four DRY failure modes. (§11.2.4)
- [ ] I can define the stopping rule for abstraction ("two implementations, a domain name, or a volatile dependency"). (§11.2.4)
- [ ] I can name my naming rules, especially units and the four-name-per-concept rule. (§11.2.5)
- [ ] I can fix four smells with the named refactoring for each. (§11.2.6)
- [ ] I can draw hexagonal ports and adapters and name six boundary leaks. (§11.2.7)
- [ ] I can name the five DDD building blocks and the aggregate rules. (§11.2.8)
- [ ] I can defend an anemic model for a *named* case, not in general. (§11.2.9)
- [ ] I can describe the legacy workflow: characterisation test → seam → small refactor → strangler. (§11.2.10)

## C. Architectural styles

- [ ] I can argue the monolith is the default, with four reasons microservices do not fix. (§11.3.1)
- [ ] I know four ways to enforce module boundaries, and that architecture tests are the one that works. (§11.3.2)
- [ ] I can name the seven leaks of layered architecture. (§11.3.3)
- [ ] I can state microservices' three real gains and ten costs. (§11.3.4)
- [ ] I can complete the six-criterion decision card and say what the score means. (§11.3.5)
- [ ] I can explain dual-write and implement an outbox relay with a claim. (§11.3.6)
- [ ] I know the three CQRS levels and the read-your-writes options. (§11.3.7)
- [ ] I can give the honest event-sourcing trade-off, including upcasters and erasure. (§11.3.8)
- [ ] I can name the four serverless constraints and the function-per-request trap. (§11.3.9)
- [ ] I can define a BFF and name its three jobs and three non-jobs. (§11.3.10)
- [ ] I know what a mesh buys, what it costs, and that the control plane must be out of the data path. (§11.3.11)
- [ ] I can talk about CRDTs and quorum replication without overclaiming. (§11.3.12)
- [ ] I can walk the six questions and defend a decision without saying "it depends". (§11.3.13)

## D. Low-level design — can I build these cold?

- [ ] Parking lot: atomic allocation, two strategies, indexed queries, fee rounding. (§11.4.1)
- [ ] LRU cache: O(1) both ways, `peek` semantics, TTL, single-flight, LFU variant. (§11.4.2)
- [ ] Rate limiter: three algorithms, Redis atomicity, fail-open/closed, idle eviction. (§11.4.3)
- [ ] Elevator: state machine with a transition table, reversal rules, dispatch strategies. (§11.4.4)
- [ ] Splitwise: integer money, Σ=0 invariant, greedy simplification, deletion reversal. (§11.4.5)
- [ ] Tic-tac-toe: immutability, undo, minimax + alpha-beta, a property test. (§11.4.6)
- [ ] Notification service: preferences precedence, cascade, dedupe key, retries, DLQ. (§11.4.7)
- [ ] Logger: early exit, bounded buffer, single-flight drain, redaction, dedupe. (§11.4.8)
- [ ] Library system: title vs copy, reservation hold window, renewal rules, fine policy. (§11.4.9)
- [ ] I have implemented and **run** at least six of the ten from-scratch problems. (§11.4 Problems)

## E. Interview performance

- [ ] I can produce a class diagram for all nine case studies in under three minutes each.
- [ ] I can state the state and invariants for all nine without looking.
- [ ] For each case study I can name the "tricky part" and the concurrency answer in one sentence.
- [ ] I can give an extension point for every case study that a real product would need.
- [ ] I have critiqued all twelve review scenarios and ranked defects by severity.
- [ ] I can answer any of the 40 Q&A questions in under 90 seconds with the trade-off included.
- [ ] I can do all 60 rapid-fire items in 15 seconds each.

## F. Final self-check — the three questions that decide the interview

- [ ] **Can I name a requirement behind every abstraction I create?** If not, I am guessing and should revert.
- [ ] **Can I name the trade-off I paid for each design decision?** If not, it was not a decision.
- [ ] **Can I state what I deliberately did NOT do, and why?** This is the strongest signal of senior judgement.

---

## Where to spend the remaining evening

Rank your gaps by *frequency × severity* and fix at most three:

1. **Any unchecked item in section A or B** — the fundamentals get asked in almost every system-design round, including frontend and data roles. Fix these first.
2. **Any unchecked item in section C** — the decision framework (C13) is the single most-likely artifact in a senior interview. If you cannot complete the decision card, practise that.
3. **Any unchecked item in D or E** — pick the case studies you cannot draw from memory and rehearse them out loud, then implement the two you have never coded.

Do not spend the evening re-reading. Spend it **producing**: draw the diagrams, write the code, say the answers out loud. Design skill is procedural, not declarative — you improve it by running the procedure under time pressure, not by re-reading the manual.

---

# PHASE 11 COMPLETE

You now have the vocabulary (patterns, SOLID, DDD), the structural toolkit (ports and adapters, modular monoliths, strangler fig), the architectural decision framework, nine drilled low-level designs with their state and invariants, ten implementations you can rebuild cold, twelve broken designs you can critique, forty answered questions, and sixty rapid-fire definitions.

The three things that actually decide these interviews are not the patterns. They are:

1. **Naming the requirement behind every abstraction** — and being willing to delete the abstraction when there is not one.
2. **Naming the trade-off you paid** for each structural decision, out loud, before the interviewer asks.
3. **Naming what you deliberately did not do**, and why. Scope discipline is the clearest signal of senior judgement, and it is the cheapest thing to demonstrate.

Carry the same discipline into Phase 12.
